# Dietary Supplements: Pros, Cons and Verdicts

Every product below carries an explicit verdict, an evidence grade, the dose that was actually tested, and its pros and cons listed separately so neither can hide the other.

> **This is not medical advice.** It cannot replace a clinician who knows your history, medication list and blood results.

| | |
|---|---|
| Products with an explicit verdict | 66 |
| Worth taking, for named groups | 10 |
| With published evidence of harm | 14 |
| Sourced food composition rows | 517 |
| Where food can replace the supplement dose | 20 |
| Fabricated references found | 0 |

## 1. How to read this

Two columns do independent work and must be read together. **Verdict** says what to do. **Grade** says how confident the literature is about the answer, and the answer can be no.

| Verdict | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **TAKE** (Take it) | Worth taking if you are in the group named under Who. Evidence is strong for that group and for that purpose only. |
| **CONSIDER** (Consider) | Defensible in one narrow situation. Outside that situation there is no case for it. |
| **SKIP** (Skip it) | No good evidence of benefit for the marketed claim. Money wasted rather than harm done, unless a Cons entry says otherwise. |
| **AVOID** (Avoid) | Published evidence of harm in randomised trials, prospective registries or national surveillance. |

| Grade | Evidence base |
|---|---|
| **A** | Consistent meta-analyses of randomised trials with clinical endpoints. |
| **B** | Meta-analyses with surrogate endpoints, or good trials with mixed results. |
| **C** | Small or short randomised trials only. |
| **D** | Mechanistic, animal or observational evidence only, or the claim has been refuted. |

### The food data asks two separate questions

| Badge | Question it answers |
|---|---|
| **In food** | Does the substance occur in ordinary food at all? Rich, trace only, manufactured, or from a plant nobody eats as food. |
| **Dose from food** | Could you reach the dose used in the trials by eating? This is the question that actually decides whether food can stand in for the pill. |

> **Worked example.** Vitamin E is genuinely rich in food: one tablespoon of wheatgerm oil covers the daily reference intake by itself. But the dose in the harm trials was 400 IU, which is 268 mg of alpha-tocopherol, roughly 18 times the reference intake, and no realistic diet reaches it. So vitamin E reads Rich in food and Dose not reachable at the same time, and both are true. Of 66 products, 44 have rich food sources but only 20 let you reach the supplement dose by eating.


### The nine misreadings this document is built to prevent

1. A high grade does not mean it works. It means we are confident about the answer, and the answer can be no. Selenium is Grade A in this document because Cochrane established with HIGH certainty that it does NOT prevent cancer. Read the Grade column together with the Verdict column, never on its own.
2. A grade attaches to one specific claim, not to the substance in general. Vitamin D3 is Grade A for correcting documented deficiency and Grade D for preventing cancer or cardiovascular disease. Those are the same molecule and two different questions.
3. An official statement of insufficient evidence is not an endorsement. The USPSTF gives multivitamins an I statement. That means the trials could neither prove benefit nor rule out harm. The industry quotes it as vindication.
4. No upper limit set means nobody has assessed it. It is a gap in the science, not a finding of safety. Most botanicals and every probiotic in this document fall into that gap.
5. Risk ratios below 1 mean fewer events, above 1 mean more events. When the confidence interval crosses 1, no effect was demonstrated. That is the entire logic of Figure 5.
6. Relative reductions are not absolute reductions. REDUCE-IT is quoted everywhere as a 25% reduction. The absolute reduction was 4.8 percentage points over 4.9 years, meaning about 21 people take the drug for one to benefit.
7. The number on a mineral label is usually the compound, not the mineral. Magnesium oxide is about 60% magnesium, magnesium citrate about 16%. A capsule labelled 500 mg magnesium citrate delivers roughly 80 mg of magnesium.
8. The safety record of a food does not transfer to a concentrated extract of it. Drinking green tea is fine. Green tea extract has caused more than 100 published cases of liver injury, 8% of which went to transplant.
9. A certification mark says nothing about whether the product works. USP Verified, NSF and Informed Sport all certify content and purity. None of them assesses efficacy, and USP Verified does not screen for substances banned in sport.

## 2. Why the market looks like this

Under the US Dietary Supplement Health and Education Act of 1994, supplements are legally a category of food. There is no pre-market approval for safety or efficacy, and no statutory cap on the dose of an ingredient. Global market about USD 209.5bn in 2025; the industry grew from USD 4bn and about 4,000 products in 1994 to about USD 200bn worldwide and 50,000-80,000 products, while FDA inspects roughly 5% of manufacturing facilities per year. Of roughly 44,000 health claims submitted in the EU, about 4,600 were assessed and about 250 authorised.

### What is actually in the bottle

| Finding | Rate | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Melatonin gummies inaccurately labelled | 88% | 22 of 25; content 74-347% of label |
| FDA-recalled products still spiked ~34 mo later | 67% | 18 of 27, JAMA 2014 |
| Amazon immune-support products with wrong labels | 57% | 17 of 30, JAMA Netw Open 2022 |
| Products sold as SARMs with no labelled SARM | 48% | 21 of 44, JAMA 2017 |
| Botanicals failing DNA authentication | 27% | 5,957 products, 37 countries |
| Ayurvedic products with lead, mercury or arsenic | 21% | 193 products; rasa shastra 40.6% |

## 3. Verdict table

| Verdict | Product | Grade | Who it is for |
|---|---|---|---|
| **TAKE** | Folic acid | A | Anyone capable of becoming pregnant. |
| **TAKE** | Vitamin D3 (cholecalciferol) | A | People with serum 25(OH)D below about 30 nmol/L: dark skin at high latitude, covered clothing, housebound, northern-European winter. |
| **TAKE** | Vitamin B12 (cobalamin) | B | Vegans and long-term vegetarians, metformin users, people on a proton pump inhibitor for over two years, post-bariatric patients, pernicious anaemia. |
| **TAKE** | Iron | A | Menstruating women with ferritin at or below about 50 µg/L, pregnancy, blood donors, post-bariatric patients. |
| **TAKE** | Iodine | A | Preconception, pregnancy and lactation, vegans, and anyone avoiding both dairy and fish. |
| **TAKE** | Creatine monohydrate | A | Anyone doing resistance training, and older adults in particular. Also vegetarians and vegans, whose baseline stores are lower. |
| **TAKE** | Protein powder | A | People doing resistance training who are currently falling short of 1.6 g/kg/d from food. |
| **TAKE** | Psyllium husk (ispaghula) | B | People with raised LDL cholesterol, irritable bowel syndrome of either subtype, or chronic constipation. |
| **TAKE** | Caffeine | A | Almost anyone training, trained or untrained. Also shift workers and the sleep-deprived, for the separate alertness effect. |
| **TAKE** | Omega-3 (EPA and DHA) | B | People with severe hypertriglyceridaemia. Separately, pregnant women with genuinely low baseline DHA, and people who eat essentially no fish. |
| **CONSIDER** | Magnesium | B | People with documented low magnesium, chronic proton pump inhibitor or diuretic users, alcohol use disorder, and treated hypertensives. |
| **CONSIDER** | Zinc lozenges | B | Someone at the very start of a cold who will actually follow the lozenge schedule. |
| **CONSIDER** | Melatonin | B | Jet lag eastward across five or more time zones, delayed sleep-wake phase disorder, shift work, and blind people with free-running rhythms. |
| **CONSIDER** | Probiotics, named strain only | B | Children on a course of antibiotics, adults on antibiotics with elevated C. difficile risk, and exclusively breastfed infants with colic. |
| **CONSIDER** | Berberine | B | Adults with type 2 diabetes or prediabetes not reaching glycaemic targets, as an add-on, with a prescriber informed. |
| **CONSIDER** | Vitamin C | C | Smokers, heavy alcohol users, people on very restricted diets, and those under extreme physical stress such as marathon runners. |
| **CONSIDER** | Coenzyme Q10 / ubiquinol | B | Patients with heart failure with reduced ejection fraction, already on guideline therapy, with cardiology input. |
| **CONSIDER** | Alpha-lipoic acid | B | People with symptomatic diabetic distal symmetric polyneuropathy, specifically positive sensory symptoms such as burning and paraesthesia. |
| **CONSIDER** | Beta-alanine | C | Athletes whose limiting factor is acidosis in the one to four minute window: middle distance, rowing, combat sports, high-repetition lifting. |
| **CONSIDER** | Dietary nitrate / beetroot | C | Recreationally active people rather than elite athletes, and separately anyone using it for blood pressure. |
| **CONSIDER** | St John's wort | B | Adults with mild to moderate unipolar depression who take NO other medication whatsoever and no hormonal contraception. |
| **CONSIDER** | Lactase and pancreatic enzymes | C | Adults with hydrogen-breath-test-confirmed lactose maldigestion, and patients with objectively confirmed exocrine pancreatic insufficiency. |
| **SKIP** | Multivitamin / multimineral | A | Genuine deficiency or high-probability deficiency: post-bariatric, malabsorption, strict vegans, long-term metformin users, heavy alcohol use, the housebound. |
| **SKIP** | Collagen peptides | B | Nobody, on the best available analysis. |
| **SKIP** | Glucosamine and chondroitin | B | At most, people with moderate to severe knee osteoarthritis pain, on subgroup data only. |
| **SKIP** | NMN (nicotinamide mononucleotide) | D | Nobody demonstrably. |
| **SKIP** | Nicotinamide riboside (NR) | C | People with peripheral artery disease have the single best-supported claim, and it is thin. |
| **SKIP** | Resveratrol | D | Possibly people who already have type 2 diabetes. The healthy-ageing consumer is in the null subgroup. |
| **SKIP** | Spermidine | D | Nobody demonstrably. |
| **SKIP** | Curcumin / turmeric extract | C | At most, people with symptomatic knee osteoarthritis, if the meta-analytic signal survives the bias problem. |
| **SKIP** | MSM (methylsulfonylmethane) | D | No population has a defensible claim. |
| **SKIP** | Astaxanthin | D | Nobody with a demonstrated clinical outcome. |
| **SKIP** | HMB | D | On the balance of umbrella reviews, at most untrained beginners or clinically muscle-wasting populations. |
| **SKIP** | Ginkgo biloba | A | Nobody, for preventing dementia. Possibly people with established dementia, on low-certainty evidence. |
| **SKIP** | Valerian | D | On present evidence, nobody reliably. |
| **SKIP** | Rhodiola rosea | D | Possibly people with subjective mental fatigue, on evidence too weak to justify confidence. |
| **SKIP** | Lion's mane (Hericium erinaceus) | C | Possibly older adults with mild cognitive impairment, on the basis of a single 30-person trial. |
| **SKIP** | Bacopa monnieri | C | Healthy adults willing to take it daily for 12 weeks for a modest change in processing speed. |
| **SKIP** | 5-HTP | C | No group can be identified with confidence. |
| **SKIP** | Vitamin K2 (MK-7 and MK-4) | C | Nobody with solid evidence of clinical benefit. |
| **SKIP** | Generic multi-strain probiotic blends | B | See the strain-specific entry in the Consider tier. A blend with no strain designators has nothing behind it. |
| **SKIP** | B. infantis 35624 (single strain) | D | On current evidence, no clearly identifiable group. |
| **SKIP** | Krill oil | C | At most, people wanting a given rise in omega-3 index from a smaller dose. That is a biomarker advantage. |
| **SKIP** | Cod liver oil | D | Arguably nobody, given modern alternatives. |
| **SKIP** | ALA from flaxseed, chia or walnut oil | C | People who eat no marine products at all and will not take algal oil. It at least maintains essential fatty acid status. |
| **SKIP** | Algal oil (vegan DHA and EPA) | C | Vegans and vegetarians, people avoiding fish contaminants, and people with fish or shellfish allergy. |
| **SKIP** | Wheat bran and insoluble fibre supplements for IBS | D | Nobody with irritable bowel syndrome. |
| **SKIP** | Over-the-counter digestive enzyme blends | D | No identified group. Use a matched single enzyme for a diagnosed deficiency instead. |
| **SKIP** | L-glutamine for gut repair | C | Narrowly, post-infectious diarrhoea-predominant IBS with objectively documented increased intestinal permeability. |
| **SKIP** | Prebiotics: inulin, FOS, GOS | C | Adults with chronic constipation and habitually low fibre intake who tolerate fermentation. |
| **SKIP** | Saffron, SAMe and other mood botanicals | C | At most, adults with mild to moderate depression taking no serotonergic medication, and only with a clinician involved. |
| **SKIP** | Quercetin and taurine | C | At most, people with elevated blood pressure or components of metabolic syndrome, for a small surrogate effect. |
| **AVOID** | Beta-carotene supplements | A | Essentially nobody at supplemental doses. Narrow exceptions: clinical vitamin A deficiency and erythropoietic protoporphyria. |
| **AVOID** | Vitamin E at or above 400 IU/d | A | Documented deficiency, which in practice means fat malabsorption: abetalipoproteinaemia, cholestatic liver disease, cystic fibrosis, short bowel. |
| **AVOID** | Selenium at 200 µg/d | A | People with genuinely low selenium status: low-soil regions, long-term parenteral nutrition, severe malabsorption. |
| **AVOID** | Preformed vitamin A at or above 10,000 IU | D | Clinical vitamin A deficiency in low-income settings, fat malabsorption, cystic fibrosis. In replete Western adults, nobody. |
| **AVOID** | High-dose vitamin B6 | B | People on isoniazid, cycloserine, hydralazine or penicillamine, where B6 is protective against drug-induced neuropathy. Pregnant women with nausea and vomiting, at obstetric direction. |
| **AVOID** | Green tea extract (EGCG) | B | Nobody for the marketed indications. Drinking green tea is fine and is a different exposure. |
| **AVOID** | Ashwagandha (Withania somnifera) | C | No population with a demonstrated clinical need. Anyone with liver disease should be steered away entirely. |
| **AVOID** | Weight-loss, fat-burner and energy blends | D | No identifiable group. This is the worst-behaving corner of the entire supplement market. |
| **AVOID** | Bodybuilding and test-booster products | D | Nobody. Any muscle gain is attributable to undeclared controlled substances the user did not consent to take. |
| **AVOID** | Sexual-enhancement products | A | Nobody, from any retailer, at any price. |
| **AVOID** | High-dose folic acid plus B12 for cardiovascular prevention | D | Nobody for cardiovascular prevention. Periconceptional folic acid at 400 µg is a completely different indication and should not be conflated with this. |
| **AVOID** | Antioxidants during chemotherapy | D | Nobody. Documented deficiency should be corrected under oncology supervision, with timing discussed against the chemotherapy schedule. |
| **AVOID** | Probiotics in critical illness | B | Nobody in critical illness. See the strain-specific entry for the two indications where probiotics do work. |
| **AVOID** | Calcium supplements on top of an adequate diet | B | People with genuinely low dietary calcium, below about 600-700 mg/d, particularly institutionalised elderly, and patients on long-term glucocorticoids. |

## 4. Take it

### Folic acid

**Verdict:** TAKE &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** 400 µg/d, from at least one month before conception. 4 mg/d if a previous pregnancy was affected.
- **Who it is for:** Anyone capable of becoming pregnant.

**Pros**

- Neural tube defects RR 0.31 (95% CI 0.17-0.58) across 5 trials and 6,708 births. This is the strongest single result in the whole supplement literature.
- The MRC Vitamin Study (n=1,817 high-risk women) showed a 72% protective effect at 4 mg/d.
- Population fortification works at scale: US grain fortification prevents about 1,326 affected births per year.
- Costs a few pounds a year. No meaningful downside at 400 µg.

**Cons**

- Never take it alone when B12 status is unknown. Folic acid corrects the anaemia of B12 deficiency while the neurological damage silently progresses. This is the stated basis of the upper limit.
- High-dose folic acid plus B12 taken for cardiovascular reasons raised cancer incidence (HR 1.21) and cancer mortality (HR 1.38) in pooled Norwegian trials. That is a different product at a different dose for a different purpose, and it should not be confused with the 400 µg periconceptional dose.
- Upper limit 1,000 µg/d of synthetic folate, which applies to supplements and fortified food but not to folate from whole food.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** No food source, manufactured
- **Dose from food:** Dose partly reachable
- **Data confidence:** High

Folic acid (pteroylmonoglutamic acid) is manufactured by chemical synthesis, a Waller-type condensation of a triaminopyrimidinone with a three-carbon unit and p-aminobenzoyl-L-glutamic acid, giving a fully oxidised molecule carrying a single glutamate that does not occur in unfortified food. Ordinary food supplies reduced folate polyglutamates instead, chiefly 5-methyltetrahydrofolate, and USDA's component structure keeps 'folic acid' and 'folate, food' as separate entries: beef liver, lentils, spinach, chickpeas, asparagus and boiled broccoli all read folic acid 0 with folate food equal to folate total. Two distinct answers therefore sit inside one word, so they are separated here: the nutrient class folate is rich in ordinary food, while the marketed molecule folic acid enters the food supply only through fortification and enrichment, which is what every food row below is. Enriched pasta and enriched white rice are the other mandated US enrichment vehicles; their contents are label-dependent per product and no verified figure was available, so they get no row.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Ready-to-eat breakfast cereal, folic-acid fortified (brand-specific) | Label-dependent. A product declaring 400 ug folic acid per serving works out near 1,000 ug/100 g at a 40 g serving, but serving weights differ by brand. | 1 serving (about 40 g, per label) | 400 ug folic acid (680 ug DFE) for a product fortified at that level | 100% of a 400 ug periconceptional folic acid dose in one bowl | Indicative and unconfirmed: category figure for US cereals fortified to 400 ug folic acid per serving, quoted from the verification report and not traceable to a single FoodData Central record. Current US labels declare folate in ug DFE against a 400 ug DFE DV, so the folic acid content has to be read off the specific product. |
| Yeast extract spread (Marmite type), folic-acid fortified (single analysed sample, brand-specific) | 2,990 ug folic acid (plus 793 ug food folate; 5,880 ug DFE) | 1 tsp (6 g) | 179 ug folic acid (353 ug DFE) | 45% of a 400 ug periconceptional folic acid dose | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 167717, SR Legacy. Single analysed profile: fortification level is set per product, so this figure does not carry across brands or across the UK, AU and US formulations of the same brand. USDA's own components do not reconcile exactly (3,790 total minus 2,990 folic acid = 800, printed food folate 793). |
| Instant oatmeal, flavoured, dry, fortified (QUAKER maple and brown sugar) | 114 ug folic acid (208 ug DFE) | 1 packet (43 g) | 49.0 ug folic acid (89 ug DFE) | 12% of a 400 ug folic acid dose | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173907 (folic acid 114 ug, folate food 14 ug, folate total 128 ug, DFE 208 ug) |
| Bread, wheat, commercially prepared from enriched flour | 20 ug folic acid + 65 ug food folate (99 ug DFE) | 1 slice (29 g) | 5.8 ug folic acid (28.7 ug DFE) | 1.5% of a 400 ug folic acid dose | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 172686, SR Legacy |

- **To match the supplement dose:** Two routes with different answers. As folic acid, the 400 ug periconceptional dose is reachable in one sitting only from fortified products: one bowl of ready-to-eat cereal fortified to 400 ug folic acid per serving is the entire dose, whereas matching it from yeast extract spread takes about 13 g (a bit over two teaspoons), from instant fortified oatmeal 8.2 packets, and from enriched wheat bread about 69 slices. As natural food folate the equivalent target is 680 ug DFE (400 x 1.7), and this is where the verification pass forced a correction: the submission's cheapest option was 376 g of cooked lentils, but simmered chicken liver at 578 ug food folate per 100 g (FDC 171061) needs only 118 g, under three 44 g livers, which is an ordinary portion, and pan-fried lamb liver at 400 ug DFE per 100 g (FDC 172533) needs 170 g. The plant routes stay awkward: 376 g cooked lentils (1.9 cups), 219 g prepared edamame (1.4 cups), 300 g dried sunflower kernels, 351 g raw spinach (about twelve 30 g cups), 395 g cooked chickpeas, 456 g cooked asparagus (about thirty spears) or 630 g boiled broccoli. So the plain answer is that a person can eat their way to the folate equivalent of the 400 ug dose through liver or through fortified food, and cannot realistically do it through leafy greens; the UK high-risk 5 mg dose (8,500 ug DFE, 1.47 kg of chicken liver or 4.7 kg of cooked lentils every day) is unreachable by any route. Liver carries its own pregnancy restriction on preformed vitamin A grounds, which is why it is rarely offered as the answer to the population that needs the dose.
- **Food versus supplement:** Folic acid is not bioidentical to food folate and the difference cuts both ways. It must be reduced by dihydrofolate reductase before entering one-carbon metabolism, and human DHFR capacity is low: unmetabolised folic acid appears in serum above a threshold of about 266 ug of folic acid per meal (Kelly et al., Am J Clin Nutr 1997;65:1790-5, PMID 9174474), which a 400 ug tablet exceeds. That is why the adult UL of 1,000 ug/day applies to folic acid from supplements and fortified food with no UL on food folate: high folic acid can correct the megaloblastic anaemia of B12 deficiency while neurological damage continues, masking the diagnosis. Food folate has no toxicity ceiling and the synthetic form does. Running the other way, the quantity of food folate is not the only obstacle. Cuskelly, McNulty and Scott randomised 62 women to an extra 400 ug/day as a folic acid supplement, as folic-acid-fortified food, as natural food folate, or as dietary advice: red cell folate rose only in the supplement and fortified-food arms and did not move in the food-folate arms despite documented increases in intake (Lancet 1996;347:657-9, PMID 8596381). The two trials that established the neural-tube-defect effect used synthetic folic acid, 4 mg/day in the MRC Vitamin Study (Lancet 1991;338:131-7, PMID 1677062) and 0.8 mg/day in a multivitamin (Czeizel and Dudas, N Engl J Med 1992;327:1832-5, PMID 1307234). Substituting leafy greens for the tablet before pregnancy has never been tested against that endpoint. On L-5-methyltetrahydrofolate (calcium methylfolate), the marketed 'MTHFR-friendly' alternative: EFSA's 2022 derivation of DFE factors for it judged 5-MTHF more bioavailable than folic acid at supplement doses of 400 ug/day and above (2.0 versus 1.7; PMC9399872), so the case against it is not absorption. It is that no periconceptional trial has been run on it, and the neural-tube-defect evidence base is specifically folic acid (Czeizel, Ann Nutr Metab 2011, PMID 21865678).
- **Absorption:** Food folate is roughly 50% available against about 85% for folic acid eaten with food and close to 100% fasting, which is where the 1.7 and 2.0 DFE multipliers come from (Suitor and Bailey, J Am Diet Assoc 2000;100:88-94, PMID 10646010). Food folate is a polyglutamate that must be deconjugated at the brush border before absorption, and it is chemically fragile. USDA Table of Nutrient Retention Factors Release 6 (2007) gives folate retention of 45% for legumes boiled 45-75 min and drained (code 0521), 30% for legumes boiled 2-2.5 h (code 0541), 60% for greens boiled in water to cover and drained (code 3005) and 90% for potatoes baked in the skin (code 3301). A cup of dried lentils simmered for an hour loses over half its folate, so a composition table value describes the cooked food only when the cooking matches. Folic acid in a fortified food or a tablet is stable and its dose is what the label says.
- **Data note:** Every per-100 g composition value, gram weight and per-portion figure in the food rows and the dose comparison traces to a named USDA FoodData Central record and recomputes exactly, and all six cited PMIDs resolve to the stated journal, volume and pages. Two qualifications: the fortified-cereal row is an indicative label figure rather than a sourced composition record, and one uncited bioavailability claim was removed rather than kept

*References: Cochrane CD007950.pub3 (PMID 26662928); MRC PMID 1677062; Williams PMID 25590678; Ebbing PMID 19920236*

### Vitamin D3 (cholecalciferol)

**Verdict:** TAKE &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** 800-2,000 IU/d, taken daily. Never as an annual or monthly bolus.
- **Who it is for:** People with serum 25(OH)D below about 30 nmol/L: dark skin at high latitude, covered clothing, housebound, northern-European winter.

**Pros**

- Correcting genuine deficiency is real medicine: it treats rickets and osteomalacia, and no other intervention does.
- With calcium, it cuts hip fracture in frail institutionalised elderly, RR 0.84 (0.74-0.96) across 9 trials and 49,853 people.
- Deficiency is genuinely common in the target groups: 17.5% of non-Hispanic Black Americans are below 30 nmol/L versus 2.1% of white Americans, and 17.7% of Europeans are below it in the October to March window.
- Costs almost nothing. Generic D3 at 1,000 IU is a few pounds a year.

**Cons**

- It does not prevent cancer or cardiovascular disease. VITAL (n=25,871, 5.3 years): invasive cancer HR 0.96 (0.88-1.06), major cardiovascular events HR 0.97 (0.85-1.12). D-Health (n=21,315): all-cause mortality HR 1.04. ViDA (n=5,108): cardiovascular disease HR 1.02.
- Vitamin D alone does nothing for fractures. Cochrane: hip fracture RR 1.12 (0.98-1.29) without calcium. The calcium is doing the work.
- More is actively worse, and this is the least-marketed finding in the field. Annual 500,000 IU increased falls (IRR 1.15) and fractures (IRR 1.26). Monthly 60,000 IU raised the proportion of people falling from 47.9% to 66.9%. Three years at 4,000 or 10,000 IU/d caused dose-dependent LOSS of radial bone density versus 400 IU/d.
- The 30 ng/mL threshold that drives most deficiency marketing came from the Endocrine Society, and that society walked it back in 2024. Its current guideline suggests against routine testing in healthy adults and against empiric supplementation above the reference intake in healthy under-75s.
- Contraindicated in hypercalcaemia, primary hyperparathyroidism and granulomatous disease such as sarcoidosis or tuberculosis. Upper limit 4,000 IU/d.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose partly reachable
- **Data confidence:** Low

Oily fish, egg yolk and UV-treated mushrooms supply measurable vitamin D, and fortified fat spreads, milk, breakfast cereals and juices add small amounts, so this is a genuine dietary nutrient rather than a manufactured one. The RICH label needs qualifying on two counts: the food base is narrow (oily fish, egg yolk, UV-treated mushrooms), and two of the stronger rows below are not ordinary foods, since cod liver oil behaves as a supplement and fortified orange juice is a manufactured delivery vehicle. Population evidence points the same way, because SACN concluded the 10 µg/day RNI is difficult to achieve from natural food sources and set it as a year-round RNI for everyone aged 4 and over; UK mean intakes from food are around a third of that (the often-quoted NDNS figures of 2.8 µg/day at ages 19 to 65 and 3.3 µg/day in over-65s are carried here as uncorroborated, since the full SACN report could not be retrieved to confirm them).

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Mushrooms, maitake, UV-treated, raw | up to 56.1 µg vitamin D2 (2,244 IU); measured range 0.1 to 56.1 µg depending on growing method | 1 cup diced (70 g) | 39.3 µg (1,571 IU) at the top of the range | 393% of the 10 µg/400 IU UK RNI at the top value; 39% of a 4,000 IU supplement | USDA ARS Nutrient Data Laboratory, Vitamin D in Mushrooms (NFNAP), cited in the original submission's own caveat text; indicative and unconfirmed, and the range is so wide that the upper value should not be treated as typical |
| Salmon, sockeye, cooked, dry heat (wild) | 16.7 µg vitamin D (668 IU) | half fillet (155 g) | 25.9 µg (1,035 IU) | 259% of the 10 µg/400 IU UK RNI; 26% of a 4,000 IU supplement | USDA SR Legacy cooked sockeye salmon, 14.20 µg per 85 g serving, confirmed via FDC mirror. Arithmetic recomputes exactly under the CoFID total-D convention, but indicative and unconfirmed: the CoFID dataset could not be retrieved and not one of its codes could be verified |

USDA is about 3x lower, so the single figure in the submission is replaced by the range |

USDA whole raw egg gives a lower 2.0 µg/100 g = 1.0 µg (41 IU) per 50 g egg, which is confirmed, so the CoFID figure runs about 1.6x the US value |

Included as a negative control, since the conclusion holds either way |

- **To match the supplement dose:** 1,000 IU (25 µg), the low end of ordinary supplement doses, is about 150 g of cooked sockeye salmon every day, or 155 g to 463 g of grilled herring depending on which herring value holds. 4,000 IU (100 µg) is roughly 600 g of sockeye salmon, 621 g to 1.85 kg of herring, about 109 large egg yolks, or about 890 g of UV-treated portabella mushrooms daily. A 50,000 IU (1,250 µg) weekly loading dose has no food equivalent: 7.5 kg of sockeye salmon, or 7.8 to 23.1 kg of grilled herring. One food gets close on mass alone, UV-treated maitake at the top of its measured range, where 178 g would carry 4,000 IU, and that content is unlabelled, spans 0.1 to 56.1 µg/100 g by growing method and is D2 rather than D3. Above roughly 50 degrees north, cutaneous synthesis is effectively zero from October to March, so there is no sunlight to close the winter shortfall.
- **Food versus supplement:** Mushroom vitamin D is D2 (ergocalciferol), a different molecule from the D3 in fish, egg yolk and almost all supplements, and D3 raises serum 25(OH)D more effectively, with the gap widest for intermittent bolus dosing (Tripkovic et al., Am J Clin Nutr 2012;95:1357-64, PMID 22552031, DOI 10.3945/ajcn.111.031070, mean difference 34.10 nmol/L for bolus dosing, P = 0.0002; the advantage is lost with daily dosing, P = 0.10). UV mushroom content is wildly variable and usually unlabelled: USDA measured UV-treated portabella at 3.4 µg/100 g from one producer and 25.6 µg/100 g from another, maitake from 0.1 to 56.1 µg/100 g by growing method, and untreated white mushrooms at 0.1 to 0.2 µg/100 g, so buying ordinary mushrooms because 'mushrooms have vitamin D' gets you essentially nothing. Species and provenance matter as much as the food name: the salmon row is wild sockeye, while farmed Atlantic salmon, which is what most shoppers buy, runs several-fold lower, so 'salmon' on a pack does not deliver the sockeye figure. Cod liver oil is nominally a food but behaves as a supplement and carries 18,000 µg retinol per 100 g (CoFID 17-488, unconfirmed), so a tablespoon delivers about 8,160 IU of preformed vitamin A alongside the D3, which rules it out in pregnancy. Several real UK vehicles are missing from the table for want of a verifiable figure and should not be forgotten: fortified margarine and fat spreads (a statutory UK vitamin D vehicle for decades, and a larger contributor to UK intake than the US fortified orange juice listed), fortified breakfast cereals, kippers and other smoked herring, canned red sockeye (higher than the canned pink listed), herring and cod roe, and sun-dried shiitake for D2. Oily fish delivers D3 inside a fat matrix with EPA/DHA, protein, iodine and selenium; a capsule delivers D3 alone.
- **Absorption:** Vitamin D from both food and capsules is fat-soluble and absorbed better with a meal containing fat, which oily fish and egg yolk supply themselves. Form matters more than source: D2 from mushrooms is less effective per microgram than D3 (PMID 22552031). Egg yolk additionally contains 25-hydroxycholecalciferol, an already-hydroxylated metabolite that supplements do not normally provide. Composition tables bundle 25-OH-D3 into the headline total under the convention total = cholecalciferol + 5 x 25-OH-D3, so a 'vitamin D' number for eggs, trout or canned salmon is not strictly comparable to the D3 in a capsule; the canned pink salmon (12.1 + 5 x 0.30 = 13.6), trout (7.19 + 5 x 0.20 = 8.19) and whole egg (2.30 + 5 x 0.18 = 3.20) totals here all follow it.
- **Data note:** The single richest confirmed food (cooked sockeye salmon) and the UV portabella and fortified orange juice values check out, but most rows rest on McCance and Widdowson CoFID 2021 codes and not one CoFID code or value in the submission could be independently confirmed, because the dataset was not retrievable. Where USDA could be checked against CoFID the two diverged badly in this group: egg yolk vitamin D was overstated 2.4x and herring 3x. The maitake, eel and swordfish figures are indicative only, and the NDNS population intake figures could not be traced to the SACN report

*References: VITAL PMID 30415629; D-Health PMID 35026158; ViDA PMID 28384800; Cochrane PMID 24729336; Herrick PMID 31076739; Cashman PMID 26864360; Endocrine Society PMID 38828931; Sanders PMID 20460620; Bischoff-Ferrari PMID 26747333; Burt PMID 31454046*

### Vitamin B12 (cobalamin)

**Verdict:** TAKE &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** 50-250 µg/d, or 1,000-2,000 µg once or twice weekly. Deficiency treatment: 1,000-2,000 µg/d orally.
- **Who it is for:** Vegans and long-term vegetarians, metformin users, people on a proton pump inhibitor for over two years, post-bariatric patients, pernicious anaemia.

**Pros**

- The deficiency is real and the neurological damage is irreversible once established, so correcting it early genuinely matters.
- Oral works as well as injection. Cochrane found 1,000 µg/d oral matched intramuscular, and at 2,000 µg/d oral was better by 680 pg/mL. Injections are a business model.
- Risk in the named groups is well quantified: metformin lowers B12 by 19% over 4.3 years; two or more years of PPI use gives OR 1.65.
- No tolerable upper intake level has been needed, because no adverse effect has been found from excess.

**Cons**

- It does nothing for energy, mood or cognition in people who are not deficient. That is the entire marketing claim and it has no support.
- Do not treat with folate alone if B12 status is unknown, for the reason given under folic acid.
- Cyanocobalamin should be avoided in Leber hereditary optic neuropathy and in renal failure. Use hydroxocobalamin instead.
- High-dose biotin in the same B-complex product interferes with immunoassays including troponin, which can mimic or mask a heart attack on a blood test.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** High

Rich in animal foods and effectively absent from plant foods, because cobalamin is synthesised only by bacteria and archaea: every reliable source is an animal that accumulated bacterial B12, or a bacterially fermented or fortified product. The single word RICH is population-dependent and hides a reversal, so state it plainly: for an omnivore B12 is one of the easiest nutrients in the food supply, while for someone on an unfortified plant-based diet the same evidence makes it effectively absent, the only credible source being dried purple laver at 2.33 ug per 3 g sheet from a product whose content varies about elevenfold by origin and degrades with toasting and storage. On the contested plant candidates: spirulina is not usable, since 83% of its cobamide is pseudovitamin B12 (adeninylcobamide), inactive in humans, with only 17% real B12, and microbiological assays cannot tell them apart, which is why the labels claim high B12 (Watanabe et al., J Agric Food Chem 1999;47:4736-41, PMID 10552882); nori is usable and is the only credible plant source, carrying five biologically active cobalamin forms with adenosyl- and methylcobalamin about 60% of the total, and it corrected B12 status in deficient rats (Takenaka S et al., Br J Nutr 2001;85:699-703, PMID 11430774); tempeh is unreliable at 0.7-8.0 ug/100 g depending on which bacteria happened to contaminate the Rhizopus fermentation; mushrooms are not usable, with dried shiitake averaging 5.61 ug/100 g dry weight (range 1.3-12.7), black trumpet and golden chanterelle 1.09-2.65 ug/100 g dry weight and most other species about 0.09 ug/100 g or trace; chlorella ranges from zero to several hundred ug/100 g between products and cannot be relied on (all from Watanabe et al., Nutrients 2014;6:1861-73, PMID 24803097). Two further claims in the original submission, that kimchi and fermented vegetables sit below 0.1 ug/100 g and that lion's mane contains the inactive B12[c-lactone] analogue, could not be traced to the cited review and are recorded here as indicative and unsourced.

Named because this item's own conclusion is that fortified food or a tablet is the answer for the one population with a real gap. Cite the specific product label. |

- **To match the supplement dose:** Against the 2.4 ug/day RDA there is no gap for anyone eating animal food: one 3 oz portion of cooked clams is 35 times the RDA, a slice of beef liver 20 times, a can of sardines 3.4 times. Against the supplement dose the gap cannot be closed. Oral B12 products are typically 500-1,000 ug, and 1,000-2,000 ug for treating deficiency. To have 1,000 ug physically present in a meal takes 1.01 kg of cooked clams, 1.17 kg of pan-fried lamb liver, 1.42 kg of braised beef liver, 4.17 kg of cooked blue mussels, 11.2 kg of canned sardines, 35.7 kg of cooked salmon, or 222 kg of whole milk, which is about 216 litres (the submission's '222 litres' confused grams with litres; 1,000/0.0045 = 222,222 g, and at about 1.03 kg/L that is 216 L). No one could eat any of those. The comparison is also the wrong one, because intrinsic-factor-mediated absorption saturates at about 1.5-2.0 ug per meal (Watanabe, Exp Biol Med 2007;232:1266-74, PMID 17959839), so everything above roughly 2 ug in a single sitting is absorbed only by passive diffusion at on the order of 1%. A 1,000 ug tablet works because 1% of 1,000 ug bypasses the receptor route, which no food can imitate and none needs to unless that route is broken. For a strict vegan the picture inverts: nothing reliably supplies even the 2.4 ug RDA except dried nori, and Watanabe puts the requirement at about 4 g/day of dried purple laver, which is about one and a third 3 g sheets every day (the submission said one and a half) from a product varying about elevenfold by origin. Fortified foods or a tablet are the honest answer.
- **Food versus supplement:** Supplements use cyanocobalamin, a form not found in food, where the cyanide ligand is an artefact of the purification step and is removed intracellularly. Food B12 is protein-bound and needs gastric acid and pepsin to release it before intrinsic factor can take it up, so proton pump inhibitors, atrophic gastritis, gastric surgery and metformin knock out food B12 while leaving free crystalline B12 in a tablet or fortified cereal largely usable. Here the supplement is the mechanistically correct choice, and not merely the convenient one. The form risk with algae products runs opposite to the usual concentration worry: a spirulina capsule sold as a B12 source delivers mostly inactive pseudovitamin B12, and the microbiological assay behind the label number could not distinguish it from the real thing, so the label overstates delivered B12 rather than the dose being too strong. Note also that USDA gives yeast extract spread only 0.50 ug B12/100 g (FDC 167717), which is 0.03 ug per 6 g teaspoon: yeast does not make B12, so any yeast product that is a real source has been fortified and the number belongs to the fortification.
- **Absorption:** About 50% of dietary B12 is assumed absorbed from a normal meal, but it varies sharply by food: fish 42%, sheep meat 56-89%, chicken 61-66% and eggs under 9% (Watanabe 2007, PMID 17959839). The egg figure matters because eggs are routinely offered to vegetarians as a B12 food; on absorbed B12 a large egg delivers about 0.04 ug, which is 1.7% of the RDA rather than the 19% the content figure implies. Cooking costs real B12. USDA Table of Nutrient Retention Factors Release 6 (2007) gives 70% for roasted beef (code 0601), 60% for beef braised or simmered without the drippings (0651/0653), 65% for roasted chicken (0805), 60% for liver simmered without drippings (1152), 90% for baked lean finfish (2301), 60% for shellfish boiled in the shell (2701) against 90% steamed (2702), and 30% for milk held at heat for an hour (2153). Keeping the pan juices recovers much of it, which is why the with-drippings factors run exactly 15 points higher. One methodological point earned by the corrected mussel row: where USDA publishes a cooked composition entry, use it, because a hand-applied retention factor ignores the moisture loss that concentrates B12 and in this case halved the figure for the food people actually eat.
- **Data note:** for the animal-food rows: every per-100 g value and gram weight traces to a named FoodData Central record and every per-portion figure and percentage recomputes, and the Watanabe and Takenaka citations resolve to the stated journals, volumes and pages. Lower in two places that are stated rather than hidden: the nori figures and the whole vegan-diet conclusion rest on a single compiled review (Watanabe 2014) whose own product spread is about elevenfold, and the kimchi and lion's mane claims could not be traced to any cited source and are marked indicative. The 3 oz portion basis for lamb liver and Pacific oyster is stated by this dataset, not taken from a USDA household measure

*References: Cochrane PMID 29543316; de Jager BMJ 2010 PMID 20488910; Lam JAMA 2013 PMID 24327038; Pawlak review*

### Iron

**Verdict:** TAKE &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** Test ferritin first. Treatment 60-120 mg elemental iron. Ferrous sulfate 325 mg delivers 65 mg elemental.
- **Who it is for:** Menstruating women with ferritin at or below about 50 µg/L, pregnancy, blood donors, post-bariatric patients.

**Pros**

- Grade A for treating iron-deficiency anaemia and for pregnancy outcomes, across 57 trials and 48,971 women.
- Fatigue improves in non-anaemic iron-deficient women, but only below a ferritin threshold of about 50 µg/L. Above that, nothing happens.
- Cheap and generic. Ferrous sulfate is the reference standard and there is no evidence that premium salts work better.

**Cons**

- Alternate-day dosing is oversold. The absorption physiology is real (21.8% versus 16.3% fractional absorption) but the only adequately sized randomised trial with haemoglobin as the endpoint found the same final ferritin either way. Treat it as a tolerability strategy, not a superiority one.
- Never supplement anaemia that has not been shown to be iron-deficient. Anaemia of inflammation and haemochromatosis both get worse. Unexplained anaemia in an adult needs investigation, not a pill.
- Contraindicated in hereditary haemochromatosis (HFE C282Y homozygotes) and in transfusion-dependent anaemias such as thalassaemia and myelodysplastic syndrome.
- Acute iron overdose was historically a leading cause of fatal poisoning in children under six, which is why unit-dose packaging exists. Keep it locked away.
- Gastrointestinal side effects are the main reason people stop. Upper limit 45 mg/d (US); EFSA sets no limit but a safe level of 40 mg/d.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose partly reachable
- **Data confidence:** High

Iron is genuinely abundant in ordinary food, in two chemically distinct forms that behave differently. Haem iron (offal, red meat, shellfish, blood sausage) is absorbed by a dedicated transporter and is largely indifferent to the rest of the plate; non-haem iron (legumes, seeds, greens, fortified flour) is the bulk of most people's intake and is absorbed at a fraction of the rate, heavily modified by vitamin C, polyphenols, phytate and calcium. Haem accounts for 77 percent of total iron in raw lean beef, 81 percent in lamb, 88 percent in pork and 74 percent in chicken (PMID 26433293), higher than the 40 percent assumed in the older Monsen equation; those fractions were measured in raw lean meat, so the haem share of a braised product is probably slightly lower than the calculations below imply. Reference point used in the table: EFSA PRI 16 mg/d for premenopausal women, 11 mg/d for men (EFSA, Dietary Reference Values for nutrients Summary report, approved 4 December 2017, doi 10.2903/sp.efsa.2017.e15121, Tables 5 and 7; underlying opinion EFSA Journal 2015;13(10):4254).

| Molasses (non-haem) | 4.72 mg | 1 tbsp (20 g) | 0.94 mg | 5.9% | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168820 (SR Legacy), per-100 g value supplied by the verification report. Portion conventional, per-portion derived. |
| Spinach, raw (non-haem) | 2.71 mg | 1 cup raw leaves (30 g) | 0.81 mg | 5% on paper, roughly 0.02 mg actually absorbed | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168462 (SR Legacy) |
| Wholemeal bread (non-haem) | 2.47 mg | 1 slice (32 g) | 0.79 mg | 5% | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 172688 (SR Legacy) |
| Dried apricots, sulphured, uncooked (non-haem) | 2.66 mg | 6 halves (21 g) | 0.56 mg | 3.5% | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173941 (SR Legacy), USDA measure 1 half = 3.5 g |
| Clams, cooked (folklore exception, listed to correct it) | 2.81 mg | 85 g (3 oz) | 2.4 mg | 15% | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID per verification report note (cooked 2.81 mg, canned 2.68 mg). Clams are not a rich iron food in current USDA data despite their reputation. Portion conventional, per-portion derived. |

- **To match the supplement dose:** Two supplement doses need two answers. A nutritional dose (14 to 18 mg elemental iron, the EU labelling reference level) is comfortably reachable by eating: 1 cup of boiled lentils (6.6 mg) plus 85 g of braised lamb's liver (7.0 mg) plus 1 oz of pumpkin seeds (2.3 mg) is 15.9 mg in one day, and a single 85 g portion of braised pig's liver is 15.2 mg on its own. A therapeutic dose is out of reach. The absorption and treatment trials gave 60 mg and 120 mg of elemental iron as labelled ferrous sulfate, which is a salt weight of about 325 mg for 65 mg of iron (PMID 29032957). Matching 65 mg of iron by total content takes roughly 363 g of braised pig's liver, 785 g of lamb's liver, 3.3 kg of sirloin steak, 1.95 kg (about 10 cups) of boiled lentils, or 2.4 kg (about 80 cups) of raw spinach in a single day. By absorbed iron the food burden is about 1.6 times larger than the submission claimed, because the trial it cites measured cumulative fractional absorption of 16.3 percent on consecutive days and 21.8 percent on alternate days at 60 mg/d, and 11.8 percent for a single 120 mg dose, rather than the 10 percent assumed. At the source's own 16.3 percent a 65 mg dose delivers about 10.6 mg absorbed. Absorbing 10.6 mg as haem iron at 25 percent requires 42.4 mg of haem iron, about 1.94 kg of braised lean lamb shoulder (2.7 mg total iron per 100 g, 81 percent haem in raw lean; FDC 174331 and PMID 26433293). Absorbing the same 10.6 mg from non-haem food at 5 percent requires 212 mg of non-haem iron, about 6.4 kg of cooked lentils per day. Correcting a diagnosed deficiency cannot be done with food; preventing one can.
- **Food versus supplement:** Three traps, and the first is narrower than usually stated. (1) The retinol hazard belongs to liver, not to offal as a class. Braised lamb liver carries 7,490 ug retinol activity equivalents per 100 g, which is 6,367 ug in an 85 g portion, and braised beef liver 9,440 ug RAE per 100 g (raw beef liver 4,970 ug RAE; FDC 172532, 168626, 169451). That is why liver is contraindicated in pregnancy in most national guidance. The hazard separates cleanly by organ: braised lamb kidney is 12.4 mg iron with 137 ug RAE (FDC 174355) and braised pork kidney 5.29 mg iron with 78 ug RAE (FDC 167860), so kidney beats every muscle-meat row on iron without the vitamin A problem. The earlier claim that the iron benefit and the vitamin A hazard travel together and cannot be separated is withdrawn. (2) Liver's iron is largely ferritin and haemosiderin iron, so its headline 8.28 mg is less absorbable than the haem fraction of plain muscle meat implies, and muscle meat with a smaller number can deliver more absorbed iron. (3) Supplement iron behaves unlike food iron: a 60 mg dose raises hepcidin and suppresses absorption of the next dose, which is why alternate-day dosing absorbed 21.8 percent against 16.3 percent on consecutive days (PMID 29032957). Food iron arriving in small amounts across the day never triggers that. On spinach, the correction runs both ways. Isotope work found that potassium oxalate added to a low-oxalate kale meal did not change non-haem iron absorption (11.5 percent against 10.7 percent, P=0.86), so the textbook oxalate explanation does not survive testing. But the spinach meal in the same study absorbed 24 percent less than the matched kale meal without reaching significance (P>0.16), and although the paper reports spinach as significantly higher in calcium and polyphenols it does not attribute the absorption difference to them. The honest reading is that spinach iron was numerically lower in a single small study, the oxalate mechanism is refuted, and no replacement mechanism is established. Either way a 30 g bowl of raw spinach leaves contains 0.81 mg of iron and yields a rounding error.
- **Absorption:** Haem iron is absorbed at roughly 15 to 35 percent and non-haem at 2 to 20 percent, with iron status the dominant variable. Across whole diets rather than single meals, bioavailability is 14 to 18 percent for mixed diets and 5 to 12 percent for vegetarian diets in people with no iron stores, and the authors note that iron status generally has a greater effect than diet composition (PMID 20200263). Vitamin C: the source concludes that about 50 mg of ascorbic acid in each main meal is desirable for optimum effect, with the most pronounced effects in meals high in inhibitory ligands such as phytates and tannins (PMID 3700141). The '25 mg' lower bound and the 'several-fold' multiplier claimed earlier are not in that source and have been dropped. When the same amount of ascorbic acid is given across a complete diet over days, the facilitating effect is far less pronounced (PMID 11124756): squeezing lemon on your lentils helps, and it does not turn plant iron into meat iron. Tea and coffee inhibition, with numbers: a cup of coffee cut non-haem absorption from a hamburger meal by 39 percent and tea by 64 percent; instant coffee at double strength drove absorption from 5.88 percent to 0.53 percent; coffee one hour before the meal did no harm, while coffee one hour after inhibited as much as coffee with the meal (PMID 6402915). Black tea inhibited non-haem absorption by 79 to 94 percent, peppermint tea by 84 percent, cocoa by 71 percent and chamomile by 47 percent, dose-dependently on total polyphenols, and adding milk made no difference (PMID 10999016). That is polyphenol inhibition; phytate is a separate inhibitor sitting inside the wholegrains and legumes themselves. Practical consequence: the highest-yield single change for someone with low iron stores is moving tea and coffee to between meals rather than with or after them.
- **Data note:** Every per-100 g figure carried over from the submission matched FoodData Central to the digit on independent retrieval, and all cited PMIDs and DOIs resolved to papers saying what they are cited as saying. Two lower-confidence spots are flagged in the rows themselves: the blood sausage entry is a US proxy whose UK equivalent could not be confirmed, and the eleven newly added rows use conventional portion sizes rather than USDA's own measure table, so their per-portion figures are derived rather than tabulated

*References: Cochrane 2024; Stoffel PMID 29032957; von Siebenthal PMID 38021373; Verdon BMJ 2003*

### Iodine

**Verdict:** TAKE &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** 150 µg/d as potassium iodide. 250 µg/d in pregnancy and lactation (WHO).
- **Who it is for:** Preconception, pregnancy and lactation, vegans, and anyone avoiding both dairy and fish.

**Pros**

- Universal salt iodisation is the single most successful micronutrient intervention in public health, and it works because iodine is the sole substrate for thyroid hormone.
- The deficiency has quietly returned in the UK and parts of Europe. UK schoolgirls had a median urinary iodine of 80.1 µg/L against a WHO adequacy threshold of 100, with 51% mildly deficient.
- The requirement rises about 50% in pregnancy, and the window for fetal brain development does not reopen.

**Cons**

- Correcting status during pregnancy may be too late. MITCH randomised 832 mildly deficient pregnant women to 200 µg/d and found a verbal IQ difference of -0.7 in their children at age 5-6. Fix it before conception.
- Avoid kelp and bladderwrack entirely. Content is wildly variable and can deliver many times the upper limit in a single dose.
- An iodine load can precipitate iodine-induced hyperthyroidism in autonomous thyroid nodules or multinodular goitre, and can cause hypothyroidism in autoimmune thyroid disease. Anyone with a thyroid history needs medical advice first.
- Upper limit 1,100 µg/d (US) but only 600 µg/d (EFSA).

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** High

Iodine occurs in ordinary food, and the answer rests on a narrow set of foods: dairy, white fish, eggs, shellfish and, where a country iodises it, table salt. Nothing else contributes meaningfully. The RICH classification carries a jurisdictional qualifier: for a UK reader it rests on dairy, white fish and eggs alone, because the UK has no salt iodisation programme, so the US iodised salt row below is not transferable and is marked as such. Iodine in milk is not intrinsic to the cow, it arrives from fortified cattle feed and iodophor teat disinfectants, which is why it is seasonal (higher in winter housing) and why organic milk is lower. Reference point used in the table: EFSA AI 150 ug/d for adults, 200 ug/d in pregnancy and lactation (EFSA, Dietary Reference Values for nutrients Summary report, approved 4 December 2017, doi 10.2903/sp.efsa.2017.e15121, Tables 5 and 7; underlying opinion EFSA Journal 2014;12(5):3660).

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Kombu, oarweed and sugar kelp, dried (hazard as well as source) | 227,600 to 780,000 ug (2,276 to 7,800 ug/g) | 1 g dried | 2,276 to 7,800 ug | 1,517% to 5,200% of EFSA AI (150 ug/d) from one gram | PMID 33889064 (Aakre et al., Food Nutr Res 2021;65:7584). Listed because these are the richest iodine foods that exist, and one gram exceeds the tolerable upper intake level many times over. See the form caveat before treating this as usable. |
| Haddock, UK data | about 325 ug | 1 fillet (120 g) | 390 ug | 260% of AI in one fillet | BDA Iodine Food Fact Sheet (UK), via the verification report. This is a compiled secondary table rather than a primary analysis, so treat the figure as indicative; it comes from the same UK data underlying the Bath-group citations used elsewhere in this item. |
| Scampi (Norway lobster), UK data | about 94 ug | 1 serving (170 g) | 160 ug | 107% of AI | BDA Iodine Food Fact Sheet (UK), via the verification report. Compiled secondary table, indicative rather than primary. |
| Cod, Atlantic, wild caught, raw | 114 ug | 1 fillet (115 g) | 131 ug | 87% of AI | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 2684444 (Foundation Foods), the record's own single portion being 115 g. Low for a UK-framed item: UK data give cod 230 ug per 120 g portion (192 ug/100 g), roughly 70 percent richer. |
| Yogurt, plain, nonfat | 58.7 ug | 1 pot (170 g) | 100 ug | 67% | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 2647437 (Foundation Foods) |
| Cows' milk, whole, 3.25% fat | 37.9 ug | 1 cup (249 g) | 94 ug | 63% | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 746782 (Foundation Foods) |
| UK retail cows' milk, conventional, winter samples | 43.8 ug (median 438 ug/kg) | 1 glass (200 ml, about 206 g) | 90 ug | 60% | PMID 28946925 (Bath et al., Br J Nutr 2017;118:525-532). Portion converted at milk density (200 ml = about 206 g); the earlier 88 ug figure had costed 200 ml as 200 g. |
| Table salt, iodised (US specification; NOT transferable to the UK) | 5,080 ug | 1/4 teaspoon (1.5 g) | 76 ug | 51% of AI in the US, and 0% for a UK reader | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 746775 (Foundation Foods); the record's measure is 6.1 g per teaspoon, so 1/4 tsp = 1.53 g. The UK has no salt iodisation programme, so a UK reader cannot count this row at all. |
| UK retail cows' milk, organic, winter samples | 32.4 ug (median 324 ug/kg) | 1 glass (200 ml, about 206 g) | 67 ug | 45% | PMID 28946925; year-round comparison organic 144.5 against conventional 249.5 ng/g, PMID 21781365. Portion converted at milk density rather than treating 200 ml as 200 g. |
| Iodine-fortified plant drink (soya, oat or rice) | 26.6 to 28.7 ug (266 to 287 ug/kg) | 1 glass (200 ml, about 206 g) | 55 to 59 ug | 37% to 39% of AI | PMID 28946925 (soya 280, oat 287, rice 266 ug/kg; three of forty-seven drinks surveyed were iodine-fortified). This is the one actionable food answer for the group the item identifies as deficient. |
| Nori (Porphyra tenera) sheets | 1,600 ug (16 ug/g) | 1 sheet (3 g) | 48 ug | 32% | PMID 15588380 (Teas et al., Thyroid 2004;14:836-841), nori being the lowest of the 12 species surveyed |
| Parmesan cheese, grated | 82.9 ug | 30 g (heaped 2 tbsp over pasta) | 24.9 ug | 17% | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 2259795 (Foundation Foods) |
| Egg, whole, Grade A large | 49.1 ug | 1 large egg without shell (50 g) | 24.6 ug | 16.4% | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 748967 (Foundation Foods); 49.1 x 0.50 = 24.55 ug. UK data independently give 25 ug per egg. |
| Almond drink, unsweetened, calcium- and vitamin-fortified, not iodine-fortified | 0.0 ug | 1 cup (240 g) | 0 ug | 0% | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 1999631 (Foundation Foods). Iodine 0.0 ug confirmed, but the record carries Ca 173 mg and vitamin A 41 ug RAE per 100 g, so 'unfortified' was wrong: it is fortified, just not with iodine. |
| Soya drink, unsweetened, calcium- and vitamin-fortified, not iodine-fortified | 0.0 ug | 1 cup (240 g) | 0 ug | 0% | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 1999630 (Foundation Foods). Iodine 0.0 ug confirmed; the record carries Ca 101 mg and 58 ug RAE per 100 g. |

- **To match the supplement dose:** There is essentially no gap. A standard iodine supplement or prenatal multivitamin supplies 150 ug, exactly the EFSA adult AI. A single 120 g UK haddock fillet delivers 390 ug, two and a half times the dose. One 115 g cod fillet delivers 131 ug. About 400 ml of milk delivers roughly 156 ug at milk density (412 g); the 151 ug figure quoted previously is what 1.6 USDA cups (398 g) gives, and the two should not be equated. Six large eggs deliver 147 ug. Half a teaspoon of iodised salt (3 g) delivers 152 ug where salt is iodised. Iodine belongs in the small category of supplements a normal eater does not need. The gap opens the moment those specific foods leave the diet, and the UK numbers are stark. Substituting three dairy portions a day (milk, yoghurt, cheese) with plant alternatives cuts the iodine those portions supply from 124 ug to 2.6 ug, a 97.9 percent loss, taking the contribution to the 150 ug recommendation from 83 percent down to 1.8 percent (PMID 35373723). The same market survey identified 146 milk alternatives and 76 yoghurt alternatives, excluded 48 organic products, and found 29 milk alternatives and 4 yoghurt alternatives iodine-fortified; that is 28 percent and 6 percent of the non-organic subset, or 19.9 percent and 5.3 percent of all products identified. The 28 and 6 percent figures quoted earlier against the full denominators were wrong. Against those, 88 percent and 73 percent were fortified with calcium; no cheese alternative and no fish alternative was iodine-fortified; and organic plant alternatives cannot legally be fortified. This shows up in real people: in NDNS years 7 to 9, adults and children consuming milk alternatives exclusively had an iodine intake of 94 ug/d against 129 ug/d for cows' milk consumers, and a median urinary iodine concentration of 79 ug/l against 132 ug/l, which classifies the milk-alternative group as iodine deficient by the WHO criterion while the milk drinkers were sufficient (PMID 32993817). Unfortified milk-alternative concentrations were a median 7.3 ug/kg, 1.7 percent of winter conventional cows' milk; the three fortified products found in 2015 sat at 266 to 287 ug/kg (PMID 28946925).
- **Food versus supplement:** Kelp is the dangerous answer, and the danger is dosing variability rather than iodine itself. Across 12 species of commercially sold edible seaweed, iodine ranged from 16 ug/g in nori to over 8,165 ug/g in processed kelp granules from Laminaria digitata sold as a salt substitute, a 510-fold spread, and within a single species it varied with preharvest conditions (514 ug/g in sun-bleached blades, 6,571 ug/g in fresh juvenile blades) (PMID 15588380). One gram of those granules is 54 times the adult AI. A 2021 survey of 96 commercial macroalgae products found 128 to 62,400 ug of iodine per portion in wholefood kelp products and 5 to 5,600 ug per supplement dose, that for 54 of the 96 products a single serving or dose would exceed the tolerable upper intake level, and that species labelling and declared iodine content were frequently inadequate or inaccurate (PMID 33889064). A kelp tablet is therefore an unlabelled dose of a potent thyroid-active substance, and excess iodine causes both hypothyroidism and hyperthyroidism. If iodine is the goal, potassium iodide at a known 150 ug, or milk, fish and eggs, are the correct answers and kelp is not. Second, milk: iodine there comes from feed and dairy hygiene, so it moves with farming practice. Organic milk was 42.1 percent lower in iodine than conventional in a 172-sample UK retail survey (median 144.5 against 249.5 ng/g; PMID 21781365), and winter milk runs higher than summer. Someone switching to organic milk has quietly cut their main iodine source by nearly half without changing anything visible. Third, iodised salt: the USDA entry in the table is a US product, the UK has no iodisation programme, and that is precisely why the Bath group repeatedly identify milk, dairy and fish as the main UK iodine sources. Fourth, the plant drinks in the table are fortified products, with calcium and vitamin A, that skip iodine. That is the mechanism behind the 97.9 percent substitution loss rather than an absence of fortification technology.
- **Absorption:** Iodine bioavailability is the simple case. Iodide from milk, iodised salt and potassium iodide supplements is almost completely absorbed, and urinary iodine excretion is used directly as the intake biomarker on the standard assumption that about 90 percent of ingested iodine is excreted in urine (PMID 25274294). There is no meaningful absorption advantage to any form and no matrix effect worth chasing: 150 ug from a fillet of cod, a pot of yoghurt or a tablet are close to interchangeable systemically. The one real form difference sits at the top end. Organically bound iodine in seaweed is released variably during digestion and cooking, and boiling kelp leaches a large fraction of its iodine into the water, so a seaweed dose is uncertain in both directions. Two further practical points: iodine and selenium interact, because the deiodinase enzymes that convert thyroxine to the active hormone are selenoproteins, so iodine repletion in a selenium-poor person behaves differently; and goitrogens in raw brassicas and cassava compete with iodide uptake, which matters only when iodine intake is already marginal.
- **Data note:** for the core of the item. All USDA figures matched FoodData Central to the digit, and the Bath-group and Nicol PMIDs were confirmed verbatim including the 97.9 percent loss, the 94 against 129 ug/d NDNS intakes, the 79 against 132 ug/l urinary concentrations, the 438 and 324 ug/kg milk medians and the 42.1 percent organic deficit. Two rows are weaker and marked in place: the haddock and scampi figures come from a compiled UK secondary table (BDA Iodine Food Fact Sheet) rather than a primary analysis, so they are indicative

*References: Vanderpump Lancet 2011 PMID 21640375; MITCH PMID 29030199; Cochrane PMID 28260263*

### Creatine monohydrate

**Verdict:** TAKE &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** 3-5 g/d. Loading is optional: 20 g/d for 6 days and 3 g/d for 28 days reach the same muscle saturation.
- **Who it is for:** Anyone doing resistance training, and older adults in particular. Also vegetarians and vegans, whose baseline stores are lower.

**Pros**

- The best-evidenced ergogenic aid on the market. In older adults doing resistance training, +1.37 kg lean tissue mass (95% CI 0.97-1.76) over training alone, across 22 randomised trials.
- Extremely cheap. Monohydrate costs pennies per dose and is the form used in essentially every trial.
- Long safety record: no signal of harm in healthy kidneys across years of use in trials.

**Cons**

- The cognition claim is weaker than the marketing. Pooled memory effect SMD 0.31 (0.18-0.44), and EFSA formally concluded in 2024 that a cause-and-effect relationship with cognitive function has NOT been established.
- Do not pay for HCl, buffered, liquid or micronised premium forms. They are price increases with no comparative evidence behind them.
- Existing chronic kidney disease or dialysis requires nephrology supervision, because the safety data are in healthy kidneys only.
- Expect 1-2 kg of water weight in the first weeks. That is intracellular water, not fat, but it matters if you compete in a weight class.
- No official upper limit exists. The commonly quoted safety envelope comes from an industry-affiliated position stand, and the actual sentence is absence of evidence of harm rather than demonstrated safety.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Medium

Creatine is a genuine dietary constituent, confined to the skeletal muscle of animals: Harris 1997 measured about 30 mmol/kg (393 mg/100 g) in uncooked beef, chicken and rabbit muscle. Organ distribution is the trap, because ox heart holds 295 mg/100 g while ox liver holds only 30 mg/100 g despite the liver being where creatine is synthesised. Plant foods contain none, so for a vegetarian or vegan the dietary status is effectively zero rather than RICH; baseline muscle total creatine was 117 mmol/kg dry mass in vegetarians against 130 in non-vegetarians.

PMID 7817065; DOI 10.2165/00007256-199418040-00005. | Ox liver, uncooked (the textbook answer that fails) | 30 mg creatine (2.3 mmol/kg) | 1 serving, 150 g | 45 mg | 0.9% of a 5 g dose | Harris RC et al., Res Vet Sci 1997;62(1):58-62. PMID 9160426 (2.3 mmol/kg = 30.2 mg/100 g, confirmed; the paper also found no phosphocreatine in any sample and degradation to creatinine in dried or processed products) |
| Cow's milk | 10 mg creatine | 1 glass, 200 mL (206 g) | 21 mg | 0.4% of a 5 g dose | Balsom 1994, PMID 7817065; DOI 10.2165/00007256-199418040-00005. INDICATIVE AND UNCONFIRMED: review-compiled table, no primary analysis traceable. |

- **To match the supplement dose:** On the verified Harris figure of 393 mg/100 g, 3 g/day of creatine takes 763 g of raw lean beef and 5 g/day takes 1,272 g, about six 200 g steaks every day, carrying roughly 280 g of protein and its full fat and calorie load. On Balsom's slightly higher 4.5 g/kg for beef it is 667 g and 1,111 g. Balsom's herring figure is the highest on the list and would still need 500-770 g of raw herring daily, and that figure is indicative only. Cooking makes the plate-equivalent worse, since creatine converts to inert creatinine, so the raw-basis numbers are the optimistic case. Nobody eats this way.
- **Absorption:** Creatine monohydrate is close to completely absorbed and dietary creatine from meat is also efficiently absorbed, so absorption is not the limiting step; dose size is. The responsive variable is baseline muscle store: vegetarians started at 117 mmol/kg dry mass versus 130 in non-vegetarians and showed larger increases in total creatine, phosphocreatine, lean tissue and work performance after supplementation (Burke DG et al., Med Sci Sports Exerc 2003;35(11):1946-55, PMID 14600563). Habitual omnivore intake is roughly 1-2 g/day against a whole-body turnover of about 2 g/day in a 70 kg male, with the balance made endogenously (PMID 7817065), which is why a supplement of 3-5 g/day is a two- to five-fold multiple of anything a diet delivers. Cooking losses are the one real bioavailability penalty for the food form: in cooked beef, creatine and taurine are the compounds most reduced, partly by conversion to creatinine and partly by loss into the cooking juices (Purchas RW, Rutherfurd SM, Pearce PD, Vather R, Wilkinson BHP, Meat Sci 2004;68(2):201-7, PMID 22062229).
- **Data note:** The five Harris 1997 rows (beef, chicken, rabbit, ox heart, ox liver) were confirmed against the paper down to the mmol/kg conversions, and the whole dose-gap arithmetic was re-derived and held. Five of the ten rows (pork loin, herring, salmon, cod, milk) rest entirely on a single 1994 narrative review's compiled table with no traceable primary analysis, and no composition database carries creatine, so those figures cannot be raised above indicative

*References: Kreider PMID 28615996; Hultman PMID 8828669; Chilibeck PMID 29138605; Xu Front Nutr 2024; EFSA PMID 39564533*

### Protein powder

**Verdict:** TAKE &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** Whatever brings total daily protein to about 1.6 g/kg. The powder itself has no special property.
- **Who it is for:** People doing resistance training who are currently falling short of 1.6 g/kg/d from food.

**Pros**

- The nutrient is Grade A. Protein supplementation on top of resistance training adds 2.49 kg to 1RM strength and 0.30 kg of fat-free mass across 49 randomised trials and 1,863 people.
- The dose-response is precisely mapped: fat-free mass gains plateau at 1.62 g/kg/d, so you know exactly when to stop paying for more.
- Convenient and shelf-stable, which is a real advantage for people who cannot cook three protein-containing meals.
- Kidney fear is unfounded in healthy people. The reassuring data are solid, though they apply to healthy kidneys only.

**Cons**

- The benefit is real and small. Three hundred grams of extra muscle over months of training is what the meta-analysis actually found. Food protein does the same job.
- Branched-chain amino acids sold alongside are redundant and mechanistically incoherent. No human study has ever quantified muscle protein synthesis from oral BCAAs alone, and both intravenous studies found BCAAs DECREASED it. A 25 g whey serving already contains 5-6 g of BCAAs plus the six essential amino acids BCAAs lack.
- Heavy-metal loading is a genuine issue in plant and organic powders, which carry more cadmium and lead than whey. Third-party testing matters here more than in most categories.
- Chronic kidney disease stage 3 or above requires a renal dietitian to set the protein prescription.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** High

Protein powder is itself a food fraction, so there is no molecule here that food lacks: whey and casein are dairy by-products, soya and pea isolates are legume fractions. Ordinary single portions deliver 5-53 g each, with grilled turkey breast fillet at 35.0 g/100 g and fresh Parmesan at 36.2 g/100 g topping the table. This is the one item on the list where everyday food comfortably beats the supplement.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|

| Milk, semi-skimmed, pasteurised | 3.5 g protein | 1 large glass, 250 mL (258 g) | 9.0 g | 8% of 112 g/day | CoFID 2021, food code 12-313 (verified) |

| Cheddar cheese, English | 25.4 g protein | 1 matchbox piece, 30 g | 7.6 g | 7% of 112 g/day | CoFID 2021, food code 12-346 (verified) |
| Pumpkin seeds | 24.4 g protein | 1 handful, 30 g | 7.3 g | 7% of 112 g/day | CoFID 2021 (value identified by the verification report; food code not stated there) |
