- **To match the supplement dose:** A 1.6 g/kg target is 112 g/day for a 70 kg adult and food covers it without powder. Worked day: 2 boiled eggs (14.1 g) plus a 170 g pot of Greek style yogurt (9.7 g) plus a 130 g grilled chicken breast (41.6 g) plus 200 g cooked red lentils (16.2 g) plus 250 mL semi-skimmed milk (9.0 g) plus 30 g Cheddar (7.6 g) totals 98.2 g; add one 100 g can of tuna in brine and it reaches 123 g, over target. A 100 kg athlete at 1.6 g/kg (160 g) gets there by adding a 150 g grilled turkey breast fillet (52.5 g) or a 170 g rump steak (52.7 g). A shopper does better than the table suggests on dairy, since modern strained pots and skyr run roughly 10-12 g/100 g against CoFID's traditional Greek style yogurt at 5.7 g. The gap appears only at very high targets combined with poor appetite, a low-calorie diet or exclusively plant sources, where food volume becomes the binding constraint. Powder solves convenience and calorie density.
- **Absorption:** Absorption differences between food and powder are about speed and amino acid pattern, not quantity absorbed. Animal-source protein is more digestible and more leucine-dense per gram than most plant sources: roast chicken breast is 31.0 g protein and 2.33 g leucine per 100 g, that is 7.5% of protein as leucine (USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171477, SR Legacy NDB 5064, both values verified). Whey is faster and more leucine-rich per gram than whole food, casein and whole-food meals are slower, and for total daily protein this difference is small. One genuine food-form penalty: composition tables report protein as nitrogen multiplied by a conversion factor, so figures for plant foods with non-protein nitrogen slightly overstate usable protein, and cooking method changes yield per gram bought (raw beef mince is 19.7 g/100 g versus 21.8 g/100 g stewed as water is lost, CoFID 18-469 and 18-470, both verified).
- **Data note:** All 12 original CoFID codes and protein values were queried against the dataset itself and matched to the decimal, including the three the verifier specifically suspected (cottage cheese 9.4, boiled egg 14.1, red lentils 8.1), as did the beef mince pair and the USDA leucine figure. The seven added rows come from CoFID 2021 as identified in the verification report; food codes were supplied for turkey breast and Parmesan only, so the other five carry the dataset attribution without a code

*References: Morton PMID 28698222; Jager PMID 28642676; Wolfe PMID 28852372; Kato PMID 27322029*

### Psyllium husk (ispaghula)

**Verdict:** TAKE &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** About 10 g/d, always with a full glass of fluid. Separate from other oral drugs by about two hours.
- **Who it is for:** People with raised LDL cholesterol, irritable bowel syndrome of either subtype, or chronic constipation.

**Pros**

- LDL cholesterol falls 0.33 mmol/L (95% CI -0.38 to -0.27) at a median 10.2 g/d across 28 trials, and the apolipoprotein B evidence is graded HIGH quality, which is rare anywhere in this field.
- Irritable bowel syndrome: relative risk of persistent symptoms 0.83 (0.73-0.94), NNT 7. Very few gut products have a number this good.
- Cheap, generic and unglamorous, which is why it is under-sold rather than over-sold.
- Works in both constipation-predominant and diarrhoea-predominant IBS, because it is a gel-forming fibre rather than a stimulant.

**Cons**

- No hard-outcome trial exists. The LDL reduction is a surrogate endpoint, so nobody has shown psyllium prevents a heart attack.
- Contraindicated in dysphagia and in known or suspected bowel obstruction, stricture, impaction or ileus. This is an explicit FDA contraindication, not a formality.
- It must be taken with enough fluid. Bed-bound patients with poor fluid intake should not use it.
- It delays absorption of other oral drugs taken at the same time, which matters for narrow-therapeutic-index medicines.
- Wheat bran, sold beside it under identical digestive-health signage, is a different product and is null in IBS (RR 0.90) and often makes it worse. Do not substitute.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Medium

Psyllium husk is itself a food ingredient, the milled seed coat of Plantago ovata, so the supplement and the food are the same material and nothing is extracted or concentrated. Among ordinary foods, the genuine analogues for viscous gel-forming fibre are oat and barley beta-glucan and the mucilage of ground flaxseed and chia; konjac glucomannan and guar gum are also sold as foods and are more viscous still, though no confirmed per-portion figure was available for them here. Legumes are not in the same class and have been dropped from that claim: their soluble fibre measures 0.41-1.36 g/100 g and, as this entry's own bioavailability section says, it ferments proximally and behaves differently at the same gram intake. Beta-glucan is also chemically distinct from psyllium's arabinoxylan gel.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Psyllium husk powder, plain (food-grade, unflavoured) | 67 g soluble fibre and 11 g insoluble fibre as declared on the label record; the 78 g total is the sum of those two declarations rather than a measured value. Independent analyses of psyllium husk put total dietary fibre at roughly 71-85 g/100 g. | 1 tbsp (9 g declared serving in the same record) | 6.0 g soluble fibre; about 7.0 g total fibre on the summed figure | 9 g of husk is 90% of the 10 g/day husk dose in the Bijkerk IBS trial (PMID 19713235), computed on husk mass; the 7.0 g of total fibre is 23% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake, computed on fibre mass | USDA FoodData Central branded record 1742558 (NOW Psyllium Husk Powder). This is a manufacturer label declaration, not a laboratory analysis, and the record's own 'Fiber, total dietary' field reads 0 g, so the 78 g total is arithmetic on the two declared fractions. The 71-85 g/100 g range comes from independent analyses of psyllium husk. |
| Oat bran, raw | 15.4 g total dietary fibre; oat bran beta-glucan is roughly 5.5-8% w/w | 1 cup dry (94 g) | 14.5 g total dietary fibre, of which roughly 5.2-7.5 g is beta-glucan | 48% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake; the beta-glucan alone is 1.7-2.5x the 3 g/day beta-glucan target | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 168872 (total fibre, verified). Beta-glucan share from Stevenson et al., Int J Food Sci Nutr 2012;63(8):1001-13, DOI 10.3109/09637486.2012.687366 (oat soluble fibre 3-7% of dry weight; oat bran about 5.5-8% beta-glucan). The submission's Hughes and Grafenauer 2021 citation has been removed: that paper is a packaged-product label audit and contains no grain composition data. |
| Chia seed, dried | roughly 27-34 g total dietary fibre, a substantial part of it viscous mucilage (the mucilage fraction is not quantified in the source used here) | 1 oz (28 g), conventional serving | 7.6-9.5 g total dietary fibre | 25-32% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake; no soluble-fibre comparison against the psyllium dose is possible because the mucilage fraction is unquantified here | Fibre range as supplied in the verification report for ground flaxseed and chia (roughly 27-34 g/100 g); indicative and not traced to a specific composition record in this pass. Portion weight is a conventional serving, not from a source record. |
| Red kidney beans, canned, drained | 1.36 g soluble fibre, 5.77 g insoluble fibre, 7.13 g total | 1 cup (177 g) | 2.4 g soluble fibre; 12.6 g total fibre | 36% of the roughly 6.7 g of soluble fibre in a 10 g psyllium husk dose | Portion weight USDA FoodData Central 175194. Soluble and insoluble split from Li BW, Andrews KW, Pehrsson PR, J Food Compos Anal 2002;15(6):715-723, DOI 10.1006/jfca.2002.1096. The paper is genuine and correctly cited but its per-food table could not be retrieved, so these fractions are indicative and unconfirmed. |
| Flaxseed (linseed), ground | roughly 27-34 g total dietary fibre with a large viscous mucilage fraction (fraction not quantified in the source used here) | 2 tbsp ground (about 14 g), conventional serving | 3.8-4.8 g total dietary fibre | 13-16% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake | Fibre range as supplied in the verification report for ground flaxseed and chia (roughly 27-34 g/100 g); indicative and not traced to a specific composition record in this pass. Portion weight is a conventional serving, not from a source record. |
| Barley, pearled, cooked | 3.8 g total dietary fibre (cooked, hydrated weight) | 1 cup cooked (157 g) | 6.0 g total dietary fibre | 20% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 170285, verified. Barley soluble fibre of 3-11% w/w (Stevenson et al., DOI 10.3109/09637486.2012.687366) is a dry-grain figure and is not applied to this cooked weight. |
| Oatmeal, regular, cooked with water | 0.42 g soluble fibre, 1.23 g insoluble fibre, 1.65 g total (AOAC 991.43) | 1 cup cooked (234 g) | 0.98 g soluble fibre; 3.9 g total fibre | 15% of the roughly 6.7 g of soluble fibre in a 10 g psyllium husk dose | Portion weight USDA FoodData Central 173905. Soluble and insoluble fractions from Li BW et al., DOI 10.1006/jfca.2002.1096; the data table could not be retrieved, so these fractions are indicative and unconfirmed. |

- **To match the supplement dose:** The 10 g/day husk dose that beat placebo in primary-care IBS (Bijkerk 2009, BMJ 339:b3154, PMID 19713235) is about one heaped tablespoon of a product sold as food, and 9 g of husk powder already supplies 6.0 g of soluble fibre, so for psyllium itself the gap is zero. Matching that 6-7 g of soluble fibre from other ordinary foods is a different question: at 0.42 g/100 g you would need roughly 1.6 kg of cooked porridge (about 6.8 cups), and canned kidney beans at 1.36 g/100 g would take about 493 g, which is 2.8 cups at 177 g per cup, corrected from the submission's 3 cups. Both of those soluble-fibre fractions come from one study table that could not be retrieved, so treat the kilogram figures as indicative. The separate and easier target of 3 g/day of oat or barley beta-glucan is reachable by eating: about 67-75 g of dry rolled oats at 4-4.5% w/w, about 40-55 g of oat bran at 5.5-8%, or about 75 g of barley at 4%. The submission's 40-50 g of dry oats applied an oat-bran-level percentage to whole oats and has been corrected.
- **Food versus supplement:** There is no extract-versus-food problem because psyllium husk is unprocessed plant material, but three real caveats apply. Physical: the husk must be taken with a full glass of fluid, since a gel-forming dose swallowed dry can obstruct the oesophagus or bowel. Compositional: 'soluble fibre' on a label is not one substance. Psyllium's arabinoxylan gel is viscous and poorly fermented, while inulin carries the same soluble-fibre label and ferments fast with gas production, which is the opposite of what an IBS patient wants, so gram-for-gram substitution is a mistake. Processing: oat beta-glucan's benefit tracks viscosity, which depends on molecular weight, and milling, extrusion and prolonged heating degrade it, so a heavily processed oat product with an identical gram figure on the label is not equivalent to whole rolled oats.
- **Absorption:** Nothing here is absorbed, so bioavailability means viscosity, water-holding and fermentability. Psyllium holds water through the whole colon with a low gas yield, which is why it helps both constipation and diarrhoea; legume soluble fibre and inulin are largely fermented in the proximal colon and behave quite differently at the same gram intake. Analytical method is a genuine source of confusion: AOAC 991.43 gravimetric soluble-fibre values run systematically higher than the enzymatic-chemical (Englyst/NSP) values used in UK tables, so soluble-fibre numbers from different tables should not be mixed. Head-to-head evidence supports the distinction between fibre classes: at the same 10 g dose, psyllium beat placebo at one and two months while wheat bran did not (PMID 19713235).
- **Data note:** The USDA total-fibre values (oat bran 15.4, barley 3.8) and the Bijkerk trial details were confirmed. Two weaknesses remain: the headline psyllium composition rests on a branded manufacturer-label record whose own total-fibre field reads 0 g, and every soluble/insoluble split for oatmeal and legumes comes from one 2002 table that could not be retrieved. The added flax and chia figures are the verification report's own range, not a traced composition record

*References: Jovanovski PMID 30239559; Moayyedi PMID 25070054; Bijkerk BMJ 2009 PMID 19713235; Reynolds Lancet 2019 PMID 30638909*

### Caffeine

**Verdict:** TAKE &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** 3-6 mg/kg body mass, about 60 minutes before exercise. For a 70 kg person that is 210-420 mg.
- **Who it is for:** Almost anyone training, trained or untrained. Also shift workers and the sleep-deprived, for the separate alertness effect.

**Pros**

- The most reliably ergogenic legal substance in this document. An umbrella review of 11 systematic reviews containing 21 meta-analyses, all of moderate or high quality, found benefit for aerobic endurance, strength, muscle endurance, power, jumping and speed.
- Cheap, well characterised, and the dose-response is known.
- The mechanism is central rather than metabolic, so it works by reducing perceived effort and pain, which is why it helps across so many different tasks.

**Cons**

- Genotype changes the sign of the effect. CYP1A2 AA carriers gain (SMD 0.30), AC carriers gain a little (0.16), and CC carriers get WORSE (SMD -0.22). Most people do not know their genotype.
- 9 mg/kg brings a high incidence of side effects with no added benefit. More is not better past 6 mg/kg.
- The fat-burner claim is the oversold part of the category. Thermogenesis is real and trivial.
- Contraindicated or needing care in uncontrolled hypertension, arrhythmia, anxiety and panic disorder. EFSA caps pregnancy at 200 mg/d, and derives no safe intake for children.
- Fluvoxamine is a potent CYP1A2 inhibitor and can raise caffeine exposure severalfold. Taken late, it damages the sleep that training depends on.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** High

Caffeine in ordinary drinks sits at the same scale as the supplement dose: USDA gives home brewed coffee 40 mg/100 g and restaurant espresso 212 mg/100 g, and the Brazilian caffeine table gives espresso 279 mg/100 mL. Guarana seed powder at roughly 2-6 g/100 g and instant coffee powder on a dry basis at roughly 3-4 g/100 g overshoot a full dose from a spoonful, though neither figure was traced to a primary analysis in this review. Tea, matcha, cola and dark chocolate all contribute at tens of milligrams per serving, and yerba mate belongs in the same group though no figure was available to place it.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Guarana seed powder | roughly 2,000-6,000 mg caffeine (2-6 g/100 g), about 25-75 times brewed coffee per gram | 1 tsp, 3 g | 60-180 mg | 30-90% of a 200 mg dose | Approximate literature range supplied by the verification report as the single richest natural caffeine source. INDICATIVE AND UNCONFIRMED: no primary analytical source was traced in this review. |
| Espresso (Brazilian caffeine content table, BraCaffT) | 279 mg per 100 mL | 1 cup, 40 mL | 112 mg | 56% of a 200 mg dose | Rocha PLA, Lima ALC, Saunders B, Reis CEG. Nutrients 2022;14(20):4417. PMID 36297100; DOI 10.3390/nu14204417, Table 1 (value confirmed) |
| Coffee, brewed from grounds, prepared with tap water | 40 mg caffeine | 1 mug, 240 mL (240 g) | 96 mg | 48% of a 200 mg dose; 46% of 210 mg (3 mg/kg at 70 kg) | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171890 (SR Legacy, NDB 14209); FDC ID, NDB number, description and value all verified |
| Energy drink | 30 mg per 100 mL | 1 can, 250 mL | 75 mg | 38% of a 200 mg dose | Nutrients 2022;14(20):4417, Table 1. PMID 36297100 (confirmed) |

| Matcha, green tea powder | 1,890-4,440 mg per 100 g (18.9-44.4 mg/g) | 1 tsp, 2 g | 38-89 mg | 19-44% of a 200 mg dose | Kochman J, Jakubczyk K, Antoniewicz J, Mruk H, Janda K. Molecules 2020;26(1):85. PMID 33375458; DOI 10.3390/molecules26010085 (range confirmed verbatim, citation year defensible) |
| Dark chocolate (Brazilian table, higher value) | 114 mg per 100 g | 1 serving, 45 g | 51 mg | 26% of a 200 mg dose | Nutrients 2022;14(20):4417, Table 1. PMID 36297100 (confirmed) |
| Tea, green, brewed | 20 mg per 100 mL | 1 cup, 200 mL | 40 mg | 20% of a 200 mg dose | Nutrients 2022;14(20):4417, Table 1. PMID 36297100 (confirmed) |
| Tea, black, brewed | 18 mg per 100 mL | 1 cup, 200 mL | 36 mg (measured range across brewed white, green and black teas 14-61 mg per 6-8 oz serving) | 18% of a 200 mg dose | Nutrients 2022;14(20):4417 (DOI 10.3390/nu14204417), confirmed; range from Chin JM et al., J Anal Toxicol 2008;32(8):702-4, DOI 10.1093/jat/32.8.702 |
| Cola soft drink | 9 mg per 100 mL | 1 can, 350 mL | 32 mg | 16% of a 200 mg dose | Nutrients 2022;14(20):4417, Table 1. PMID 36297100 (confirmed) |
| Chocolate, dark, 70-85% cacao solids | 80 mg caffeine (plus 802 mg theobromine) | 4 squares, 25 g | 20 mg | 10% of a 200 mg dose | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170273 (SR Legacy, NDB 19904); both values verified |

- **To match the supplement dose:** An ergogenic 3-6 mg/kg is 210-420 mg for a 70 kg adult, which is two to four mugs of home brewed coffee at 96 mg each; a 200 mg pre-workout dose is about two mugs or three single espresso shots. What food cannot deliver is a known dose. Measured caffeine in caffeinated specialty coffees ranged 58-259 mg per dose, mean 188 mg (SD 36) for a 16 oz cup, and the same beverage bought from the same outlet on six consecutive days ranged 259-564 mg, a 2.2-fold swing in what the customer thought was an identical drink. Decaffeinated versions still contained up to 17.7 mg per dose. Matcha spans 18.9-44.4 mg/g, so one teaspoon is anywhere from 38 to 89 mg. Reaching the dose from drinks is trivial; hitting it within plus or minus 20% is not, which is why trials use capsules.
- **Food versus supplement:** The caveat is inverted here. Anhydrous caffeine powder is the same molecule and is dangerous precisely because it is concentrated: a level teaspoon is several grams, in the range associated with fatal poisoning, whereas the same amount as brewed coffee would be 30 to 50 mugs and physically self-limiting. Whole-drink forms carry matrix effects that change the experience and the risk profile. Coffee brings chlorogenic acids and, when unfiltered, diterpenes that raise LDL; tea and matcha bring L-theanine and catechins that alter the subjective stimulation; energy drinks bring sugar, taurine and often glucuronolactone, so a caffeine dose from a can is never only caffeine. Guarana concentrates the same molecule in a seed powder, which is how many energy products reach their potency without looking like a caffeine tablet. Tea and coffee polyphenols also inhibit non-haem iron absorption when taken with meals, a cost the capsule does not impose. Energy drink and specialty coffee servings are large and easy to stack, and label values are nominal rather than analysed.
- **Absorption:** Caffeine is essentially completely absorbed from every form tested, so there is no meaningful absorption penalty for the food route; the differences are in rate and in dose certainty. Liquids reach peak plasma concentrations faster than capsules, chewing gum faster still, and a hot drink sipped over 20 minutes produces a flatter curve than a capsule swallowed at once, which matters for timing a pre-exercise dose and not for total exposure. Analytical values differ between databases for the same drink because brewing dominates: USDA lists brewed coffee at 40 mg/100 g and espresso at 212 mg/100 g (FDC 171890, 171891), while the Brazilian table lists espresso at 279 mg/100 mL (DOI 10.3390/nu14204417). Both are correct for what they measured, which is the whole point: the food is the variable, and the absorption is not.
- **Data note:** for the database and BraCaffT rows. All four USDA FDC IDs were verified down to NDB number, description and nutrient value; all six BraCaffT values, the matcha range and every McCusker variability figure were confirmed verbatim. The two added rows, guarana and instant coffee powder, rest on approximate ranges from the verification report with no primary source traced, and are labelled indicative. Yerba mate is a known omission with no figure to place it

*References: Guest ISSN PMID 33388079; Grgic PMID 30926628; Barreto PMID 37844569; EFSA J 2015;13(5):4102*

### Omega-3 (EPA and DHA)

**Verdict:** TAKE &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** 4 g/d of prescription-grade product if fasting triglycerides are at or above 500 mg/dL. Otherwise eat oily fish.
- **Who it is for:** People with severe hypertriglyceridaemia. Separately, pregnant women with genuinely low baseline DHA, and people who eat essentially no fish.

**Pros**

- Triglyceride lowering is genuine pharmacology: 4 g/d cuts triglycerides by at least 30% in severe hypertriglyceridaemia, and the AHA endorses this specific indication.
- REDUCE-IT was strongly positive in statin-treated patients with established cardiovascular disease: primary composite 17.2% versus 22.0%, HR 0.75 (0.68-0.83), an absolute reduction of 4.8 percentage points over 4.9 years.
- Pregnancy: Cochrane found preterm birth before 37 weeks RR 0.89 (0.81-0.97) and before 34 weeks RR 0.58 (0.44-0.77), both graded high quality, across 70 trials and 19,927 women.

**Cons**

- It does not prevent cardiovascular disease at supplement doses. Cochrane, 86 trials and 162,796 people: all-cause mortality RR 0.97 (0.93-1.01) at HIGH certainty, stroke RR 1.02. VITAL and ASCEND both missed their primary endpoints at 1 g/d.
- The two big high-dose trials contradict each other. STRENGTH tested 4 g/d of EPA plus DHA against corn oil in 13,078 people and was flatly null (HR 0.99), stopped for futility. REDUCE-IT used a mineral-oil placebo that raised LDL, apolipoprotein B and hs-CRP in the control arm, so part of its 25% may be placebo-arm harm. Quote both trials or neither.
- Atrial fibrillation risk rises with dose: HR 1.25 (1.07-1.46), dose-dependent across 7 trials. Prior atrial fibrillation is a reason to stay away.
- Bleeding risk on warfarin, direct oral anticoagulants, aspirin and clopidogrel. REDUCE-IT showed serious bleeding 2.7% versus 2.1%.
- Product quality is poor. Half of North American products exceeded voluntary oxidation limits, and 38% of Australian products exceeded primary oxidation limits, with premium pricing giving no advantage. Buy encapsulated, unflavoured and in date.
- Pregnancy is not straightforward either: ORIP (n=5,486, 900 mg/d) was null and showed excess very-large-for-gestational-age infants. Benefit concentrates in women with low baseline DHA.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose partly reachable
- **Data confidence:** High

Oily fish supply EPA and DHA in gram quantities per ordinary portion, so this is one of the few supplement categories where food competes with capsules at physiological doses. One 143 g herring fillet or one 130 g salmon portion delivers 1.8-2.9 g EPA+DHA, roughly seven to twelve times a single day's EFSA Adequate Intake of 250 mg (EFSA Journal 2010;8(3):1461, DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2010.1461; EFSA DRVs summary 2017). The lower end of that range is deliberate: the USDA farmed-salmon entry reflects older analyses, and in over 3,000 Scottish farmed salmon EPA+DHA fell from 2.74 g to 1.36 g per 100 g wet weight between 2006 and 2015 as feed shifted from fish oil to vegetable oil (Sprague 2016, DOI 10.1038/srep21892), giving 1.8 g for the same 130 g portion. Treat the farmed-salmon figures below as an upper bound and roughly halve them for current farmed fish. The richest sources in the USDA database are not fillets at all: caviar carries 6,540 mg EPA+DHA per 100 g and mixed fish roe 2,343 mg, both above cooked herring.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Herring, Atlantic, cooked, dry heat | EPA 909 mg, DHA 1,100 mg (total 2,009 mg) | 1 fillet (143 g) | EPA 1,300 mg, DHA 1,573 mg (total 2,873 mg) | 1,149% of one day's 250 mg AI (11.5 days' worth); 72% of a 4 g/day pharmacological dose | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175117 (SR Legacy), confirmed on re-check |

- **To match the supplement dose:** Two doses with two different answers. The physiological dose (EFSA AI 250 mg/day, evidence range 250-500 mg/day) is easily eaten: one 143 g herring fillet a week averages 410 mg/day, one 130 g farmed salmon portion 399 mg/day, one wild salmon portion 342 mg/day. Two mackerel fillets, two 92 g tins of sardines or two 45 g tins of anchovies a week give 258-302 mg/day. In portion terms 250-500 mg/day is 0.6-1.3 salmon portions, 0.6-1.2 herring fillets, 1.7-3.3 mackerel fillets, 1.9-3.9 tins of sardines, 1.9-3.8 tins of anchovies, 2.8-5.6 trout fillets, or 1.2-2.4 USDA 172 g cans of albacore per week. Tinned light tuna is the weakest realistic route at 370 mg per 165 g can, which is 5 cans a week for 250 mg/day and 9 cans a week for 500 mg/day. The pharmacological dose of 4 g/day used in trials and in prescription products (AHA Science Advisory, Circulation 2019, DOI 10.1161/CIR.0000000000000709; REDUCE-IT used 4 g icosapent ethyl) cannot be eaten. It requires 199 g/day of herring (1.4 kg/week), 186 g/day of farmed salmon on 2006-era composition or 294 g/day on 2015 composition (1.3-2.1 kg/week), 288 g/day of pickled herring, 333 g/day of mackerel (3.8 fillets a day), 407 g/day of sardines (4.4 tins a day), 195 g/day of anchovies (4.3 tins a day, with the sodium load that implies), 61 g/day of caviar (about four tablespoons a day), or 464 g/day of tinned albacore. Above roughly 1 g/day of EPA+DHA, supplements are the only realistic route.
- **Food versus supplement:** Whole fish and fish oil are not interchangeable in the evidence base. VITAL randomised 25,871 adults to a 1 g/day capsule supplying 840 mg EPA+DHA (460 mg EPA, 380 mg DHA) and found no reduction in major cardiovascular events (HR 0.92, 95% CI 0.80-1.06) or cancer incidence (HR 1.03) over 5.3 years (Manson 2019, PMID 30415637). The benefit at 4 g/day in REDUCE-IT came from purified prescription EPA rather than a food-equivalent mixture, and high-dose omega-3 carries an atrial fibrillation signal that eating fish does not. On contaminants, the direction of the tradeoff is clear but the numbers are weaker than they look: the FDA/EPA per-species mercury means quoted in the earlier draft (1.45 ppm Gulf of Mexico tilefish, 1.00 swordfish, 0.98 shark, 0.73 king mackerel, 0.35 albacore, 0.13 canned light tuna, 0.01-0.08 for sardine, salmon, anchovy, Atlantic mackerel, trout and herring) could not be retrieved from any live FDA or EPA page, so treat every one of them as indicative and unconfirmed. The tilefish figure in particular should be re-checked, since FDA's published Gulf of Mexico mean is usually cited as 1.123 ppm. What the surviving EPA page does confirm is the qualitative placement: anchovies, Atlantic mackerel, sardines and trout are all 'Best Choices'. Independent synthesis in Karimi 2012 (PMID 22732656) gives grand means of 0.882 ppm for shark and 0.883 ppm for tilefish, and 0.450 ppm for fresh or frozen tuna, which is not the canned product and should not be read as such. The earlier claim that high-mercury predators are also mediocre omega-3 sources has been withdrawn: it rested on a swordfish figure of 108 mg EPA per 85 g with no FDC ID that could not be traced, and an EPA-only figure understates a DHA-dominant fish. Dioxins run the other way, concentrating in the fat of oily fish. EFSA cut the tolerable weekly intake to 2 pg TEQ/kg body weight and reported it exceeded across all European age groups with fatty fish among the top contributors (EFSA Journal 2018;16(11):5333, DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2018.5333, document confirmed; the '5 to 15 fold' exceedance figure inside it was not independently verified). Refined fish oils must meet 1.75 pg WHO-TEQ/g fat for dioxins and 6.0 pg/g fat for dioxins plus dioxin-like PCBs under Regulation (EU) 2023/915 (limits not re-verified in this pass), so a purified concentrate can carry a lower contaminant load per gram of EPA+DHA than the fish it came from. One processing detail: draining oil-packed tuna pours away omega-3, and it takes both fatty acids with it. Canned white tuna in oil retains 66 mg EPA and 178 mg DHA per 100 g after draining, a total of 244 mg, against 233 mg EPA and 629 mg DHA (862 mg total) for the water-packed product, a 72% loss of total EPA+DHA (USDA FDC 175157 versus 175158, both confirmed).
- **Absorption:** Chemical form changes absorption measurably. Using natural triglyceride fish oil as the 100% reference, the bioavailability index was 124% for re-esterified triglyceride and 73% for ethyl ester (Dyerberg 2010, PMID 20638827, both values confirmed). The 91% figure for free fatty acid and the 3.1-3.6 g/day matched dose range are consistent with the paper as usually cited but were not independently confirmed. Most cheap concentrates are ethyl esters, which also depend more on a fat-containing meal for absorption, so a 1,000 mg ethyl-ester capsule taken on an empty stomach does not deliver what the label implies. Fish is delivered as natural triglyceride inside a fat and protein matrix and needs no such allowance. Preparation matters more than cooking method: canning in oil and then draining removes omega-3, whereas canning in water and draining does not.
- **Data note:** All eleven USDA composition rows resolved to exactly the described food with the stated EPA and DHA values, and roughly 60 dose-gap calculations recomputed correctly. The two defects were invented portion weights, now replaced with USDA's own measures. Confidence drops only on the peripheral material: the FDA/EPA per-species mercury means, the EU 2023/915 dioxin limits and the swordfish EPA figure are unconfirmed and are labelled as such

*References: Cochrane PMID 32114706; REDUCE-IT PMID 30415628; STRENGTH PMID 33190147; Gencer PMID 34612056; Cochrane pregnancy PMID 30480773; ORIP PMID 31509674; AHA PMID 31422671*

## 5. Consider it, in one narrow situation

### Magnesium

**Verdict:** CONSIDER &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** 365 mg/d elemental for blood pressure. Note magnesium oxide is 60% elemental, citrate 16%, bisglycinate 14%.
- **Who it is for:** People with documented low magnesium, chronic proton pump inhibitor or diuretic users, alcohol use disorder, and treated hypertensives.

**Pros**

- Blood pressure falls by a real amount in the right people: -5.97 mmHg systolic in the hypomagnesaemic and -7.68 mmHg in treated hypertensives.
- Proton-pump-inhibitor-induced hypomagnesaemia is an established, under-recognised clinical problem that this genuinely fixes.
- Cheap, and dietary intake is genuinely low in many Western diets.

**Cons**

- In replete normotensive people the pooled blood pressure effect did not reach significance. The general-population heart-health pitch is unsupported.
- The sleep claim, now the dominant marketing angle, rests on 3 trials and 151 older adults, all at moderate to high risk of bias, and the review authors called the literature substandard for making recommendations.
- The 600 mg/d migraine dose exceeds the US supplemental upper limit of 350 mg and is 2.4 times the EFSA limit of 250 mg. Diarrhoea is the dose-limiting effect.
- Contraindicated without supervision in chronic kidney disease stage 4-5 and dialysis, where hypermagnesaemia causes bradycardia, hypotension and respiratory depression. Also in myasthenia gravis.
- Chelates tetracyclines, fluoroquinolones and bisphosphonates. Separate by 2-4 hours.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** High

Magnesium sits in the chlorophyll molecule and in the outer layers of seeds and grains, so every unrefined plant food carries some; nuts, seeds, cocoa, wholegrains, legumes and dark leafy greens are all meaningful contributors, and a mixed diet built on them reaches the adult RDA (420 mg/day men 31+, 320 mg/day women 31+, Institute of Medicine 1997) with no supplement. Refining is what strips it, and the honest like-for-like comparison is CoFID wholemeal bread at 66 mg/100 g against white bread at 22 mg/100 g (11-981 and 11-1145), a three-fold gap. The wheat bran figure of 389 mg/100 g (CoFID 11-906) is a dry milling fraction, so setting it against a roughly 40%-water baked loaf inflates the effect of refining to eighteen-fold and should not be used that way.

(Br J Nutr 2004;91:601-606) measured fractional magnesium absorption of 26.7% from a spinach meal against 36.5% from kale and concluded the penalty is partly offset by spinach's higher native magnesium, which leaves roughly 42 mg absorbed per cooked cup against roughly 11 mg for a cooked cup of kale. Spinach is one of the better absorbed-magnesium portions on this list, and the 5.1% logic that disqualifies it for calcium does not transfer to magnesium |

- **To match the supplement dose:** Supplements are sold at 200 to 400 mg elemental magnesium, which is the whole daily requirement, and ordinary food covers that range easily. A worked day: 1 oz pumpkin seeds (156 mg) plus 1 cup cooked black beans (120 mg) plus 1 oz 70-85% dark chocolate (65 mg) is 341 mg before anything else on the plate is counted. Single-food routes are absurd by comparison: 400 mg from dark chocolate alone means 175 g of bar, roughly 1,000 kcal, and 400 mg from wholemeal bread alone means 533 g, about 17 slices. Nobody needs a single-food route, because the mixed-diet route is three normal portions. The safety asymmetry matters more than the arithmetic: the Institute of Medicine set the 350 mg/day upper limit for supplemental magnesium only, and it explicitly excludes magnesium naturally present in food and water, so 700 mg of magnesium from seeds and beans carries no upper limit while 400 mg from tablets is already over the supplemental ceiling. A comparable 250 mg/day EU limit for magnesium added through supplements or fortified foods is normally attributed to the SCF 2001 opinion; the source text could not be retrieved during verification, so that figure is carried here as indicative and unconfirmed.
- **Food versus supplement:** The difference is dose kinetics rather than chemistry. Food delivers magnesium in small amounts across a day inside a matrix that slows release, so the unabsorbed fraction stays small and osmotic diarrhoea does not happen. A 400 mg bolus of a soluble magnesium salt leaves a large unabsorbed load in the gut lumen, and that load is the mechanism of the laxative effect that set the upper limit in the first place. Magnesium citrate and magnesium hydroxide are marketed as laxatives at doses close to supplement doses. In anyone with reduced renal clearance (CKD stage 4-5, elderly patients on multiple drugs) supplements can cause symptomatic hypermagnesaemia, while food magnesium is handled by renal excretion across the normal intake range. There is no situation where the food form is the risky one.
- **Absorption:** Net absorption of dietary magnesium is 40 to 60% at an intake averaging 380 mg/day, falling to 15-36% at higher daily intakes of 550 to 850 mg (Institute of Medicine DRI report 1997, Chapter 6, NCBI Bookshelf NBK109816). NIH ODS quotes 30-40% for public guidance, so the working figure should be the lower half of the DRI range rather than the 50% midpoint. Phytate is the main food inhibitor and can cut fractional absorption by up to 60% in a dose-dependent way, so the magnesium in bran, seeds and legumes is not fully realised. Oxalate matters much less than the textbook version implies: Bohn et al. (Br J Nutr 2004;91:601-606) measured 26.7% fractional absorption from a spinach meal against 36.5% from kale and concluded the spinach penalty is at least partly counterbalanced by its higher native magnesium content, so a cooked cup of spinach delivers roughly 42 mg absorbed against roughly 11 mg from a cooked cup of kale. Supplement forms differ sharply among themselves: Firoz and Graber (Magnesium Research 2001;14(4):257-262, PMID 11794633) measured fractional absorption of about 4% for magnesium oxide against 9-11% for the lactate, aspartate and chloride forms, and magnesium oxide is the cheapest and most widely sold form. A 400 mg magnesium oxide tablet can therefore deliver less absorbed magnesium than a handful of pumpkin seeds while producing the loose stools.
- **Data note:** for composition and arithmetic: verification checked every cited FDC ID against the USDA SR Legacy April 2018 dataset and every CoFID code against CoFID 2021, and found no fabricated identifier, no wrong per-100 g value and no wrong portion weight, with all per-portion multiplications reproducing. Two lower-confidence pockets are flagged in place: the mineral water row carries no sourced figure at all, and the flaxseed, wheat germ, wheat bran and cocoa portion weights are conventions rather than dataset portions. The EU SCF 250 mg/day added-magnesium limit is consistent with the standard figure but was not confirmable from the source text

*References: Argeros Hypertension 2025 PMID 41000008; Mah & Pitre PMID 33865376; Peikert PMID 8792038*

### Zinc lozenges

**Verdict:** CONSIDER &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** 75-100 mg/d elemental, split into 6-8 lozenges, started within 24 hours of symptom onset and stopped when symptoms resolve.
- **Who it is for:** Someone at the very start of a cold who will actually follow the lozenge schedule.

**Pros**

- Cold duration shortens. Hemila found -33% (21-45%) across lozenge trials, with no additional benefit above 100 mg/d.
- Cheap, and the treatment window is short enough that the copper risk does not apply.
- Zinc for acute childhood diarrhoea in deficient populations is a separate and much stronger indication, used as standard of care in low-income settings.

**Cons**

- The evidence is genuinely contested, and honest reporting means saying so. Cochrane 2024 rated duration LOW certainty at -2.37 days, found prevention null and severity null. Hemila published a methodological rebuttal alleging Cochrane pooled a 190 mg/d lozenge trial with a 0.046 mg/d nasal trial, a 4,300-fold dose difference.
- Chronic intake above 40 mg/d causes copper depletion, which produces sideroblastic anaemia, neutropenia and an irreversible myelopathy that mimics subacute combined degeneration. This is the reason the treatment must be short.
- Formulation matters and most retail lozenges are wrong. Citric acid and sorbitol bind zinc and stop it being released.
- Chelates fluoroquinolones and tetracyclines. Nasal zinc caused permanent anosmia and should never be used.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** High

Two separate questions share a mineral name here, and they get different answers. Zinc the nutrient is abundant in ordinary food: oysters are the outlier of the entire composition table, red meat and offal follow, and wheat germ, seeds and legumes carry useful amounts, so on the question of whether the substance is in food the answer is RICH. Zinc lozenges are not a nutritional product: the trials used more than 75 mg/day of elemental zinc, most commonly 80 to 92 mg/day and in two trials 192 to 207 mg/day, delivered as slowly dissolving zinc acetate or gluconate lozenges taken roughly every two hours while awake (PMID 28515951), and the mechanism depends on free zinc ions released in the mouth and throat over 20 to 30 minutes in contact with the pharyngeal mucosa. Food zinc is swallowed and absorbed in the small intestine, so no amount of it reproduces the intended local effect, which is why dose_from_food is NO while food_status is RICH. Reference point used in the table: EFSA PRI 7.5 mg/d for adult women and 9.4 mg/d for adult men at a phytate intake of 300 mg/d (EFSA, Dietary Reference Values for nutrients Summary report, approved 4 December 2017, doi 10.2903/sp.efsa.2017.e15121, Tables 5 and 7; underlying opinion EFSA Journal 2014;12(10):3844).

| Wheat germ, crude | 12.3 mg | 1 oz (28 g) | 3.4 mg | 46% of PRI, high phytate | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168892 (SR Legacy), per-100 g value supplied by the verification report. Portion conventional, per-portion derived. |
| Hemp seed, hulled | 9.9 mg | 3 tbsp (30 g) | 3.0 mg | 40% of PRI, high phytate | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170148 (SR Legacy), per-100 g value supplied by the verification report. Portion conventional, per-portion derived. |
| Sesame seed kernels, toasted | 10.2 mg | 1 oz (28 g) | 2.9 mg | 38% of PRI, high phytate | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170152 (SR Legacy), per-100 g value supplied by the verification report. Portion conventional, per-portion derived. |
| Lentils, boiled without salt | 1.27 mg | 1 cup (198 g) | 2.5 mg | 34% on paper, high phytate (see bioavailability) | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 172421 (SR Legacy) |
| Chickpeas, boiled without salt | 1.53 mg | 1 cup (164 g) | 2.5 mg | 33% on paper, high phytate | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173757 (SR Legacy) |
| Pumpkin seed kernels, roasted, unsalted | 7.64 mg | 1 oz (28 g) | 2.2 mg | 29% on paper, high phytate | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170557 (SR Legacy) |

Included as the everyday dairy contributor that also raises zinc absorption from phytate-rich meals (PMID 7361691). Portion conventional, per-portion derived. |
| Shiitake mushrooms, dried | 7.66 mg | 4 dried caps (15 g) | 1.1 mg | 15% of PRI | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168436 (SR Legacy), per-100 g value supplied by the verification report. The 7.66 mg is a dried-weight figure and the portion is dried weight to match. Portion conventional, per-portion derived. |
| Wholemeal bread | 1.77 mg | 1 slice (32 g) | 0.57 mg | 8% on paper, high phytate | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 172688 (SR Legacy) |

- **To match the supplement dose:** The lozenge dose fails on arithmetic and then fails again on route. Arithmetically, hitting the 80 mg/day of elemental zinc used in the trials (PMID 28515951) every day for the duration of a cold takes 204 g of raw oyster meat, roughly 14 to 15 medium oysters; or 102 g of cooked oysters; or 88 g of canned oysters, about 11 of them; or 479 g of toasted wheat-germ cereal; or 989 g of braised lean beef chuck; or 1.10 kg of braised lamb shoulder; or 1.05 kg of pumpkin seed kernels; or 5.23 kg of boiled chickpeas; or 6.30 kg of boiled lentils. Only oysters make it look even arithmetically plausible, and 14 raw oysters a day for a week is a Vibrio and norovirus decision rather than a nutrition one. Closing the arithmetic would still not reproduce the product, because the lozenge trials work through free Zn2+ released in the oropharynx and held against the mucosa, and swallowed zinc from food or from a capsule bypasses that route entirely. On scale, 80 to 92 mg/day is roughly 11 to 12 times the EFSA female PRI of 7.5 mg/d (10.7 and 12.3 times); the 'eight times' figure quoted earlier corresponds to the male PRI of 9.4 mg/d, not the female one named in the sentence. Separately, and this is the part food answers easily, the nutritional dose is trivially reachable: 85 g of braised lean beef chuck (6.9 mg) or 6 medium raw oysters (33.4 mg) meets or vastly exceeds the 7.5 to 9.4 mg PRI in one portion.
- **Food versus supplement:** Zinc gluconate lozenges shortened colds by 28 percent and zinc acetate lozenges by 40 percent, a 12-point difference that was not statistically significant (95% CI -12 to +36), and there is no evidence that doses above 100 mg/day do better than 80 to 92 mg/day (PMID 28515951). The salt matters less than people claim and the lozenge format matters more. Zinc capsules, tablets, syrups and food are not substitutes in this indication, because the local contact is the point. Two safety notes follow from the numbers: 80 to 92 mg/day is roughly 11 to 12 times the EFSA female PRI and well above nutritional upper levels, defensible only for a few days because sustained high zinc antagonises copper absorption; and this is a short pharmacological course with an actual end date, unlike a nutrient. One earlier claim about the oyster records has to be withdrawn. Cooking does concentrate oyster zinc by moisture loss (raw 89.0 g water, cooked 78.2 g; dry matter 11.0 g against 21.8 g, so solids exactly double and zinc exactly doubles from 39.3 to 78.6 mg per 100 g). Canning does not work that way: canned oysters hold 85.1 g water and 14.9 g dry matter, only 1.35 times the raw solids, yet 2.3 times the zinc at 91.0 mg, which is 1.7 times richer per gram of solids (611 mg zinc per 100 g dry matter against 357 raw and 361 cooked). The canned entry reflects different material or different analyses rather than moisture loss. Per-100 g comparisons across preparation states remain misleading either way unless the yield is tracked.
- **Absorption:** Oysters are the outlier of the entire food composition table. At 39.3 mg/100 g raw and up to 91.0 mg/100 g canned they carry four to twelve times the zinc of red meat, which is itself the next best category, and they are why oysters answer every zinc question. Beyond oysters, absorption efficiency does most of the work and phytate is the variable. Human whole-body-retention studies of matched cereal meals found fractional zinc absorption of 26.8 percent from a rye bread meal containing 100 umol of phytic acid, falling to 8.4 percent from oatmeal porridge containing 600 umol, a threefold loss driven by phytate alone (PMID 2824731). Fractional absorption is not the whole story, and the earlier version of this section had the direction of the bread comparison backwards. A related study found that a lower absolute amount of zinc was absorbed from white bread than from the same amount of wholemeal bread, because wholemeal supplies more zinc and the phytate penalty on fractional absorption did not overturn that content advantage; white bread only won once both breads were enriched with zinc chloride. Adding milk products did improve zinc absorption from the wholemeal meal (PMID 7361691). Replacing 25 percent of the beef protein in a composite meal with defatted soy flour lowered both the zinc content and the zinc absorbed, while the same substitution in a chicken meal changed neither (PMID 6893252). EFSA builds phytate straight into the requirement: the adult male PRI rises from 9.4 mg/d at 300 mg phytate/day to 11.7, 14.0 and 16.3 mg/d at 600, 900 and 1,200 mg, and the female PRI from 7.5 to 9.3, 11.0 and 12.7 mg/d across the same range, ratios of 1.73 and 1.69. A high-legume, high-wholegrain diet therefore needs about 70 percent more zinc on the label to deliver the same absorbed zinc. On mitigations, the evidence is narrower than previously claimed: the cited review covers added Aspergillus niger phytase (12 iron studies, 5 zinc studies) and states that for zinc not enough information is available to determine optimal activities (PMID 24050000), so added phytase is supported while soaking, sprouting and sourdough fermentation need a fermentation-specific source. Animal protein and milk in the same meal do raise absorption, and vegetarians consequently sit at the top of the EFSA PRI range rather than the bottom (PMID 12936958).
- **Data note:** All eleven original per-100 g figures were confirmed to the digit against FoodData Central, the Hemila lozenge meta-analysis was confirmed on every parameter (dose threshold, the 80 to 92 and 192 to 207 mg/day bands, 40 against 28 percent by salt, no benefit above 100 mg/day), and the EFSA phytate-graded PRI table matched the source text. The six added rows use conventional portion sizes rather than USDA measure-table entries, so their per-portion figures are derived and are flagged as such in place

*References: Cochrane Nault PMID 38719213; Hemila PMID 28515951; Hemila & Chalker PMID 39478818*

### Melatonin

**Verdict:** CONSIDER &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** 0.5-5 mg. Timing is the active ingredient, not dose. For phase advance, 0.5 mg taken 2-4 hours before dim-light melatonin onset.
- **Who it is for:** Jet lag eastward across five or more time zones, delayed sleep-wake phase disorder, shift work, and blind people with free-running rhythms.

**Pros**

- Circadian phase-shifting is real pharmacology with a mapped phase-response curve. For jet lag across five or more zones the NNT is 2, and 9 of 10 trials were positive.
- In delayed sleep-wake phase disorder, sleep onset advanced by 0.67 hours and endogenous melatonin onset by 1.18 hours.
- Short-term safety is good and it is not habit-forming in the way sedatives are.

**Cons**

- For ordinary insomnia it barely does anything: 7 minutes off sleep latency and 8 minutes more total sleep across 19 trials and 1,683 people. The AASM guideline explicitly suggests clinicians NOT use it for insomnia.
- Retail dose accuracy is the worst documented in this document. 22 of 25 gummy products were inaccurately labelled, at 74-347% of the declared amount, and one product labelled 3 mg contained no melatonin at all but 31.3 mg of CBD. A separate analysis of 31 products found -83% to +478% of label and serotonin contamination in 8 of them.
- Not for children. The gummy format plus up to 347% overdosing plus palatability is a bad combination.
- Retail doses of 5-10 mg are far above what the pharmacology needs and can shift the clock the wrong way if taken at the wrong time.
- Cochrane specifically flagged caution in people on warfarin and people with epilepsy. Fluvoxamine markedly raises melatonin exposure via CYP1A2 inhibition.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Trace amounts only
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Medium

Melatonin is genuinely present across a wide range of plant and animal foods, and the richest of them are not the fruits and nuts usually named: roasted coffee, red wine and germinated legumes carry far more than tart cherries or walnuts. Even so, every realistic portion sits one to four orders of magnitude below a 0.5-5 mg supplement dose. The supplement is a separate article from the dietary substance: commercial melatonin is chemically synthesised rather than extracted from food, so a product sold as natural melatonin is a manufactured molecule and would be labelled SYNTHETIC if this field described the pill instead of the substance.

Compiled secondary figure, primary measurement not re-traced here. The value is for the dry grounds, not for the brewed cup. |

Whole egg is about 75% water, giving a fresh-weight equivalent near 1.5 ng/g.
