Night-milked milk runs several-fold higher and is sold commercially on that basis, so one value understates the range. |

- **To match the supplement dose:** 1 mg requires roughly 104 g of roasted coffee grounds, the closest any food comes, and grounds are not consumed whole (brewing transfers an uncharacterised fraction to the cup); or 7.7 L of red wine; or about 918 g of dry-matter germinated lentils; or 1.9 kg dry-matter germinated kidney beans; or 5.3 kg of white mustard seed; or 9.7 kg of dried goji at the review value of 103 ng/g dry weight; or 23 kg of fenugreek seed, 34 kg of sunflower seed, 42 kg of tomato, 74 kg of Montmorency cherries, 89 kg of strawberries, 286 kg of walnuts, 485 kg of Balaton cherries, 818 kg of almonds, about 655-670 kg of egg (13,100-13,400 eggs) and about 64,500 L of milk. Pistachios cannot be given a single number: the point estimate is 383 kg but the reported SD (3096 pg/g) exceeds the mean (2609 pg/g), so the true requirement is uncertain by roughly an order of magnitude in either direction. Halve all of these for a 0.5 mg dose, multiply by five for 5 mg. The submission's headline claim that goji berries are the one food a human could theoretically eat to a dose does not survive correction, because it rested on an unreplicated outlier; at the authoritative value no food in the table reaches a supplement dose in an edible quantity.
- **Food versus supplement:** Food melatonin is not a smaller version of a tablet. Tart cherry juice concentrate measurably raises urinary 6-sulfatoxymelatonin and improved actigraphy-measured sleep efficiency at 2 x 30 mL/day (Howatson et al. Eur J Nutr 2012;51(8):909-916, PMID 22038497, verified), yet no melatonin content figure for the concentrate could be sourced here, so the one preparation with a positive human sleep outcome remains uncosted; the melatonin delivered is a fraction of a microgram, and most of the effect probably comes from the polyphenol matrix and tryptophan availability. Cultivar swamps everything: Montmorency and Balaton are the same species and differ 6.5-fold. Two melatonin-rich botanicals bear on this list for context: Scutellaria (huang-qin) at about 7,110 ng/g dry weight and St John's wort at about 4,490 ng/g dry weight, the latter classified NOT_FOOD in its own entry while being among the richest melatonin-bearing plants on record. On the pistachio dispute: one 2014 spectrofluorimetric paper reported roughly 233 ug/g dry weight, about 89,300 times the LC-MS/MS value, and it carries a published Expression of Concern (original PMID 24878439; EoC Spectrochim Acta A 2019;217:322, PMID 31027610). At that disputed figure 4.3 g of pistachios, about seven kernels, would supply 1 mg, which is exactly why the number should be treated as unconfirmed rather than as a food-based melatonin strategy. The Expression of Concern record lists only the four original authors and states no replication failure, so the submission's attribution of it to a German federal risk-assessment body has been removed. On the supplement side, doses commonly sold at 3-10 mg are ten to thirty times a physiological replacement dose, independent testing of commercial products has repeatedly found large deviations from label content plus occasional serotonin contamination, and melatonin is a prescription-only medicine in the UK and much of the EU while sold freely as a supplement in the US.
- **Absorption:** Absolute oral bioavailability of melatonin is low and highly variable, averaging about 15% (reported range roughly 9-33%, with individual values spanning a five-fold spread), because up to 85% is removed on first pass, mainly by hepatic CYP1A2. CYP1A2 modifiers therefore matter more than the source: caffeine raises plasma melatonin from a given dose, smoking lowers it. Nothing is known about whether the nanogram to low-microgram quantities in a food matrix follow the same kinetics, and at those quantities the question is close to academic. Anyone comparing natural melatonin in pistachios with a tablet is comparing a sub-microgram intake against a supra-physiological one.
- **Data note:** The cherry values, the walnut value, the pistachio LC-MS/MS value, the milk value, the disputed 233 ug/g figure and the 89,300x ratio were all verified against primary sources, and the bioavailability figure matches the established literature. Against that, the entry's original anchor (goji) had to be replaced with a review value, the egg figure had to be re-based from dry solids to fresh weight, and the ten added rows all rest on a single compiled secondary review table (PMC5409706) whose primary measurements were not re-traced in this pass

*References: Burgess PMID 20410229; van Geijlswijk PMID 21120122; Ferracioli-Oda PMID 23691095; Cohen JAMA 2023 PMID 37097362; Erland & Saxena PMID 27855744; Sateia AASM PMID 27998379*

### Probiotics, named strain only

**Verdict:** CONSIDER &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** Antibiotic-associated diarrhoea in children: at least 5 billion CFU/d. C. difficile prevention: 10-50 billion CFU/d. Infant colic: L. reuteri DSM 17938 at 100 million CFU/d.
- **Who it is for:** Children on a course of antibiotics, adults on antibiotics with elevated C. difficile risk, and exclusively breastfed infants with colic.

**Pros**

- Paediatric antibiotic-associated diarrhoea: 8% versus 19%, RR 0.45 (0.36-0.56), NNT 9 at the higher dose. This is a genuinely useful number.
- C. difficile prevention during antibiotics: RR 0.50 (0.38-0.64), 1.6% versus 3.2%.
- Infant colic in exclusively breastfed babies: NNT 2.6 for treatment success at day 21, which is a striking number for a benign intervention with a defined stop point.
- Pouchitis maintenance after antibiotic-induced remission has a real randomised result: remission maintained in 17 of 20 versus 1 of 16.

**Cons**

- The category claim is unfalsifiable marketing. Evidence attaches to a named strain at a defined dose. Disaggregate 82 trials and 10,332 IBS patients by strain and GRADE certainty collapses to low or very low almost everywhere.
- C. difficile prevention has NNT 65 at LOW certainty, so 64 of 65 people get nothing. The absolute benefit scales with baseline risk.
- The largest trial ever run was null. PLACIDE randomised 2,981 hospitalised patients over 65 and found no effect on either antibiotic-associated diarrhoea (RR 1.04) or C. difficile.
- The AGA recommends AGAINST probiotics for irritable bowel syndrome outside clinical trials.
- There is a real harm signal in the critically ill. In predicted severe acute pancreatitis, mortality was 16% versus 6% (RR 2.53) with 9 cases of bowel ischaemia, 8 fatal, versus zero on placebo. The FDA also warned providers after a preterm infant died of probiotic-associated sepsis.
- Absolute contraindications: central venous catheter, neutropenia, severe immunocompromise, critical illness, and preterm infants outside a supervised protocol.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** No food source, manufactured
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** High

SYNTHETIC here means no dietary source, not an artificial molecule: a named probiotic strain is a live organism, a single clonal isolate held in a culture bank, grown in industrial fermenters, freeze-dried and encapsulated. No food contains it. Fermented foods do carry large numbers of live lactic acid bacteria, but the species and strains present are whichever organisms grow in that food and its starter, never the deposited trial strain. Because probiotic efficacy is demonstrated strain by strain, a food supplying a different strain of the same species is a different product. Live-organism loads for fermented foods are given under the generic multi-strain entry.

*No food rows: there is no ordinary food that supplies this in a meaningful amount.*

- **To match the supplement dose:** The gap is categorical and no quantity of food closes it. Named-strain doses in trials run from about 1x10^8 to 1x10^10 CFU/day; Bifidobacterium longum 35624 (formerly classified as infantis) was effective at 1x10^8, a figure the source trial expressed as CFU/mL rather than CFU/day (PMID 16863564). A single cup of kefir at 2.5x10^8 CFU/mL supplies roughly 6x10^10 live lactic acid bacteria (DOI 10.3390/foods15101616), so the raw CFU total is easily exceeded by food. The fraction of those cells belonging to the trialled strain is zero, which makes the CFU comparison meaningless for a strain-specific claim.
- **Food versus supplement:** This is the most important form caveat in the group. Clinical evidence for probiotics is strain-specific, and often formulation-specific and indication-specific on top of that. Yoghurt, kefir, sauerkraut, kimchi, miso, natto and tempeh do not contain the strains tested in the trials, so evidence generated with a named strain does not transfer to any fermented food, and does not transfer to a different strain of the same species either. Eating fermented food is a reasonable dietary habit with its own emerging evidence base, and it is a separate thing from taking a named strain at a defined dose for a specific clinical effect. Two practical points: heat kills the organisms, so a pasteurised or cooked ferment (canned sauerkraut, miso stirred into boiling soup, baked tempeh) delivers no live cells; and species-level labelling on a supplement such as 'contains Lactobacillus acidophilus' without a strain designation carries no more transferable evidence than a food does.
- **Absorption:** Survival of gastric acid and bile is strain-dependent, and the delivery matrix changes it: the fat and buffering capacity of a dairy food improves survival relative to a bare capsule, while enteric coating or microencapsulation is used to achieve the same in supplements. Dose is not monotonic, which is the clearest evidence that formulation matters as much as CFU count. In a 362-patient dose-ranging trial in primary-care IBS, B. infantis 35624 at 1x10^8 was significantly superior to placebo and to all other doses, while 1x10^6 and 1x10^10 were not; the paper attributes the failure of the highest dose to significant formulation problems (Whorwell et al., Am J Gastroenterol 2006;101:1581-1590, PMID 16863564). Two generalisability caveats the submission omitted: the trial enrolled women only, and doses were expressed as CFU/mL in the paper rather than CFU/day. A higher label CFU is therefore not better, and label CFU at manufacture is not the same as viable CFU at end of shelf life.
- **Data note:** The claim is categorical (no food contains a deposited trial strain) and the supporting trial was verified: 362 patients, doses 1x10^6, 1x10^8 and 1x10^10, with the 1x10^8 arm significantly superior to placebo and all other doses. The one detail that failed verification, the gelling mechanism for the top dose, has been removed and replaced with the paper's own wording

*References: Cochrane Guo PMID 31039287; Cochrane CD006095.pub5 PMID 40931979; Sung PMID 29279326; PLACIDE PMID 23932219; Goodoory & Ford PMID 37541528; AGA PMID 32531291; PROPATRIA PMID 18279948*

### Berberine

**Verdict:** CONSIDER &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** 500 mg two or three times daily with meals. Note dihydroberberine and phytosome forms are more bioavailable and are not milligram-equivalent.
- **Who it is for:** Adults with type 2 diabetes or prediabetes not reaching glycaemic targets, as an add-on, with a prescriber informed.

**Pros**

- The effect is real and clinically sized, which is unusual in this document: fasting glucose -0.82 mmol/L, HbA1c -0.63%, across 37 trials and 3,048 patients. That HbA1c reduction is in the range of a real drug.
- Total adverse events were lower than comparators in the pooled analysis (RR 0.73), and it does not cause hypoglycaemia on its own.

**Cons**

- The marketing understates the pharmacology rather than overstating it, and that is the danger. This behaves like a drug and should be treated as one.
- The most dangerous interaction in this document: 600 mg/d for three months raised ciclosporin trough concentrations by 88.9% in 52 renal transplant recipients. Note that this interaction dose is BELOW the typical glycaemic dose.
- Berberine alone showed no statistical advantage over lifestyle change. The benefit appears as an add-on to an existing oral hypoglycaemic.
- The trial base is dominated by small Chinese trials that meta-analysts repeatedly describe as having low methodological quality.
- No upper limit exists. Toxicology review reports dose-dependent gastrointestinal ulceration, immunotoxicity, phototoxicity, neurotoxicity and cardiotoxicity. Contraindicated in pregnancy and in neonates (kernicterus risk).

**Food sources**

- **In food:** From a plant not eaten as food
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** High

Berberine is an isoquinoline alkaloid from the roots, rhizomes and stem bark of medicinal plants: Berberis vulgaris (common barberry), B. aristata, B. asiatica and B. lycium, Coptis chinensis (huang lian) rhizome, Hydrastis canadensis (goldenseal), Mahonia aquifolium (Oregon grape) and Phellodendron amurense. None of these organs is eaten as food. Accumulation is highest in root tissue, 1.6-4.3% of dry weight across Berberis species, and B. vulgaris bark carries over 8% total alkaloids of which about 5% is berberine (Neag et al., Front Pharmacol 2018, PMC6111450, verified). Barberry fruit is the one part genuinely eaten, as zereshk in Iranian rice dishes, and it is the wrong organ: the whole-plant survey reporting up to 26,482 ug/g berberine in root and no detectable alkaloids in fruit pulp across 96 accessions was done on Berberis darwinii, the Chilean michay, which is a congener of the zereshk species rather than the same plant, and in that study leaves and seeds did carry detectable (lower) alkaloid levels, so only the pulp was non-detect (Molecules 2025;30(8):1849, PMID 40333914). The species question is closed by a separate group working directly on B. vulgaris fruit, which reports that the flowers, fruit pulp and seeds have little or no significant alkaloid content and describes their fruit extract as nearly devoid of berberine (Iran J Pharm Res 2011;10(1):97-104, PMID 24363687).

*No food rows: there is no ordinary food that supplies this in a meaningful amount.*

- **To match the supplement dose:** Trials use 500 mg two or three times daily, 1000-1500 mg/day. At the 5% berberine content of B. vulgaris root bark that is 20-30 g of dried root bark per day, which is a herbal decoction rather than a meal. Via the edible fruit the gap is not a large number, it is undefined: berberine is below the limit of detection in barberry pulp, so no portion size reaches the dose at all. A generous serving of zereshk polow uses perhaps 30-50 g of dried berries spread across several people and contributes no measurable berberine. Coptis rhizome and goldenseal root would need 1-3 g of dried herb and neither is eaten as food.
- **Food versus supplement:** This is the clearest case in the set where a food and its supplement share only a plant name. Eating zereshk supplies vitamin C, anthocyanins and organic acids, with no pharmacological berberine effect. The isolated alkaloid at 1000-1500 mg/day behaves like a drug: it inhibits CYP3A4 and CYP2D6 and is a P-glycoprotein substrate and inhibitor, so it can raise levels of co-administered medicines; it commonly causes constipation, cramping and diarrhoea; and it is contraindicated in pregnancy and in neonates because berberine displaces bilirubin from albumin and has been linked to kernicterus risk. Whole-plant root extracts also carry berbamine, palmatine and, in goldenseal, hydrastine, so a barberry root extract and a purified berberine HCl capsule are not the same product. Goldenseal is additionally a conservation concern and a frequent target of species adulteration.
- **Absorption:** Berberine's oral bioavailability is under 1%, measured at 0.36-0.68% in rats, with roughly 99.5% eliminated during gastrointestinal and hepatic first pass through poor solubility, self-aggregation in gastric acid, P-glycoprotein efflux and CYP-mediated intestinal metabolism (Expert Opin Drug Metab Toxicol 2023;19(3), PMID 37057922). Much of berberine's metabolic effect is thought to be exerted in the gut lumen and on the microbiome rather than systemically, so low bioavailability does not mean inactive. It does mean trace amounts from any food would be pharmacologically irrelevant, and it means formulation comparisons (plain salt, phytosome, micelle) can change exposure by an order of magnitude at the same labelled dose.
- **Data note:** Every quantitative claim in this entry was checked and matched its source verbatim: the 1.6-4.3% root range, the 8% total alkaloid / 5% berberine figure for B. vulgaris bark, the 26,482 ug/g root maximum, the 96-accession pulp non-detect, and the 20-30 g root-bark dose equivalent. The NOT_FOOD classification was challenged and upheld

*References: Xie PMID 36467075; Lan PMID 25498346; Dong PMID 23118793; ciclosporin interaction PMID 16133554*

### Vitamin C

**Verdict:** CONSIDER &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** 200 mg/d if taken regularly. Higher doses do not shorten colds further and cause osmotic diarrhoea.
- **Who it is for:** Smokers, heavy alcohol users, people on very restricted diets, and those under extreme physical stress such as marathon runners.

**Pros**

- It cures scurvy, which is an unambiguous deficiency disease, and scurvy still occurs in food-insecure and alcohol-dependent populations.
- Under extreme physical stress such as marathons and sub-arctic exercise, prophylactic vitamin C halved cold incidence. That subgroup effect is real.
- Smokers genuinely need 35 mg/d more than non-smokers because of higher turnover.

**Cons**

- It does not prevent colds in the general population: RR 0.97 (0.94-1.00) across 29 comparisons and 11,306 people. This is the founding claim of the modern supplement industry and it is false.
- Duration falls only 8% in adults and 14% in children, which is roughly half a day.
- Intravenous vitamin C in sepsis is HARMFUL. The LOVIT trial (n=863) found more death or persistent organ dysfunction in the treatment arm.
- Avoid with a history of calcium oxalate kidney stones or hyperoxaluria, and in renal impairment, because ascorbate is metabolised to oxalate.
- Upper limit 2,000 mg/d, set on osmotic diarrhoea.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** High

Genuinely rich and cheap in ordinary food, and the verification pass challenged this classification and upheld it as the strongest in the set: one 119 g red pepper (152 mg) or one 55 g guava (125 mg) exceeds the 90 mg adult male RDA on its own, with no trace-amount inflation anywhere in the list. Synthetic ascorbic acid is manufactured (Reichstein or two-step fermentation from glucose), but it is the identical molecule, so the synthetic label carries no penalty here. Percentages below are given against both DRI values, since the RDA is 90 mg for men and 75 mg for women and quoting only the male figure understates every food's contribution for women by 20%. Rose hips, camu-camu, chilli peppers, papaya and Brussels sprouts are further rich sources for which no verified figure was available, so they get no row.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Blackcurrants (currants, european black), raw | 181 mg | 1 cup (112 g) | 203 mg | 225% of the 90 mg RDA for men (271% of the 75 mg RDA for women); 20% of a 1,000 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173963, SR Legacy |
| Sweet red pepper, raw | 128 mg | 1 medium pepper (119 g) | 152 mg | 169% of the 90 mg RDA for men (203% of the 75 mg RDA for women); 15% of a 1,000 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170108, SR Legacy |
| Guavas, common, raw | 228 mg | 1 fruit without refuse (55 g) | 125 mg | 139% of the 90 mg RDA for men (167% of the 75 mg RDA for women); 13% of a 1,000 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173044, SR Legacy |
| Orange juice, raw (freshly squeezed) | 50.0 mg | 1 cup (248 g) | 124 mg | 138% of the 90 mg RDA for men (165% of the 75 mg RDA for women); 12% of a 1,000 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 169098, SR Legacy |
| Kale, raw | 93.4 mg | 100 g raw (a generous salad serving) | 93.4 mg | 104% of the 90 mg RDA for men (125% of the 75 mg RDA for women); 9.3% of a 1,000 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168421 for the 93.4 mg/100 g figure as verified in the verification report; the 100 g portion basis is stated here rather than taken from a USDA household measure |
| Strawberries, raw | 58.8 mg | 1 cup halves (152 g) | 89.4 mg | 99% of the 90 mg RDA for men (119% of the 75 mg RDA for women); 8.9% of a 1,000 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 167762, SR Legacy |
| Acerola (west indian cherry), raw | 1,680 mg | 1 fruit (4.8 g) | 81 mg | 90% of the 90 mg RDA for men (108% of the 75 mg RDA for women); 8.1% of a 1,000 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171686; the 1,680 mg/100 g value, the 4.8 g fruit weight and the 81 mg per fruit are as given in the verification report. The richest verified source in the database, and it ranks mid-list here only because one fruit weighs under 5 g; 60 g, about 12 fruits, is a full 1,000 mg. |
| Oranges, raw, all commercial varieties | 53.2 mg | 1 medium fruit, 2-5/8 inch (131 g) | 69.7 mg | 77% of the 90 mg RDA for men (93% of the 75 mg RDA for women); 7.0% of a 1,000 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 169097, SR Legacy |
| Kiwifruit, green, raw | 92.7 mg | 1 fruit, 2 inch diameter (69 g) | 64.0 mg | 71% of the 90 mg RDA for men (85% of the 75 mg RDA for women); 6.4% of a 1,000 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168153, SR Legacy |
| Broccoli, cooked, boiled, drained, without salt | 64.9 mg (raw broccoli is 89.2 mg/100 g, FDC 170379: boiling costs 27%) | half cup chopped (78 g) | 50.6 mg | 56% of the 90 mg RDA for men (67% of the 75 mg RDA for women); 5.1% of a 1,000 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 169967; raw comparison FDC ID 170379, SR Legacy |
| Potatoes, baked, flesh and skin, without salt | 9.6 mg | 1 medium potato (173 g) | 16.6 mg | 18% of the 90 mg RDA for men (22% of the 75 mg RDA for women); 1.7% of a 1,000 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170093, SR Legacy |
| Spinach, raw | 28.1 mg | 1 cup (30 g) | 8.4 mg | 9% of the 90 mg RDA for men (11% of the 75 mg RDA for women); 0.84% of a 1,000 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168462, SR Legacy |

- **To match the supplement dose:** This is the one item in the set where the supplement dose is physically reachable by eating. A 1,000 mg tablet equals 60 g of raw acerola, about 12 small fruits, or 439 g of guava (8 fruits), or 552 g of blackcurrants, or 781 g of raw red pepper (about 6.6 medium peppers), or 1.07 kg of raw kale, or 1.70 kg of strawberries, or 1.88 kg of orange (14 medium oranges), or 2.0 kg of fresh orange juice, which is about 1.9 litres (the submission's '2.0 litres' confused grams with litres). A 500 mg dose is 3.3 red peppers or 7 oranges. Eccentric for most of that list, and trivially easy through acerola, so a person genuinely could eat this dose. The real gap is that the dose above roughly 200 mg is pointless rather than unreachable. Levine et al. gave 30-2,500 mg/day under depletion-repletion control: bioavailability was complete at a single 200 mg dose and fell off at 500 mg and above with the absorbed excess appearing in urine, while leucocytes saturated at 100 mg/day and plasma at 1,000 mg/day (PNAS 1996;93:3704-9, PMID 8623000, DOI 10.1073/pnas.93.8.3704). Four or five servings of ordinary fruit and vegetables reach the physiologically useful plateau, and the 1,000 mg tablet mostly funds expensive urine. The number that should temper any composition table is what survives handling: see cooking and storage below.
- **Food versus supplement:** Effectively NONE on the molecule. Synthetic and food-derived vitamin C are chemically identical and all steady-state human comparative bioavailability studies show no difference, with some acute pharmacokinetic studies showing minor transient differences of no physiological consequence (Carr and Vissers, Nutrients 2013;5:4284-304, PMID 24169506, DOI 10.3390/nu5114284). That kills the claim that natural vitamin C is better absorbed, and it means acerola, camu-camu and rose hip products carry no absorption advantage; most such products are in any case standardised by adding synthetic ascorbic acid, so the buyer gets the synthetic molecule with fruit powder attached. Note that acerola still earns a food row on content alone, at 1,680 mg/100 g the richest verified source in the database, which is a separate matter from the debunked absorption claim. What the whole food genuinely adds is the rest of the matrix, potassium, folate, carotenoids, flavonoids and fibre, none of which is in the tablet and none of which the vitamin C evidence is about. Where a caveat does bite is the textbook recommendation to eat spinach: its vitamin C is modest at 28.1 mg/100 g, a realistic 30 g cup gives 8.4 mg, and boiling extracts most of what is there, moving 60% of the vitamin C and 50% of the flavonoids of fresh-cut spinach into the cooking water (Gil, Ferreres and Tomas-Barberan, J Agric Food Chem 1999;47:2213-7, PMID 10794612). Recommending boiled spinach for vitamin C comes close to recommending the cooking water. Raw kale at 93.4 mg/100 g is the green that actually delivers, three times the spinach figure.
- **Absorption:** Absorption is near-complete up to about 200 mg per dose via the sodium-dependent transporter SVCT1, declines above 500 mg, and plasma saturates at 1,000 mg/day (PMID 8623000). Absorption is therefore not the variable that matters; retention before eating is. Cooking and storage losses, all from USDA Table of Nutrient Retention Factors Release 6 (2007), ascorbic acid column: greens boiled in water to cover and drained retain 55% (code 3005), greens boiled in a little water and drained 60% (3004), greens stir-fried 85% (3015), greens cooked from frozen and drained 60% (3019); other vegetables boiled in water to cover and drained 75% (3775), steamed 85% (3784); potatoes baked in the skin 80% (3301), boiled pared and drained 75% (3308), and only 25% when boiled after storage and browned (3311); canned fruit 50% (0154) and dried fruit 20% (0158). Steaming beats boiling and draining consistently: in broccoli every method except steaming caused significant loss of vitamin C and chlorophyll (Yuan et al., J Zhejiang Univ Sci B 2009;10:580-8, PMID 19650196, DOI 10.1631/jzus.b0920051). Storage compounds it: 15 days at 4 C across 19 fruits and vegetables lowered vitamin C and cut total phenolics by up to 29.67%, though total anthocyanins showed minimal change in that study despite the title (Galani et al., Antioxidants 2017;6(3):59, PMID 28737734). Practical upshot: a week-old boiled vegetable can deliver a quarter of its table value, so the 27% loss between raw broccoli at 89.2 mg/100 g and boiled broccoli at 64.9 mg/100 g is the optimistic case rather than the worst one.
- **Data note:** All ten original per-100 g values and all gram weights matched named FoodData Central records exactly, including the raw and boiled broccoli split, and every per-portion figure recomputed. All five cited PMIDs resolve to the stated journal, volume and pages with findings matching the abstracts, and eleven retention factors were verified row by row against Release 6. Two smaller qualifications: the acerola and kale figures come from the verification report's own FDC lookups rather than the original submission, and the 100 g kale portion basis is stated by this dataset rather than taken from a USDA household measure

*References: Cochrane Hemila PMID 23440782; LOVIT PMID 35704292*

### Coenzyme Q10 / ubiquinol

**Verdict:** CONSIDER &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** 100 mg three times daily, the Q-SYMBIO regimen. Ubiquinol is more bioavailable and doses do not transfer 1:1 from ubiquinone.
- **Who it is for:** Patients with heart failure with reduced ejection fraction, already on guideline therapy, with cardiology input.

**Pros**

- Q-SYMBIO is the only clinical-endpoint result in the whole longevity and metabolic category: n=420, 2 years, major adverse cardiovascular events 15% versus 26%, HR 0.50 (0.32-0.80).
- Genuine primary CoQ10 deficiency is a real, rare condition where replacement is the treatment.

**Cons**

- Q-SYMBIO is a single unreplicated trial. The NIHR health technology assessment covering 26 trials and 2,250 participants flagged important concerns about risk of bias, plausibility of effect sizes and applicability, and found all-cause mortality RR 0.68 (0.45-1.03), not significant.
- For statin-associated muscle symptoms, which is where most retail CoQ10 is actually sold, it does nothing: weighted mean difference -0.42 (-1.47 to 0.62) and adherence RR 0.99 across 7 trials.
- Warfarin interaction: CoQ10 is structurally similar to vitamin K, and one cross-sectional analysis of patients presenting with anticoagulant overdose found an association with bleeding.
- Expensive relative to everything else in this document, and the 1,200 mg observed safe level comes from a risk assessment whose authors were affiliated with an industry trade body.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Medium

Organ meats and oily fish are genuine, substantial sources, so the substance question and the dose question separate cleanly here. Pork heart at 118.1-282 mg/kg is the richest food in the literature, beef heart is 113 mg/kg, chicken heart 92.3-192 mg/kg, chicken liver 116.2-132.2 mg/kg and mackerel dark flesh 43-67 mg/kg. Total habitual dietary intake is only 3.8 mg/day in women and 5.4 mg/day in men (Mattila & Kumpulainen 2001), so a single 100 g portion of heart is two to five days of ordinary intake. That is what RICH means in this dataset. It does not mean the supplement dose is reachable, which it is not. Supplement CoQ10 is produced by microbial fermentation or chemical synthesis, not extracted from beef.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |

| Pork heart, cooked | 11.8-28.2 mg CoQ10 (118.1-282 mg/kg) | 1 serving (100 g) | 11.8-28.2 mg | 3.9-9.4% of a 300 mg dose; 2-7 days' worth of total ordinary dietary CoQ10 | CoQ10 food-composition literature as compiled in the verification review (118.1-282 mg/kg, the richest food source on record). Added because its omission is what let the submission present beef heart as the best case; the specific primary reference was not stated in the review, so treat the provenance as indicative even though the value is widely reproduced |
| Chicken hearts, cooked | 9.2-19.2 mg CoQ10 (92.3-192 mg/kg) | 1 serving (100 g) | 9.2-19.2 mg | 3.1-6.4% of a 300 mg dose | Vongsawasdi & Noomhorm 2014 (chicken heart 92.3-192 mg/kg). The submission's 116.2-132.2 mg/kg is chicken LIVER, and the Pravst-derived table contains no chicken heart row at all. | Chicken liver, cooked | 11.6-13.2 mg CoQ10 (116.2-132.2 mg/kg) | 1 serving (100 g) | 11.6-13.2 mg | 3.9-4.4% of a 300 mg dose | Confirmed in multiple reviews (Bioactive Compounds in Meat 2014; Pathera et al. 2017). Added on its own merits: this is the food the submission's chicken heart row was actually quoting |
| Beef heart, trimmed, cooked | 11.3 mg CoQ10 (113 mg/kg) | 1 serving (100 g) | 11.3 mg | 3.8% of a 300 mg dose; about 2-3 days' worth of total ordinary dietary CoQ10 | Pravst, Zmitek & Zmitek 2010, Crit Rev Food Sci Nutr 50(4):269-280, PMID 20301015, DOI 10.1080/10408390902773037 (value confirmed against a reproduction of the paper's table; the original is paywalled) |
| Mackerel, dark (red) flesh | 4.3-6.7 mg CoQ10 (43-67 mg/kg); white flesh only 1.1-1.6 mg/100 g | 1 fillet (140 g) | 1.5-9.4 mg depending on how much of the dark lateral muscle is eaten | 0.5-3.1% of a 300 mg dose | Pravst et al. 2010, DOI 10.1080/10408390902773037 (both flesh values confirmed) |

| Sardines, canned in oil, drained, with bones | 0.5-6.4 mg CoQ10 (5-64 mg/kg, a 13-fold spread between samples) | 1 standard tin, drained (92 g) | 0.46-5.9 mg | 0.15-2.0% of a 300 mg dose | Pravst et al. 2010, DOI 10.1080/10408390902773037 (confirmed); food identity and portion USDA FDC 175139, ID not confirmed this session |
| Beef liver, pan-fried | 3.9-5.0 mg CoQ10 (39-50 mg/kg) | 1 serving (100 g) | 3.9-5.0 mg | 1.3-1.7% of a 300 mg dose | Pravst et al. 2010, DOI 10.1080/10408390902773037 (confirmed); food identity USDA FDC 168627, ID not confirmed this session |
| Soybean oil | 5.4-28 mg CoQ10 (54-280 mg/kg) | 1 tbsp (13.6 g) | 0.7-3.8 mg | 0.24-1.3% of a 300 mg dose | Pravst et al. 2010, DOI 10.1080/10408390902773037 (confirmed) |
| Olive oil | 4.0-16 mg CoQ10 (40-160 mg/kg) | 1 tbsp (13.5 g) | 0.54-2.2 mg | 0.18-0.72% of a 300 mg dose | Pravst et al. 2010 table, same source as the soybean oil row already present. Added because soybean oil was the only oil listed |
| Avocado | 1.0 mg CoQ10 (10 mg/kg) | 1 medium avocado, flesh (150 g) | 1.5 mg | 0.5% of a 300 mg dose | Pravst et al. 2010 table (added from the same table the other rows come from) |

| Sesame seeds, pistachios, walnuts and hazelnuts | 1.7-2.3 mg CoQ10 (sesame 18-23, pistachio 20, walnut 19, hazelnut 17 mg/kg) | 1 small handful (30 g) | 0.51-0.69 mg | 0.17-0.23% of a 300 mg dose | Pravst et al. 2010 table (added from the same table; grouped because the four values are within 25% of each other) |
| Parsley, fresh | 0.8-2.6 mg CoQ10 (8-26 mg/kg) | 1/4 cup chopped (15 g) | 0.12-0.39 mg | 0.04-0.13% of a 300 mg dose | Pravst et al. 2010 table (added from the same table) |

- **To match the supplement dose:** A 300 mg/day dose needs 1.06 kg of pork heart at its richest measured value, rising to 2.54 kg at its low end; 1.6 to 3.3 kg of chicken hearts; 2.65 kg of beef heart; or 4.7 kg of the very richest sardines, about 51 tins, rising past 60 kg if the tins sit at the low end of the measured range. The submission's '2.7 kg of beef heart' was presented as the floor only because pork heart, the richest source of all, was missing. Against a whole day's ordinary dietary CoQ10 of 3.8-5.4 mg, a 300 mg capsule is about 56 to 79 days of eating in one dose and a 100 mg capsule about 19 to 26 days. The one boundary case worth stating: 355 g of pork heart at its richest measured value does reach 100 mg, which is a real meal in some cuisines but not a diet anyone sustains. Anyone whose reason for taking CoQ10 is a claimed pharmacological effect at 100-300 mg cannot get there by eating; anyone whose reason is a low-CoQ10 diet can move the needle with a weekly portion of heart, liver or oily fish.
- **Food versus supplement:** Significant. CoQ10 is a large lipophilic quinone (MW 863) and crystalline ubiquinone powder in a dry tablet absorbs very poorly; commercial products use oil suspensions, solubilisates or reduced-form ubiquinol precisely because the raw molecule does not dissolve. Absorption is saturable, so a 300 mg dose does not deliver proportionally more than a 100 mg dose. Food has one genuine advantage: CoQ10 in meat and fish comes packaged with fat, and supplemental CoQ10 taken with a fatty meal absorbs materially better, so a capsule swallowed on an empty stomach may deliver less than the label implies. Cooking costs some of the food content, with reported losses of roughly 15-32% frying pork cutlets and about 15% grilling beef. Most body CoQ10 is made endogenously and the biosynthetic pathway shares its early steps with cholesterol synthesis, which is the basis of the statin argument; neither food nor supplement changes that pathway.
- **Absorption:** Low and formulation-dependent for the supplement, which is the honest headline. Ubiquinol (reduced) and solubilised formulations achieve higher plasma levels than plain crystalline ubiquinone, and all forms depend on dietary fat and bile for uptake. In the food matrix CoQ10 sits in mitochondrial membranes and is co-ingested with fat, so uptake per milligram is probably as good as or better than a dry capsule, but the milligram totals are 10 to 80 times too small: the richest single portion in the table is 28.2 mg against a 300 mg dose. The reduced-to-oxidised split also differs between foods: a Japanese survey of 70 food items found ubiquinol-10 above 20 ug/g in pork shoulder, bovine liver, chicken heart, horse mackerel, young yellowtail and soybean oil (Kubo H, Fujii K, Kawabe T, Matsumoto S, Kishida H, Hosoe K, 'Food content of ubiquinol-10 and ubiquinone-10 in the Japanese diet', J Food Compos Anal 2008;21(3):199-210, DOI 10.1016/j.jfca.2007.10.003), so ubiquinol is not an exclusively supplemental form.
- **Data note:** Eight of the Pravst table values were confirmed, along with the habitual intake figures and every per-portion conversion built on them, but the confirmation came from a reproduction of the table rather than the paywalled original. The two richest rows are weaker: pork heart's provenance was not pinned to a specific primary reference, and chicken heart comes from a secondary compilation after the submission's figure turned out to belong to chicken liver. The USDA FDC IDs are unconfirmed

*References: Q-SYMBIO PMID 25282031; Kennedy PMID 32179207; Claxton NIHR HTA PMID 35076012*

### Alpha-lipoic acid

**Verdict:** CONSIDER &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** 600 mg/d orally. The strong evidence is for the intravenous route at the same dose, which consumers cannot access.
- **Who it is for:** People with symptomatic diabetic distal symmetric polyneuropathy, specifically positive sensory symptoms such as burning and paraesthesia.

**Pros**

- Licensed as a drug for diabetic polyneuropathy in Germany, which is a higher bar than any supplement in this document clears.
- The intravenous meta-analysis (4 trials, 1,258 patients) found consistent relief of positive neuropathic symptoms.
- Secondary endpoints in the long oral trial did improve, including neuropathy impairment scores.

**Cons**

- The definitive oral trial failed. NATHAN 1 randomised 460 patients to 600 mg/d for 4 years and missed its primary endpoint (P=0.105).
- Serious adverse events in that trial were 38.1% on alpha-lipoic acid versus 28.0% on placebo, an excess that is rarely mentioned in marketing.
- It is a leading trigger of insulin autoimmune syndrome (Hirata disease) in people carrying HLA-DRB1*04 alleles, producing anti-insulin antibodies and severe hypoglycaemia in non-diabetics. Almost nobody knows their HLA status.
- No upper limit has been set by any authority.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Trace amounts only
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Low

TRACE was challenged and upheld, and the qualitative point matters more than the numbers: alpha-lipoic acid exists in food only as lipoyllysine, covalently attached to lysine residues in the E2 subunits of mitochondrial keto-acid dehydrogenases. Free racemic ALA, which is what a capsule contains, does not occur in food at all. The measured food quantities are in the range 0.003 to 0.40 mg per 100 g, four to five orders of magnitude below a 600 mg capsule. Supplemental ALA is chemically synthesised, so the supplement is best read as a synthetic compound with a bound, chemically different trace analogue in food.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Heart (turkey, pork, calf, cow, chicken), raw | 0.21-0.40 mg lipoyllysine (2.11-3.99 ug/g fresh weight: turkey 3.99, pork 3.46, calf 3.32, cow 3.12, chicken 2.11) | 1 serving (100 g) | 0.21-0.40 mg | 0.04-0.07% of a 600 mg dose | Kaminska A & Chwatko G 2020, 'Estimation of lipoyllysine content in meat and its antioxidative capacity', J Agric Food Chem 68(39):10992-10999, PMID 32886880, DOI 10.1021/acs.jafc.0c03778. Indicative and unconfirmed: the citation resolves but the per-tissue table is behind the ACS paywall, and the abstract reports only method validation plus one worked example (turkey heart homogenate, lipoic acid 0.12 and lipoyllysine 0.88 umol/L), which back-converts to roughly the right magnitude. Note also that the Lodge 1997 dataset ranks bovine kidney above heart, the reverse of this ranking, and that discrepancy is unresolved |
| Kidney (beef/pork), raw | 0.07-0.14 mg lipoyllysine (0.71-1.40 ug/g fresh weight) | 1 serving (100 g) | 0.07-0.14 mg | 0.01-0.02% of a 600 mg dose | Kaminska & Chwatko 2020, PMID 32886880. Indicative and unconfirmed for the same reason; the Lodge 1997 dataset places kidney above heart, which would move this row to the top of the table |
| Liver (beef/pork), raw | 0.06-0.12 mg lipoyllysine (0.56-1.17 ug/g fresh weight) | 1 serving (100 g) | 0.06-0.12 mg | 0.01-0.02% of a 600 mg dose | Kaminska & Chwatko 2020, PMID 32886880. Indicative and unconfirmed; liver is bottom of the organ ranking in both datasets, which is the one point they agree on |
| Broccoli, raw | about 0.010 mg lipoyllysine (0.94 +/- 0.33 ug/g DRY weight; about 89% water, giving 10.3 ug/100 g fresh) | 1 cup chopped (91 g) | about 0.0094 mg (9.4 micrograms) | 0.0016% of a 600 mg dose | Lodge JK et al. 1997, J Appl Nutr 49:3-11, reproduced by Moini et al. 2002 and tabulated by the Linus Pauling Institute (dry-weight value and the dry-to-fresh conversion both confirmed); fresh/dry basis from USDA FDC 170379, ID not confirmed this session |
| Spinach, raw | about 0.028 mg lipoyllysine (3.15 +/- 1.11 ug/g DRY weight; spinach is about 91% water, giving 27-28 ug per 100 g fresh) | 1 cup raw leaves (30 g) | about 0.0085 mg (8.5 micrograms) | 0.0014% of a 600 mg dose | Lodge JK et al. 1997, J Appl Nutr 49:3-11, reproduced by Moini et al. 2002 and tabulated by the Linus Pauling Institute (highest plant value; dry-weight figure and conversion both confirmed); fresh/dry basis from USDA FDC 168462, ID not confirmed this session |
| Tomato, raw | about 0.003 mg lipoyllysine (about 0.56 ug/g DRY weight; about 94.5% water) | 1 medium tomato (123 g) | about 0.0038 mg (3.8 micrograms) | 0.0006% of a 600 mg dose | Lodge JK et al. 1997, J Appl Nutr 49:3-11, as tabulated by the Linus Pauling Institute. The spinach and broccoli dry-weight values from this dataset were confirmed; the tomato value is plausible but was not directly confirmed, so treat it as indicative |

- **To match the supplement dose:** Using the confirmed plant figures, a 600 mg dose is about 2,143 kg of raw spinach a day, roughly 71,000 cups, and broccoli is similar. Using the richest meat value (turkey heart at 0.40 mg/100 g) it is 150 kg of heart a day, though that meat figure is itself unconfirmed. The arithmetic understates the problem. What food contains is lipoyllysine, lipoic acid amide-bonded to protein-bound lysine, and human lipoamidase activity releases very little free lipoic acid from that bond, so the free-ALA yield from eating spinach is close to unmeasurable rather than merely small. A 600 mg fasting capsule produces micromolar plasma peaks of free racemic lipoic acid; no quantity of food produces a detectable free-ALA peak at all. This is the clearest food-supplement discontinuity in the group.
- **Food versus supplement:** Large, and it cuts against the supplement. Supplemental ALA is chemically synthesised as a 50:50 racemate of R- and S-lipoic acid, while nature makes only the R enantiomer and only protein-bound, so the capsule contains a molecule (S-lipoic acid) that does not exist in food. Absorption differs by design as well: capsules are dosed fasting because food reduces ALA absorption by roughly 20-30%, whereas the trace food form is unavoidably eaten with food. On safety, the whole food is unremarkable and the concentrated free acid is not. High-dose free ALA has been linked to insulin autoimmune syndrome with severe hypoglycaemia in genetically susceptible people, and there is a paediatric accidental-overdose literature with seizures and death. Spinach has done none of that. If you want the antioxidant-vegetable benefit, eat the vegetables; if you want the 600 mg neuropathy dose from the ALADIN and SYDNEY trials, understand that it is a synthetic drug-dose racemate with its own risk profile.
- **Absorption:** Free supplemental ALA is absorbed reasonably well but cleared very fast, with a plasma half-life on the order of half an hour, and the R enantiomer reaches higher plasma levels than S. Food lipoyllysine is a different question: it is not a bioavailable source of free lipoic acid, because the lipoyl group stays bound to the enzyme protein and is largely digested along with it rather than released as the free dithiol. The measurement literature reflects this, noting that free lipoic acid in tissues is low if present at all and that essentially all of it is protein-bound. Spinach carries the usual oxalate caveat for other purposes, but oxalate is not why spinach fails as a lipoic acid source; it fails because there is almost none there and what is there is chemically locked up.
- **Data note:** The three richest rows, which set the top of the ranking and the headline dose gap, come from a paper whose per-tissue table sits behind the ACS paywall and could not be traced past a method-validation abstract. They are also contradicted on rank order by the Lodge dataset, which puts kidney above heart. Only the Lodge plant values (spinach 3.15 and broccoli 0.94 ug/g dry weight) and their dry-to-fresh conversions were independently confirmed, and the tomato value was not. The qualitative conclusion is unaffected, since every candidate figure is four to five orders of magnitude below a 600 mg dose

*References: NATHAN 1 PMID 21775755; Ziegler IV meta-analysis PMID 14984445; BMJ Case Rep PMID 42493219*

### Beta-alanine

**Verdict:** CONSIDER &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** 4-6 g/d for at least 2-4 weeks, split into doses of about 1.6 g to limit paraesthesia. Total accumulated dose matters more than daily dose.
- **Who it is for:** Athletes whose limiting factor is acidosis in the one to four minute window: middle distance, rowing, combat sports, high-repetition lifting.

**Pros**

- The mechanism is sound and confirmed: it raises intramuscular carnosine, which buffers protons.
- The IOC consensus statement places buffering agents on its short list of supplements with robust performance evidence.
- Cheap, and paraesthesia aside, no established contraindication or drug interaction exists.

**Cons**

- The overall pooled effect is SMD 0.18 (0.08-0.28) across 40 studies and 1,461 people. Statistically significant and practically tiny.
- In the 0.5 to 10 minute window where it should work best, exercise CAPACITY improved (SMD 0.50) but exercise PERFORMANCE did not (SMD 0.11, 95% CI -0.20 to 0.42, non-significant). Capacity is a laboratory measure; performance is what a race is.
- The meta-analysis is routinely quoted for the flattering subgroup and never for the null one.
- No long-term safety data beyond a few months, and no upper limit set by any authority.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** High

Free beta-alanine, the molecule in the tub, is manufactured and no food supplies it in that form, which is a question about form rather than about dietary presence. What food supplies is beta-alanine bound into the histidine dipeptides carnosine and anserine, hydrolysed after ingestion, at hundreds of milligrams of beta-alanine equivalent per 100 g of white muscle: 346 mg/100 g in commercial broiler breast and 767 mg/100 g in the native Korean strain measured in the same study. Per portion that is 589 mg and 1,304 mg, 18% and 41% of a 3.2 g daily dose, at or above the per-portion contribution creatine makes, and isomolar oral L-carnosine raised muscle carnosine as much as free beta-alanine did (65.8% versus 64.2%), so the dietary route is biologically real. The earlier TRACE label was wrong by the dataset's own yardstick; poultry dark meat is the weak part of the picture, at 19.51-36.19 mg carnosine per 100 g.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Chicken breast, native Korean strain HH, raw (carnosine 511.04 plus anserine 1,525.80 mg/100 g) | 767 mg beta-alanine equivalent (carnosine x 0.394 plus anserine x 0.371) | 1 skinless breast, 170 g raw | 1,304 mg beta-alanine equivalent | 41% of a 3.2 g/day dose | Ali M, Lee SY, Park JY, Jung S, Jo C, Nam KC. Comparison of Functional Compounds and Micronutrients of Chicken Breast Meat by Breeds. Food Sci Anim Resour 2019;39(4):632-642. PMID 31508593; DOI 10.5851/kosfa.2019.e54 (values confirmed in full text). Added because the original cited this paper but used only its lowest strain. |
| Chicken breast, commercial broiler, raw (carnosine 257.94 plus anserine 660.38 mg/100 g) | 346 mg beta-alanine equivalent (carnosine x 0.394 plus anserine x 0.371) | 1 skinless breast, 170 g raw | 589 mg beta-alanine equivalent (439 mg carnosine, 1,123 mg anserine) | 18% of a 3.2 g/day dose | Ali M et al., Food Sci Anim Resour 2019;39(4):632-642. DOI 10.5851/kosfa.2019.e54 (both dipeptide values and the 346 mg conversion re-derived and confirmed) |
| Chicken breast, Thai native and black-bone breeds, raw | 428.08-553.93 mg carnosine, i.e. 169-218 mg beta-alanine equivalent from carnosine alone | 1 skinless breast, 170 g raw | 728-942 mg carnosine (287-371 mg beta-alanine equivalent) | 9-12% of a 3.2 g/day dose (carnosine only; anserine not reported) | Khumpeerawat P, Duangjinda M, Phasuk Y. Animals (Basel) 2021;11(7):1987. PMID 34359114; DOI 10.3390/ani11071987 (range confirmed) |
| Beef muscle, raw (Japanese Black/Wagyu steers, 21 muscles) | 187.5 mg carnosine (8.291 umol/g wet tissue, range across the 21 muscles 0.27-418.7 mg/100 g) plus 26.5 mg anserine (1.104 umol/g) = 84 mg beta-alanine equivalent | 1 steak, 200 g raw | 375 mg carnosine plus 53 mg anserine = 167 mg beta-alanine equivalent | 5% of a 3.2 g/day dose | Mashima D et al., Anim Sci J 2019;90(4):604-609. PMID 30811817; DOI 10.1111/asj.13185, Table 2 (means confirmed). This is one specialty breed, so it should not be read as beef generally. |
| Chicken thigh, fresh (dark meat, the poor relation) | 19.51-36.19 mg carnosine = 7.7-14.3 mg beta-alanine equivalent; the ordinary commercial broiler sits at the top of that range at 36.19 | 2 boneless thighs, 150 g | 29-54 mg carnosine (12-21 mg beta-alanine equivalent) | 0.4-0.7% of a 3.2 g/day dose | Barido FH et al., Foods 2022;11(19):3006. PMID 36230082; DOI 10.3390/foods11193006, Table 4 (all four breed values confirmed) |

- **To match the supplement dose:** Carnosine is 39.4% beta-alanine by mass (89.09/226.23) and anserine 37.1% (89.09/240.26). The lowest dose shown to work is 3.2 g/day for 4 weeks, which raised muscle carnosine 42.1%. From commercial broiler breast at 346 mg beta-alanine equivalent per 100 g, counting both dipeptides, that is 923 g of raw chicken breast every day for the whole loading month, roughly 210 g of protein from one food; counting carnosine alone it is 3.15 kg/day. The carnosine-dense native strain at 767 mg/100 g cuts the requirement to 417 g/day, a large but conceivable portion, except that the breed is not what a supermarket sells. Beef takes 3.8 kg of raw steak per day and chicken thigh over 20 kg. No food delivers free beta-alanine at all.
- **Food versus supplement:** MAJOR, and it cuts both ways. Ingested carnosine does not reach muscle intact: after histidine dipeptides were given in chicken broth at 40 mg/kg beta-alanine equivalent, carnosine was undetectable in plasma while plasma beta-alanine peaked at 428 uM, because serum carnosinase hydrolyses the dipeptide. So the food form works by liberating beta-alanine, and it does so at roughly half the peak concentration of an equal weight of free beta-alanine (833 uM at the same 40 mg/kg dose). Isomolar oral L-carnosine nonetheless raised muscle carnosine as much as 6.4 g/day of beta-alanine (65.8% versus 64.2%), so the dipeptide route is biologically equivalent and only the volume of food is impossible (Harris RC et al., Amino Acids 2006;30(3):279-89, PMID 16554972, DOI 10.1007/s00726-006-0299-9, every figure confirmed). Two further points: cooking significantly reduces meat carnosine, measured in lamb longissimus and semimembranosus after 90 min at 70 C (Purchas RW et al., Meat Sci 2004;66(3):629-37, PMID 22060873), and the paraesthesia that makes people split beta-alanine doses is a property of a large free-amino-acid bolus that food never produces.
- **Absorption:** Quantified head to head. At 40 mg/kg body weight, plasma beta-alanine peaked at 833 uM from free beta-alanine and at 428 uM from an equivalent load of histidine dipeptides in chicken broth, with both returning to baseline by 2 h and urinary loss of beta-alanine under 5% (PMID 16554972). Muscle carnosine responses after 4 weeks: 42.1% at 3.2 g/day, 64.2% at 6.4 g/day, and 65.8% for isomolar L-carnosine. The food matrix costs about half the peak plasma concentration per gram and does not block the pathway; the binding constraint is that meat holds only 0.08-0.77% beta-alanine equivalent by weight. Content varies severalfold with muscle type and breed: carnosine across 21 beef muscles ranged 0.012-18.508 umol/g wet tissue, that is 0.27-418.7 mg/100 g (PMID 30811817), and glycolytic white muscle holds far more than red oxidative muscle, so 'chicken' is not one number.
- **Data note:** for the values shown. Every dipeptide figure was checked against the full texts of Ali 2019, Khumpeerawat 2021, Mashima 2019 and Barido 2022, the 39.4%/37.1% mass fractions and the 346 mg conversion were re-derived, and all of Harris 2006 was confirmed figure for figure. One caveat on coverage rather than accuracy: pork loin, turkey breast, tuna and other pelagic fish and rabbit were never checked, so the claim that chicken breast is the richest ordinary source is untested

*References: Saunders PMID 27797728; Trexler ISSN PMID 26175657; IOC PMID 29540367*

### Dietary nitrate / beetroot

**Verdict:** CONSIDER &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** At least 6 mmol/d (372 mg) of nitrate, taken for more than 3 days, 2-3 hours before exercise. Read the label in mmol, not millilitres.
- **Who it is for:** Recreationally active people rather than elite athletes, and separately anyone using it for blood pressure.

**Pros**

- Time to exhaustion improves (SMD 0.33), as do total distance (0.42), muscular endurance (0.48) and peak power (0.25), across 180 primary studies and 2,672 participants.
- The blood pressure effect is more consistent than the performance effect: systolic -3.55 mmHg (-4.55 to -2.54) across 22 controlled trials.
- Non-athletes and recreationally active people benefit most, which is unusual and makes it accessible.

**Cons**

- Time trial performance, the one outcome that maps onto racing, is NULL. So is VO2max. The umbrella review is blunt about this.
- Antibacterial mouthwash abolishes the effect entirely, because the pathway depends on commensal bacteria on the tongue.
- Ergogenic doses of 6-8 mmol exceed the EFSA and JECFA acceptable daily intake for nitrate of 3.7 mg/kg body weight per day in a single serving. That is a real regulatory tension, not a technicality.
- Additive hypotension with organic nitrates such as glyceryl trinitrate and isosorbide mononitrate, and with PDE5 inhibitors such as sildenafil and tadalafil. Care with any antihypertensive.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** High

Vegetables are the dietary source and several reach supplement-level concentrations. EFSA pooled 25,306 leafy vegetable samples: rocket had the highest median nitrate of any vegetable at 4,800 mg/kg, with rhubarb at 2,808, amaranth 2,660, lamb's lettuce 2,130, mixed lettuce 1,878, beet leaves 1,770, curled lettuce 1,628, oak-leaf lettuce 1,553, chard 1,510, Belgian endive 1,475, white radish 1,256, black radish 1,245, beetroot root 1,100 and cos lettuce 1,097, ahead of spinach at 785. This is the one entry on the list where a normal portion of a normal food matches the dose used in trials.

- **To match the supplement dose:** At 62 mg per mmol a 6 mmol target is 372 mg of nitrate. At EFSA median concentrations that is 78 g of rocket, 132 g of rhubarb, 140 g of amaranth or 175 g of lamb's lettuce, all portions a person would actually eat, or exactly 100 mL of concentrated beetroot shot, since 70 mL measured 4.2 mmol and 140 mL 8.4 mmol. Beetroot root itself takes 338 g, butterhead lettuce 188 g, iceberg 441 g, raw spinach 474 g and celery 537 g. Wylie's dose-response data put the useful target higher than 6 mmol: 4.2 mmol changed no exercise response, 8.4 mmol reduced steady-state oxygen uptake 1.7% and extended time to task failure 14%, and 16.8 mmol gave no further gain. An 8 mmol target is 496 mg of nitrate, which is about 103 g of rocket or 133 mL of concentrate.
- **Food versus supplement:** SUBSTANTIAL, mostly about dose uncertainty and a regulatory conflict. Vegetable nitrate varies enormously: rocket ran from 1,528 mg/kg at the 5th percentile to 7,340 at the 95th, and the highest single leafy sample EFSA saw was 19,925 mg/kg in an oak-leaf lettuce grown under cover in Norway. Beetroot's mean (1,379 mg/kg) is well above its median (1,100) because of a long tail, so a salad or a home-pressed juice cannot be dosed. The regulatory tension is concrete: EFSA calculated that eating more than 47 g of rocket at the median nitrate concentration would on its own exceed the acceptable daily intake for nitrate, and a rocket-heavy salad pattern pushed modelled intake to 330 mg/person/day. So the food route to 6 mmol means routinely exceeding the nitrate ADI, which EFSA judged acceptable in the context of overall vegetable benefits and which is still not nothing. Processing matters too: nitrate in red beet and kohlrabi was reduced by up to 50% by fermentation, and boiling leaches nitrate into the water, so pickled beetroot and boiled-and-drained spinach are weaker than the raw figures suggest. The pathway also depends on oral bacteria reducing nitrate to nitrite, so antibacterial mouthwash blunts the effect regardless of source. Spinach is the classic textbook nitrate answer and a mediocre one: its median is six times lower than rocket, and it is separately a bad iron recommendation because its oxalate binds the iron.
- **Absorption:** Nitrate from vegetables and from a juice concentrate behave the same because it is a small inorganic anion, absorbed essentially completely in the upper small intestine, then concentrated in saliva by the enterosalivary circulation and reduced to nitrite by oral bacteria before any nitric oxide is formed. Wylie's dose-response work showed plasma nitrite rising dose-dependently with peak change at 2-3 h after 4.2, 8.4 and 16.8 mmol, while the physiological response saturated: 8.4 and 16.8 mmol produced similar improvements, so more nitrate is not more effect. The practical differences are therefore in the food rather than the gut: concentration varies severalfold with species, season, greenhouse versus open-field growing and region (EFSA 2008), juice concentrates are standardised while salad is not, and cooking water discards nitrate. A whole-food route is entirely viable; it wants rocket, rhubarb or amaranth rather than beetroot to be efficient.
- **Data note:** Roughly 25 EFSA statistics across Tables 7, 9 and 10 were queried against the document and matched exactly, as were five contextual claims verbatim and the whole Wylie dose-response set. All seven original per-portion conversions at 62 mg/mmol and all six whole-vegetable gram requirements were re-derived and held. The thirteen added vegetables come from the same EFSA tables as identified in the verification report; their medians were taken from that report and the individual table rows were not independently re-read here

*References: Poon umbrella review PMID 40085422; Bahadoran PMID 29141968; Govoni PMID 18793740*

### St John's wort

**Verdict:** CONSIDER &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** 900 mg/d of standardised extract, usually 300 mg three times daily. Trials ran 4-12 weeks.
- **Who it is for:** Adults with mild to moderate unipolar depression who take NO other medication whatsoever and no hormonal contraception.

**Pros**

- It genuinely works for mild to moderate depression. Cochrane pooled 29 trials and 5,489 patients: response-rate ratio 1.28 (1.10-1.49) in the 9 larger trials.
- Side-effect burden is lower than standard antidepressants in the trials, which is the main reason patients seek it out.
- In the EU it is handled as a herbal medicinal product rather than a food supplement, so product quality is better controlled than for most botanicals.

**Cons**

- This has the most dangerous interaction profile in this entire document. Hyperforin is a potent pregnane X receptor ligand and therefore a strong inducer of CYP3A4, CYP2C9, CYP1A2 and P-glycoprotein.
- Documented consequences: oral contraceptive failure with breakthrough bleeding and unintended pregnancy; loss of ciclosporin and tacrolimus levels leading to transplant rejection; antiretroviral failure with indinavir; reduced digoxin and warfarin effect; and serotonin syndrome when combined with SSRIs.
- The efficacy estimate is unstable. The response-rate ratio was 1.28 in the 9 larger trials but 1.87 in the 9 smaller ones, and there is a strong country-of-origin effect concentrated in German-speaking countries.
- Photosensitivity, and it should be stopped before elective surgery.
- Practically speaking this is suitable for almost nobody, because almost everyone is on something.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** From a plant not eaten as food
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Medium

St John's wort is the dried flowering aerial parts of Hypericum perforatum, a European meadow plant with no culinary use anywhere. It is consumed only as a medicine: tea, tincture, oil macerate or standardised dry extract. The two constituents that define it, hypericin (a naphthodianthrone) and hyperforin (a prenylated phloroglucinol), are confined to Hypericum species and are absent from the food supply, so there is no food route at any dose. Pharmacopoeial and commercial standards specify 0.3% hypericin, and the well-studied extract WS 5570 is an 80% ethanolic extract at a drug-to-extract ratio of 3:1 to 7:1 containing 0.12-0.28% hypericin and 5-6% hyperforin. One point worth carrying across from the melatonin entry: Hypericum perforatum is itself among the richest melatonin-bearing botanicals on record at roughly 4,490 ng/g dry weight, which is a curiosity about the plant rather than a reason to eat it.

*No food rows: there is no ordinary food that supplies this in a meaningful amount.*

- **To match the supplement dose:** Standard antidepressant dosing is 300 mg of standardised extract three times daily, 900 mg/day, delivering roughly 1.1-2.5 mg of hypericin (900 mg x 0.12-0.28%) and 45-54 mg of hyperforin. At a 3:1 to 7:1 drug-to-extract ratio that corresponds to about 2.7-6.3 g of dried herb per day. There is no food equivalent to compute at any portion size, because hypericin and hyperforin do not occur in foods. This is a complete absence rather than a large multiplier.
- **Food versus supplement:** The most consequential caveat in the whole set, and it runs opposite to the usual pattern: here the concentrated product is the dangerous one and there is no safe whole-food version to fall back on. Hyperforin is a potent activator of the pregnane X receptor and therefore a strong inducer of CYP3A4, CYP2C19 and P-glycoprotein. It lowers plasma levels of combined oral contraceptives (breakthrough bleeding and contraceptive failure are documented), ciclosporin and tacrolimus (with reported transplant rejection, including graft loss), warfarin, digoxin, HIV protease inhibitors and non-nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitors, irinotecan and imatinib, and several anticonvulsants. Combined with SSRIs, SNRIs, triptans or tramadol it can precipitate serotonin syndrome. Hypericin causes dose-dependent photosensitisation. Preparation type changes the risk profile substantially: hyperforin is lipophilic and unstable, so a water infusion delivers far less of it than an ethanolic extract, and low-hyperforin extracts were developed specifically to reduce interactions. A tea, a tincture and a capsule labelled with the same herb weight are not interchangeable, and treating this plant as a gentle food-adjacent botanical is the central error.
- **Absorption:** Hyperforin is well absorbed from oil-containing or ethanolic preparations, has a long elimination half-life on the order of nine hours and accumulates with three-times-daily dosing, which is why enzyme induction builds over one to two weeks and takes a similar period to wash out after stopping. Hypericin absorption is slow, low and variable, with a much longer half-life still. Both are lipophilic, so an aqueous tea extracts a small and inconsistent share, particularly of hyperforin, and hypericin is light-sensitive and degrades in poorly stored material. Since there is no food form, the practical absorption question is preparation rather than matrix, and the answer is that interaction risk tracks hyperforin exposure rather than the labelled hypericin percentage most products still advertise.
- **Data note:** The NOT_FOOD classification was challenged and upheld, and the hyperforin dose (45-54 mg/day) and dried-herb equivalent (2.7-6.3 g/day) both recompute correctly. The hypericin figure had to be corrected by recomputation from the entry's own specification, and the only quantitative composition figure in the original entry carried no citation at all and has been withdrawn, so the entry now rests entirely on the extract specification rather than on any measured plant-material content

*References: Cochrane Linde PMID 18843608; Nicolussi PMID 31742659; Moore PNAS DOI 10.1073/pnas.130155097*

### Lactase and pancreatic enzymes

**Verdict:** CONSIDER &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** Lactase 3,000-9,000 FCC units with the first bite of dairy. Pancrelipase is prescribed in lipase units per meal and titrated.
- **Who it is for:** Adults with hydrogen-breath-test-confirmed lactose maldigestion, and patients with objectively confirmed exocrine pancreatic insufficiency.

**Pros**

- These are real enzymes replacing real deficiencies. Pancreatic enzyme replacement raises the coefficient of fat absorption from 63.1% to 83.7% in chronic pancreatitis, which is an objective physiological measurement rather than a symptom score.
- Lactase lets people keep dairy in the diet rather than eliminating a whole food group.
- Pancrelipase is under-prescribed rather than over-sold, which is the opposite of the pattern in this document.

**Cons**

- Generic digestive enzyme complexes treat a condition that does not exist. There is no recognised syndrome of generalised enzyme insufficiency in healthy adults, and the pancreas has roughly 90% functional reserve before malabsorption appears.
- Using enzyme blends to self-treat undiagnosed persistent gut symptoms delays the diagnosis of coeliac disease, inflammatory bowel disease, pancreatic disease and colorectal cancer. That is the main harm.
- Consumer enzyme products are dosed in non-comparable proprietary activity units by design, so cross-product comparison is impossible.
- Pancrelipase is porcine-derived, which matters for observant Muslim and Jewish patients and for vegetarians, and cystic fibrosis patients must not exceed the weight-based ceiling because of fibrosing colonopathy risk.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** No food source, manufactured
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Medium

This item bundles two manufactured preparations with different answers, and the single SYNTHETIC code fits the products rather than the activities. Lactase: graded on the product it is SYNTHETIC, a microbial beta-galactosidase produced by fermentation of Aspergillus oryzae, A. niger or the yeast Kluyveromyces lactis and then purified, and the mammalian brush-border lactase it substitutes for is a membrane enzyme of the small intestinal epithelium present in no food; graded on the enzyme activity it is TRACE, because live yoghurt and kefir cultures carry microbial beta-galactosidase into the gut, which is why lactase-non-persistent people tolerate fermented dairy better than milk. Pancreatic enzyme replacement: NOT_FOOD. Pancreatin and pancrelipase are extracts of porcine pancreas glands, and the source review states that the preparations with demonstrated efficacy are porcine-derived (de la Iglesia et al., eClinicalMedicine 2026;94:103880, PMID 42011215). The nearest food is pancreas eaten as offal (sweetbread), which is not a realistic route.

*No food rows: there is no ordinary food that supplies this in a meaningful amount.*

- **To match the supplement dose:** Pancreatic enzymes: absolute. Recommended dosing is a minimum of 40,000-50,000 units of lipase with main meals and 20,000-25,000 with snacks, taken with every meal for life (PMID 42011215). No food supplies active lipase in those quantities. Raw pancreas is the only tissue that contains the enzymes at all, it is not eaten raw in any normal diet, cooking denatures them, the delivered activity would be unmeasurable and uncontrolled, and without enteric protection gastric acid would destroy most of what survived. Lactase: there is one genuine partial route, since live yoghurt and kefir carry microbial beta-galactosidase into the gut, but that only handles the lactose in the fermented product itself and does not give a dose-adjustable enzyme to take with an arbitrary lactose-containing meal, which is the point of a lactase capsule. No peer-reviewed per-100 g beta-galactosidase activity figure for retail yoghurt was retrieved here, so no number is given; that is a sourcing gap in this dataset rather than an absence in the literature.
- **Food versus supplement:** For pancreatin the caveats are pharmaceutical rather than dietary. Dose must be titrated to steatorrhoea, meal fat content and disease severity; the enzymes must be enteric-coated to survive gastric acid; the capsules are taken during the meal rather than before or after; and the porcine origin is a real issue for vegetarian, vegan, Muslim and Jewish patients and should be disclosed. Nothing sold as a plant-based or vegan pancreatic enzyme has demonstrated equivalence for pancreatic exocrine insufficiency, and treating a diagnosed insufficiency with a supermarket digestive enzyme blend risks continued fat malabsorption, weight loss and fat-soluble vitamin deficiency. For lactase the honest framing is that this is a targeted, cheap, low-risk aid rather than a nutrient: the alternative food strategy of hard cheese, live yoghurt and small lactose doses spread through the day works for many people, and lactase capsules matter mainly when the meal is unavoidable and the lactose dose is large.
- **Absorption:** Both are enzymes, so bioavailability means surviving intact and active to the site of action rather than being absorbed. Lactase works in the lumen of the upper small intestine and is itself digested, so timing relative to the lactose load determines the effect. Pancreatic lipase is irreversibly denatured below about pH 4, which is why uncoated pancreatin is largely wasted, why the field has moved to pH-sensitive enteric microspheres, and why an emerging approach adds bicarbonate to raise local pH around the pancreatin. Coadministered acid suppression is sometimes needed to make the enzymes work at all. None of this can be replicated by eating anything.
- **Data note:** The PERT dosing figures and the porcine origin were confirmed against the cited review (PMID 42011215), and the manufacturing routes for microbial lactase are standard and uncontested. The lactase side is weaker: the fermented-dairy beta-galactosidase route is well established qualitatively but no per-100 g activity figure for retail yoghurt was retrieved, so the partial food route cannot be quantified or compared against a capsule dose

*References: de la Iglesia-Garcia PMID 27941156; Gan PMID 29212278; de Vrese PMID 25042846; Munasinghe PMID 20204691*

## 6. Skip it

### Multivitamin / multimineral

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** One tablet daily. There is no established effective dose because there is no established clinical effect in replete adults.
- **Who it is for:** Genuine deficiency or high-probability deficiency: post-bariatric, malabsorption, strict vegans, long-term metformin users, heavy alcohol use, the housebound.

**Pros**

- For someone with a genuine deficiency it is a cheap way to cover several nutrients at once, and that is a real use.
- Doses are usually at reference-intake level, so a plain multivitamin rarely pushes any single nutrient near its upper limit on its own.
- COSMOS-Web and COSMOS-Mind reported a memory signal, which is the only positive finding in this entry and is weak.

**Cons**

- It does not prevent cardiovascular disease, cancer or death. Physicians Health Study II (n=14,641, 11.2 years): major cardiovascular events HR 1.01 (0.91-1.10).
- The largest dataset points the wrong way. Pooled US cohorts (n=390,124, up to 27 years, 164,762 deaths): daily multivitamin users had HIGHER all-cause mortality, HR 1.04 (1.02-1.07). This is observational and probably reflects sick people starting supplements, but it is not the direction the marketing implies.
- The USPSTF gives it an I statement, meaning the evidence is insufficient to weigh benefits against harms. The industry reports this as vindication, which it is not.
- The nutrient-gap argument has never converted into a clinical outcome in a healthy replete adult, and this is a roughly 8 billion US dollar per year category resting on it.
- Vitamin K content antagonises warfarin, so switching brands or starting and stopping the product shifts INR. Treat it as a dosing variable.
- Risk comes from stacking. A multivitamin plus a separate high-dose single nutrient is how people cross an upper limit without noticing.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose partly reachable
- **Data confidence:** Medium

This is a product category rather than a molecule, and almost every nutrient inside it is abundant in ordinary food, which is why the US Preventive Services Task Force concluded in 2022 that the evidence is insufficient to recommend multivitamins for preventing cardiovascular disease or cancer, and recommended against beta-carotene and vitamin E supplements outright. The nutrients are food-abundant while the pill itself is manufactured: folic acid is not a natural food form, and cyanocobalamin occurs in food only in traces, so the accurate statement is that neither is the predominant natural form, and both are present in fortified foods, which this entry treats as a legitimate route for B12. Shortfalls that are real in populations are a short list: the 2020-2025 Dietary Guidelines for Americans name calcium, potassium, vitamin D and dietary fibre as nutrients of public health concern, with iron and folate added in pregnancy, and UK survey data adds magnesium, selenium and iron in younger women, though the specific NDNS below-LRNI percentages carried in the original submission (magnesium 9% of men and 13% of women, selenium 25.8% of males and 50.3% of females, iron about a quarter of younger women) could not be traced to a named survey round, age band or table and are indicative and unconfirmed here.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Clams, mixed species, cooked, moist heat (vitamin B12) | 98.89 ug B12 | 3 oz (85 g) | 84.1 ug | 3,504% of the 2.4 ug RDA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171975 |
| Beef liver, braised (B12, folate, haem iron) | 70.58 ug B12; 253 ug folate; 6.54 mg iron | 1 slice (68 g) | 48.0 ug B12; 172 ug folate; 4.4 mg iron | 2,000% of the B12 RDA; 43% of the 400 ug folate target; 24% of the 18 mg iron RDA for premenopausal women | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168626, including the 68 g slice. Retinol content is 9,442 ug RAE/100 g, so liver is unsuitable in pregnancy and should not be eaten more than about weekly |
| Herring, Atlantic, cooked, dry heat (vitamin B12 and vitamin D) | 13.14 ug B12; 5.4 ug vitamin D | 1 fillet (143 g) | 18.8 ug B12; 7.7 ug vitamin D | 783% of the 2.4 ug B12 RDA; 51% of the 15 ug vitamin D RDA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175117, including the 143 g fillet |
| Haddock, grilled (iodine) | 421 ug iodine | 1 fillet (120 g) | 505 ug | 361% of the 140 ug UK RNI (240% of the 150 ug US RDA) | CoFID 16-376. Added on verification: much richer than the cod used in the original entry and omitted from it. The 120 g fillet is a conventional weight rather than a sourced portion, so the per-portion figure is indicative |
| Oysters, eastern, wild, cooked, moist heat (zinc) | 78.6 mg zinc | 6 medium (42 g) | 33.0 mg | 300% of the 11 mg RDA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171980, including the '6 medium = 42 g' portion |
| Cod, flesh only, baked (iodine) | 161 ug iodine | 1 fillet (180 g) | 290 ug | 207% of the 140 ug UK RNI (193% of the 150 ug US RDA) | Iodine from CoFID 16-373; fillet weight from USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171956 (1 fillet = 180 g). CoFID cod entries span 110-276 ug/100 g by cooking method, so any single figure is approximate. Percentage recomputed against the 140 ug UK RNI, since the composition is UK data |
| Crimini / brown mushrooms exposed to ultraviolet light, raw (vitamin D2) | 31.9 ug vitamin D | 1 cup diced (86 g) | 27.4 ug | 183% of the 15 ug RDA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168557. Added on verification: outranks the portabella chosen in the original entry. The 86 g diced cup is USDA's portabella portion weight applied here for comparability, so the per-portion figure is indicative. Only UV-treated mushrooms carry this, and the pack has to say so |
| Brazil nuts, dried, unblanched (selenium) | 1,917 ug selenium | 1 kernel (5 g) | 96 ug | 174% of the 55 ug RDA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170569, including the '1 kernel = 5 g' portion. This is a mean, and it is the most soil-dependent number in food composition: nuts from selenium-poor Amazonian soils run an order of magnitude lower and nuts from selenium-rich soils higher, so no individual nut can be assumed to match it. Six kernels (28.35 g) at the USDA mean give 544 ug, above the 400 ug/day US upper limit, so the sensible instruction is one or two nuts rather than a handful |
| Portabella mushrooms exposed to ultraviolet light, raw (vitamin D2) | 28.4 ug vitamin D (1,135 IU) | 1 cup diced (86 g) | 24.4 ug | 163% of the 15 ug RDA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170143, including the 86 g diced cup. UV white mushrooms are 26.2 ug/100 g (FDC 169376). Only UV-treated mushrooms carry this; ordinary shop mushrooms have almost none, and the pack has to say so |
| Mackerel, salted (vitamin D) | 25.2 ug vitamin D | 3 oz (85 g) | 21.4 ug | 143% of the 15 ug RDA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168149. Added on verification. The 85 g weight is the standard 3 oz serving rather than a USDA portion-table entry, so the per-portion figure is indicative |
| Salmon, sockeye, canned, drained solids (vitamin D) | 21.0 ug vitamin D | 3 oz (85 g) | 17.9 ug | 119% of the 15 ug RDA; 179% of the 10 ug UK RNI | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173693.
