Added on verification: this row alone disproves the original claim that UV mushrooms are the only food reaching 15 ug in one portion |
| Rainbow trout, farmed, cooked, dry heat (vitamin D) | 19.0 ug vitamin D | 3 oz (85 g) | 16.2 ug | 108% of the 15 ug RDA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173718. Added on verification |
| Salmon, sockeye, cooked, dry heat (vitamin D) | 16.7 ug vitamin D (670 IU) | 3 oz fillet portion (85 g); USDA also lists 0.5 fillet = 155 g | 14.2 ug per 3 oz; 25.9 ug per half fillet | 95% of the 15 ug US RDA per 3 oz, 173% per half fillet; 142% of the 10 ug UK RNI per 3 oz | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173692. The half-fillet portion is USDA's own and clears the RDA on its own, which is why the 'unbridgeable gap' framing was dropped |
| Crab, brown meat (iodine) | 333 ug iodine | 40 g, the brown meat from a dressed crab | 133 ug | 95% of the 140 ug UK RNI | CoFID 16-391. Added on verification. The 40 g portion is a conventional weight rather than a sourced portion, so the per-portion figure is indicative |
| Lentils, mature seeds, boiled without salt (folate) | 181 ug folate | 1 cup cooked (198 g) | 358 ug | 90% of the 400 ug/day target | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 172421 (total folate and DFE both 181 ug). Fortified breakfast cereals, chickpeas, asparagus and edamame are also substantial folate sources; verification named them as omissions but supplied no figures, so they are named here rather than tabulated |
| Cod liver oil (vitamin D) | 250 ug vitamin D | 1 tsp | 11.3 ug | 75% of the 15 ug RDA; 113% of the 10 ug UK RNI | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173577. Added on verification: the most concentrated vitamin D entry in the whole USDA table and the historical answer to winter deficiency. Retinol content is high, so the same teaspoon carries a preformed vitamin A load that counts toward the 3,000 ug/day upper limit |
| Spinach, boiled, drained (good for folate, the wrong answer for iron) | 146 ug folate; 3.57 mg iron | 1 cup cooked (180 g) | 263 ug folate; 6.4 mg iron on the label | 66% of the 400 ug folate target; 36% of the 18 mg iron RDA nominally, of which roughly 1-2% is absorbed | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168463. The folate figure was added on verification, which noted the original entry used this row solely as an iron warning while ignoring a real folate contribution. The iron warning stands: non-haem iron complexed with oxalate is among the least absorbable iron in the food supply, and spinach should not be offered as an iron food |
| Milk, whole, 3.25% fat, with added vitamin D (iodine and vitamin D) | 31 ug iodine; 1.3 ug vitamin D | 1 cup / 240 ml (244 g) | 76 ug iodine; 3.2 ug vitamin D | 54% of the 140 ug UK iodine RNI; 21% of the 15 ug vitamin D RDA | Iodine from CoFID 12-596 (whole milk, pasteurised, average); vitamin D from USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171265, with the portion weight taken from the same USDA entry. Iodine percentage recomputed against the 140 ug UK RNI in place of the 150 ug figure the original mislabelled as an RNI |
| Fat spread, fortified (vitamin D) | 17.5 ug vitamin D | 1 tbsp (14 g) | 2.5 ug | 16% of the 15 ug RDA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171415. Added on verification as the main UK non-fish vitamin D vehicle. The 14 g tablespoon is a conventional weight, so the per-portion figure is indicative |
| Egg, whole, hard-boiled (vitamin D) | 2.2 ug vitamin D | 1 large egg (50 g) | 1.1 ug | 7% of the 15 ug RDA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173424. Eggs are also one of the four main iodine sources the NHS lists |
| Kombu (dried kelp), shown as a hazard rather than a source (iodine) | 440,670 ug iodine | 1 g of dried kombu | 4,407 ug | 3,148% of the 140 ug UK RNI from a single gram, a 31.5-fold overshoot | CoFID 13-339, the highest iodine value in the whole CoFID dataset. One gram clears the EFSA adult upper level of 600 ug/day several times over (that upper level matches the standard SCF 2002 figure but could not be confirmed from the source text during verification). Wakame is 16,830 ug/100 g and nori 1,470 ug/100 g (CoFID 13-341 and 13-340), a 300-fold spread across three seaweeds sold in the same shop, and kelp-induced thyroid dysfunction is documented. Kelp is never a sensible iodine recommendation, which is why it is placed last despite the largest number on the list |

- **To match the supplement dose:** There is no single food equivalent of a multivitamin, because a multivitamin is not a nutrient: one tablet supplies roughly 100% of the reference intake for 20 or more nutrients at once, and the only thing matching that profile is a varied diet, which by definition already supplies them. So the answer has to be given per nutrient, and it splits. For most of the tablet (vitamin C, vitamin A, thiamine, riboflavin, niacin, B6, vitamin E, vitamin K, zinc, selenium, copper, iodine) the food gap for anyone eating a normal mixed diet is zero. Vitamin D is reachable but rarely reached: the original claim that UV-treated mushrooms are the only food to hit 15 ug in one portion is arithmetically false, because 3 oz of canned sockeye salmon gives 17.9 ug (119% of the 15 ug RDA), 3 oz of farmed rainbow trout 16.2 ug (108%), a USDA half-fillet (155 g) of cooked sockeye 25.9 ug (173%), and a teaspoon of cod liver oil 11.3 ug (75%), alongside a cup of UV portabella at 24.4 ug. Reaching 15 ug/day from food needs about 90 g of cooked sockeye every day, or 1.15 litres of fortified milk, or 14 large eggs; the UK 10 ug RNI needs about 60 g of oily fish daily or 770 ml of fortified milk. So the vitamin D case for supplementing is behavioural (few people at temperate latitudes eat oily fish daily through winter) rather than compositional, and it argues for vitamin D alone rather than a 25-ingredient tablet. Periconceptional folic acid is a genuine exception: 400 ug/day is food-achievable in bulk (about 220 g of cooked lentils daily) but the trial evidence for preventing neural tube defects is for supplemental folic acid, and food folate is both less bioavailable and more variable, so food substitution is not supported. B12 on a vegan diet is the second real exception, since there is no reliable unfortified plant source, so a supplement or fortified food is mandatory. Two mismatches run the other way: the potassium in a multivitamin is capped at about 99 mg against a 3,500 mg reference intake, and the calcium is typically 100-200 mg, so the tablet cannot address either of the shortfalls the guidelines name, and fibre, the fourth named shortfall, is absent from every tablet. Iodised salt, the global public-health iodine vehicle, belongs in this list as well; verification named it as a complete omission but supplied no composition figure, so no row is given for it.
- **Food versus supplement:** This category contains the clearest documented case of a whole food being safe while the isolated concentrate is harmful. Carotenoid-rich vegetables are consistently associated with lower lung cancer risk, while beta-carotene as a supplement increased lung cancer incidence in smokers and asbestos-exposed workers in the ATBC and CARET trials, and the USPSTF now recommends against it with moderate certainty that harms exceed benefits. Vitamin E supplements carry the same recommendation against. Preformed vitamin A as retinol in a tablet counts toward the 3,000 ug/day upper limit and is teratogenic at high intake, while beta-carotene in carrots does not convert quickly enough to reach toxicity. Two food rows on this list carry the same warning as the tablet: beef liver at 9,442 ug RAE/100 g and cod liver oil are both concentrated retinol sources, so neither is a free lunch. Folic acid in a tablet can partially mask the haematological signs of B12 deficiency while neurological damage continues, an effect food folate has not been shown to produce. Stacking is the underappreciated risk: a multivitamin alongside fortified breakfast cereal and a fortified plant drink can push folic acid, retinol, niacin and iron past their upper limits in someone who believes they are taking one tablet. Within the tablet itself, iron competes with zinc and copper for shared transporters and calcium interferes with iron uptake, so a combined product delivers less of each than the label states. And no tablet carries the fibre, the food-level potassium, or the polyphenols that account for much of the association between diet quality and outcomes, which is the structural reason a pill is not a diet.
- **Absorption:** Form differences run in both directions, so blanket claims fail here. Iron: haem iron from meat and fish absorbs at roughly 15-35% and is largely insensitive to the rest of the meal, while non-haem iron from plants and from the ferrous salts in tablets ranges from about 2% to 20% depending on phytate, polyphenols, calcium and vitamin C eaten at the same time. Spinach iron sits at the bottom of that range because oxalate binds it, so the textbook answer is the wrong answer. Folate: folic acid from a supplement taken with food is roughly 85% bioavailable against about 50% for naturally occurring food folate, so here the synthetic form genuinely outperforms the food. Vitamin B12: food B12 is protein-bound and needs gastric acid and pepsin to release it, so older adults with atrophic gastritis and long-term proton pump inhibitor or metformin users often absorb crystalline B12 from a tablet better than B12 from a chop. Vitamin D: the D2 in UV-treated mushrooms raises and sustains serum 25(OH)D less efficiently than the D3 in oily fish and most supplements, so 24.4 ug from a cup of portabella is not equivalent to 24.4 ug from salmon, and the mushroom rows should not be read as interchangeable with the fish rows despite ranking near them. Vitamin K: the K1 in leafy greens is poorly released from the plant matrix and cleared fast, while the long-chain MK-7 in natto reaches higher and far longer-lasting serum concentrations, so a portion of natto and a portion of kale are not interchangeable despite sharing the label term. All four fat-soluble vitamins need fat in the same meal, which is why a multivitamin swallowed with black coffee delivers less than the panel claims.
- **Data note:** The composition data is HIGH quality: verification traced every cited FDC ID and CoFID code to the source datasets with exact matches on descriptions, per-100 g values and portion weights. Two things pull the item down. The UK NDNS below-LRNI percentages that motivate the whole shortfall argument are UNVERIFIABLE as cited, with no survey round, age band or table given, and figures quoted to one decimal place with no year attached are not checkable. The EFSA 600 ug/day iodine upper level is consistent with the standard SCF 2002 figure but its source text could not be extracted during verification. Several added rows also carry conventional rather than dataset portion weights (haddock, crab brown meat, salted mackerel, UV crimini, fortified fat spread), and each is flagged in place

*References: USPSTF PMID 35727271; O Connor PMID 35727272; Loftfield PMID 38922615; PHS II*

### Collagen peptides

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** 2.5-10 g/d of hydrolysed collagen for skin trials. UC-II is a different molecule dosed at about 40 mg/d and is not interchangeable.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody, on the best available analysis.

**Pros**

- There is a weak, moderate-certainty signal for knee osteoarthritis pain: SMD -0.58 (-0.98 to -0.18) across 4 trials and 507 patients. All included trials had high risk of bias.
- Safety is benign. The problem with collagen is efficacy, not harm.

**Cons**

- This is the cleanest documented case of funding bias in the supplement literature. Myung and Park pooled 23 trials and 1,474 participants. Headline: collagen improved skin hydration, elasticity and wrinkles. Subgroup analysis: studies WITHOUT pharmaceutical-company funding showed no effect on any of the three, and high-quality studies showed no effect. The effect exists only in industry-funded, lower-quality trials.
- Collagen is a poor-quality protein. It lacks tryptophan and is a poor source of essential amino acids, so it displaces better protein in the diet.
- Marine collagen in fish allergy; bovine sourcing matters for religious dietary restriction and BSE-region concerns.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** High

RICH was challenged and upheld, and this is the one entry whose trial dose is genuinely reachable by eating, because the supplement is simply hydrolysed collagen and several ordinary foods are almost pure collagen. Gelatine is thermally hydrolysed collagen at 85.6 g protein per 100 g with tryptophan recorded as 0.000 g. Plain pork crackling is 61.3 g protein per 100 g with an unmistakable collagen amino-acid signature (glycine 11.9 g, proline 7.3 g per 100 g). Trial doses are 2.5 to 15 g/day over 3 to 18 months (Paul, Leser & Oesser 2019, confirmed). Chicken skin, beef tendon, chicken feet and tinned fish eaten with skin and soft bones all add collagen; chicken feet and cartilage now have published GAG and collagen assays, but none of those sources yields a per-100 g collagen figure I can quote here, so they are named without numbers. Bone broth is the popular food answer and the unreliable one.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Pork skins, plain (pork scratchings / crackling / chicharron) | 61.3 g protein, of which the amino-acid profile indicates roughly 53-55 g is collagen-type protein (glycine 11.9 g and proline 7.3 g per 100 g; glycine is 19.4% of protein mass here against 22.2% in USDA's own gelatine entry); tryptophan only 0.12 g | 1 oz bag (28 g) | about 17 g protein, of which about 15 g is collagen-type protein | about 150% of a 10 g collagen dose; comparable to the 15 g top-end trial dose | USDA FoodData Central, SR Legacy 'Snacks, pork skins, plain'. Confirmed to the decimal: USDA per 28.35 g gives protein 17 g, glycine 3.378 g, proline 2.059 g, tryptophan 0.033 g, which scale to 11.9, 7.3 and 0.116 g per 100 g |
| Gelatine, dry powder, unsweetened (leaf or powdered gelatine) | 85.6 g protein, effectively all collagen hydrolysate; glycine about 19.0 g (22.2% of protein mass); proline about 12.3 g; tryptophan 0.000 g | 1 sachet/envelope (7 g) | about 6.0 g collagen protein | 60% of a 10 g collagen-peptide dose; 240% of the 2.5 g low-end trial dose | USDA FoodData Central, SR Legacy 'Gelatins, dry powder, unsweetened' (protein 85.6 g/100 g, corrected from the submission's 85.7; tryptophan 0.000 g). Collagen identity: gelatine is partially hydrolysed collagen, see Paul et al. 2019, Nutrients 11(5):1079, PMID 31096622 |
| Aspic or gelatine-set jelly (made at 1 sachet per 500 mL) | about 1.2 g collagen protein | 1 bowl (150 g) | about 1.8 g collagen protein | about 18% of a 10 g dose; about 72% of the 2.5 g low-end trial dose | Derived arithmetic, not a measured composition value: the gelatine row above (85.6 g protein/100 g) at a stated recipe ratio of one 7 g sachet per 500 mL. Scale it to whatever ratio you actually use |

- **To match the supplement dose:** There is essentially no gap. One 7 g sachet of ordinary supermarket gelatine delivers about 6.0 g of collagen protein, which is 60 per cent of a 10 g dose and 240 per cent of the 2.5 g low-end trial dose; two sachets sit squarely inside the 2.5 to 15 g/day range used in the research. A 28 g bag of plain pork crackling delivers about 15 g of collagen-type protein, matching the top-end trial dose at a fraction of the price of branded peptides. Bone broth is the one route that fails: amino acid content of broths made to a standardised recipe was significantly lower than a 20 g reference collagen dose for hydroxyproline, glycine and proline, and non-standardised preparations varied enormously, with commercially available broths lower than self-prepared ones (Alcock, Shaw & Burke 2019). If you want a defined collagen dose from food, weigh out gelatine rather than simmering bones.
- **Food versus supplement:** Moderate, and worth stating precisely. Collagen contains no tryptophan at all (USDA records gelatine tryptophan as 0.000 g/100 g) and is low in cysteine, methionine and histidine, so it is an incomplete protein with a PDCAAS effectively at zero and must not be counted as general protein intake; modelling suggests up to about 36 per cent of total daily protein can be collagen before indispensable amino acid balance suffers (Paul et al. 2019, PMID 31096622, confirmed). Hydrolysis is a real difference: collagen peptides are enzymatically cut to 2-5 kDa fragments that dissolve in cold liquid and are absorbed partly as di- and tripeptides, whereas gelatine gels and must be dissolved hot, and native collagen in skin and tendon must first be cooked and digested. On the food side, pork crackling brings a large load of saturated fat and salt with its collagen, which a sachet of gelatine does not, so swapping crackling for collagen powder is a trade-off rather than a free win.
- **Absorption:** Genuinely different by form, and here the supplement has a defensible argument. Hydrolysed collagen peptides are absorbed rapidly and hydroxyproline-containing dipeptides such as prolyl-hydroxyproline appear intact in plasma, which is the mechanistic basis of the collagen-peptide literature; plasma amino acid comparisons of dairy against collagen protein confirm the distinctive glycine, proline and hydroxyproline signature of collagen ingestion. Gelatine, already partly hydrolysed, behaves similarly, and a 7 g sachet delivers 6.0 g of that same protein. Native collagen in skin, tendon and bone requires prolonged moist cooking to become digestible at all, which is exactly why bone broth quantities are unpredictable and why standardised broths fell significantly short of a 20 g reference collagen dose. Vitamin C is required for the hydroxylation step in endogenous collagen synthesis, so collagen intake without adequate vitamin C is pointless, and eating hydroxyproline does not bypass that requirement.
- **Data note:** Both principal food rows are USDA SR Legacy entries that were checked against the source to the decimal for protein, glycine, proline and tryptophan, and the 7 g sachet and 28 g bag arithmetic recomputes correctly. The trial dose range (2.5-15 g/day over 3-18 months) and the 36 per cent protein-substitution ceiling were both confirmed in Paul et al. 2019, and the Alcock bone broth citation and its 20 g reference dose were confirmed. The one derived row (aspic) is labelled as derived

*References: Myung & Park Am J Med 2025 PMID 40324552; Lin PMID 37717022; PMID 33742704*

### Glucosamine and chondroitin

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** Glucosamine 1,500 mg/d plus chondroitin 1,200 mg/d, the GAIT regimen. Form matters and is routinely ignored.
- **Who it is for:** At most, people with moderate to severe knee osteoarthritis pain, on subgroup data only.

**Pros**

- In the GAIT moderate-to-severe pain stratum the combination beat placebo, 79.2% versus 54.3% response (P=0.002). This was pre-specified but underpowered, and the authors themselves cautioned against over-reading it.
- Cochrane found a short-term chondroitin benefit: 10% absolute pain improvement, NNT 5, at low to moderate quality.
- As safe as placebo in the trials.

**Cons**

- The definitive trial was null. GAIT was NIH-funded, n=1,583, 24 weeks, with a celecoxib active control, designed specifically to remove industry influence. Glucosamine +3.9 percentage points over placebo (P=0.30), chondroitin +5.3 (P=0.17), combination +6.5 (P=0.09). Celecoxib worked; the supplements did not.
- Cochrane found glucosamine benefit ONLY for the Rotta-brand crystalline glucosamine sulfate preparation. Pooled non-Rotta preparations failed to show any pain benefit. Almost nothing sold in shops is the Rotta preparation.
- The industry sells cartilage regeneration. No trial has shown cartilage regeneration.
- This is the best-documented supplement-anticoagulant interaction in this document: 20 FDA MedWatch reports linking glucosamine plus chondroitin to raised INR, bruising and bleeding on warfarin, plus 21 WHO database reports of raised INR.
- Shellfish-derived glucosamine matters in shellfish allergy.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** SYNTHETIC (glucosamine) / RICH in cartilage-containing foods (chondroitin)
- **Dose from food:** Dose partly reachable
- **Data confidence:** Low

The two compounds have different answers and must not share one label. Glucosamine is SYNTHETIC: no ordinary food contains free glucosamine, and glucosamine hydrochloride is manufactured by hydrolysing chitin from crustacean shell or Aspergillus niger fermentation biomass with food-grade concentrated hydrochloric acid at 100 C, then filtering, crystallising, washing and flash-drying. Shell and fungal mycelium are neither digestible nor eaten, so the feedstock is not a food route. Chondroitin is a different case, and the submission's SYNTHETIC label was wrong for it by its own admission that chondroitin sulfate is extracted from cartilage. Chondroitin sulfate in edible chicken keel cartilage has been quantified at 189 +/- 33 mg per gram of cartilage, and GAG content has been quantified in chicken feet and in traditionally stewed pig trotters. Chicken cartilage (yakitori nankotsu), chicken feet, pig trotters and shark fin are ordinary foods in Chinese, Japanese and Korean cuisine, so for chondroitin the honest answer is rich in a small set of real but culturally specific foods.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Chicken keel / soft cartilage (yakitori nankotsu) - CHONDROITIN only | 15.6-22.2 g chondroitin sulfate (189 +/- 33 mg per gram of cartilage) | 2 yakitori nankotsu skewers (about 30 g cartilage) | 4.7-6.7 g chondroitin sulfate as stated; about 1.4-2.3 g if the source figure is per gram of DRIED cartilage and the portion is fresh | 390-555% of a 1,200 mg chondroitin dose as stated; still 117-194% on the more conservative fresh-weight reading. Zero per cent of any glucosamine dose | Br Poult Sci 2014, chicken keel cartilage chondroitin sulfate extraction (189 +/- 33 mg CS per g cartilage), cited via the verification review rather than read directly. Indicative on one specific point: the moisture basis of the per-gram figure is not stated in the material available, which is why both readings are given. Related but non-quantifiable here: GAG content has been assayed in chicken feet (J Food Sci 2020) and in stewed pig trotters (Foods 2022), both expressed as mg GAG per gram of total collagen, which does not convert to a per-portion figure |

- **To match the supplement dose:** Split by compound. Chondroitin: a 1,200 mg daily dose corresponds to roughly 5.4 to 7.7 g of chicken keel cartilage at 189 +/- 33 mg/g, about 6 g at the central value, so two yakitori nankotsu skewers already exceed it several times over. Even if that figure turns out to be per gram of dried cartilage, a 30 g fresh portion would still carry roughly 1.4 to 2.3 g, which is 117 to 194 per cent of the dose, so the conclusion holds on either moisture basis. Glucosamine: there is no food quantity at all, and the informative comparison is endogenous. Human endogenous glucosamine production is 4 to 20 g/day with a median around 14 g/day, so the standard GAIT-trial 1,500 mg adds about 10.7 per cent on top of the median, or 7.5 to 37.5 per cent across the range. Prawn and crab shells are the industrial feedstock but shell is indigestible and not eaten, so eating prawns delivers no useful glucosamine.
- **Food versus supplement:** Several that matter, and they differ by compound. Chemical form is not interchangeable in the trial literature: the European positive trials used a specific patented crystalline glucosamine SULFATE while the large NIH GAIT trial used glucosamine HYDROCHLORIDE, and the results differ, so a shelf product labelled simply 'glucosamine' is not evidence-matched to any particular result. Vegetarian or shellfish-free glucosamine from Aspergillus niger fermentation is the same manufactured molecule from a different feedstock, not a food-derived alternative; EFSA's opinion on the fungal route states that the process is the same as the shellfish process except for the raw material. Shellfish-derived glucosamine is a purified sugar rather than shellfish protein, so it is not the classic tropomyosin allergen route, though labels warn and cautious avoidance is reasonable. Chondroitin product content notoriously deviates from label claim because it is an extracted polydisperse polysaccharide from a bovine or shark by-product, which raises quality-control and sourcing questions. On the food side, cartilage-containing dishes carry chondroitin in a cooked matrix at a concentration that varies with cut and preparation, so a nankotsu skewer is a real chondroitin source rather than a measured dose.
- **Absorption:** Poor and first-pass-limited for glucosamine: oral glucosamine is largely metabolised on first pass and plasma concentrations reach only low micromolar, far below the millimolar concentrations at which glucosamine affects chondrocytes in cell culture, which is the central pharmacological criticism of the category. Absorbed glucosamine enters cells through glucose transporters, a process facilitated by insulin, and is phosphorylated to glucosamine-6-phosphate, the same intermediate the body already makes at 4 to 20 g/day (median about 14 g/day, EFSA ANS Panel 2013 opinion on (6S)-5-methyltetrahydrofolic acid, glucosamine salt; the frequently quoted 230 mg/kg body weight per day is a back-calculation from that median for a 60 kg adult, 14,000/60 = 233, not an EFSA-quoted value). Chondroitin sulfate is a large sulfated polysaccharide of tens of kDa and is absorbed mainly after depolymerisation into smaller fragments and constituent sugars, so what reaches the circulation is not the intact molecule from the capsule. Food-borne chondroitin faces the same depolymerisation step plus the need to liberate the glycosaminoglycan from a cooked cartilage matrix, so a cartilage portion that exceeds the dose on paper is not equivalent to a measured dose in effect.
- **Data note:** The single numeric food row rests on one 2014 poultry-science paper reached through the verification review rather than read directly, and its moisture basis is unstated, which is why the per-portion figure is given on two readings. The chicken feet and pig trotter assays exist but are expressed per gram of total collagen and cannot be converted to a portion. Glucosamine has no food data at all, by nature rather than by omission. The endogenous 4-20 g/day figure is quoted verbatim in the literature but its primary EFSA opinion could not be opened this session

*References: GAIT PMID 16495392; Cochrane chondroitin PMID 25629804; Cochrane glucosamine PMID 15846645*

### NMN (nicotinamide mononucleotide)

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** 250-2,000 mg/d in trials, most commonly 250 mg/d for 12 weeks. No dose has improved a clinical outcome.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody demonstrably.

**Pros**

- It reliably raises blood NAD+ two to threefold, so the pill is absorbed and metabolised as claimed.
- One 60-person trial in older adults with impaired physical function reported maintained walking speed and improved subjective sleep at 250 mg/d for 12 weeks. Single small trial.

**Cons**

- This is the purest biomarker-for-outcome substitution in the supplement market. Raising NAD+ is not a health outcome.
- Two independent meta-analyses found nothing. Eight randomised trials in 342 people, and separately 12 studies in 513 people: no effect on fasting glucose, fasting insulin, HbA1c, HOMA-IR or any lipid fraction.
- A systematic review of 10 trials in 437 people found grip strength 29.9 kg before and 30.5 kg after, with skeletal muscle mass index unchanged. Non-significant.
- No contraindications are characterised because no adequately powered safety population exists. No upper limit has been set and EFSA has not authorised it as a novel food.
- Avoid in pregnancy, lactation and active malignancy. NAD+ is a substrate for tumour metabolism and no oncology safety data exist.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Trace amounts only
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Medium

NMN really is present in ordinary plant and animal foods. Mills et al. 2016 Table 1, confirmed row by row against the primary source, puts every food it measured between 0 and 1.88 mg/100 g, and a 2025 HPLC-DAD/LC-MS-MS plant survey spans 40 to 13,000 ug/100 g, richest in broccoli and green beans. The submission's framing of 'sub-milligram-per-100 g levels, roughly 100 to 1,000 times below a single capsule' is withdrawn as self-contradicting: at the 2025 survey ceiling of 13 mg/100 g one 100 g portion sits about 19 times below a 250 mg capsule, and it is not sub-milligram. TRACE still holds against a 250 to 1,000 mg dose. Cow milk is not a meaningful NMN source, since its NAD+ precursor pool is dominated by nicotinamide and nicotinamide riboside (Trammell et al. 2016). Supplement NMN is manufactured by microbial fermentation or enzymatic/chemical synthesis and is never extracted from broccoli or edamame.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Edamame (green soybeans), shelled, cooked from frozen | 0.47-1.88 mg NMN | 1 cup shelled (155 g) | 0.73-2.91 mg | 0.3-1.2% of a 250 mg dose; 0.07-0.3% of a 1,000 mg dose | Mills et al. 2016, Cell Metab 24(6):795-806, PMID 28068222, DOI 10.1016/j.cmet.2016.09.013, Table 1 (value confirmed against the primary table); food identity USDA FDC 168411, ID not confirmed this session |
| Avocado, raw, flesh only | 0.36-1.60 mg NMN | 1 medium avocado, flesh (150 g) | 0.54-2.40 mg | 0.2-1.0% of a 250 mg dose | Mills et al. 2016, PMID 28068222, Table 1 (confirmed); food identity USDA FDC 171705, ID not confirmed this session |
| Broccoli, raw | 0.25-1.12 mg NMN (Mills, confirmed). The 2025 plant survey names broccoli one of its two richest NMN foods and reports a whole-survey ceiling of 13 mg/100 g, which if it belongs to broccoli would make this the richest row in the table | 1 cup chopped (91 g) | 0.23-1.02 mg on the confirmed Mills range; up to about 11.8 mg if the 2025 survey ceiling applies to broccoli | 0.09-0.4% of a 250 mg dose on Mills; up to 4.7% on the survey ceiling | Mills et al. 2016, PMID 28068222 (confirmed). Upper figure from Alegre & Pastore 2025, CyTA J Food 23(1), DOI 10.1080/19476337.2025.2458753: indicative and unconfirmed, since only the whole-survey range is in the accessible abstract and the full text returns 403. Food identity USDA FDC 170379, ID not confirmed this session |
| Green beans, cooked | 0.04-13 mg NMN (the whole-survey range, 40-13,000 ug/100 g). Green beans is named with broccoli as one of the two richest NMN plant foods, but no per-food figure is published in the accessible abstract | 1 cup (125 g) | 0.05-16.3 mg across the survey range | 0.02-6.5% of a 250 mg dose | Alegre & Pastore 2025, DOI 10.1080/19476337.2025.2458753. Indicative and unconfirmed: the range is survey-wide, not green-bean-specific, and the full text could not be retrieved |
| Cabbage, raw | 0.0-0.90 mg NMN | 1 cup shredded (89 g) | 0-0.80 mg | 0-0.32% of a 250 mg dose | Mills et al. 2016, PMID 28068222, Table 1 (added: this row was dropped from the submission even though its upper bound exceeds tomato and beef, both of which were kept) |
| Mushroom, raw | 0-1.01 mg NMN (some samples below detection) | 1 cup sliced (70 g) | 0-0.71 mg | 0-0.28% of a 250 mg dose | Mills et al. 2016, PMID 28068222, Table 1 (confirmed) |
| Beef, raw (muscle) | 0.06-0.42 mg NMN | 1 steak (150 g) | 0.09-0.63 mg | 0.04-0.25% of a 250 mg dose | Mills et al. 2016, PMID 28068222, Table 1 (confirmed) |
| Tomato, raw | 0.26-0.30 mg NMN | 1 medium tomato (123 g) | 0.32-0.37 mg | 0.13-0.15% of a 250 mg dose | Mills et al. 2016, PMID 28068222, Table 1 (confirmed) |
| Shrimp | 0.22 mg NMN | 1 serving (100 g) | 0.22 mg | 0.09% of a 250 mg dose | Mills et al. 2016, PMID 28068222, Table 1 (added: second of the two primary-table rows the submission dropped without comment) |
| Cucumber, peel specifically (not the flesh) | 0.65 mg NMN in peel; 0.56 mg in seed | peel pared from 1 medium cucumber (about 30 g of peel) | about 0.20 mg | 0.08% of a 250 mg dose | Mills et al. 2016, PMID 28068222, Table 1 (confirmed); food identity USDA FDC 168409, ID not confirmed this session |

- **To match the supplement dose:** A 250 mg dose is 13.3 kg of edamame a day at its richest measured value (1.88 mg/100 g) and 53 kg at the low end of the same food (0.47 mg/100 g); a 1,000 mg dose is 53 to 213 kg. Broccoli has to be quoted across both sources: at the 2025 plant survey's 13 mg/100 g ceiling a 250 mg dose is 1.9 kg, about 21 cups, while at Mills' 0.25 to 1.12 mg/100 g it is 22 to 100 kg. The honest span is therefore 1.9 to 100 kg, and the submission's flat '22 to 100 kg' overstated the gap by up to about elevenfold at the high-value end. Avocado: 104 to 463 whole avocados a day. Cucumber peel, the most quoted NMN food, is the least useful of all: 1,282 cucumbers pared, corrected from the 1,250 originally stated. No one eats any of this, and even the corrected best case rests on a survey ceiling that cannot be pinned to broccoli specifically.
- **Food versus supplement:** Substantial. NMN is a phosphorylated nucleotide and is not absorbed intact in any large quantity: it is dephosphorylated in the gut to nicotinamide riboside and then to nicotinamide before or during absorption, so an oral NMN capsule is partly a nicotinamide delivery system. Food NMN arrives as a minor component of a mixed NAD+ vitamer pool (nicotinamide, nicotinic acid, NR, NAD+ itself) inside intact cells and is measured after extraction, which is not the same as being released by chewing and digestion. Supplement material is manufactured by fermentation or enzymatic synthesis, never extracted from broccoli or edamame despite the marketing imagery. Regulatory status differs too: the FDA has taken the position that NMN is precluded from being marketed as a US dietary supplement, while it is sold freely elsewhere, so 'food-derived' framing on a label carries no compositional meaning.
- **Absorption:** Food NMN quantities are too small for bioavailability to matter. For the supplement form, human and mouse tracer work shows the oral route delivers NAD+ precursors largely after conversion to nicotinamide riboside and nicotinamide rather than as intact NMN (Trammell et al. 2016, Nat Commun 7:12948, DOI 10.1038/ncomms12948, for the NR side of the same pathway). Ordinary diets already supply 15-20 mg/day of niacin equivalents feeding the same NAD+ pathway, thousands of times more molar precursor than the NMN in any vegetable portion, so the scarcity premise behind NMN supplementation is not a dietary-shortfall argument.
- **Data note:** Every Mills Table 1 value and per-portion conversion was confirmed against the primary table, and the PMID and DOI both resolve correctly. The upper end of the picture, and therefore the corrected best-case dose gap, rests on the Alegre & Pastore 2025 abstract alone, whose per-food values could not be retrieved (full text 403). The eight USDA FDC IDs used for food identity could not be checked because the FDC API rate-limited the session

*References: PMID 39531138; PMID 39116016; Wen PMID 39221308*

### Nicotinamide riboside (NR)

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** 250-1,000 mg/d commercially. EFSA assessed 300 mg/d as safe. Trials used up to 2,000 mg/d.
- **Who it is for:** People with peripheral artery disease have the single best-supported claim, and it is thin.

**Pros**

- The NICE trial (n=90, 6 months) improved 6-minute walk distance by 17.6 m versus placebo, and by 31.0 m in those taking at least 75% of pills.
- More human trial data exist than for NMN, and EFSA has actually assessed its safety, which is more than most items here can say.

**Cons**

- More trials than NMN and a worse hit rate, which is informative. The pattern is consistent: NAD+ rises two to threefold, the clinical endpoint does not move.
- Long COVID (n=58, 2,000 mg/d, 20 weeks): NAD+ up 2.6 to 3.1-fold, no significant improvement in cognition, fatigue, sleep or mood.
- Obese insulin-resistant men (n=40, 2,000 mg/d, 12 weeks): insulin sensitivity, endogenous glucose production, glucose disposal and oxidation were all unimproved, in the authors own words.
- Mild cognitive impairment trials at 1,000 mg/d were null.
- EFSA caps pregnancy and lactation at 230 mg/d. Sum total nicotinamide exposure if also taking prescription niacin. As with NMN, no oncology safety data exist.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Trace amounts only
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Medium

TRACE was challenged and upheld. NR is a real dietary vitamer, best characterised in cow milk, where Trammell et al. 2016 measured 4.3 +/- 2.6 umol/L across 18 raw samples (5.1 +/- 2.6 umol/L in the subset free of S. aureus, against nicotinamide at 7.3 +/- 1.5 umol/L). That is 1.1 to 1.3 mg per litre. Plant foods carry 62 to 1,600 ug per 100 g, with wild chicory, banana and orange named the highest (Alegre & Pastore 2025). Every one of these is three orders of magnitude below a 250 to 1,000 mg capsule. Commercial NR chloride is synthesised, not extracted from milk.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Orange, raw | somewhere in the survey-wide plant range of 0.062-1.6 mg NR; named among the three highest plant sources, so probably near the top, but no per-food figure is published | 1 medium orange (131 g) | 0.08-2.1 mg across the survey range | 0.03-0.7% of a 300 mg dose | Alegre & Pastore 2025, CyTA J Food 23(1), DOI 10.1080/19476337.2025.2458753. Indicative and unconfirmed: only the whole-survey range appears in the accessible abstract, the Taylor & Francis full text returns 403, and the ranking among these three plant rows is therefore driven by portion size rather than by measured content |
| Banana, raw | somewhere in the survey-wide plant range of 0.062-1.6 mg NR; named among the three highest plant sources | 1 medium banana (118 g) | 0.07-1.9 mg across the survey range | 0.02-0.6% of a 300 mg dose | Alegre & Pastore 2025, DOI 10.1080/19476337.2025.2458753. Indicative and unconfirmed; the submission's 'upper bound ~1.6 mg' for banana specifically was fabricated precision, since the 1.6 figure is the ceiling of the whole survey |
| Wild chicory greens | somewhere in the survey-wide plant range of 0.062-1.6 mg NR; named first among the three highest plant sources, so it is the food most likely to carry the 1.6 mg/100 g ceiling | 1 serving cooked greens (100 g) | 0.06-1.6 mg across the survey range | 0.02-0.5% of a 300 mg dose | Alegre & Pastore 2025, DOI 10.1080/19476337.2025.2458753. Indicative and unconfirmed; added because it was named the richest of the three and omitted from the submission |
| Cow milk, whole, fresh (pasteurised) | 0.11-0.13 mg NR (4.3-5.1 uM at MW 255.25 = 1.1-1.3 mg/L) | 1 cup (244 g / 237 mL) | 0.26-0.31 mg | 0.09-0.10% of a 300 mg dose; 0.026-0.031% of a 1,000 mg dose | Trammell et al. 2016, J Nutr 146(5):957-963, PMID 27052539, DOI 10.3945/jn.116.230078 (the 4.3 +/- 2.6 uM measurement is Trammell's own, not Ummarino's as the submission stated). Ummarino et al. 2017, Food Chem 221:161-168, PMID 27979136, reports no such figure; its headline finding is that human milk is the richest NMN source. Food identity USDA FDC 171265, ID not confirmed this session |

- **To match the supplement dose:** A 300 mg dose is 231 to 273 litres of milk a day, roughly 970 to 1,150 cups, and a 1,000 mg dose is 769 to 909 litres. One 250 mg capsule holds as much NR as 192 to 227 litres of milk. The submission quoted only the single lowest concentration (1.1 mg/L), which inflated each of these figures by 15 to 20 per cent; the range above spans Trammell's 4.3 umol/L mean and his bacteria-free 5.1 umol/L subset. From the richest plant value in the 2025 survey (1.6 mg/100 g) a 300 mg dose is about 19 kg a day. Milk is genuinely the best dietary source of NR and it is still useless as a route to a trial dose.
- **Food versus supplement:** Important. Commercial NR is nicotinamide riboside chloride, a synthesised salt, while the NR in milk is a fragile glycosidic nucleoside that degrades on storage and is reduced in milk from mastitic (S. aureus positive) cows; Trammell also found conventional milk carried more NR than organic. The food number is a moving target for that reason. More significant, the whole diet already supplies plenty of the pathway's dominant precursor, nicotinamide, at 15-20 mg/day of niacin equivalents, so NR supplementation is not correcting a dietary gap in NAD+ precursors, it is a pharmacological attempt at a different pharmacokinetic profile. High-dose nicotinamide-family precursors also carry methyl-group and flushing or hepatic considerations at gram level that a glass of milk does not.
- **Absorption:** NR is absorbed orally and raises blood NAD+ in humans, which was the point of the original demonstration (Trammell et al. 2016, Nat Commun 7:12948, DOI 10.1038/ncomms12948). Absorption is not the constraint; food quantities are microgram-scale, at 0.26-0.31 mg per cup of milk against a 250-1,000 mg capsule. There is a route difference too: much oral NR is cleaved to nicotinamide in the gut and liver, so a fraction of any dose acts as plain vitamin B3, which is abundant in food. The submission's claim that milk NR is 'free in solution and not bound to milk proteins' inverts the source and has been removed: Trammell's WaterLOGSY NMR gave positive signals from the four aromatic protons of the nicotinamide moiety consistent with association with a skim-milk protein fraction, with stronger binding in conventional than organic milk.
- **Data note:** The milk figure is traceable to the primary full text and the micromolar-to-mg/L conversion was independently recomputed, but all three plant rows rest on a paywalled paper whose per-food values are not retrievable, so their content and their rank order are unconfirmed

*References: Wu PMID 41357333; Dollerup PMID 29992272; McDermott NICE PMID 38871717; Orr PMID 37994989*

### Resveratrol

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** Trials span 150 mg/d to gram doses. The EU caps synthetic trans-resveratrol at 150 mg/d in supplements, adults only.
- **Who it is for:** Possibly people who already have type 2 diabetes. The healthy-ageing consumer is in the null subgroup.

**Pros**

- An 11-trial meta-analysis found significant improvement in glucose control and insulin sensitivity in people who already had diabetes, and explicitly no effect in non-diabetic people.

**Cons**

- It is the cautionary tale the rest of this category has not learned from. Sirtris was acquired by GSK for 720 million US dollars and the development programme was discontinued.
- The best-designed human trial was null: 150 mg/d for 6 months in overweight adults, no effect on insulin sensitivity (adjusted Matsuda index 5.18 versus 5.50 on placebo).
- It can make exercise worse. In aged men undergoing training, resveratrol BLUNTED the training-induced cardiovascular improvements, the opposite of the marketed exercise-mimetic claim.
- Antiplatelet activity, so care with warfarin and antiplatelets. Oestrogen-sensitive conditions warrant caution. Gram doses cause gastrointestinal intolerance, and the oncology programme saw renal adverse events.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Trace amounts only
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Medium

Resveratrol occurs in red wine, grapes, peanuts and some berries, at concentrations three to five orders of magnitude below trial doses; red wine averages 1.9 mg trans-resveratrol per litre, so a whole litre holds less than one capsule. Supplement resveratrol is not grape-derived: it is made synthetically or extracted from Japanese knotweed root (Polygonum cuspidatum). Knotweed is not entirely inedible, since young itadori shoots are eaten as a spring vegetable in Japan and foraged in the UK and North America and itadori tea is made from the plant, so the accurate statement is that it is not a food in Western diets, has no composition-table entry, and the root used for extraction is not the part anyone eats. Three sources that belong in the food picture carry no verified figure here and so get no row: peanut skins (the redskin testa, the richest stilbene fraction of the peanut, which is why the roasted-kernel value is explicitly for peanuts without seed coats), red and purple grape juice (the non-alcoholic route the ethanol argument implies), and the wider stilbene foods (muscadine grapes, bilberries, blueberries, cranberries, pistachios, cocoa), so the roughly 5 mg/day dietary ceiling rests on population intake studies rather than on rows built here.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Table grapes, fresh (cv. Napoleon, mature, untreated) | about 0.5 mg resveratrol per 100 g of whole fruit | 1 serving (200 g, roughly a large bunch) | about 1 mg resveratrol, which the authors note is in the range supplied by a glass of red wine | 0.7% of a 150 mg dose per 200 g serving | Cantos E, Garcia-Viguera C, de Pascual-Teresa S, Tomas-Barberan FA, Effect of postharvest ultraviolet irradiation on resveratrol and other phenolics of cv. Napoleon table grapes, J Agric Food Chem 2000;48(10):4606-12, PMID 11052707, DOI 10.1021/jf0002948 (abstract verified: 200 g of mature untreated Napoleon grapes provides approximately 1 mg, rising to 2 mg after UVB and 3 mg after UVC). Berry skin values 0.03 to 29.5 mg/kg of skin across five cultivars: Geana EI et al. 2015, PMID 27904334, citation verified but values not in the abstract |
| Peanuts, boiled (the richest peanut form) | 0.51 mg per 100 g (5.138 +/- 2.849 ug/g) | 1 cup boiled peanuts (100 g) | 0.51 mg | 0.3% of a 150 mg dose | Sobolev VS, Cole RJ, trans-Resveratrol content in commercial peanuts and peanut products, J Agric Food Chem 1999;47(4):1435-9, DOI 10.1021/jf9809885. Citation verified exactly, but the paper is paywalled and the value could not be read, so it is indicative and unconfirmed; it is the widely reproduced figure from that paper and the per-portion arithmetic recomputes correctly |
| Red wine | 0.19 mg trans-resveratrol per 100 mL (1.9 +/- 1.7 mg/L mean across a large review of red wines; corroborated sample spread 0.361 to 1.972 mg/L). Trans-piceid, the glucoside, averages about three times higher at up to 29.2 mg/L | 1 standard glass (175 mL) | 0.33 mg trans-resveratrol at the mean (0.06 to 0.35 mg across the corroborated sample spread) | 0.2% of a 150 mg dose per glass | Stervbo U, Vang O, Bonnesen C, A review of the content of the putative chemopreventive phytoalexin resveratrol in red wine, Food Chemistry 2007;101(2):449-57, DOI 10.1016/j.foodchem.2006.01.047, with the mean independently corroborated by Weiskirchen S, Weiskirchen R, Adv Nutr 2016, PMID 27422505, PMC4942868. The previously claimed maximum of 14.3 mg/L and the varietal claim naming St Laurent have been removed: neither could be traced, and the corroborating Geana 2015 study names Pinot Noir and Feteasca Neagra as highest |
| Peanut butter | 0.032 mg per 100 g (0.324 +/- 0.129 ug/g) | 2 tablespoons (32 g) | 0.010 mg (10 ug) | 0.007% of a 150 mg dose | Sobolev VS, Cole RJ, J Agric Food Chem 1999;47(4):1435-9, DOI 10.1021/jf9809885. Citation verified exactly; the value sits behind a paywall and could not be read, so it is indicative and unconfirmed, though widely reproduced and the per-portion arithmetic recomputes correctly |
| Peanuts, roasted (kernels without seed coats) | 0.0055 mg per 100 g (0.055 +/- 0.023 ug/g). Independent survey of Virginia, runner and Spanish types across four locations: 0.03 to 0.14 ug/g, explicitly for peanuts without seed coats. The redskin testa, removed here, is the richest stilbene fraction of the peanut | 1 oz (28 g), about a small handful | 0.0015 mg (1.5 ug) | 0.001% of a 150 mg dose | Sobolev VS, Cole RJ, J Agric Food Chem 1999;47(4):1435-9, DOI 10.1021/jf9809885; Sanders TH et al., Occurrence of resveratrol in edible peanuts, J Agric Food Chem 2000, DOI 10.1021/jf990737b. The 0.03 to 0.14 ug/g corroborating range is verified (via Weiskirchen 2016, PMC4942868) |

- **To match the supplement dose:** A 150 mg resveratrol dose equals 79 litres of average red wine (150 divided by 1.9 mg/L), about 105 standard 750 mL bottles or roughly 450 glasses, every day. Even at the top of the corroborated sample spread of 0.361 to 1.972 mg/L it is still 76 litres a day. From grapes it is 30 kg daily; from boiled peanuts 29 kg, and from roasted peanuts 2.7 tonnes. Trials commonly use 150 to 1,000 mg/day and the SRT501 cancer studies used up to 5 g/day, all of it manufactured. Measured population intakes sit far lower again (a Spanish study found a median of 100 ug/day and a mean of 933 ug/day for resveratrol plus piceid, and French wine consumption yields about 0.2 mg/day), and no plausible diet gets past about 5 mg/day.
- **Food versus supplement:** Three separate mismatches. First, the supplement is not a food extract: commercial resveratrol is synthetic or comes from Polygonum cuspidatum root, which is not a Western food and not a composition-table food, so the grape imagery on the label is marketing rather than sourcing. Second, wine's stilbenes are mostly the glucoside trans-piceid, at about three times the aglycone concentration, arriving inside a large mixed polyphenol and ethanol matrix, so wine does not behave as a dilute resveratrol capsule. Third, and decisive: recommending wine as a resveratrol source is a bad recommendation regardless of dose, because ethanol is a Group 1 carcinogen and the alcohol harm arrives at one glass while the resveratrol dose would require a hundred bottles. Separately, high-dose resveratrol (2.5 to 5 g/day) causes diarrhoea and nausea and inhibits CYP3A4, CYP2C9 and sulfotransferases, giving it real interaction potential (warfarin, for example) that a glass of wine does not have.
- **Absorption:** Oral resveratrol is well absorbed (about 70% of the dose crosses the enterocyte) but systemic bioavailability of the unchanged aglycone is under 1 to 2% because of near-immediate intestinal and hepatic glucuronidation and sulfation; plasma peaks from dietary quantities sit in the low nanomolar range, while in vitro activity is generally demonstrated at micromolar concentrations. Piceid, the form dominant in grapes and much of wine, must be deglycosylated before absorption, adding another step. Gram doses are needed to reach micromolar plasma levels, which is precisely why the food route is closed.
- **Data note:** The wine mean (1.9 +/- 1.7 mg/L), the Napoleon grape figures, the roasted-peanut corroborating range and the roughly 5 mg/day dietary ceiling were all verified or independently corroborated, and about a dozen unit conversions recompute correctly. The boiled peanut and peanut butter values sit behind a paywall and are indicative, and the wine maximum of 14.3 mg/L had to be removed as untraceable

*References: de Ligt PMID 32492138; Liu PMID 24695890; Gliemann J Physiol; EU Decision 2016/1190*

### Spermidine

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** 0.9 mg/d was the dose in the definitive trial. Commercial products supply roughly 1-6 mg/d.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody demonstrably.

**Pros**

- The autophagy mechanism is genuine in yeast, flies, worms and mice, and lifespan is extended in those organisms.

**Cons**

- The one well-conducted human test failed in exactly the target population. SmartAge was 12 months, double-masked, placebo-controlled, in 100 adults aged 60-90 with subjective cognitive decline. Primary outcome difference -0.03 (95% CI -0.11 to 0.05), P=0.47, against a pre-registered primary endpoint.
- The observational case rests on dietary intake epidemiology, and spermidine-rich foods are wheat germ, legumes, mushrooms and aged cheese. That is a dietary pattern, not a molecule.
- Dietary intake from food is typically several-fold higher than most supplements provide, which by itself undermines the supplement premise.
- Wheat-germ-derived products are unsuitable in coeliac disease and wheat allergy. Polyamines are growth-supporting and elevated in many tumours, and polyamine depletion is an active oncology strategy, so avoid during active malignancy.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Medium

RICH was challenged and upheld. This is the one entry where ordinary food beats the supplement outright: wheat germ, dried soybeans, mushrooms, green peas, fermented soy and some aged cheeses all carry milligram-per-portion quantities. Measured intakes tabulated by Munoz-Esparza et al. 2019 run from 33.1 umol/day in Turkey to 103.1 umol/day in Spain, with Western populations at 54.7 to 103.1 umol/day, which is 7.9 to 15.0 mg/day. Trial capsules delivered 0.9 mg/day in the 12-month SmartAge trial. The diet supplies roughly ten times the capsule. Atiya Ali et al. 2011 also names chicken liver, corn and shellfish among its high-spermidine foods, and chicken liver in particular would be a useful non-wheat source, but no per-food figure for them could be sourced here, so they are not given rows.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Green peas, frozen, cooked | 5.4 mg spermidine (54.4 mg/kg) | 1 cup (160 g) | 8.7 mg | about 870% of a 1 mg capsule; 58-110% of a whole day's typical dietary spermidine | Munoz-Esparza et al. 2021, Foods 10(8):1752, PMID 34441529, DOI 10.3390/foods10081752 (confirmed); corroborated at 46.6 mg/kg by Atiya Ali et al. 2011, Food Nutr Res 55:5572, PMID 21249159, DOI 10.3402/fnr.v55i0.5572 (cross-check confirmed) |
| Mushrooms, raw (cultivated varieties) | 8.9-10.9 mg spermidine on a FRESH-weight basis | 1 cup sliced (70 g) | 6.2-7.6 mg | about 620-760% of a 1 mg capsule | Atiya Ali et al. 2011, PMID 21249159, measured 88.6 mg/kg fresh, and Munoz-Esparza et al. 2021, PMID 34441529, gives 54-109 mg/kg as the fresh-weight ceiling for the mushroom, legume and pepper group. The submission's 128.5 mg/kg 'fresh' was a back-calculation from the paper's 1,658.9 mg/kg DRY-MATTER figure and exceeded the paper's own fresh-weight ceiling, so it has been dropped |
| Soybeans, dried, raw | 3.3-20.7 mg spermidine (Atiya Ali 33.2-62.1 mg/kg; Munoz-Esparza 1,425 nmol/g = 20.7 mg/100 g) | 1/4 cup dry (46 g) | 1.5-9.5 mg | 150-950% of a 1 mg capsule; roughly 10-120% of a whole day's typical dietary spermidine | Munoz-Esparza et al. 2019, Front Nutr 6:108, PMID 31355206, DOI 10.3389/fnut.2019.00108 (nmol/g conversion confirmed); Atiya Ali et al. 2011, PMID 21249159, which is 3-6x lower and was already cited elsewhere in the submission. Both are given because the two sources genuinely disagree |
| Wheat germ, raw (industrially separated) | 22-35 mg spermidine (2,437 nmol/g = 35.4 mg/100 g; independent LC-MS/MS 221-337 mg/kg = 22.1-33.7 mg/100 g) | 2 tbsp (14 g) | 3.1-5.0 mg | 310-500% of a 1 mg/day supplement capsule | Munoz-Esparza et al. 2019, PMID 31355206 (conversion confirmed); Mohajeri et al. 2023, Food Sci Nutr 11(11), PMID 37970387, DOI 10.1002/fsn3.3650 (confirmed as spermidine-specific values, not the total-polyamine figure) |
| Natto and other fermented soy products | 4.3-10.9 mg spermidine (43.0-108.9 mg/kg, the range reported for legumes and soy derivatives rather than a natto-specific measurement) | 1 pack natto (50 g) | 2.2-5.4 mg | 220-540% of a 1 mg capsule | Munoz-Esparza et al. 2021, PMID 34441529 (group range confirmed; the group-versus-food distinction is stated because the figure is not natto-specific) |
| Cheddar, matured 12 months (aged hard cheese) | 20.0 mg spermidine (199.5 mg/kg) in one Swedish survey; other surveys are far lower, most cheeses under 1 mg/100 g (<69 nmol/g) with one blue cheese at 3.8 mg/100 g (262 nmol/g) | 1 slice (30 g) | 6.0 mg by the high figure, 0.3-1.1 mg by the low figures | 30-600% of a 1 mg capsule, depending which survey you believe | Atiya Ali et al. 2011, PMID 21249159 (matured cheddar 199.5 mg/kg, confirmed); Munoz-Esparza et al. 2019, PMID 31355206 (most cheeses <69 nmol/g, blue cheese 262 nmol/g, both confirmed) |

- **To match the supplement dose:** The gap runs backwards. Two tablespoons of wheat germ (14 g) supply 3.1 to 5.0 mg, three to five times the 0.9 mg/day dose used in the SmartAge trial. A cup of cooked green peas supplies about 8.7 mg and a cup of sliced mushrooms 6.2 to 7.6 mg, each close to an entire ordinary day's intake. Against habitual intakes of 7.9 to 15.0 mg/day, a typical 1 mg capsule adds 6.7 to 12.7 per cent on top of what the person already eats, which is inside the day-to-day variation of a normal diet, and matching a day's dietary spermidine from capsules would take 8 to 15 of them. Anyone who wants more spermidine should buy wheat germ, which costs a fraction of the capsules and delivers several times the dose.
- **Food versus supplement:** Real, in both directions. Most 'spermidine' products are wheat germ extracts standardised to about 1 mg rather than purified spermidine, so the label compound is the same molecule as in the food and there is no extraction advantage. Against that, aged cheese and natto carry biogenic amines (histamine, tyramine, putrescine) that matter for people on MAO inhibitors or with histamine intolerance, so the richest fermented sources are not risk-free foods even though they are ordinary ones. Polyamines are proliferative signalling molecules and concentrated long-term supplementation lacks the observational safety record that food-level intake has. The Bruneck cohort (Kiechl et al. 2018, Am J Clin Nutr 108(2):371-380, PMID 29955838) supports dietary intake, reporting mortality by intake thirds of 40.5, 23.7 and 15.1 deaths per 1,000 person-years; the 9 to 11.5 mg/day figures the submission attributed to it are not retrievable from the paper and have been dropped. Wheat germ also goes rancid quickly, so buy small and refrigerate.
- **Absorption:** Dietary polyamines are absorbed readily in the upper small intestine, gut bacteria synthesise more, and circulating levels are homeostatically buffered, so intake is not the only input. A triple-blind randomised placebo-controlled crossover trial gave 12 healthy volunteers 15 mg/day of spermidine in two 5-day phases, about 15 times a typical capsule and 1.0 to 1.9 times a rich day's diet; plasma spermine rose significantly while spermidine and putrescine did not change, which the authors read as presystemic conversion of spermidine to spermine (Senekowitsch et al. 2023, Nutrients, PMID 37111071). Either way, dosing well above dietary levels did not raise circulating spermidine. Processing of the food matters more than any supplement choice: commercially germinated wheat grain measured 19.22-24.25 mg/kg spermidine against 220.65-337.2 mg/kg for industrially separated wheat germ. Those ranges imply an 89 to 94 per cent drop, about 92 per cent mean to mean, and the paper's own headline figure of 85.52 per cent is inconsistent with its own data.
- **Data note:** The wheat germ, green pea, natto and cheddar figures were all confirmed against their primary sources, including the nmol/g conversions and the cross-checks. Two rows are less settled because two cited sources disagree by 3-6x (soybeans) or because the submission's fresh-weight figure exceeded the source's own ceiling (mushrooms), and both are now presented as ranges. Two claims were dropped as unverifiable: the Bruneck mg/day intakes and a supposed 3.3 mg/day follow-up trial dose

*References: SmartAge PMID 35616942; protocol PMID 31039826*

### Curcumin / turmeric extract

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** About 1,000 mg/d of curcuminoids in the knee osteoarthritis trials. Culinary turmeric is a different exposure entirely.
- **Who it is for:** At most, people with symptomatic knee osteoarthritis, if the meta-analytic signal survives the bias problem.

**Pros**

- A Bayesian network meta-analysis of 23 studies and 2,175 knee osteoarthritis patients found pain on a visual analogue scale -1.63 (-2.91 to -0.45) and total WOMAC -18.85.
- Culinary use in food is entirely reasonable and carries no established risk.

**Cons**

- It was classified in the Journal of Medicinal Chemistry as both a PAINS (pan-assay interference compound) and an invalid metabolic panacea: an unstable, reactive, nonbioavailable compound and therefore a highly improbable lead. That is a formal medicinal-chemistry verdict, not an opinion.
- The dose on the label is disconnected from systemic exposure. Unconjugated plasma curcumin stays below 2 nM even at high dose with piperine, against the 1-100 micromolar concentrations used in the in-vitro work that generated the claims. Adding piperine does not raise unconjugated plasma curcumin.
- The osteoarthritis trials are mostly industry-funded with high heterogeneity.
- Turmeric is the most commonly used of the six botanicals linked to liver injury in US survey data (236 of 9,685 respondents), and 10 DILIN cases published in 2023 included five hospitalisations and one death from acute liver failure. Seven of ten carried HLA-B*35:01.
- Antiplatelet activity raises bleeding risk with warfarin and antiplatelets. Piperine inhibits CYP3A4 broadly, so herb-drug interaction risk with cytotoxics and targeted agents is real.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Medium

Turmeric is an ordinary culinary spice, the dried ground rhizome of Curcuma longa, and it carries curcumin at 3.14% by weight, so one level teaspoon delivers 94 mg. Measured against the JECFA/EFSA acceptable daily intake for curcumin of 3 mg/kg body weight (210 mg for a 70 kg adult), a single teaspoon supplies 45% of the ADI, which is a rich dietary source on any normal reading; the earlier TRACE label came from scoring food against a 1,000 mg capsule, which is the dose question and is answered separately below. Turmeric is effectively the only meaningful food source, and commercial curry powder is much weaker. Fresh turmeric rhizome is the same food in unprocessed form and is increasingly sold in UK and US supermarkets, but no verified composition figure for it is available here, so it gets no row.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Turmeric, ground (dried rhizome powder, as sold as a spice) | 3.14 g curcumin per 100 g (3.14% w/w, the mean for pure turmeric powder and the highest concentration measured across the 28 spice products analysed, which spanned both turmeric and curry powders). Total curcuminoids are conventionally quoted at 1.4 to 1.6 times curcumin alone, roughly 4.5 to 5 g/100 g, since curcumin is 60 to 70% of the curcuminoid fraction | 1 level teaspoon (3.0 g) | 94 mg curcumin. 1 tablespoon (9.4 g) = 295 mg curcumin | 9.4% of a 1,000 mg curcuminoid dose per teaspoon; 30% per tablespoon. Per teaspoon it is also 45% of the 210 mg JECFA/EFSA ADI for a 70 kg adult | Tayyem RF, Heath DD, Al-Delaimy WK, Rock CL. Curcumin content of turmeric and curry powders. Nutr Cancer 2006;55(2):126-31, PMID 17044766, DOI 10.1207/s15327914nc5502_2 (3.14% verified against the abstract). Portion weights 1 tsp = 3.0 g and 1 tbsp = 9.4 g independently confirmed for Spices, turmeric, ground; the FDC ID 172231 previously cited could not be confirmed against a live lookup. The 1.4 to 1.6 times curcuminoid ratio and the derived 4.5 to 5 g/100 g are indicative and unconfirmed: Tayyem measured curcumin only and reports no curcuminoid partition |
| Curry powder (commercial blend, containing turmeric) | 0.05 to 0.58 g curcumin per 100 g (0.05 to 0.58% w/w) | 1 tablespoon (about 6 g) used in a dish for two to four people | 3 to 35 mg curcumin in the whole dish | 0.3 to 3.5% of a 1,000 mg dose for the entire dish | Tayyem 2006, PMID 17044766, reports only that curry powders, with one exception, had relatively small amounts of curcumin present with great variability. The 0.05 to 0.58% bounds are indicative and unconfirmed, and every figure derived from them inherits that status. The tablespoon weight is a household approximation |

- **To match the supplement dose:** A 1,000 mg curcuminoid capsule equals about 21 to 32 g of turmeric powder every day: 32 g counting curcumin only at 3.14% w/w, about 21 g counting all three curcuminoids at roughly 4.7% (a conventional ratio the cited source does not establish). That is 7 to 11 level teaspoons, close to a third of a supermarket spice jar per day, and nobody eats that. A generous curry portion carries 1 to 2 g of turmeric, or 30 to 60 mg of curcumin, and a heavy South Asian intake of 2 to 2.5 g turmeric per day yields 63 to 79 mg of curcumin, so the capsule is about 13 to 16 times a heavy dietary intake on a curcumin basis, and about 8.5 to 11 times if the comparison is made on total curcuminoids. There is no eating route to the dose.
- **Food versus supplement:** This is the single most important form gap in the whole list. Culinary turmeric has no liver-injury signal; 95% curcuminoid extracts, especially those boosted with piperine or reformulated as phytosomes, micelles or nanoparticles, are now a leading identified botanical cause of drug-induced liver injury in US and Australian case series, with a reported HLA-B*35:01 association. The EFSA/JECFA acceptable daily intake for curcumin (E 100) is 3 mg/kg body weight per day, 210 mg for a 70 kg adult: one teaspoon of turmeric in food (94 mg curcumin) sits at 45% of it, while a single 1,000 mg curcuminoid capsule is about five times over it. Concentrated extracts also inhibit CYP3A4 and UGT enzymes and interact with anticoagulants, which food-level turmeric does not. Second caveat cutting the other way: scaling up culinary turmeric is not a safe workaround either, because turmeric powder in some supply chains has been adulterated with lead chromate for colour (well documented in Bangladeshi turmeric), and gram-level daily dosing of spice-grade powder magnifies that exposure.
- **Absorption:** Curcumin is close to a worst case for oral absorption: poor solubility, extensive intestinal and hepatic glucuronidation and sulfation, and rapid biliary excretion leave systemic exposure at or below detection limits after dietary amounts. Co-administered piperine 20 mg raised curcumin bioavailability roughly 20-fold in humans (Shoba et al. 1998, PMID 9619120, verified as a 2000% increase), and modern phytosome, micellar and nanoparticle formulations claim 10 to 185-fold increases over unformulated powder. The practical consequence is that milligram equivalence between spice and supplement misleads in both directions: a formulated 500 mg extract can deliver more systemic curcumin than 30 g of raw powder, and the traditional practice of blooming turmeric in hot oil with black pepper is a crude version of the same trick.
- **Data note:** The headline 3.14% curcumin figure, the two USDA portion weights, the per-teaspoon and per-tablespoon arithmetic, the 210 mg ADI and Shoba's 20-fold piperine effect all verified exactly. The curry powder range, the curcumin-to-total-curcuminoid ratio and the FDC ID could not be traced to their cited sources

*References: Nelson J Med Chem 2017 PMID 28074653; iScience 2025 PMC12144411; Zhao PMID 38036015; Halegoua-DeMarzio PMID 36252717; Likhitsup PMID 39102266*

### MSM (methylsulfonylmethane)

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** Trials used 2 g/d (the 2023 trial) up to 6 g/d (the 2006 pilot). No effective dose is established.
- **Who it is for:** No population has a defensible claim.

**Pros**

- A single 88-person trial reported a significant between-group difference in a knee quality-of-life composite score at 12 weeks.
- Very low toxicity signal at the doses used.

**Cons**

- The meta-analysis found 3 randomised trials and 326 patients, of which only ONE studied MSM at all. The other two studied DMSO. Reported pain reduction was a non-significant 6.34 mm on a visual analogue scale.
- The 2008 systematic review covering 6 studies and 681 osteoarthritis patients found only 52 had ever received MSM in a controlled trial. That is the entire human evidence base.
- It is present in a large share of joint supplements, which inflates the price of combination products for no measured benefit.
- No characterised drug interactions, largely because almost nobody has looked. No pregnancy or lactation data.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Trace amounts only
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Low

TRACE survives as a label, since nobody claims food delivers grams of MSM, but the evidence behind it is void and the entry's food table has been withdrawn in full. Every number in the widely circulated MSM food table (cow's milk 3.30 mg/kg, coffee 1.60, tomato 0.86, alfalfa 0.77, tea 0.30, beer 0.18, Swiss chard 0.18, corn 0.11) comes from Pearson, Dawson & Lackey 1981, which is titled 'Naturally occurring levels of dimethyl sulfoxide in selected fruits, vegetables, grains, and beverages'. It measured DMSO, a different compound; a 2026 review reproduced those eight values under an MSM heading, and the mislabelling has propagated from there. The accurate statement is that the MSM content of ordinary foods is essentially unquantified in the accessible literature. Bulk supplement MSM is manufactured by oxidising DMSO with hydrogen peroxide or nitrogen dioxide and purifying by distillation or crystallisation, so this is functionally a synthetic bulk chemical with an unmeasured natural presence.

*No food rows: there is no ordinary food that supplies this in a meaningful amount.*

- **To match the supplement dose:** The gap cannot be computed, and the submission's figures (909 kg of milk for a 3 g dose, 1,875 litres of coffee, 450 kg of milk for the 1.5 g low end) are withdrawn because every one of them was built on DMSO measurements. Qualitatively the answer is still no: supplement doses are 1.5 to 6 g/day, no food has been shown to contain MSM at anything approaching that, and no MSM deficiency has ever been described. Anyone quoting a per-cup or per-kilogram MSM figure is quoting a mislabelled DMSO table.
- **Food versus supplement:** Yes, and it is mostly about honesty in marketing. Commercial MSM is made by oxidising DMSO, so it is chemically identical to whatever trace natural molecule exists but industrially produced; purity grades differ (distilled versus crystallised) and residual DMSO or solvent is a real quality variable between brands. The common marketing move of pointing at garlic, onions and brassicas as natural MSM foods is wrong: those foods are rich in allicin, thiosulfinates and glucosinolates, which are different sulfur chemistries, and eating them does not deliver MSM. Dietary sulfur adequacy comes from methionine and cysteine in protein, a separate question entirely. No whole food substitutes for an MSM capsule, and equally there is no evidence anyone is MSM-deficient.
- **Absorption:** Absorption is not the limiting factor. MSM is a small (94 Da), highly water-soluble, uncharged molecule, absorbed close to completely after oral dosing and distributed widely including across the blood-brain barrier, which is why it is detectable in cerebrospinal fluid. Circulating MSM in people who take no supplements comes partly from diet and partly from bacterial and endogenous metabolism of other sulfur compounds. Because uptake is near-complete either way, the food-versus-supplement comparison reduces entirely to dose, and the dose cannot currently be stated for food: the ratios the submission gave (about 1:3,700 per cup of milk, which it rounded to 1:4,000) rest on a DMSO measurement and are withdrawn.
- **Data note:** The entire food dataset for this entry was traced to a paper that measured dimethyl sulfoxide rather than dimethyl sulfone, so all seven food rows, all per-portion figures, all percentages of dose and both dose-gap calculations have been removed rather than corrected. The secondary review that relabelled the table is real and correctly cited (PMC13071705), which is precisely how the error travels. No verified quantification of MSM in any ordinary food is available to put in its place

*References: Brien PMID 19474240; Brien PMID 18417375; Toguchi PMID 37447322*

### Astaxanthin

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** 4-12 mg/d in skin trials. EFSA acceptable daily intake is 0.2 mg/kg body weight, so 14 mg/d for a 70 kg adult.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody with a demonstrated clinical outcome.

**Pros**

- Small improvements in skin moisture (ES 0.53) and elasticity (ES 0.77) across 9 oral randomised trials.
- One oxidative-stress marker, advanced oxidation protein products, fell significantly after exercise.

**Cons**

- The core selling point is a test-tube potency ratio, often quoted as 1,000 times coenzyme Q10 at quenching singlet oxygen. That is an in-vitro number with no demonstrated clinical consequence, and it is exactly the kind of assay-derived claim the PAINS critique warns about.
- Wrinkle depth, the outcome consumers actually buy it for, was NOT significant (-0.26, 95% CI -0.58 to 0.06).
- In exercise trials MDA, TBARS, superoxide dismutase, sulfhydryls, creatine kinase and IL-6 were all non-significant, directly contradicting the antioxidant marketing.
- EFSA noted that children exceed the acceptable daily intake with 8 mg/d supplements, so the common commercial 12 mg dose exceeds the safe intake for a 40 kg adolescent.
- Krill-derived astaxanthin in shellfish allergy. Reported 5-alpha-reductase inhibition is poorly characterised and matters in hormone-sensitive conditions.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose partly reachable
- **Data confidence:** Medium

Astaxanthin is genuinely present at milligram levels in ordinary seafood, so this is the strongest food case in the set. Wild sockeye salmon carries 26 to 38 mg per kg of flesh, meaning one 140 g fillet supplies 3.6 to 5.3 mg, the same order of magnitude as a capsule. Several standard sources in the same literature carry no verified figure here and so get no row: salmon roe (ikura and sujiko), wild chinook, pink and chum salmon, Arctic char, red sea bream, and the shell-on preparations (crayfish, small crab, head-on prawns) where the pigment actually concentrates.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Wild sockeye salmon (Oncorhynchus nerka), flesh | 2.6 to 3.8 mg per 100 g (26 to 38 mg/kg flesh). A Raman study of retail fillets measured lower, 1.42 mg/100 g (14.2 mg/kg), showing real variability | 1 fillet (140 g) | 3.6 to 5.3 mg (2.0 mg using the lower retail measurement) | 30 to 44% of a 12 mg clinical dose per fillet | Ambati RR et al., Astaxanthin: sources, extraction, stability, biological activities and its commercial applications, Mar Drugs 2014;12(1):128-52, PMID 24402174, PMC3917265, DOI 10.3390/md12010128, Figure 1, verified, including the note that 165 g of salmon per day supplies 3.6 mg. Retail fillet measurement: Hikima J et al., On-site direct detection of astaxanthin from salmon fillet using Raman spectroscopy, Mar Biotechnol 2017;19(2):157-63, PMID 28378103, verified |
| Rainbow trout, large, flesh | 0.6 mg per 100 g for European-market trout, 2.5 mg per 100 g for Japanese-market trout (6 and 25 mg/kg) | 1 fillet (140 g) | 0.84 to 3.5 mg | 7 to 29% of a 12 mg dose | Ambati RR et al., Mar Drugs 2014;12(1):128-52, PMID 24402174, PMC3917265, Figure 1 (both market figures verified) |
| Farmed Atlantic salmon, flesh | 0.6 to 0.8 mg per 100 g (6 to 8 mg/kg flesh) | 1 fillet (140 g) | 0.84 to 1.12 mg | 7 to 9% of a 12 mg dose | Ambati RR et al., Mar Drugs 2014;12(1):128-52, PMID 24402174, PMC3917265, Figure 1 (verified) |
| Wild coho salmon, flesh | 0.70 mg per 100 g (7.0 mg/kg, measured on retail fillet) | 1 fillet (140 g) | 0.98 mg | 8% of a 12 mg dose | Hikima J et al., On-site direct detection of astaxanthin from salmon fillet using Raman spectroscopy, Mar Biotechnol 2017;19(2):157-63, PMID 28378103 (abstract verified) |
| Antarctic krill (Euphausia superba), whole, wet weight | about 0.8 to 1.0 mg per 100 g of whole wet krill (8 to 10 mg/kg), converted from 37.41 mg/kg in dried krill meal at roughly 78 to 80% water in the live animal | 100 g of whole krill | about 0.8 to 1.0 mg | 7 to 8% of a 12 mg dose | Zheng Y et al., Prevention of the quality degradation of Antarctic krill (Euphausia superba) meal through two-stage drying, Foods 2024;13(11):1706, PMC11171497, DOI 10.3390/foods13111706 (full text verified: 37.41 mg/kg is the best of five drying treatments, which ranged 33.99 to 39.00 mg/kg). That figure is dried krill meal, a feed and aquaculture ingredient rather than a human portion, and the paper gives no value for raw or frozen krill, so the wet-weight conversion here is indicative. The previously claimed 30 to 40 mg/kg for frozen krill has been dropped as unsourced and inconsistent with the fact that removing water concentrates the pigment |
| Shrimp / prawns, peeled cooked meat (Pandalus borealis) | about 0.86 mg per 100 g of peeled meat (8.6 ug/g, the low end of a reported 8.6 to 70 ug/g span). The high end of that span belongs to shell and head fractions and whole shell-on products, which are a different food from peeled meat | 100 g peeled cooked shrimp, about 12 large prawns | about 0.86 mg | 7% of a 12 mg dose | Jensen HA, Hansen LT, Boknaes N, Mejlholm O, Jacobsen C, Dalgaard P, Northern shrimp (Pandalus borealis): a review on biology, catch, processing, quality changes, shelf-life and product safety, Crit Rev Food Sci Nutr 2025, DOI 10.1080/10408398.2025.2505241. Citation genuine but paywalled, so the 8.6 to 70 ug/g span is indicative and unconfirmed; Ambati's Table 1 attributes 0.12% dry weight to the same species, about an order of magnitude apart |

- **To match the supplement dose:** A 12 mg daily dose, the commonest in the clinical literature, equals 315 to 460 g of wild sockeye salmon every day at 26 to 38 mg/kg, or about 550 g using the cited review's own dietary anchor of 3.6 mg per 165 g portion. From farmed Atlantic salmon it is 1.5 to 2.0 kg per day, from peeled shrimp meat about 1.4 kg, and from whole wet krill 1.2 to 1.5 kg. A single 140 g sockeye fillet already covers 30 to 44% of the dose, which no other entry here manages, so a meaningful fraction is reachable by eating while the full dose is not: sustaining 350 to 500 g of oily fish daily would push past standard advice on methylmercury and persistent organic pollutants.
- **Food versus supplement:** Chemical form differs by source. Salmon astaxanthin is largely free or protein-bound; krill and Haematococcus astaxanthin is esterified with fatty acids; and much supplement and feed astaxanthin is synthetic astaxanthin dimethyldisuccinate, a different stereoisomer mixture from the natural 3S,3'S form.
