This matters for the safety arithmetic: EFSA's acceptable daily intake of 0.2 mg/kg body weight per day (14 mg for a 70 kg adult, established by the FEEDAP Panel in 2019 and replacing the earlier 0.034 mg/kg) was derived from a rat carcinogenicity study on the synthetic material and then applied to the algal form. On that ADI, a 12 mg capsule is 86% of the limit and sits just under it, doses of about 14 mg and above exceed it, and a 24 mg capsule is roughly 1.7 times over, while a salmon fillet stays well below. A safety review of 87 human studies, including 35 at 12 mg/day or more, found no safety concerns with natural astaxanthin and argues the synthetic-derived ADI should not govern it (Brendler & Williamson, Phytother Res 2019;33(12):3090-3111, PMID 31788888, DOI 10.1002/ptr.6514). One honest twist: the astaxanthin in farmed salmon is itself an added feed pigment, so eating farmed salmon for astaxanthin means eating a supplemented animal.
- **Absorption:** Astaxanthin is a lipophilic xanthophyll and absorption depends on fat and micelle formation: uptake from a lipid-based formulation or a fatty meal is severalfold higher than from a dry powder taken fasted, and esterified forms must first be hydrolysed by pancreatic lipase. Overall human bioavailability is low and highly variable (single-digit percentages), which cuts in favour of the food: salmon arrives pre-packaged with its own lipid and a food matrix, so the plasma difference between a 4 mg fillet and a 12 mg dry capsule is smaller than the milligram gap implies.
- **Data note:** Every salmonid figure verified exactly against its source (sockeye 26 to 38 mg/kg, Atlantic 6 to 8, trout 6 and 25, the 165 g / 3.6 mg anchor, retail sockeye 14.2 and coho 7.0 mg/kg), as did the EFSA ADI and the Brendler review. The salmonid values still rest on a compiled review figure, the shrimp span is paywalled and indicative, and the krill row is a wet-weight reconstruction rather than a measured food value

*References: Zhou PMID 34578794; Ma PMID 35091276; You PMID 41710469; EFSA PMID 32874213*

### HMB

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** 3 g/d, usually as three 1 g doses. No dose or duration rescues the result in trained people.
- **Who it is for:** On the balance of umbrella reviews, at most untrained beginners or clinically muscle-wasting populations.

**Pros**

- An umbrella review of 11 studies and 41 datasets found small but significant pooled effects on muscle mass (ES 0.21), muscle strength index (0.27) and fat-free mass (0.22).
- No established contraindication, no drug interaction and not a doping risk.

**Cons**

- In trained and competitive athletes it is a flat null across every endpoint. Six randomised trials, n=193: bench press effect size 0.00, leg press 0.09, body mass -0.01, fat-free mass 0.16, fat mass -0.20, all non-significant and described by the authors as trivial. Results were unchanged by dose, duration, training level or dietary co-intervention.
- An umbrella review of 15 systematic reviews found only 5 favoured HMB for lean soft-tissue mass; 10 reported no difference or insufficient evidence.
- Its reputation was built on a handful of early trials from a small number of affiliated laboratories and never replicated independently in trained athletes.
- One of the more expensive ingredients per gram, so it displaces spend from protein and creatine, which work.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** No food source, manufactured
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** High

HMB (beta-hydroxy-beta-methylbutyrate) is manufactured, by halogenation of diacetone alcohol with sodium hypobromite or by esterification routes from diacetone alcohol and acetic acid derivatives, at plant scales around 1.5 tonnes per month, with microbial routes emerging (Galactomyces reessii 29.0 g/L, engineered E. coli 17.7 g/L). No food composition database carries an HMB value, and the frequently repeated claim that it occurs in catfish, grapefruit and alfalfa has no quantified composition analysis behind it; an independent literature query returned five papers, none with a food figure. Endogenous production of 0.3-0.4 g/day in healthy adults is metabolism rather than a dietary source, so the earlier TRACE label overstated the dietary presence.

*No food rows: there is no ordinary food that supplies this in a meaningful amount.*

- **To match the supplement dose:** There is no food HMB figure to work from, so the gap has to be argued through the precursor. About 80% of leucine is directed to protein synthesis, and approximately 5-10% of alpha-ketoisocaproate, the leucine transamination product, is converted to HMB; treating that 5-10% as a leucine-to-HMB yield is generous, so the arithmetic below is a ceiling. Even on that generous reading, 3 g of HMB implies about 60 g of leucine per day, which is 2.6 kg of cooked chicken breast at 2.33 g leucine per 100 g, carrying roughly 800 g of protein. At the optimistic 10% figure it is 30 g of leucine, about 1.3 kg of cooked breast and 400 g of protein. Measured endogenous output in healthy adults is only 0.3-0.4 g/day, 8 to 20 times below the 3-6 g/day used in trials, and eating more protein or leucine does not move it, because the bottleneck is the branched-chain pathway rather than the intake.
- **Food versus supplement:** The supplement is a manufactured molecule and not a food extract, so there is no whole-food-versus-extract comparison to make and no reason to describe HMB as something a diet can supply. Regulatory reviews treat 3-6 g/day as generally safe while noting that no international consensus on an optimal dose exists. The one form question that matters commercially is calcium HMB monohydrate versus free-acid HMB, which differ in absorption kinetics rather than in origin. Primary source for the manufacturing, endogenous production and conversion figures: Zhou S, Liu G, Wang Z, Lei Z, Chen W, Wang C, Foods 2025;14(8):1294, PMID 40282696, DOI 10.3390/foods14081294.
- **Absorption:** There is no food form whose absorption can be compared, so the only meaningful statement is about the pathway. Dietary leucine is not a usable precursor at supplement scale: about 80% of leucine goes to protein synthesis, and roughly 5-10% of alpha-ketoisocaproate, the leucine transamination product, is converted to HMB, which is why raising protein or leucine intake lifts endogenous HMB only marginally and total endogenous output stays at 0.3-0.4 g/day (DOI 10.3390/foods14081294). Between the two supplement forms, free-acid HMB is reported to reach peak plasma concentration faster and higher than calcium HMB monohydrate, which is the main practical bioavailability distinction; both bypass the leucine pathway entirely, which is precisely why the supplement exists.
- **Data note:** All five manufacturing and endogenous production figures were confirmed verbatim against Foods 2025;14(8):1294, the leucine-route arithmetic was re-derived and held, and the negative claim that no database carries an HMB value and that the catfish/grapefruit/alfalfa story has no quantified backing survived independent adversarial checking. The item has no food rows because no defensible food figure exists

*References: Sanchez-Martinez PMID 29249685; Phillips PMID 35818771; Bideshki PMID 39797501*

### Ginkgo biloba

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** 240 mg/d of EGb 761, which is the dose that failed in both prevention trials.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody, for preventing dementia. Possibly people with established dementia, on low-certainty evidence.

**Pros**

- The 2026 Cochrane review of 82 studies and 10,613 participants found uncertain benefit for global clinical status at 6 months in people with cognitive complaints, at low certainty.
- Unusually good long-term human safety data for a botanical: 240 mg/d for about 6 years in GEM without a safety signal.

**Cons**

- It is the strongest example in this document of a product that survived its own definitive trials. GEM randomised 3,069 older adults to 240 mg/d for a median 6.1 years: all-cause dementia HR 1.12 (0.94-1.33), Alzheimer disease HR 1.16 (0.97-1.39). The point estimates are above 1.
- GuidAge replicated the null in 2,854 adults over 5 years.
- The bleeding question is genuinely contested rather than settled. A meta-analysis of 18 trials in 1,985 adults found no significant effect on platelet aggregation or coagulation parameters, but case reports of bleeding exist and stopping before elective surgery is prudent.
- Only standardised extracts have any data behind them. Unstandardised leaf products have none.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** From a plant not eaten as food
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Medium

The supplement is a concentrated leaf extract, and ginkgo leaf is not a food: it is processed with acetone-water into a standardised extract, of which EGb 761 is the reference, at a drug-to-extract ratio of 35-67:1 containing 22-27% flavone glycosides and 5-7% terpene lactones (2.8-3.4% ginkgolides A, B and C plus 2.6-3.2% bilobalide) with ginkgolic acids held below 5 ppm. The same species does have a real food form, and this is what a flat NOT_FOOD label conceals: ginkgo NUTS are eaten in Japanese chawanmushi, Chinese congee and Korean dishes, and they are chemically a different proposition from the leaf. Seeds carry ginkgotoxin (4'-O-methylpyridoxine and its 5'-glucoside), a vitamin B6 antagonist, at total mature-stage contents of 249-296 ug/g measured across three Chinese samples (249.30, 295.62 and 267.85 ug/g; Gong H et al., J Agric Food Chem 2018;66(30):7916-7922, PMID 29975518). Seeds do contain flavonoids and terpene trilactones occur in several tissues, but nothing in the seed approaches the 24%/6% standardised profile of a 50:1 leaf extract, so the edible organ does not supply the medicinal one.

*No food rows: there is no ordinary food that supplies this in a meaningful amount.*

- **To match the supplement dose:** Clinical dosing is 120-240 mg/day of EGb 761. At a 35-67:1 drug-to-extract ratio that corresponds to 4.2-16 g of dried ginkgo leaf per day, and dried leaf is not something people eat; a leaf tea extracts only part of the terpene lactones and none of the concentration. Via the edible nut the gap cannot be closed at all, because the nut does not carry the leaf profile, and attempting it would be dangerous rather than merely futile: acute poisoning has been reported after 10-50 cooked seeds at one sitting, Hong Kong's Centre for Food Safety advises no more than 10 kernels a day for adults and three for children, and cooking does not destroy ginkgotoxin because it is heat-stable. Adult intoxication with seizures is documented (PMC7007935).
- **Food versus supplement:** Two separate caveats, and both cut against food substitution. First, the safe food (the nut) and the studied medicine (the leaf extract) share no active profile; a bowl of ginkgo-nut congee is not a low dose of EGb 761. Second, the food itself has a hard ceiling: ginkgotoxin blocks pyridoxal-5'-phosphate-dependent enzymes including glutamate decarboxylase, so excess intake causes vomiting, irritability and convulsions, with children at much higher risk. Ginkgotoxin is present in leaf tissue as well, at far lower levels than in seed, and in a typical standardised leaf extract it sits below a 9 ppm limit of detection (Planta Med 2009, PMID 19288402; Toxicon 2021, PMID 34425140; J Nat Prod 2014, PMID 24432981); that 9 ppm figure supports the manufacturing argument better than the false absence claim it replaces. On the extract side, the concentration step is what makes standardisation possible and also what concentrates the hazards manufacturers must strip out: ginkgolic acids are strongly allergenic and are capped at 5 ppm, and unstandardised or adulterated ginkgo products (rutin or quercetin spiking to hit a flavonoid assay) are a recurring market problem. Ginkgo extract also inhibits platelet aggregation and interacts with anticoagulants, and lowers efavirenz and some other drug levels through CYP induction.
- **Absorption:** The two marker classes behave completely differently. Terpene lactones (ginkgolides A and B, bilobalide) are small, reasonably lipophilic and well absorbed after oral dosing of the standardised extract. The flavonol glycosides are not absorbed intact: gut microbiota hydrolyse them to quercetin, kaempferol and isorhamnetin aglycones, which are then heavily glucuronidated and sulfated, so plasma concentrations of the labelled compounds are very low and highly variable between people. This is the practical reason a leaf infusion is not a weak extract: hot water recovers a modest and inconsistent share of the terpene lactones, and the flavonoid fraction ends up as conjugated metabolites regardless. For the nut, the compound with real oral kinetics is ginkgotoxin, which is absorbed efficiently and has been measured in the serum of poisoning patients.
- **Data note:** The EGb 761 specification and the derived 4.2-16 g/day dried-leaf equivalent were verified, as was the ginkgotoxin poisoning literature. Two of the entry's original factual claims failed verification and were corrected: the seed ginkgotoxin lower bound and the assertion that ginkgotoxin is absent from the leaf. A separate seed range of 170-404 ug/g could not be traced to the cited source and has been dropped rather than restated

*References: GEM DeKosky PMID 19017911; GuidAge Vellas PMID 22959217; Cochrane Wieland PMID 41641880*

### Valerian

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** Roughly 300-600 mg of extract 30-120 minutes before bed. No effective dose is established.
- **Who it is for:** On present evidence, nobody reliably.

**Pros**

- The umbrella review characterises it as having a good safety profile, which is the strongest positive statement the evidence supports.
- The 2006 review found a statistically significant benefit on a dichotomous improved-sleep outcome, relative risk 1.8 (1.2-2.9).

**Cons**

- The 2006 authors reported publication bias, considerable heterogeneity and variable preparations in the same paper that produced the positive number. The positive number does not survive its own caveats.
- The 2024 umbrella review of 8 systematic reviews concluded there is NO evidence of efficacy for the treatment of insomnia, with subjective improvements not corroborated by objective measures.
- The AASM guideline suggests clinicians NOT use valerian for sleep-onset or sleep-maintenance insomnia.
- Additive sedation with benzodiazepines, Z-drugs, barbiturates, opioids, antihistamines and alcohol. Case reports of hepatotoxicity exist, mostly with multi-herb combination products.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** From a plant not eaten as food
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Medium

Valerian is the root and rhizome of Valeriana officinalis, a European perennial. It is not a food in any cuisine; it is a bitter, strongly odorous drug taken as a tea, tincture or dried-extract capsule. The constituents credited with the sedative effect, valerenic acid and its relatives plus the flavonoids 6-methylapigenin, hesperidin and linarin, are found in Valeriana species and nowhere in the food supply. Valerian root has been used at low levels as a flavouring in some beverages, which puts trace amounts into a few products, and those are flavour levels, orders of magnitude below anything sedative. Nothing you would call a food supplies valerenic acid.

*No food rows: there is no ordinary food that supplies this in a meaningful amount.*

- **To match the supplement dose:** Typical use is 400-900 mg of dried root extract before bed, or 2-3 g of dried root as an infusion. A quality target of at least 3 mg/g valerenic acid in the dried root has been recommended for commercial material and marketed extracts are sold around 0.8% valerenic acid, so a night's dose is roughly 3-8 mg of valerenic acid. No food at any portion size supplies measurable valerenic acid, so there is no achievable eating quantity to compute. The gap is total rather than large.
- **Food versus supplement:** Valerian is the textbook example of a herb where the preparation determines the chemistry. Valerenic acid concentrations fall significantly during storage, with the greatest loss at 30 C in low humidity (Food Chemistry 2009, changes in valerenic acid content of valerian root during long-term storage), so an old capsule and a fresh one are different products. Valepotriates present in some preparations are unstable and degrade to baldrinals, and aqueous versus ethanolic extraction recovers quite different fractions, which is one reason valerian trials are so inconsistent. Practical cautions: additive sedation with alcohol, benzodiazepines and antihistamines; occasional paradoxical stimulation; rare hepatotoxicity reports, usually in multi-herb products. A valerian tea and a standardised extract capsule are not interchangeable at the same nominal gram amount.
- **Absorption:** Human data are limited. Valerenic acid is detectable in plasma after oral dosing with a short half-life of roughly one to two hours, which fits its use as a bedtime agent rather than a daytime one, and the flavonoid constituents undergo extensive glucuronidation. Because there is no food form there is no food-matrix comparison; the meaningful variable is extraction, since valerenic acid is lipophilic and is recovered far better by ethanol than by a water infusion, so a tea delivers a small and poorly characterised fraction of what a labelled milligram amount of extract implies.
- **Data note:** The NOT_FOOD classification was challenged and upheld and the valerenic-acid dose arithmetic (3-8 mg per night) was verified as correct. The 3 mg/g quality target and the 0.8% extract figure each rest on a single secondary citation, and the storage-stability claim was not individually verified in the numeric pass

*References: Valente umbrella review PMID 38359657; Bent PMID 17145239; Sateia AASM PMID 27998379*

### Rhodiola rosea

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** Commercial convention is 200-600 mg/d of extract standardised to about 3% rosavins. There is no evidence-based dose.
- **Who it is for:** Possibly people with subjective mental fatigue, on evidence too weak to justify confidence.

**Pros**

- Three of five randomised trials on mental fatigue were positive, so there is a signal, however unreliable.

**Cons**

- The systematic review screened 206 articles, included 11 trials, and found that ALL of them exhibit either a high risk of bias or reporting flaws that hinder assessment of their true effect. No two studies reported the same outcome, so meta-analysis was impossible.
- Only 2 of 6 physical-fatigue trials were positive.
- The premium adaptogen positioning has essentially no regulatory-grade support. EFSA has authorised no health claim.
- No upper limit can be stated because dose-ranging and toxicity data do not exist. Theoretical additive serotonergic effects with SSRIs, SNRIs and MAOIs, and activation risk in bipolar disorder.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** From a plant not eaten as food
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Medium

The supplement is the rhizome and root of Rhodiola rosea (roseroot), an arctic-alpine succulent in the Crassulaceae, standardised on rosavins (rosavin, rosin, rosarin) and salidroside. The rhizome is a folk-medicine drug of Scandinavia and Russia and is not eaten as food. The plant is not entirely outside the food record: young leaves and shoots have been eaten raw or cooked like spinach and stems cooked like asparagus in Greenland, Alaska and northern North America, and Inuit use mixed leaves and buds with marine fat. That food use involves the aerial parts while rosavins are rhizome constituents, and composition data for the edible shoots are sparse, so the culinary history does not translate into a dietary source of the marker compounds. Rosavins are also close to unique to R. rosea, which is why species substitution is such a persistent problem.

*No food rows: there is no ordinary food that supplies this in a meaningful amount.*

- **To match the supplement dose:** Typical dosing is 200-600 mg/day of extract standardised to 3% rosavins and 1% salidroside (the SHR-5 pattern), roughly 6-18 mg rosavins and 2-6 mg salidroside per day. The dried-rhizome equivalent depends entirely on the content assumed, and the 0.01-3.08% rosavin / 0.07-2.91% salidroside range cited here is COMMERCIAL PRODUCT content rather than measured rhizome, so raw-material content is not established from that source. Stating the assumption explicitly: at 1-3% rosavins, 0.2-1.8 g of dried rhizome carries a daily dose; at 0.1-0.5%, 1.2-18 g; at the 0.01% floor of the cited range, 60-180 g. The submission's flat 0.5-2 g figure silently assumed the top of the range and is optimistic by up to two orders of magnitude. On any assumption this is a herbal dose of a root and no food supplies it, and the edible young shoots cannot be costed at all because the composition work is on rhizome material.
- **Food versus supplement:** Rhodiola has an unusually bad identity record. An analysis of US-market Rhodiola supplements found rosavin and salidroside contents ranging from 0.01% to 3.08% and 0.07% to 2.91% respectively, with substantial departures from label claims, alongside heavy metal and pesticide residue screening (The quality and safety of Rhodiola rosea supplements on the U.S. market, PLoS One 2026, PMC12810810, verified). Those are finished-product figures, not raw-material figures, a distinction the submission blurred. Rhodiola crenulata is a common substitute; it contains salidroside but little or no rosavin, so a product standardised only on salidroside can be a different species. Wild R. rosea is under harvesting pressure and CITES-listed in Appendix II. Field material varies enormously by clone and site, with inter-clonal variation of 92.8% reported for salidroside in Norwegian collections, so growing region and harvest season change the product more than the label does.
- **Absorption:** Salidroside is absorbed rapidly and hydrolysed to tyrosol, and rosavins are cinnamyl glycosides subject to gut microbial deglycosylation, so plasma exposure reflects metabolites rather than the labelled markers; controlled human pharmacokinetic data are limited and no reliable percentage figure can be given. Because there is no food form, the practical absorption question is the extraction solvent: hydroalcoholic extracts recover both rosavins and salidroside, while water infusions of the root recover the glycosides preferentially. Chewing raw shoots, the only genuinely food-like route, has never been characterised.
- **Data note:** The NOT_FOOD classification was challenged and upheld and the cited biomarker ranges were verified as real and on-topic, but those ranges are commercial-product contents that the submission reused as raw-material contents, so the dried-rhizome dose equivalent is now given as a wide assumption-dependent range rather than a figure. No composition data exist for the edible aerial shoots, so that route cannot be quantified at all

*References: Ishaque BMC Complement Altern Med PMID 22643043*

### Lion's mane (Hericium erinaceus)

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** 3,000 mg/d of dried fruiting body for 16 weeks in the pivotal trial. Fruiting body and mycelium contain different compounds and are not interchangeable.
- **Who it is for:** Possibly older adults with mild cognitive impairment, on the basis of a single 30-person trial.

**Pros**

- It is a food mushroom with a long history of dietary use, so the baseline safety expectation is reasonable.
- In the pivotal trial, dementia screening scores improved at weeks 8, 12 and 16.

**Cons**

- The pivotal trial randomised 30 Japanese adults, 15 per arm, to a dementia SCREENING scale. Scores then fell significantly 4 weeks after stopping. There is no meta-analysis and no trial with a hard endpoint.
- The only other cited trial is explicitly self-described as a pilot.
- This is one of the largest gaps between marketing volume and evidence volume in the entire document. The neurogenesis claim rests on in-vitro nerve growth factor stimulation.
- Most retail products do not disclose whether they contain fruiting body or mycelium, so you cannot match the product to the trial.
- Mushroom allergy is a real contraindication. Theoretical antiplatelet effects are uncharacterised in humans.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Low

This is the one entry whose supplement organism is an ordinary culinary food. Hericium erinaceus is sold fresh in Asian markets and increasingly in Western ones, cooked like scallops or shredded into stir-fries, and it is the same organism used to make the fruiting-body supplement. The best-known human trial used no exotic extract: Mori and colleagues gave 3 g/day of dried Hericium powder, four 250 mg tablets containing 96% dry powder three times daily, to older adults with mild cognitive impairment (Phytother Res 2009;23(3):367-372, PMID 18844328, verified). RICH describes this one species in its several presentations (fresh, dried powder, dried slices), which are one food rather than several independent sources. The important limit is by tissue: the newer and more mechanistically interesting trials use erinacine-A-enriched MYCELIUM, and no amount of the edible fruiting body supplies erinacines. Other edible Hericium species exist, notably H. coralloides and H. americanum, which matters here because the composition paper the submission cited was about H. coralloides rather than H. erinaceus.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Lion's mane (Hericium erinaceus), fresh fruiting body, raw before cooking | 8-11 g dry mushroom matter (fresh H. erinaceus is broadly 89-92% water) | 100 g, a generous side portion or about one third of a medium fruiting body | 8-11 g dry matter | roughly 270-370% of the 3 g/day dried-powder dose used in the Mori MCI trial | Moisture given as the published H. erinaceus band of 89-92% rather than a point value. The submission's 91.68% came from J Fungi 2025;11(11):785 (PMID 41295165 / PMC12653868), which is titled Domestication Cultivation and Nutritional Analysis of Hericium CORALLOIDES and measured only a wild Tibetan H. coralloides strain (SH001); that species misattribution has been removed and no H. erinaceus primary moisture measurement was re-traced in this pass, so this range is indicative. Trial dose from Mori et al., Phytother Res 2009;23(3):367-372, PMID 18844328 (verified). The polysaccharide content the submission attached to this row (0.40-0.47 g per 100 g fresh, derived from 4.85-5.68% of dry weight) has been withdrawn because that range is not present in the cited paper. |
| Lion's mane (Hericium erinaceus), dried powder or dried slices, the same food in a dehydrated form | 100 g dried mushroom material (H. erinaceus dry-weight composition: protein 20.08 g, fat 5.1 g, carbohydrate 61.1 g, ash 6.8 g per 100 g) | 1 tablespoon powder (5 g), or 10 g of dried slices rehydrated to roughly 100 g | 5 g dried material per tablespoon; 10 g dried per slice portion | 167% of the 3 g/day trial dose per tablespoon of powder; 333% per 10 g of dried slices | Proximate values are genuine H. erinaceus figures but appear in J Fungi 2025;11(11):785 (PMC12653868) only as a literature comparison quoted from that paper's reference [29]; they were not measured there and the original source was not traced in this pass, so they are indicative for provenance. That paper's own measured values are for H. coralloides (protein 15.4 +/- 1.2, fat 3.5 +/- 0.5, carbohydrate 64.3, ash 6.8 +/- 0.8 g/100 g DW) and are not used here. Trial dose from Mori et al., PMID 18844328 (verified). This row consolidates the submission's separate powder and dried-slice rows, which were one food in two presentations. |

- **To match the supplement dose:** For the fruiting-body dose there is essentially no gap. At 8-11% dry matter, the 3 g/day of dried powder used in the Mori trial is about 27-38 g of fresh lion's mane, a small side portion, or 3 g of dried slices, or a rounded half-tablespoon of powder. A 100 g fresh portion supplies 8-11 g of dry matter, roughly 270-370% of that dose. The gap reappears with a concentrated extract: 3 g/day of a nominal 8:1 hot-water extract corresponds to about 24 g of dried mushroom, roughly 218-300 g of fresh mushroom a day, which is a lot of mushroom and still not absurd. For erinacine-A products the answer is NO at any portion size, because erinacines are mycelial and the fruiting body sold for cooking does not contain them at meaningful levels.
- **Food versus supplement:** The compound classes split cleanly by tissue and by solvent, and this is where most consumer confusion lives. Hericenones C-H occur in the fruiting body; erinacines A-I occur in the mycelium, with erinacine biosynthesis genes consistently downregulated in fruiting-body tissue and no detectable erinacine production there (Fungal Biol Biotechnol 2025, PMC11969743, verified). The Taiwanese trials in early Alzheimer's disease and in depression used erinacine-A-enriched mycelium capsules at around 5 mg/g erinacine A, three 350 mg capsules a day giving 1,050 mg mycelia and about 5.25 mg erinacine A over 49 weeks (PMC7283924, verified), so their results cannot be transferred to eating the mushroom, and equally the Mori fruiting-body result cannot be transferred to a mycelium product. Solvent matters as much as tissue: hericenones are lipophilic and are recovered by ethanol while hot-water extraction recovers polysaccharides, so a dual extract and a hot-water extract deliver different chemistry at the same milligram figure. The specific polysaccharide numbers the submission used to make that point (4.85-5.68% of dry weight for the fruiting body and 3.62% beta-glucan for a hot-water extract) are withdrawn here, because neither is present in the paper cited for them. Many cheap US supplements are mycelium grown on grain and sold with the substrate included, so a large fraction of the capsule weight is starch; a starch assay or a low beta-glucan number relative to labelled polysaccharide is the tell. Safety is good for a food, with occasional contact dermatitis and respiratory allergy reports, and scattered case reports of skin reactions with supplement use.
- **Absorption:** The fungal beta-glucans are not absorbed intact; a water-soluble 1-3/1-6 beta-glucan of about 13.3 kDa has been characterised from the fruiting body, and such polymers act on gut and gut-associated immune receptors rather than entering circulation, so how much reaches the blood is the wrong question for the polysaccharide fraction. The small molecules are the ones with systemic potential: erinacine A is lipophilic, crosses the blood-brain barrier in rodents and is the compound behind the neurotrophic claims, while hericenones are also lipophilic and their human absorption is poorly characterised. Practically, fat in the pan helps the hericenone fraction, a water-based soup extracts the polysaccharides, and the raw mushroom is not the right comparator for either since chitinous cell walls need heat to break down. The relevant food-versus-supplement difference here is which molecules are present at all rather than absorption efficiency.
- **Data note:** Every composition figure in this entry traces to one paper that turned out to be about a different species: J Fungi 2025;11(11):785 analysed only Hericium coralloides, and the submission truncated its title to Hericium. The 91.68% moisture that generated every dose-gap number was an H. coralloides measurement, the H. erinaceus proximate values appear in that paper only as an untraced quotation from its reference [29], and the 4.85-5.68% polysaccharide range is absent from the paper altogether. The replacement moisture figure used here is the published 89-92% band rather than a specific re-traced H. erinaceus measurement. What is solid is the dose side: the Mori 3 g/day protocol, the 4 x 250 mg x 3 arithmetic, the 5 mg/g erinacine A figure and the tissue-partition claim were all verified

*References: Mori PMID 18844328; Li PMID 32581767*

### Bacopa monnieri

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** 300-320 mg/d of extract standardised to 50-55% bacosides, for at least 12 weeks. It is not an acute agent.
- **Who it is for:** Healthy adults willing to take it daily for 12 weeks for a modest change in processing speed.

**Pros**

- The meta-analysis restricted inclusion to chronic dosing of 12 weeks or more, which is methodologically sound, and found two statistically significant results.

**Cons**

- Read the actual endpoints. The two significant results across 9 trials and 437 analysable subjects were a Trail Making Test B change of -17.9 MILLISECONDS and a choice reaction time change of about 10 ms. Millisecond-scale processing speed is being sold as memory and learning.
- The meta-analysis authors explicitly said larger comparative trials are needed before any recommendation.
- Nothing supports a memory or dementia effect.
- Gastrointestinal adverse effects (nausea, cramping, increased stool frequency) are the commonest problem. Cholinergic activity means caution with donepezil, rivastigmine and galantamine, and with anticholinergics. Animal work suggests thyroid hormone effects, so care with levothyroxine.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose partly reachable
- **Data confidence:** High

Bacopa monnieri (brahmi, water hyssop) is a real leafy vegetable in parts of India, which makes it the exception among the herbal entries here. Ethnobotanical surveys in West Bengal's Terai-Dooars, Maharashtra, Assam and northeast India record it among wild edible greens gathered and cooked, with tender shoots and leaves eaten fresh, cooked as a green, or made into chutneys and raita, so bacosides genuinely enter the food supply in a way withanolides and valerenic acid do not. RICH here describes concentration, since a culinary serving reaches a fifth to a half of the clinical dose, and it is worth being explicit that this is one regional green rather than several ordinary foods: the availability is narrow and geographically limited, which is a different axis from concentration and is where the submission's TRACE label went wrong. Note the naming trap: in parts of India brahmi refers to Centella asiatica, a different plant with different actives (asiaticosides), and market material is frequently mixed.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Bacopa monnieri (brahmi) dried aerial herb / churna powder, elite cultivar DBM-4 | 654 mg of bacoside A3 plus bacopaside II (1.57 mg/g bacoside A3 and 4.97 mg/g bacopaside II in dry herbage) | 1 teaspoon dried powder (2 g) | 13.1 mg bacosides | about 9% of the roughly 150 mg of bacosides in a standard 300 mg dose of a 50-55% bacoside extract | ICAR-Directorate of Medicinal and Aromatic Plants Research, Anand, variety release note for Bacopa monnieri DBM-4 (2020), https://icar.org.in/node/13442. Both figures, the DBM-4 designation and the CIM-Jagriti comparison were verified exactly; the verification pass called this the best-sourced food figure in the submission. Accession-level bacoside A survey in Industrial Crops and Products, PII S0926669021011134, gives the surrounding variation. |

- **To match the supplement dose:** Trials use 300 mg/day of extract standardised to 50-55% bacosides, roughly 150-165 mg of bacosides. At the measured 6.54 mg/g for the two quantified bacoside constituents, that is about 23 g of dried brahmi herb per day, roughly eleven teaspoons of churna, which nobody eats in a day. A 2 g teaspoon of dried powder supplies 13.1 mg, about 9% of the dose. Converting to fresh greens requires a fresh-to-dry ratio the cited sources do not give, so only the dried figure is quoted: a realistic culinary serving of the fresh green, the quantity that goes into a thoran or a chutney, corresponds to single-digit grams of dry matter and therefore to a few tens of milligrams of bacosides, a fifth to a half of the trial dose. Eating brahmi as a vegetable is on the same scale as supplementing it without matching it, which is not true of anything else in this list except lion's mane. Total bacoside A also includes bacopasaponin C and a jujubogenin isomer that the cited analysis does not quantify, so the real herb content is somewhat higher than 6.54 mg/g and the gap correspondingly smaller.
- **Food versus supplement:** Bacoside content in the herb varies several-fold with accession, season and harvest, which is exactly why breeding programmes select elite lines such as DBM-4, so a wild-gathered green is an unpredictable dose. Extraction matters too: bacosides are saponins recovered by hydroalcoholic extraction, and boiling a green in water for a curry leaches an unknown fraction into cooking liquid that may be discarded. The best-studied products (CDRI-08, BacoMind) are specific standardised extracts and their cognitive trial results should not be read across to eating the plant. Bacopa also has real dose-dependent gastrointestinal effects, cramping, nausea and loose stools, more prominent with the concentrated extract than with the vegetable, and it has cholinergic activity that could matter alongside cholinesterase inhibitors. Watch for Centella asiatica sold as brahmi, and for heavy metal accumulation, since Bacopa is a wetland plant with documented capacity to take up metals from sediment.
- **Absorption:** Bacosides are large triterpenoid saponin glycosides with poor membrane permeability and low oral bioavailability; the pharmacologically relevant species are thought to be the deglycosylated sapogenins (jujubogenin and pseudojujubogenin derivatives) generated by gut hydrolysis, which is why supplement makers push phospholipid-complex and nanoparticle formulations. Eating the whole green delivers the same glycosides embedded in a fibrous matrix with fat from the cooking medium, and it has never been compared head to head with an extract in humans, so the fresh-vegetable route is plausible and uncharacterised. The saponins themselves also have surfactant behaviour in the gut, which is part of why higher doses cause GI upset.
- **Data note:** The ICAR DBM-4 bacoside figures were verified exactly against the cited release note, including the variety designation, and every derived step recomputed correctly (654 mg per 100 g, 13.1 mg per 2 g teaspoon, about 9% of dose, 23 g and about eleven teaspoons for 150 mg). The one soft spot is that no fresh-to-dry ratio is available in the cited sources, so the fresh-green serving is described qualitatively rather than given a number

*References: Kongkeaw PMID 24252493*

### 5-HTP

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** 50-300 mg/d commercially. No authoritative dose exists because no usable trial established one.
- **Who it is for:** No group can be identified with confidence.

**Pros**

- The Cochrane meta-analysis produced a Peto odds ratio of 4.10 (1.28-13.15) with an NNT of 2.78, which sounds spectacular.

**Cons**

- That number comes from 64 patients in 2 trials out of 108 located. Cochrane located 108 trials and could use two. The confidence interval spans a tenfold range and the authors refused to stand behind it, concluding that because effective and safe alternative antidepressants exist, the clinical usefulness of 5-HTP and tryptophan is limited at present.
- The eosinophilia-myalgia syndrome question is unresolved. The 1989 outbreak was linked to contaminated L-tryptophan and Cochrane flagged that the possibility of a similar contaminant in 5-HTP products has not been excluded.
- Serotonin syndrome risk with SSRIs, SNRIs, MAOIs including the irreversible ones, tricyclics, tramadol, triptans, linezolid, lithium and dextromethorphan. This is a direct serotonin precursor, so the risk is mechanistic rather than theoretical.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** No food source, manufactured
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** High

5-hydroxytryptophan is not a dietary constituent. In living tissue it exists only as a transient intracellular intermediate between tryptophan and serotonin, immediately decarboxylated by aromatic L-amino acid decarboxylase, so it does not accumulate in any food at measurable levels. The commercial article is a purified single molecule, either extracted from the seeds of Griffonia simplicifolia, a West African climbing shrub whose seeds are an unusual botanical accumulator at 156 mg/g dry weight (about 16%) in a chemically characterised reference sample against literature concentrations of 10-20%, or, increasingly, produced by microbial or enzymatic biosynthesis. Griffonia seed is a pharmaceutical raw material rather than a food, which is why SYNTHETIC fits better than the NOT_FOOD label used for botanical drugs such as valerian or ashwagandha. The foods listed below are tryptophan sources, a different molecule one step upstream, scored against the WHO/FAO/UNU 2007 adult requirement of 4 mg tryptophan per kg per day (280 mg/day for a 70 kg adult) rather than against any 5-HTP dose.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Chicken breast, meat only, roasted | 362 mg tryptophan | 1 breast fillet, cooked (120 g) | 434 mg | 155% of the 280 mg/day adult tryptophan requirement | USDA FoodData Central, SR Legacy, FDC ID 171477. Verified: 0.362 g per 100 g (protein 30.9 g). |
| Salmon, Atlantic, farmed, cooked (dry heat) | 248 mg tryptophan | 1 fillet (150 g) | 372 mg | 133% of the 280 mg/day requirement | USDA FoodData Central, SR Legacy, FDC ID 175168. Verified: 0.248 g per 100 g (protein 22.1 g). |
| Cheddar cheese | 547 mg tryptophan | 1 thick slice / small matchbox (40 g) | 219 mg | 78% of the 280 mg/day requirement | USDA FoodData Central, SR Legacy, FDC ID 173414. Verified: 0.547 g per 100 g (protein 22.87 g). The verification pass flagged that this is 2.4% of protein, well above casein's roughly 1.3% tryptophan, so the USDA figure itself is likely an imputation artefact; the value is reported as the database gives it. |
| Pumpkin and squash seed kernels, dried | 576 mg tryptophan | 2 tablespoons (30 g) | 173 mg | 62% of the 280 mg/day requirement | USDA FoodData Central, SR Legacy, FDC ID 170556. Verified: 0.576 g per 100 g (protein 30.2 g). |
| Egg, whole, raw, fresh | 167 mg tryptophan | 1 large egg (50 g) | 84 mg | 30% of the 280 mg/day requirement | USDA FoodData Central, SR Legacy, FDC ID 171287. Verified: 0.167 g per 100 g, 0.083 g per 50 g egg (the 84 mg is rounding). |
| Milk, whole, 3.25% milkfat | 40 mg tryptophan | 1 glass, 200 mL (206 g) | 82 mg | 29% of the 280 mg/day requirement | USDA FoodData Central, SR Legacy, FDC ID 171265. Verified: 0.040 g per 100 g (protein 3.15 g). |

- **To match the supplement dose:** For 5-HTP itself the gap is absolute: no food contains it in measurable quantity, so no eating quantity reproduces the 50-300 mg/day used in trials. From the manufacturing raw material, 100 mg of 5-HTP sits in about 0.64 g of Griffonia seed at 156 mg/g, a striking number and irrelevant to eating, since Griffonia seed is not a food and carries other constituents. Via the dietary precursor the arithmetic runs deceptively the other way. An ordinary mixed diet supplies roughly 900-1000 mg of tryptophan a day, three to twenty times a 5-HTP dose by mass, and a single chicken breast supplies 434 mg, yet that intake does not act like 5-HTP for three reasons. Over 95% of tryptophan is degraded down the kynurenine pathway by TDO and IDO, with only about 1-2% passing through tryptophan hydroxylase to serotonin (PMC5398323; PMC9292703). Tryptophan competes with the other large neutral amino acids, leucine, isoleucine, valine, phenylalanine and tyrosine, for the LAT1 transporter at the blood-brain barrier, and every protein food raises tryptophan and its competitors together, so a high-protein meal can leave the brain tryptophan ratio flat or lower. And tryptophan hydroxylase is the regulated rate-limiting step, so raising substrate does little. 5-HTP sidesteps all three: it is past the rate-limiting hydroxylation and is decarboxylated by ubiquitous AADC. Adding 300 mg of tryptophan to a meal is therefore not equivalent to 300 mg of 5-HTP, and no realistic quantity of food makes it so.
- **Food versus supplement:** Bypassing the pathway's regulation is what makes 5-HTP a drug rather than a nutrient. AADC is present throughout peripheral tissues, so much of an oral 5-HTP dose is converted to serotonin outside the central nervous system, which produces the characteristic nausea and is why clinical protocols historically paired 5-HTP with a peripheral decarboxylase inhibitor such as carbidopa. Combined with SSRIs, SNRIs, MAOIs, tramadol, triptans or linezolid, 5-HTP carries a genuine serotonin syndrome risk that no amount of dietary tryptophan does. There is also a contamination history worth naming: the 1989 eosinophilia-myalgia syndrome epidemic was traced to a contaminant in bacterially produced L-tryptophan, and a related peak X impurity has been detected in some 5-HTP preparations, so purity of a purified plant extract or fermentation product is not a given. Note also that commercial 98% 5-HTP claims are computed on identified compounds rather than on seed weight, and one commercial extract labelled 95% assayed at about 65%. Griffonia seed should not be eaten as a food substitute; the whole seed carries other constituents and is not characterised for dietary safety. Nothing about the tryptophan-rich foods above raises comparable concerns; they are food, and the warm-milk folklore is folklore. Separately, the food table here is narrower than it should be: it contains no legume, no plant-protein concentrate and no cheese richer than cheddar, and dried spirulina, soybeans and soy protein isolate, Parmesan, sesame seeds, turkey, tuna, oats and peanuts are all absent. No figures for them could be verified in this pass, so they are named rather than tabulated, but their absence makes the requirement look harder to meet than it is.
- **Absorption:** The whole point of 5-HTP is that its absorption route differs from its precursor's. 5-HTP is absorbed from the small intestine and crosses the blood-brain barrier without competing significantly for the LAT1 large-neutral-amino-acid carrier, so plasma and brain 5-HTP rise roughly in proportion to dose, unlike tryptophan whose brain entry depends on the tryptophan-to-LNAA ratio rather than on absolute intake. Dietary tryptophan absorption from protein foods is efficient at the gut level, over 90%, and the losses that matter are metabolic rather than absorptive: hepatic TDO is induced by cortisol and by tryptophan itself, and IDO is induced by inflammation, so illness, stress and high-protein intake all divert precursor toward kynurenine. This is why the classic dietary manoeuvre for raising brain tryptophan is a carbohydrate load with little protein, using an insulin-driven fall in circulating branched-chain amino acids to improve the ratio, an effect measured in single-digit percentage changes and nothing like a 100 mg 5-HTP capsule.
- **Data note:** for what remains. All six USDA tryptophan values were confirmed against SR Legacy with no errors, all six per-portion and percent-of-requirement figures recompute correctly against the verified WHO/FAO/UNU denominator of 280 mg/day, and the Griffonia 156 mg/g figure and the 0.64 g per 100 mg calculation were verified against full text. Two Griffonia figures that could not be found in their cited source have been withdrawn rather than restated, and the cheddar value carries the verifier's caveat that the USDA number is probably an imputation artefact

*References: Cochrane Shaw PMID 11687048*

### Vitamin K2 (MK-7 and MK-4)

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** MK-7 180-720 µg/d in trials. MK-4 45 mg/d in the Japanese fracture trial, roughly 250 times the MK-7 dose.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody with solid evidence of clinical benefit.

**Pros**

- The carboxylation mechanism is real: MK-7 does carboxylate osteocalcin and matrix Gla protein, and the biomarkers move.
- Knapen 2013 (n=244 healthy postmenopausal women, 180 µg/d for 3 years) significantly slowed the age-related decline in bone mineral content and density at lumbar spine and femoral neck. Surrogate endpoint, and not at the total hip.
- No adverse effect level has been identified, so no upper limit exists.

**Cons**

- The core sales pitch, that D3 without K2 calcifies your arteries, is a marketing construct. No randomised trial has ever shown that vitamin D supplementation causes vascular calcification which K2 then prevents.
- The calcification claim has been tested and failed twice. AVADEC: 720 µg MK-7 plus vitamin D for 24 months in 365 men with aortic valve calcification, progression 275 versus 292 AU, no difference. VitaK-CAC: 360 µg MK-7 for 2 years in 167 coronary patients, coronary calcium rose from 135 to 184 AU on MK-7 anyway.
- MK-4 is worse. The impressive vertebral-fracture odds ratios in K2 marketing trace to small Japanese menatetrenone trials, and when the hypothesis was tested properly the OF study (n=4,378, 45 mg/d, 36-48 months) was NULL on new vertebral fractures. One frequently cited supporting paper has been RETRACTED for research misconduct.
- MK-4 has essentially no oral bioavailability at supplement doses. A single 420 µg dose of MK-4 was not detectable in the serum of any subject at any time point, while the same dose of MK-7 peaked at 6 hours and stayed detectable for 48 hours.
- Absolute practical contraindication with warfarin and other vitamin K antagonists. MK-7 has a long half-life, so even modest doses destabilise INR for days to weeks.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** RICH (MK-7) / TRACE (MK-4)
- **Dose from food:** Dose partly reachable
- **Data confidence:** Low

The two menaquinones need separate answers, because one merged label buries the whole point. MK-7 is genuinely rich in food, at 939 µg/100 g in natto, and that richness rests on exactly one food that most of the target population never eats; every other measured MK-7 source is trace, with cheddar at 2.7 and butter at 4.2 µg/100 g. MK-4 is trace in food across the board, at roughly 1 to 22 µg/100 g in chicken, egg yolk, full-fat dairy and meat, with goose liver paste as the one outlier at about 369 µg/100 g, while the 45 mg clinical dose is synthetic-only, since MK-4 supplements are manufactured from menadione because no food is or could be concentrated to 45 mg per dose.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Natto (fermented soybeans), MK-7 | 939 µg MK-7 | 1 standard pack (50 g) | 470 µg MK-7 | 261% of a 180 µg MK-7 capsule; 522% of a 90 µg capsule | Kamao M et al., J Nutr Sci Vitaminol 2007;53(6):464-70, PMID 18202532, DOI 10.3177/jnsv.53.464. Confirmed exactly against the PubMed record, including journal, year, volume and pages: 'MK-7 was observed characteristically in fermented soybean products such as natto (939 microg/100 g)' |
| Goose liver paste, MK-4 | about 369 µg MK-4 | 1 serving on toast (40 g) | 148 µg MK-4 | 0.33% of the 45 mg MK-4 dose; 9.8% of a 1.5 mg low-dose MK-4 pill | Schurgers and Vermeer food table value, widely cited; indicative and unconfirmed, because the paper (Haemostasis 2000;30:298-307, PMID 11356998) was confirmed to exist but its abstract does not carry the 369 figure. Added because it is the richest MK-4 food in the composition literature, about 17x barbecue chicken, and its omission produced a false statement in the original entry |

Citation confirmed exactly; the value is indicative and unconfirmed, because the abstract carries no table values and the full text is paywalled |
| Chicken liver, pan-fried, MK-4 | 12.6 µg MK-4 pan-fried (14.1 µg raw) | 1 serving, pan-fried (100 g) | 12.6 µg MK-4 | 0.03% of the 45 mg MK-4 dose; 0.84% of a 1.5 mg pill | Elder et al. 2006, PMID 16417305, Table 1; indicative and unconfirmed, table not retrievable. The cooked figure is used for the cooked portion so the moisture basis matches |
| Ground beef, medium fat, MK-4 | 8.1 µg MK-4 raw (5.1 to 7.2 µg broiled) | 1 burger (100 g) | 8.1 µg MK-4 on the raw basis; 5.1 to 7.2 µg as eaten | 0.018% of the 45 mg MK-4 dose on the raw basis; 0.011% to 0.016% as eaten | Elder et al. 2006, PMID 16417305, Table 1; indicative and unconfirmed. The per-portion figure now shows the broiled range alongside the raw value, since a burger is eaten cooked and the raw basis overstates what a cooked portion delivers |

| Egg yolk, fresh, raw, MK-4 | 15.5 µg MK-4 | 1 large yolk (17 g) | 2.6 µg MK-4 | 0.006% of the 45 mg MK-4 dose; 0.18% of a 1.5 mg pill | Elder et al. 2006, PMID 16417305, Table 2; indicative and unconfirmed, table not retrievable |

The ng/g to µg/100 g conversions are exact |
| Cheddar cheese, MK-7 | 2.7 µg MK-7 (26.78 ng/g) | 1 matchbox piece (30 g) | 0.8 µg MK-7 | 0.4% of a 180 µg MK-7 capsule | Natural and Engineering Sciences 2022, DOI 10.28978/nesciences.1036844, Table 2; indicative and unconfirmed, DOI not resolvable and the paper's calibration is doubtful, as above |
| Salmon, raw, Alaska wild, MK-4 | 0.3 µg MK-4 | 1 fillet (130 g) | 0.4 µg MK-4 | under 0.001% of the 45 mg MK-4 dose | Elder et al. 2006, PMID 16417305, Table 1; indicative and unconfirmed. Kept because fish is often wrongly listed as a K2 source and this row is the rebuttal |

- **To match the supplement dose:** MK-7 and MK-4 give opposite answers. MK-7 at supplement and trial doses of 90 to 180 µg/day is fully covered by food: one 50 g pack of natto carries 470 µg, so a pack every second or third day matches a 180 µg capsule. Nothing else comes close, since 180 µg/day from cheddar at 2.7 µg MK-7 per 100 g would take 6.7 kg of cheese daily and from butter about 4.3 kg. MK-4 is the reverse. The Japanese clinical dose, menatetrenone for osteoporosis, is 45 mg/day (45,000 µg), usually 15 mg three times daily. At the richest food value in the composition literature, goose liver paste at about 369 µg/100 g, that is 12.2 kg per day, and at barbecue chicken's 22.1 µg/100 g it is about 204 kg. Even a modest 1.5 mg consumer MK-4 pill needs 407 g of goose liver paste, 6.8 kg of chicken, or 150 kg of whole milk. Measured whole-diet MK-4 intake sits around 17 µg/day (7.3% of a mean total vitamin K intake of about 230 µg/day in Japanese women, Kamao 2007), roughly one 2,600th of the pharmacological dose. MK-4 at supplement strength is unreachable by eating.
- **Food versus supplement:** MK-7 supplements are made by Bacillus subtilis natto fermentation, the same organism that makes natto, or synthesised; the active species is all-trans MK-7, and cis-isomer contamination is a documented quality problem in commercial products that the fermented food does not have. MK-4 supplements are wholly synthetic, derived from menadione, because no food is or could be concentrated to 45 mg per dose. That said, one framing in the original entry was wrong and has to go: goose liver paste at about 369 µg/100 g puts 100 g of food within roughly 2.1 orders of magnitude of the 45 mg dose (1/122 of it), so the claim that no food comes within three orders of magnitude fails, even though the dose stays unreachable at 12.2 kg/day. Natto also brings the fermentation matrix, fibre and nattokinase, and circulating K2 after eating natto is around ten times circulating K1 after eating spinach, a comparison made with 200 g of natto against 400 g of spinach rather than gram for gram (Schurgers LJ, Vermeer C, Haemostasis 2000;30:298-307, PMID 11356998). The 'cheese is a K2 food' claim needs unpicking: cheese total vitamin K is genuinely high, at 282 ± 5.0 µg/100 g for hard cheese, 289 ± 38 for semi-soft, 440 ± 41 for blue and 506 ± 63 for soft, but roughly 90% of it is MK-9, MK-10 and MK-11 from starter bacteria (Fu et al. 2017, PMID 29955705), forms no clinical trial has tested, while the MK-7 fraction is a few µg/100 g. Cheddar is the weak exemplar for that argument, and the long-ripened Dutch and Norwegian cheeses (Gouda, Edam, Jarlsberg) are the canonical MK-9-rich ones that would test it properly; curd cheese and quark appear as K2 sources in the Schurgers and Vermeer paper itself, and tempeh, fermented soy pastes, Korean cheonggukjang, sauerkraut and fermented salami would show whether natto is uniquely high or simply the best measured. None of those could be tabulated for want of a figure. Animal MK-4 also tracks the animal's feed, since menadione is a routine poultry feed additive and Elder et al. attribute poultry MK-4 to alkylation of it, so brand, country and production system shift the numbers, and the chicken hot dog and chicken broth samples in the same study contained no detectable MK-4 at all.
- **Absorption:** MK-4 and MK-7 behave so differently that microgram-for-microgram comparison is meaningless. A single 420 µg dose of MK-7 was well absorbed, peaked at 6 hours and remained detectable in serum for 48 hours, while the identical 420 µg dose of MK-4 was undetectable in the serum of all subjects at every time point, and seven consecutive days at 60 µg/day raised only MK-7 (Sato T et al., Nutr J 2012;11:93, PMID 23140417, DOI 10.1186/1475-2891-11-93, every element confirmed). MK-7's long half-life comes from its extended isoprenoid side chain and tight lipoprotein binding. That is the mechanistic reason MK-4 is dosed in milligrams while MK-7 works in micrograms, and it means the 1 to 22 µg/100 g of MK-4 in butter, egg yolk, cheese and chicken contributes almost nothing to circulating vitamin K2 even though it is real and measurable. All menaquinones need dietary fat for absorption, which is why they sit in and are absorbed from the fat phase of dairy and eggs, and the reduced-fat versions lose most of it, with reduced-fat cheddar at 1.8 µg MK-4 per 100 g against 9.5 µg for full fat (PMID 29955705, whose fat-dependence finding was confirmed).
- **Data note:** The literature layer is strong and fully verified: Kamao 2007 (natto 939 µg/100 g, 230 µg/day total K, MK-4 at 7.3%), Fu 2017 (cheese totals and fat dependence), Sato 2012 (420 µg MK-4 undetectable) and Schurgers 2000 all confirmed exactly against PubMed, so the MK-7 side of the item rests on a confirmed number. The food-composition layer does not. Six MK-4 food values come from Elder et al. Table 1 and 2, which is a confirmed citation with paywalled tables that could not be opened, the butter and cheddar MK-7 values come from a paper whose DOI does not resolve and whose calibration the submission itself questions at about 27x the US values, and the goose liver paste figure that now anchors the MK-4 dose gap is a widely cited table value that could not be confirmed from its source

*References: AVADEC Diederichsen PMID 35465686; VitaK-CAC JAMA Cardiol 2026; Knapen PMID 23525894; OF study Inoue PMID 19082528; Sato PK PMID 23140417; retracted PMID 21778673*

### Generic multi-strain probiotic blends

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** Marketed as 50 billion CFU and similar. The dose threshold that matters is at least 5 billion CFU/d, but only for the two specific indications.
- **Who it is for:** See the strain-specific entry in the Consider tier. A blend with no strain designators has nothing behind it.

**Pros**

- If a blend happens to contain a strain with evidence at an adequate dose, it will work for that indication. The problem is you cannot tell from the label.

**Cons**

- The category claim is unfalsifiable marketing. Probiotic describes a delivery concept, not an active ingredient. NCCIH states the point plainly: if one Lactobacillus helps prevent an illness, that does not necessarily mean another kind of Lactobacillus will.
- Disaggregate 82 randomised trials and 10,332 irritable bowel syndrome patients by strain, and GRADE certainty collapses to low or very low almost everywhere. Only 24 of the 82 trials were at low risk of bias.
- Raw CFU counts are close to meaningless divorced from strain. The infant colic strain works at 100 million CFU/d, which is 100 to 1,000 times LOWER than the counts marketed on adult tubs.
- The same contraindications apply as for any live organism: central venous catheter, neutropenia, severe immunocompromise, critical illness, preterm infants.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Medium

RICH applies to one endpoint only, and it is a redefined one: live lactic acid bacteria of common genera. On that endpoint several ordinary fermented foods match or exceed supplement label doses, with yoghurt surveyed at up to 10^9 CFU/g, kefir at 2.5x10^8 CFU/mL, unpasteurised sauerkraut at about 2x10^8 CFU/g, kimchi at 10^7.14 to 10^9.23 CFU/g and natto reported at roughly 10^8-10^9 CFU/g. On the endpoint that matters for a product claim, a defined blend of characterised strains at a stated dose, no food supplies it and the honest grade would be a flat no. A reader scanning this field should not read RICH as 'food replaces this product'. The named-strain entry in this same group states that a CFU comparison is meaningless for a strain-specific claim, and that remains true here.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Kimchi, cabbage, retail | 7.14-9.23 log CFU/g lactic acid bacteria | 1 cup (150 g) | 2x10^9 to 2.5x10^11 CFU | from roughly equal to 250x a typical 1-10 billion CFU/day label dose | Rezac S, Kok CR, Heermann M, Hutkins R. Front Microbiol 2018;9:1785, PMID 30197628, DOI 10.3389/fmicb.2018.01785, Table 5 (South Korean retail), verified; portion weight USDA FDC ID 170392. |
| Natto (fermented soybean, unpasteurised) | roughly 10^8-10^9 CFU/g Bacillus subtilis var. natto | 1 cup (175 g) | roughly 1.8x10^10 to 1.8x10^11 CFU | roughly 2-175x a typical 1-10 billion CFU/day label dose | Viable count range as supplied in the verification report from the natto literature; no specific study was retrieved in this pass, so the figure is indicative and unconfirmed. Portion weight USDA FDC ID 172443. The submission gave no figure for natto at all, which was an honest gap rather than an error. |
| Kefir, milk, fermented with milk kefir grains | 8.40 log CFU/mL lactic acid bacteria (2.5x10^8/mL) plus 8.00 log CFU/mL yeasts at 48 h | 1 cup (244 mL) | about 6x10^10 lactic acid bacteria plus about 2x10^10 yeasts | 6-60x a typical 1-10 billion CFU/day label dose | Outeirino EB et al., Foods 2026;15(10):1616, DOI 10.3390/foods15101616, PMID 42195820, verified (counts at 48 h, down from 8.64 and 6.32 log at 24 h); portion weight USDA FDC ID 171265. |
| Sauerkraut, unpasteurised (raw, refrigerated) | 8.3 log CFU/g lactic acid bacteria (commercial, starter culture); 7 log CFU/g in spontaneous fermentation | 1 cup (142 g) | about 3x10^10 CFU at 8.3 log | 3-30x a typical 1-10 billion CFU/day label dose | Rezac S et al., PMID 30197628, Table 5, verified; portion weight USDA FDC ID 169279. That USDA entry is canned sauerkraut, which is pasteurised and contains no live cells. |
| Yoghurt, plain, with live cultures | below 10^4 to 10^9 CFU/g across surveyed products; commonly around 10^8 CFU/g | 1 cup (245 g) | roughly 2x10^10 CFU at 10^8 CFU/g | 2-20x a typical 1-10 billion CFU/day label dose | Rezac S et al., PMID 30197628, DOI 10.3389/fmicb.2018.01785, range verified; portion weight USDA FDC ID 171284. |
| Tempeh | 7.01 log CFU/g lactic acid bacteria in laboratory-made product; 81% of shop, production and restaurant samples above 10^7 CFU/g | 1 serving (100 g) | about 1x10^9 CFU | roughly 0.1-1x a typical 1-10 billion CFU/day label dose | Rezac S et al., PMID 30197628, Table 6, verified. The submission's 3-9 log range could not be found in the cited review, so the 10^11 per-portion ceiling and the 0.1-100x span have been withdrawn and 10^7 CFU/g is used as the demonstrated floor for most samples. |
| Miso | 2.1-7.1 log CFU/g aerobic bacteria (supermarket samples) | 1 tbsp (17 g) | up to about 2x10^8 CFU, and as low as 10^3 | at best about 2-20% of a 1-10 billion CFU/day label dose | Rezac S et al., PMID 30197628, DOI 10.3389/fmicb.2018.01785, range verified; portion weight USDA FDC ID 172442. |

- **To match the supplement dose:** For total live cells there is no gap. The source review calculates that about 10^10 cells is the intake likely needed to affect the microbiota and that this is met by 100 g of a food carrying 10^8 cells/g (PMID 30197628). One cup of kefir, one cup of yoghurt, about 120 g of raw sauerkraut, a cup of kimchi or a cup of natto each clear that bar, and kimchi at the top of its measured range delivers more live organisms in one serving than most 50-billion-CFU capsules. The gap is qualitative: identity, characterisation and consistency. Fermented food counts vary across four to six orders of magnitude between products and batches (yoghurt below 10^4 to 10^9 CFU/g), the organisms are usually not identified to strain, and none of them are the strains on the label of the blend.
- **Food versus supplement:** Fermented food is a reasonable dietary habit and a different proposition from a named strain at a defined dose. Even a generic blend tells you which species you are getting and roughly how many; a jar of sauerkraut tells you neither, and a pasteurised jar contains no live cells. The practical points that decide whether a food delivers anything: it must be labelled raw, unpasteurised or 'live cultures' and kept refrigerated; heat destroys the organisms, so miso added to boiling soup, cooked kimchi and baked tempeh contribute essentially nothing; and long-aged cheeses can be several orders of magnitude lower than young ones. Water kefir, kombucha, brine-fermented unpasteurised pickles, kvass, skyr, traditional buttermilk and young raw-milk cheeses belong on the same list, though no confirmed counts for them were available here. Conversely, a supplement claim of 50 billion CFU means little if the strains are unnamed, because probiotic effects are demonstrated strain by strain, and CFU counts on labels refer to manufacture rather than end of shelf life.
- **Absorption:** Gastric and bile survival is strain-dependent and matrix-dependent. Fermented dairy foods buffer stomach acid and their fat content improves transit survival, a genuine advantage of the food route over an uncoated capsule; supplements compensate with enteric coating or microencapsulation. Almost none of these organisms colonise permanently, so the effect is dose-dependent and ceases when intake stops, which applies equally to food and to capsules. The source review notes that many fermentation microbes are closely related to probiotic organisms and can share mechanisms, which is the honest basis for recommending fermented food, but relatedness is not equivalence and it does not license transferring a strain-specific trial result onto a jar of kimchi.
- **Data note:** The Rezac counts for kimchi, sauerkraut, yoghurt, miso and tempeh were verified against Tables 5 and 6, as were the kefir counts and the 10^10-cells threshold. Two soft spots remain: the tempeh 9 log ceiling could not be sourced and has been removed, and the natto count is a literature range supplied by the verification report without a retrievable citation

*References: Goodoory & Ford PMID 37541528; NCCIH; Cochrane CD006095.pub5 PMID 40931979*

### B. infantis 35624 (single strain)

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** 1 billion CFU/d, one capsule, assessed at 4 weeks. This is the trialled dose, not a dose shown to work.
- **Who it is for:** On current evidence, no clearly identifiable group.

**Pros**

- Composite probiotic products containing B. infantis showed small effects on abdominal pain (SMD 0.22) and bloating (SMD 0.30). Those effects belong to the composite, not to this strain.

**Cons**

- This is the clearest case of the strain-specificity argument cutting AGAINST the industry. It is the product that taught consumers to look for a strain number, and the dedicated meta-analysis of 5 studies found the SINGLE strain had no effect on abdominal pain, bloating or bowel habit satisfaction.
- The whole reputation rests on one 2006 trial plus an anti-inflammatory mechanism story about the IL-10 to IL-12 ratio.
- The AGA recommends against probiotics for irritable bowel syndrome outside clinical trials, which is this product primary market.
- Bifidobacterium species specifically have caused whole-genome-sequencing-confirmed bacteraemia in preterm infants.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** No food source, manufactured
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** High

SYNTHETIC here means no dietary source, not an artificial molecule. The strain is a live organism isolated from the ileal mucosa of a healthy human donor and has since been reclassified from Bifidobacterium longum subsp. infantis to B. longum subsp. longum 35624 (PLOS One 2016;11:e0162983, PMC5033381). It is propagated industrially from a proprietary strain bank and freeze-dried. It is present in no food, no fermented food and no other supplement brand. Comparative genome analysis identified a 35624-specific gene cluster encoding biosynthesis of its exopolysaccharide EPS624, the kind of strain-level distinction that makes substitution unsound.

*No food rows: there is no ordinary food that supplies this in a meaningful amount.*

- **To match the supplement dose:** No quantity of any food supplies this strain. The trial dose is small in absolute terms, 1x10^8 CFU, which is roughly the number of live bacteria in half a gram of unpasteurised sauerkraut at 8.3 log CFU/g, so the dose is not the obstacle and the identity is. Fermented foods can supply a thousand times more live cells than the effective dose of 35624 while supplying none of it. Note that the source trial expressed its doses as CFU/mL rather than CFU/day.
- **Food versus supplement:** The dose-response data make the point better than any argument about food. In the 362-patient dose-ranging trial, 1x10^8 of B. infantis 35624 was significantly superior to placebo for abdominal pain, the composite score, bloating, bowel dysfunction and incomplete evacuation, while 1x10^6 and 1x10^10 were not, the paper attributing the highest dose's failure to significant formulation problems (Whorwell et al., Am J Gastroenterol 2006;101:1581-1590, PMID 16863564). The trial enrolled women only, which limits generalisation and which the submission did not state. If a tenfold change in dose of the same strain in the same capsule abolishes the effect, swapping the strain for a different Bifidobacterium in a yoghurt cannot be assumed to preserve it. Anyone advised to take 35624 should be told plainly that no diet reproduces it and that a generic bifidobacterium supplement is a different product.
- **Absorption:** The strain was selected partly for surviving gastrointestinal transit, and its documented actions are immunoregulatory and barrier-related rather than nutritional: enhanced gut barrier function, accelerated intestinal transit and reduced visceral hypersensitivity. It does not permanently colonise, so the effect depends on continued daily intake. Formulation dominates delivery: the failure of the 1x10^10 arm was a formulation problem rather than evidence against high cell numbers, and it illustrates that CFU on a label is not the active variable on its own. The specific gelling mechanism the submission gave for that failure is not in the source and has been removed.
- **Data note:** The strain's origin, reclassification, exopolysaccharide gene cluster and the dose-ranging trial results were all confirmed. The only failed detail was the gelling mechanism, now replaced with the paper's own wording, and the only additions are the women-only enrolment and the CFU/mL unit

*References: Yuan PMID 28166427; Goodoory & Ford PMID 37541528; AGA PMID 32531291*

### Krill oil

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** 2 g/d for 24 weeks in the knee osteoarthritis trial. Bioavailability trials used about 1.1 g/d of actual omega-3.
- **Who it is for:** At most, people wanting a given rise in omega-3 index from a smaller dose. That is a biomarker advantage.

**Pros**

- The absorption argument has support. A 72-person trial at matched 1.1 g/d found krill oil produced about 1.5-fold higher increments in plasma EPA and DHA than fish oil.
- Contains astaxanthin, which is why it does not oxidise as readily as bulk fish oil.

**Cons**

- The joint claim collapsed under proper testing. The largest randomised trial (JAMA, n=262 with knee osteoarthritis and effusion-synovitis, 2 g/d for 24 weeks) found mean pain change -19.9 with krill oil versus -20.2 with placebo. A trial-sequential-analysis review across 5 trials and 700 patients found no significant improvement in knee pain, stiffness or lipid profile.
- It is priced as a premium product on the strength of a biomarker advantage, and delivers far less EPA and DHA per capsule than a concentrate.
- Crustacean and shellfish allergy is a genuine and under-warned contraindication.
- Same anticoagulant, antiplatelet and atrial fibrillation cautions as any omega-3 source once dose rises.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Medium

The molecules krill oil delivers, EPA and DHA, are abundant in ordinary oily fish, and a 1-2 g/day krill oil dose is smaller than what a single tin of sardines provides. The phospholipid delivery form is also obtainable from food, which the earlier draft got wrong: fish roe and caviar are membrane-lipid tissues in which a large share of the EPA and DHA is phospholipid-bound, and in lean white fish such as cod most of the (small) EPA+DHA pool sits in phospholipid rather than triglyceride. The triglyceride generalisation holds for fatty fish flesh only. One tablespoon of caviar (16 g, 1,046 mg EPA+DHA) beats a 2 g krill oil dose on both quantity and form. Krill itself is close to a non-food: Antarctic krill (Euphausia superba) is eaten as okiami in Japan and to a small extent in Russia and the Philippines, but the global catch is largely rendered into meal and oil, and the shell carries a heavy fluoride load that has to be stripped before human consumption. Nobody reaches a krill oil dose by eating krill; they reach it by eating fish or roe, or by taking capsules.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Herring, Atlantic, cooked, dry heat | EPA 909 mg, DHA 1,100 mg (total 2,009 mg) | 1 fillet (143 g) | EPA 1,300 mg, DHA 1,573 mg (total 2,873 mg) | 410-1,026% of the EPA+DHA ceiling for a typical 2 g/day krill oil dose (at most 280-700 mg) | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175117 (SR Legacy), confirmed |
| Mackerel, Atlantic, cooked, dry heat | EPA 504 mg, DHA 699 mg (total 1,203 mg) | 1 fillet (88 g) | EPA 444 mg, DHA 615 mg (total 1,059 mg) | 151-378% of a 2 g/day krill oil dose ceiling | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175120 (SR Legacy), confirmed |
| Caviar, black and red, granular | EPA 2,740 mg, DHA 3,800 mg (total 6,540 mg) | 1 tablespoon (16 g) | EPA 438 mg, DHA 608 mg (total 1,046 mg) | 149-374% of a 2 g/day krill oil dose ceiling, and it delivers a large share of that as phospholipid, which is the product claim krill oil is sold on | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 174188 (SR Legacy), composition supplied and confirmed by the verification pass |
| Anchovy, European, canned in oil, drained solids | EPA 763 mg, DHA 1,290 mg (total 2,053 mg) | 1 tin, drained (45 g) | EPA 343 mg, DHA 580 mg (total 924 mg) | 132-330% of a 2 g/day krill oil dose ceiling | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 174183 (SR Legacy), confirmed |
| Sardine, Atlantic, canned in oil, drained solids with bone | EPA 473 mg, DHA 509 mg (total 982 mg) | 1 tin, drained (92 g) | EPA 435 mg, DHA 468 mg (total 903 mg) | 129-323% of a 2 g/day krill oil dose ceiling | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175139 (SR Legacy), confirmed; krill oil EPA+DHA range from PMID 39233519 |
| Fish roe, mixed species, raw | EPA 983 mg, DHA 1,360 mg (total 2,343 mg) | 1 tablespoon (14 g) | EPA 138 mg, DHA 190 mg (total 328 mg) | 47-117% of a 2 g/day krill oil dose ceiling per tablespoon; a 100 g portion of cod or herring roe supplies 2,343 mg, and again a large share of it is phospholipid-bound | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175132 (SR Legacy), composition and 14 g tablespoon supplied and confirmed by the verification pass |

- **To match the supplement dose:** There is effectively no gap, which is the useful finding. Measured EPA plus DHA in 20 commercial krill oils from the Korean market spanned 14.2-34.8% of total fatty acids (Lee 2024, PMID 39233519, DOI 10.4014/jmb.2406.06026, range confirmed; the sampling is one national market). Converting that percentage to capsule mass overstates the content, because total fatty acids are less than capsule mass once phospholipid head groups, free sterols, astaxanthin and excipients are counted, so every figure that follows is a ceiling rather than a point estimate. A 1 g capsule carries at most roughly 140-350 mg EPA+DHA. Two capsules (2 g) deliver at most about 280-700 mg, which is matched or beaten by one 92 g tin of sardines (903 mg), one 45 g tin of anchovies (924 mg), one mackerel fillet (1,059 mg) or one tablespoon of caviar (1,046 mg). At the top of the trial range, 4 g/day of krill oil supplies at most roughly 570-1,400 mg, equal to at most 0.6-1.5 tins of sardines. Because these are ceilings, the real gap runs further in favour of fish than the raw numbers suggest. Going the other way, matching a krill oil dose by eating krill is not a real option: the catch is not sold as food outside a small Japanese and Russian market, and the shell must be removed for fluoride reasons.
- **Food versus supplement:** Two things were previously claimed to separate krill oil from food, and both claims are weaker than stated. The phospholipid form is the product claim, and fish roe and caviar supply phospholipid-bound EPA and DHA in ordinary food, so the honest version is that krill oil offers a high phospholipid share conveniently, not uniquely. Krill oil is reported at roughly 39-81% phospholipid (Xie 2019, DOI 10.1111/1541-4337.12427); the citation is real and topically correct, but the range could not be traced to a page or table in the review, so treat it as indicative and unconfirmed, and do not read the original two-decimal precision as measured. Astaxanthin is the second claim, and 'no oily fish portion supplies it in comparable concentration' should not stand: wild sockeye and other red-fleshed Pacific salmon carry astaxanthin in flesh at roughly 25-40 mg/kg, so a 130 g portion supplies about 3-5 mg against the 0.1-1.5 mg per gram of oil typical of krill capsules. That concentration could not be re-verified, so it is a challenge to the claim rather than a settled figure, but the flat denial is gone. Neither the phospholipid form nor the astaxanthin has been shown to produce a clinical outcome that fish or fish oil does not.
