Against that, krill oil is the most expensive way to buy EPA and DHA per milligram, capsule doses are small enough that many products deliver less omega-3 than one tin of fish, it is a crustacean allergen, and whole krill or poorly processed krill meal carries the fluoride load of the exoskeleton. The fluoride figures usually quoted are about 4,260 mg F/kg fat-free dry matter in the carapace against 570 mg/kg in raw muscle; neither full text was retrievable, so both values are unconfirmed and should be attributed to whichever paper actually reports them (Soevik & Braekkan, Journal of the Fisheries Research Board of Canada 1979;36(11):1414-1416, DOI 10.1139/f79-204, which was not yet called the Canadian Journal of Fisheries and Aquatic Sciences in 1979; and Tou 2007, PMID 17345959). Sustainability is a genuine consideration given that Antarctic krill is the base of the Southern Ocean food web.
- **Absorption:** The phospholipid form does appear to be absorbed at least as well as triglyceride and better than ethyl ester. In a crossover trial in 12 healthy men at a matched 1,680 mg EPA+DHA dose, incorporation into plasma phospholipids by area under the curve over 0-72 h was 80.03 +/- 34.71 %*h for krill oil, 59.78 +/- 36.75 %*h for re-esterified triglyceride fish oil and 47.53 +/- 38.42 %*h for ethyl ester fish oil, with no statistically significant difference (EPA p = 0.057, DHA p = 0.246, EPA+DHA p = 0.119) (Schuchardt 2011, Lipids Health Dis, PMC3168413, all figures confirmed). Attributing that result to the phospholipid form is not the only reading: the same paper notes that 22% of the krill oil's EPA and 21% of its DHA were present as free fatty acids, which absorb readily and offer a plausible alternative explanation. The practical implication is modest either way, since a krill capsule contains so much less EPA and DHA than a portion of fish that absorption efficiency does not close the gap.
- **Data note:** The comparator fish and roe composition is all traced to USDA records and the two human trials (Lee 2024, Schuchardt 2011) checked out exactly. The krill-specific claims are the weak part: the phospholipid percentage range, both fluoride values and the sockeye astaxanthin concentration are all unverified, and two of the three factual claims that justified the original classification turned out to be false

*References: Laslett JAMA 2024 PMID 38776073; Pimentel PMID 39126570; Loukil PMID 42144109*

### Cod liver oil

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** No established effective dose for any endpoint. The binding constraint is retinol, not omega-3.
- **Who it is for:** Arguably nobody, given modern alternatives.

**Pros**

- Historically it was an effective vitamin D and vitamin A vehicle in populations with little sun and poor diets, and that history is real.
- Natural triglyceride oils absorb well.

**Cons**

- You cannot increase the omega-3 dose without simultaneously increasing preformed vitamin A, which has a real toxicity ceiling of 3,000 µg/d and is teratogenic. That is a dose-limiting design flaw, not a feature.
- As an omega-3 source it is dilute, so reaching a therapeutic dose requires a large volume of oil and therefore a large retinol load.
- Standard obstetric advice in several countries is to avoid liver and liver-oil supplements in pregnancy.
- Typically sold as a bulk, often flavoured liquid, which is the format that performed worst in oxidation surveys. Fermented cod liver oil has no quality advantage.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** High

Everything cod liver oil supplies is available from ordinary food: EPA and DHA from oily fish and roe, vitamin D from oily fish, vitamin A from any liver. Cod liver oil is itself a traditional food-grade oil with its own USDA composition entry, which is why it appears in the list below, and it is included as the reference product rather than as an alternative to itself. What makes it distinctive is concentration: one teaspoon (4.5 g) carries 806 mg EPA+DHA, 1,350 ug RAE of preformed vitamin A (4,500 IU) and 11.2 ug of vitamin D (450 IU). Two genuine food alternatives are named without figures because no authoritative per-portion composition value could be obtained in this pass: tinned cod liver, eaten in Nordic, Baltic and Russian cuisine, and liver as a vitamin A source. Rather than estimate them, no row is given.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Herring, Atlantic, cooked, dry heat | EPA 909 mg, DHA 1,100 mg (total 2,009 mg) | 1 fillet (143 g) | EPA 1,300 mg, DHA 1,573 mg (total 2,873 mg) | Equivalent to 3.6 teaspoons of cod liver oil for EPA+DHA, with none of the vitamin A load | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175117 (SR Legacy), confirmed |
| Caviar, black and red, granular | EPA 2,740 mg, DHA 3,800 mg (total 6,540 mg) | 1 tablespoon (16 g) | EPA 438 mg, DHA 608 mg (total 1,046 mg) | Equivalent to 1.3 teaspoons of cod liver oil for EPA+DHA, again with no preformed vitamin A load | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 174188 (SR Legacy), composition supplied and confirmed by the verification pass |
| Sardine, Atlantic, canned in oil, drained solids with bone | EPA 473 mg, DHA 509 mg (total 982 mg) | 1 tin, drained (92 g) | EPA 435 mg, DHA 468 mg (total 903 mg) | Equivalent to 1.1 teaspoons of cod liver oil for EPA+DHA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175139 (SR Legacy), confirmed |
| Fish oil, cod liver (the reference product itself, taken by spoon as traditionally sold, not a food alternative to it) | EPA 6,900 mg, DHA 11,000 mg (total 17,900 mg); vitamin A 30,000 ug RAE (100,000 IU); vitamin D 250 ug (10,000 IU) | 1 teaspoon (4.5 g); 1 tablespoon = 13.6 g | 1 tsp: EPA 310 mg, DHA 495 mg (total 806 mg), vitamin A 1,350 ug RAE, vitamin D 11.2 ug. 1 tbsp: EPA 938 mg, DHA 1,496 mg (total 2,434 mg), vitamin A 4,080 ug RAE, vitamin D 34 ug | 1 tsp = 322% of one day's 250 mg EPA+DHA AI, 45% of the 3,000 ug/day vitamin A UL and 11% of the 100 ug/day vitamin D UL. 1 tbsp = 136% of the vitamin A UL on its own | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173577 (SR Legacy), every figure including both household measures and both vitamin unit conversions confirmed exact; ULs from EFSA (vitamin A, NDA Panel 2024; vitamin D, NDA Panel 2023), not re-verified in this pass |

- **To match the supplement dose:** For the EPA and DHA at a traditional teaspoon dose there is no gap, and food matches it easily: 806 mg per teaspoon is a little under one 92 g tin of sardines (903 mg) and about a quarter of a herring fillet (2,873 mg). Cod liver oil is also efficient in the other direction: 250-500 mg/day of EPA+DHA needs only 1.4-2.8 g of oil, a third to two thirds of a teaspoon, carrying 419-838 ug RAE of vitamin A, comfortably inside the 3,000 ug/day UL. The gap that opens at pharmacological doses is a toxicity gap rather than a volume one. Reaching 4 g/day of EPA+DHA takes 22.3 g of cod liver oil, about 1.6 tablespoons, delivering 6,704 ug RAE of preformed vitamin A every day, 2.2 times the EFSA adult UL. Traditional cod liver oil therefore cannot be dose-escalated to the level used in triglyceride-lowering trials, and food cannot reach 4 g/day either (see the Omega-3 entry). That is what plain fish oil and purified concentrates exist for.
- **Food versus supplement:** This is the clearest case in this group where the concentrated product is hazardous in a way the food behaviour does not suggest. The vitamin A is preformed retinol, so it accumulates and is teratogenic. Anyone already taking a multivitamin, or pregnant, can exceed the 3,000 ug RAE/day UL on one tablespoon alone (4,080 ug). Doubling up cod liver oil with a separate vitamin D supplement is the other common stacking error. Many modern products labelled cod liver oil are refined with the vitamin A and D partly stripped and re-standardised, so the USDA generic entry above describes traditional oil and not necessarily what is in a given bottle: the label governs. Contaminants concentrate in liver, which is why the EU sets a separate limit for fish liver and derived products (20.0 pg WHO-TEQ/g wet weight) alongside the 1.75 pg/g fat dioxin and 6.0 pg/g fat dioxin plus dioxin-like PCB limits for marine oils under Regulation (EU) 2023/915. Those limit values were not re-verified in this pass. Commercial cod liver oil is refined to meet them; home-rendered or artisanal liver oil is not.
- **Absorption:** Cod liver oil is a natural triglyceride and was one of the natural-triglyceride comparators assigned the 100% reference index in the Dyerberg comparison, against 124% for re-esterified triglyceride and 73% for ethyl ester (PMID 20638827, both comparator values confirmed). Its fat-soluble vitamins come dissolved in the oil, which is the ideal vehicle, and that is precisely why the vitamin A is so efficiently absorbed and why overdose is easy. There is no absorption advantage over eating oily fish for the EPA and DHA.
- **Data note:** The USDA cod liver oil record matched on every value including both household measures and both vitamin unit conversions, and the whole arithmetic block recomputed exactly (806 mg and 2,434 mg per portion, 322% of the AI, 45% and 136% of the vitamin A UL, 1.4-2.8 g for 250-500 mg carrying 419-838 ug RAE, 22.3 g for 4 g/day carrying 6,704 ug RAE). The EFSA vitamin A and D ULs and the EU 2023/915 limits are widely cited but were not confirmed in this pass, and they are load-bearing for the toxicity conclusion

*References: Dyerberg PMID 20638827; Jackowski PMID 26688721; Heller PMID 30626234*

### ALA from flaxseed, chia or walnut oil

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** Adequate intake 1.6 g/d for men and 1.1 g/d for women. No upper limit; the practical constraint is calories from oil.
- **Who it is for:** People who eat no marine products at all and will not take algal oil. It at least maintains essential fatty acid status.

**Pros**

- It is a genuinely essential fatty acid, so adequate intake matters.
- The Cochrane review found a signal for arrhythmia reduction, RR 0.73 (0.55-0.97) at moderate certainty. Note that rests on just 2 trials, 4,912 participants and 173 events.

**Cons**

- The plant-based omega-3, same benefits claim is the core deception. Conversion of ALA to EPA is a few percent and to DHA is well under one percent in adults, and it is further suppressed by high dietary linoleic acid. A flaxseed capsule does not deliver what a fish oil or algal oil capsule delivers.
- Cochrane found increased ALA probably makes little or no difference to all-cause mortality, RR 1.01 (0.84-1.20).
- Anyone relying on it to achieve DHA status is making a mistake, and that includes pregnant women and vegans wanting fetal DHA supply. Algal DHA is the correct product there.
- Note the arrhythmia finding for ALA points the opposite way to the atrial fibrillation excess seen with marine omega-3 (HR 1.25), so the two should not be treated as interchangeable in either direction.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** High

ALA is one of the easiest nutrients in this reference to obtain from food. EFSA sets the Adequate Intake at 0.5% of energy, about 1.1-1.4 g/day for a 2,000-2,500 kcal diet (EFSA Journal 2010;8(3):1461, DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2010.1461; the 0.5%-of-energy conversion checks arithmetically at 10 kcal/9 = 1.11 g and 12.5/9 = 1.39 g). A single tablespoon of ground flaxseed exceeds it. The supplement-shaped foods are not even the main dietary route in the UK and Northern Europe, where rapeseed oil supplies more ALA than flaxseed, chia and walnuts combined, and perilla oil is a higher-ALA culinary oil than flaxseed oil. The real question is not whether you can eat ALA but whether ALA becomes EPA and DHA in the body, and for DHA the answer is largely no.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Oil, perilla | ALA roughly 54-64 g (indicative) | 1 tablespoon (13.6 g) | ALA roughly 7.3-8.7 g | About 660-790% of the 1.1 g/day ALA AI. The highest-ALA edible oil in common culinary use, above flaxseed oil, and a staple in Korean and Japanese cooking | Indicative and unconfirmed. The 54-64% ALA range was supplied by the verification pass without a traceable composition record, so it is not presented as sourced; confirm against an authoritative table before publishing |
| Oil, flaxseed, cold pressed | ALA 53.4 g | 1 tablespoon (13.6 g) | ALA 7.26 g | 660% of the 1.1 g/day ALA AI | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 167702 (SR Legacy), confirmed |
| Seeds, chia seeds, dried | ALA 17.8 g | 1 oz (28.35 g, roughly 2 tablespoons) | ALA 5.05 g | 459% of the 1.1 g/day ALA AI | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170554 (SR Legacy), confirmed |
| Nuts, walnuts, english, raw | ALA 9.08 g | 1 oz (28.35 g, 14 halves) | ALA 2.57 g | 234% of the 1.1 g/day ALA AI | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170187 (SR Legacy), confirmed including the '14 halves' descriptor, which is USDA's own |
| Seeds, hemp seed, hulled | ALA 8.68 g | 1 oz (28.35 g) | ALA 2.46 g | 224% of the 1.1 g/day ALA AI. Comparable to walnuts per 100 g and a better ALA-per-calorie buy than walnut oil | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170148 (SR Legacy), per-100 g value supplied and confirmed by the verification pass; the ounce is a unit conversion, not a USDA household measure |
| Seeds, flaxseed, whole and ground | ALA 22.8 g | 1 tablespoon whole (10.3 g) or 1 tablespoon ground (7.0 g) | Whole: ALA 2.35 g. Ground: ALA 1.60 g | 213% (whole) or 145% (ground) of the 1.1 g/day ALA AI. Use the ground figure for any practical calculation, since whole seeds pass through largely undigested, see bioavailability | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 169414 (SR Legacy), confirmed; both tablespoon weights are USDA's own |
| Oil, walnut | ALA 10.4 g | 1 tablespoon (13.6 g) | ALA 1.41 g | 129% of the 1.1 g/day ALA AI. Walnut oil is a weak ALA source relative to flaxseed oil, about one fifth the concentration (10.4/53.4 = 0.195) | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171030 (SR Legacy), confirmed |
| Oil, rapeseed (canola) | ALA roughly 9 g (indicative) | 1 tablespoon (13.6 g) | ALA roughly 1.2 g | About 111% of the 1.1 g/day ALA AI on its own. Modest per tablespoon, but in UK and Northern European diets it is the dominant dietary ALA source by a wide margin because of how much of it people already use | Indicative and unconfirmed. The verification pass could not resolve an FDC record for rapeseed oil in this session, so the roughly 9 g/100 g figure is not presented as sourced; confirm before publishing |

- **To match the supplement dose:** For ALA itself there is zero gap: one tablespoon of ground flaxseed (1.60 g), one ounce of walnuts (2.57 g), one ounce of hulled hemp seed (2.46 g), one tablespoon of walnut oil (1.41 g) or one tablespoon of rapeseed oil (about 1.2 g) each meet or exceed the 1.1-1.4 g/day AI, and one tablespoon of flaxseed oil supplies 7.26 g. The gap appears only when ALA is sold as a substitute for EPA and DHA, and the two substances need separate answers. For EPA, substitution is plausible at modest intakes: Burdge and Wootton 2002 measured 21% net fractional conversion of labelled ALA to EPA in young women (PMID 12323090, DOI 10.1079/BJN2002689), so a 250 mg/day EPA target needs 1.19 g ALA/day, which is about 0.7 tablespoons of ground flaxseed. For DHA, substitution fails at any realistic intake. The same paper gives 9% conversion to DHA and 6% to DPA in women, while in young men roughly 33% of the dose was oxidised within 24 hours, EPA and DPA were the only meaningful products, and no labelled DHA was detectable in plasma at any point over 21 days (PMID 12323085, DOI 10.1079/bjn2002662). Whole-body estimates are lower still, commonly 0.1-0.5% for DHA. At 0.5% you need 50 g ALA/day, which is 219 g of flaxseed or roughly 100 mL of flaxseed oil daily; at 0.1% you need 250 g ALA/day, over a kilogram of seed. No one eats that. Feeding trials agree, with plasma DHA unchanged after a month of flaxseed in any form (Austria 2008, PMID 18689552). State it plainly: flaxseed, chia and walnut oil can substitute for EPA at modest intakes and cannot supply DHA at any realistic intake. Express any of these requirements in milled seed rather than whole, since whole flaxseed produced no significant plasma ALA rise in that trial.
- **Food versus supplement:** Form determines whether the ALA is absorbed at all, which is unusual among plant supplements. Over one month at matched ALA doses, flaxseed oil and milled flaxseed both raised plasma ALA significantly (oil higher than milled, P=0.004), while whole flaxseed produced no significant rise, and no group raised EPA or DHA (Austria 2008, PMID 18689552, DOI 10.1080/07315724.2008.10719693). Sprinkling whole seeds on food is close to decorative. Practical consequences: grind flaxseed, store it cold and use it quickly, because ALA at 53% of an oil is highly oxidisable and rancid flax oil is a pro-oxidant load rather than a benefit; flaxseed oil supplies ALA with none of the fibre or lignans of the seed; and conversion to EPA is suppressed by high dietary linoleic acid, so an ALA supplement taken alongside a diet heavy in sunflower or corn oil performs worse than the tracer figures suggest. Milled seed was also the best-tolerated form, with the oil and whole seed both causing gastrointestinal discomfort in that trial.
- **Absorption:** Two sequential losses. First, absorption and delivery: oil beats milled seed beats whole seed, with whole seed showing no measurable plasma ALA response (Austria 2008). Second, metabolic conversion, where the same enzyme chain (delta-6 desaturase, elongase, delta-5 desaturase, then peroxisomal beta-oxidation for DHA) is shared with linoleic acid and is the real bottleneck. Conversion rates differ by product and must not be interchanged: EPA 21% and DPA 6% in the tracer study in women, DHA 9% at best in the same study and undetectable in men over 21 days, with whole-body estimates commonly 0.1-0.5% for DHA. Women convert better than men, which is thought to reflect the demands of pregnancy and lactation. A third of an oral ALA dose is simply burned for energy within 24 hours.
- **Data note:** for the five USDA rows, which all resolved to the described food with the stated ALA values and USDA's own household measures, and for every per-portion and percent-of-AI figure, all of which recomputed exactly. MEDIUM overall because two added rows, perilla oil and rapeseed oil, rest on figures the verification pass could not trace and are labelled indicative, and because the hemp seed portion uses an ounce conversion rather than a USDA household measure

*References: Cochrane PMID 32114706; Gencer PMID 34612056; NASEM DRI*

### Algal oil (vegan DHA and EPA)

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** Matched to fish oil equivalents. For pregnancy the tested DHA doses are the same as fish-derived, 200-1,000 mg/d.
- **Who it is for:** Vegans and vegetarians, people avoiding fish contaminants, and people with fish or shellfish allergy.

**Pros**

- It is the correct product for vegans, who cannot obtain preformed EPA and DHA any other way, and for whom ALA conversion is inadequate.
- Statistically non-inferior to fish oil for plasma phospholipid DHA and EPA in a 74-person trial over 6 to 14 weeks.
- Avoids methylmercury, dioxins and PCBs, which is a legitimate reason to choose it in pregnancy.

**Cons**

- There is no clinical outcome trial of algal oil for any endpoint. The only evidence is a 74-person biomarker study, and the credibility it borrows comes from the fish oil outcome trials, which were largely null.
- Non-inferiority on a biomarker is not superiority, and marketing implying algal DHA is cleaner therefore better conflates contaminant avoidance with efficacy.
- Cost per milligram of DHA is usually higher than fish oil.
- Products are DHA-dominant, and DHA raises LDL more than EPA does (+6.9% versus +2.2% in a head-to-head crossover), which matters if LDL is your limiting problem.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Trace amounts only
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Medium

The classification depends entirely on who is eating, so it has to be stated by population. For an omnivore EPA and DHA are RICH in food: one herring fillet supplies 2,873 mg, one tablespoon of caviar 1,046 mg (see the Omega-3 entry). For a strict vegan the answer is TRACE, and that is the population this product exists for: raw laver (nori) has 80 mg EPA per 100 g and zero DHA, which is 2 mg of EPA per standard sheet, and dried spirulina, routinely marketed as a vegan omega-3 food, contains 0.000 g of both EPA and DHA (it does carry 823 mg/100 g of ALA, 58 mg per 7 g tablespoon, which is not the same nutrient). For a lacto-ovo vegetarian the honest answer sits between the two, because eggs supply preformed DHA at 58 mg/100 g, about 29 mg per large 50 g egg, and DHA-enriched eggs several times that. The earlier draft elided vegan and vegetarian and so overstated the case. The production organism is not a food either: Schizochytrium sp. is a single-cell marine microalga cultured in fermenters and authorised in the EU as a novel food.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Egg, whole, raw, fresh | DHA 58 mg | 1 large egg (50 g) | DHA 29 mg | 12% of a 250 mg/day DHA dose per egg; two eggs give 23%. The only meaningful preformed-DHA food available to lacto-ovo vegetarians, and irrelevant to strict vegans. DHA-enriched eggs, sold for exactly this purpose, carry several times more | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171287 (SR Legacy), value supplied and confirmed by the verification pass |
| Seaweed, laver (nori), raw | EPA 80 mg, DHA 0 mg, ALA 1 mg | 1 sheet (2.6 g; 10 sheets = 26 g) | EPA 2 mg, DHA 0 mg | 0.8% of a 250 mg/day algal oil dose for EPA; 0% for DHA | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168458 (SR Legacy), confirmed; the 2.6 g sheet derives correctly from USDA's 10 sheets = 26 g |
| Seaweed, spirulina, dried | EPA 0 mg, DHA 0 mg; ALA 823 mg | 1 tablespoon (7 g) | EPA 0 mg, DHA 0 mg; ALA 58 mg | 0% of an algal oil dose for either EPA or DHA. Included because spirulina and chlorella are frequently sold as vegan omega-3 sources and supply neither. The 58 mg of ALA per tablespoon is real but is a different fatty acid, and it does not convert to DHA in useful amounts | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170495 (SR Legacy), confirmed at zero for both EPA and DHA; the ALA value comes from the same record |

- **To match the supplement dose:** For a strict vegan the gap is unbridgeable for DHA and absurd for EPA. Typical algal oil products sit at 250 mg/day of DHA for general adult use and 450 mg/day for pregnancy and lactation. Matching 250 mg of EPA from nori would take 312 g of raw laver, about 120 sheets a day. Matching any amount of DHA from nori is impossible, since the USDA value is 0.000 g, and spirulina supplies zero of both. No seaweed portion, no fermented food and no oilseed closes this gap, which is why algal oil is one of the few supplements in this reference with a real and unavoidable role. For a lacto-ovo vegetarian eggs get partway and no further: 250 mg of DHA is about nine large eggs a day, so two eggs (58 mg) cover 23% of the target and one covers 12%. DHA-enriched eggs shorten that considerably. For an omnivore the same 250 mg of DHA is one 45 g tin of anchovies (580 mg DHA) or half a tin of sardines.
- **Food versus supplement:** The strain determines what you get, and the earlier generalisation was too broad. EFSA's assessment of strain WZU477 specifies DHA at 32.0% of total fatty acids or above, with batches measured at 40.5-46.3%, and EPA at a single value of about 0.56% (EFSA Journal 2020;18(10):6242, DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2020.6242). That is one strain, and the same opinion explicitly contrasts it with other authorised Schizochytrium strains carrying 1.3-6% EPA. So a DHA-dominant oil from this strain is not an EPA supplement, but 'a product labelled vegan omega-3 may supply almost no EPA' does not follow for the category: check the strain and the declared EPA content. On the positive side, this is one case where the concentrated product is cleaner than the food, because the algae are grown in closed fermenters rather than harvested from the sea, so algal oil avoids the methylmercury of predatory fish and the dioxin and PCB burden that makes fatty fish a leading dietary contributor to European dioxin exposure (EFSA Journal 2018;16(11):5333, document confirmed). The usual oxidation caveat applies, since a highly unsaturated oil in a clear bottle or an old capsule degrades, and a fishy aftertaste is the tell. Do not substitute algae biomass powders (spirulina, chlorella) for algal oil; they are different organisms with no DHA.
- **Absorption:** Algal oil DHA is nutritionally equivalent to DHA from fish. A randomised trial comparing algal-oil capsules with cooked salmon found the two equivalent as DHA sources by blood incorporation (Arterburn 2008, PMID 18589030, DOI 10.1016/j.jada.2008.04.020). Absorption follows the same rules as other marine oils, so triglyceride forms behave like natural fish oil and take-with-a-meal advice applies. Some DHA retroconverts to EPA in the body, which partly offsets the low EPA content of DHA-dominant algal oils, though not reliably enough to treat a DHA-only product as an EPA source.
- **Data note:** The three composition rows are all traced to USDA records and confirmed, including the two zero values that carry the debunking point, and the nori arithmetic (2 mg per sheet, 0.8% of a 250 mg dose, 312 g or about 120 sheets) recomputes exactly. The dose figures are the weak part: the 250 mg/day and 450 mg/day conditions were attributed to an EFSA opinion that contains neither, and the correct source, the Union list of novel foods under Regulation (EU) 2017/2470, was not verified in this pass. The population framing also had to be rebuilt, since the original TRACE label was applied to vegans and vegetarians together

*References: Bailey PMID 41096614; Cochrane PMID 32114706; Allaire ComparED PMID 27281302*

### Wheat bran and insoluble fibre supplements for IBS

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** 10 g/d was the comparator dose in the head-to-head primary care trial. No effective dose exists for IBS.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody with irritable bowel syndrome.

**Pros**

- As a food it contributes to total dietary fibre intake, and total fibre intake is associated with 15 to 30% reductions in all-cause and cardiovascular mortality.

**Cons**

- It is the clearest case in this document of a refuted product still sold into the exact indication where it failed. Pooled bran trials: relative risk of persistent IBS symptoms 0.90 (0.79-1.03), null. Soluble fibre by contrast gave 0.83 (0.73-0.94), NNT 7.
- In the three-arm primary care trial, bran at 10 g/d for 12 weeks did not beat placebo on symptom severity at 3 months (-58 versus -49 points, P=0.61) while psyllium did (-90 points, P=0.03). Bran also had the lowest completion rate of the three arms at 56%.
- Bran is the fibre most consistently reported to WORSEN abdominal pain and distension.
- Contraindicated with strictures, adhesions or a history of obstruction, and unsuitable in coeliac disease and non-coeliac gluten sensitivity.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Medium

Wheat bran is a food, sold as a plain milled cereal fraction, and at 42.8 g total dietary fibre per 100 g (USDA FDC 169722) it is one of the most fibre-dense ordinary cereal fractions. The submission's claim that it is among the most fibre-dense foods in any composition table is false and has been removed: corn bran at 79 g/100 g ranks first among roughly 4,000 USDA foods, nearly double wheat bran, and psyllium husk is about 78 g/100 g. RICH is demonstrated for the cereal-bran supplements specifically, which is where food comparators exist: wheat bran, corn bran, rice bran. The other common insoluble-fibre supplement base, powdered or microcrystalline cellulose and methylcellulose, is a manufactured material with no dietary equivalent, so the RICH grade does not extend to it. Availability is not the problem with this product category; the indication is.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Wheat bran, crude (plain, unprocessed) | 42.8 g total dietary fibre; 35.0-48.4 g insoluble and 1.5-4.0 g soluble | 1 cup (58 g) | 24.8 g total dietary fibre (roughly 20-28 g insoluble) | 83% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake; 5.8x the 10 g/day bran dose used in the Bijkerk IBS trial | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 169722 (42.8 g/100 g confirmed). Fibre fractions from Stevenson L et al., DOI 10.3109/09637486.2012.687366. Note an unresolved discrepancy in that source: it also states wheat soluble fibre is under 1% of dry weight, which sits below the 1.5-4.0 g/100 g quoted for the bran fraction, so the two figures are probably on different bases (whole grain versus bran). |
| Corn bran, crude | 79.0 g total dietary fibre, the highest fibre value among roughly 4,000 USDA foods | 30 g (conventional bran serving) | 23.7 g total dietary fibre | 79% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake | USDA composition data for corn bran, crude, as supplied and ranked in the verification report. The portion weight is a conventional bran serving, not from a source record. |
| Whole-grain wheat flour | 10.7 g total dietary fibre | 1 cup (120 g) | 12.8 g total dietary fibre | 43% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 168893; value is the standard USDA figure and the arithmetic was re-verified. |
| Extruded all-bran type breakfast cereal | about 29-30 g total dietary fibre | 30 g (conventional serving) | 8.7-9.0 g total dietary fibre | 29-30% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake | Fibre range as supplied in the verification report; brand-specific record not traced in this pass, so treat as indicative. Portion weight is a conventional serving. |
| Rice bran, crude | about 21 g total dietary fibre | 30 g (conventional bran serving) | 6.3 g total dietary fibre | 21% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake | Value as supplied in the verification report (about 21 g/100 g); specific USDA record not traced in this pass, so treat as indicative. Portion weight is a conventional bran serving. |
| Wheat bran flakes, ready-to-eat breakfast cereal | 16.9 g total dietary fibre | 1 NLEA serving, 0.75 cup (29 g) | 4.9 g total dietary fibre | 16% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake | Arithmetic verified (16.9 x 0.29 = 4.90), but FDC ID 173002 could not be confirmed as this food, so the per-100 g value is indicative and unconfirmed. Ready-to-eat bran flakes vary widely by brand. |
| Bread, whole-wheat, commercially prepared | 6.0 g total dietary fibre | 2 slices (56 g, at the 28 g slice USDA legacy commonly lists for this record) | 3.4 g total dietary fibre | 11% of the 30 g/day UK fibre reference intake | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 172688; the 6.0 g/100 g is confirmed, the slice weight is not. The submission's 32 g slice could not be verified, so the figures here use 28 g and should be restated if the record's slice weight is confirmed otherwise. |

- **To match the supplement dose:** The 10 g/day of bran tested against psyllium and placebo in IBS (PMID 19713235) is about 3 heaped tablespoons of plain wheat bran, which carries 4.28 g of dietary fibre. Matched on fibre, that is about 1 serving of bran flakes, not the 2 the submission claimed: one 29 g serving supplies 4.90 g, so two servings deliver more than double the fibre in the trial dose. A single cup of crude bran (58 g) is 5.8x the trial dose by mass. No supplement is needed to reach it. What the trial found is that reaching it does not help: bran was no better than placebo in months one and two, its month-three advantage did not survive worst-case analysis, and early dropout was highest in the bran arm because IBS symptoms worsened. A review of the wheat bran literature reaches the same conclusion and quotes official guidance that wheat bran should not be recommended to people with IBS (DOI 10.3109/09637486.2012.687366).
- **Food versus supplement:** The food is safe and useful; the supplement indication is the problem. Wheat bran is a genuinely effective faecal bulking agent, increasing stool by roughly 5 g per 1 g of bran consumed, at the top of the accepted 3-5.4 g range (DOI 10.3109/09637486.2012.687366), and it is a reasonable food for ordinary constipation and general fibre intake. In IBS it commonly worsens bloating, distension and pain, and it was the arm patients dropped out of. A less obvious caveat: wheat bran carries fructan (white wheat flour measures 0.75 g fructan/100 g, DOI 10.3389/fnut.2024.1446771), so bran-loading also raises FODMAP intake in exactly the patients least able to tolerate it. Insoluble-fibre supplements sold as 'IBS fibre' are marketing the wrong fibre class; the evidence in IBS favours the viscous, poorly fermented kind.
- **Absorption:** Not absorbed. The mechanism is mechanical bulking plus water-holding by arabinoxylan and cellulose, and the non-starch polysaccharide of the bran is 70% arabinoxylan, 24% cellulose and 6% beta-glucan (Stevenson et al., PMID 22716911, quotation verified). Bran accelerates small bowel transit and increases faecal weight dose-dependently. Particle size matters more than gram total, because coarse bran bulks and irritates while finely milled bran loses much of the effect, so two products with the same fibre label are not interchangeable. Only 1.5-4.0 g per 100 g is soluble, so bran contributes almost nothing to the viscous-gel effects on cholesterol, glycaemia and stool consistency that people often assume fibre delivers.
- **Data note:** The core wheat values were confirmed against USDA legacy records (wheat bran 42.8, whole-wheat bread 6.0, whole-grain flour 10.7), as were the Stevenson NSP split and the Bijkerk trial narrative. Weaknesses: the bran flakes FDC ID could not be resolved, the whole-wheat bread slice weight is unconfirmed, and the corn bran, rice bran and all-bran figures come from the verification report's compiled values with conventional rather than sourced portion weights

*References: Moayyedi PMID 25070054; Bijkerk BMJ 2009 PMID 19713235; Nagarajan PMID 26148247*

### Over-the-counter digestive enzyme blends

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** No evidence-derived dose exists. Products are dosed in non-comparable proprietary activity units by design.
- **Who it is for:** No identified group. Use a matched single enzyme for a diagnosed deficiency instead.

**Pros**

- One 120-participant double-blind placebo-controlled trial of a fungal multi-enzyme blend reported improvement in functional dyspepsia symptom and quality-of-life scores. Single centre, proprietary product, symptom-scale endpoints.

**Cons**

- The category is built on a fabricated disease. There is no recognised syndrome of generalised digestive enzyme insufficiency in healthy adults, and clinically meaningful malabsorption does not appear until roughly 90% of exocrine pancreatic function is lost.
- It borrows credibility from prescription pancreatic enzyme replacement, which treats a real, objectively diagnosable condition.
- The main harm is diagnostic delay. Self-treating undiagnosed persistent gut symptoms delays diagnosis of coeliac disease, inflammatory bowel disease, pancreatic disease and colorectal cancer.
- A properly controlled trial in autism (43 children, double-blind crossover) found no clinically significant improvement, in a population where these products were heavily promoted.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Trace amounts only
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Low

Reclassified from NOT_FOOD, which was too strong and was contradicted by this entry's own form caveat. The plant proteases do occur in ordinary food: fruit bromelain is present in edible pineapple flesh and juice with real proteolytic activity, which is exactly why fresh pineapple stops gelatin setting and canned does not; papain occurs in unripe papaya flesh as well as in the latex that is the commercial source; and actinidin, a cysteine protease, is present in ordinary edible ripe green kiwifruit, with human data comparing green (actinidin-containing) against gold (actinidin-free) kiwifruit for gastric protein digestion. Ginger (zingibain), fig latex (ficin), malted barley (diastatic amylase) and raw honey (diastase) are further real dietary enzyme sources. What remains genuinely NOT_FOOD is the other half of these blends: the amylase, protease, lipase and lactase components are Aspergillus or yeast fermentation products with no dietary presence. TRACE rather than PARTIAL because the activity in edible flesh is present without any confirmed quantification, and the parts carrying the measured activity are not eaten.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Kiwifruit, green, ripe, raw | contains actinidin, a cysteine protease; no per-100 g activity figure was retrieved in this pass | 1 fruit (about 75 g), conventional portion | proteolytic activity present but unquantified here | not expressible against a GDU-standardised bromelain dose; the units and the enzyme differ and no activity value for the flesh was confirmed | Human crossover data in healthy older adults compared green (actinidin-containing) against gold (actinidin-free) kiwifruit with beef meals for gastric protein digestion, with supporting in vitro work at gastric pH, as summarised in the verification report; no specific citation or activity value retrieved here, so the row is qualitative. This is the clearest food route for a swallowed protease and it is why the NOT_FOOD grade was withdrawn. |
| Pineapple, raw flesh | contains fruit bromelain (pI 4.6, distinct from stem bromelain at pI 9.5); no proteolytic activity value for the eaten flesh was retrieved in this pass | 1 cup chunks (about 165 g), conventional portion | proteolytic activity present but unquantified here | not expressible; the measured GDU/g values in the literature belong to basal stem, peel and core rather than to flesh | USDA FDC 169124 records the edible fruit but carries no enzyme activity value. Isoform distinction from the bromelain literature (DOI 10.3390/foods13040589). The absence of a flesh activity figure is a sourcing gap here rather than an absence in the literature. |

- **To match the supplement dose:** Bromelain supplements are dosed by activity, typically 500-2,000 mg of a preparation standardised to around 2,400 GDU/g, giving roughly 1,200-4,800 GDU per dose. On the activities reported for pineapple by-products, that is equivalent to about 0.41-1.65 g of basal stem or about 1.0-4.1 g of peel, and neither the basal stem nor the peel is eaten (those per-gram activity values are from a genuine, on-topic paper whose individual figures could not be retrieved, so treat them as unconfirmed). Expressed against food you would actually put in your mouth, the dose cannot be matched: the crown has no detectable activity, the core is usually discarded, and no sourced activity figure exists for the ripe flesh. Fresh pineapple and green kiwifruit do deliver active protease into the stomach, so the effect is not zero, but with no confirmed activity value for edible flesh there is no way to express a food equivalent of a GDU dose. For papain the source material is the latex of the unripe fruit, which is irritant and is not food. For the fungal amylase, protease and lipase components there is no food source at all.
- **Food versus supplement:** The whole food is safe and the concentrate is not always. Fresh pineapple and papaya are ordinary fruit; concentrated bromelain has anticoagulant and antiplatelet activity and interacts with warfarin and antiplatelet drugs, and papain and chymopapain are recognised respiratory and contact allergens, which EFSA flags in its allergenicity assessments of the food enzyme (the specific identifier cited in the submission, DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2026.9950, could not be confirmed; EFSA does publish opinions on papain from papaya latex, so the substance of the point stands while that reference does not). The matrix also destroys the premise: these are proteins, so heat and gastric acid inactivate them. Canned and cooked pineapple has no meaningful proteolytic activity left, which is why canned pineapple does not stop gelatin setting while fresh pineapple does, and any orally swallowed enzyme without enteric protection meets pepsin and pH 2 before reaching the small intestine. The most important caveat is scope: these blends are not equivalent to pancreatic enzyme replacement and must not be substituted for it in diagnosed pancreatic exocrine insufficiency, where the requirement is 40,000-50,000 lipase units per meal in an enteric-coated porcine preparation (PMID 42011215). Nor is the plant-derived lactase in a blend interchangeable with a dose-standardised lactase product.
- **Absorption:** Enzymes are not absorbed and do not need to be; they act in the lumen, so what matters is surviving to the right pH and place. Stem bromelain and fruit bromelain differ even in isoelectric point (pI 9.5 against 4.6), which is why extraction pH determines which enzyme you actually get and why 'bromelain' on a label does not identify a single molecule. Activity rather than milligrams is the meaningful unit, and GDU, CDU, FIP and USP units are not interconvertible, so a milligram figure on a bottle says almost nothing about what it will do. Both bromelain and papain are irreversibly denatured by cooking and largely by gastric acid unless enterically protected, so an uncoated capsule and a bowl of fresh pineapple share the same delivery problem.
- **Data note:** The quantitative core of this entry could not be verified. The pineapple by-product activities (2,909 GDU/g basal stem, 1,179 GDU/g peel, 694 GDU/g core, crown below detection) come from a genuine and on-topic paper (PMID 38397568) whose individual values were not retrieved, and every dose equivalence derives from them. The EFSA papain identifier could not be confirmed. The two food rows are qualitative because no activity figure for edible pineapple flesh or kiwifruit was retrieved. Only the supplement-side arithmetic (1,200-4,800 GDU per dose) and the bromelain isoform biochemistry check out

*References: Ullah PMID 37976892; Munasinghe PMID 20204691; de la Iglesia-Garcia PMID 27941156*

### L-glutamine for gut repair

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** 15 g/d as 5 g three times daily for 8 weeks in the only supportive gut trial.
- **Who it is for:** Narrowly, post-infectious diarrhoea-predominant IBS with objectively documented increased intestinal permeability.

**Pros**

- The one supportive trial is genuinely striking: 106 patients, 79.6% versus 5.8% achieved a 50-point reduction in IBS severity score over 8 weeks, with normalisation of intestinal permeability.
- It has a licensed pharmaceutical use in sickle cell disease, where a phase 3 trial reduced median pain crises from 4.0 to 3.0.

**Cons**

- A 14-fold difference in response rate is an effect size almost never seen in functional gastrointestinal disease, and the 5.8% placebo response is far below the 30 to 40% typical of IBS trials. That combination demands scepticism, not enthusiasm. It is a single-centre, unreplicated result.
- There is a mortality signal in the critically ill. In the REDOXS trial, high-dose glutamine given to 1,223 critically ill adults with multi-organ failure raised 28-day mortality to 32.4% versus 27.2%, adjusted OR 1.28.
- Contraindicated in hepatic impairment, cirrhosis and any history of hepatic encephalopathy, because glutamine is metabolised to glutamate and ammonia.
- It is the chemical anchor of the entire leaky gut supplement economy, and the gap between that market and the evidence is enormous.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose partly reachable
- **Data confidence:** Medium

Glutamine is one of the most abundant amino acids in ordinary dietary protein, so any protein-containing meal supplies it. One caveat governs every number below: USDA composition tables report 'glutamic acid', which after the acid hydrolysis used in amino acid analysis is the sum of glutamate and glutamine, because glutamine is converted to glutamate during hydrolysis. The figures are therefore the combined Glu plus Gln pool, and the glutamine share of that pool is roughly half in most animal proteins. Treat the values as an upper bound on glutamine content.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Vital wheat gluten (seitan base), dry | roughly 25-30 g glutamic acid (Glu + Gln pool), the densest ordinary food source by a wide margin | about 30 g dry (conventional serving, roughly 1/4 cup) | 7.5-9.0 g Glu + Gln | 50-60% of the 15 g/day glutamine dose used in the post-infectious IBS trial (PMID 30108163) | Derived figure: vital wheat gluten protein content about 75 g/100 g (USDA-derived nutrition databases) with glutamate at roughly 35% of gluten protein (cereal protein composition literature). This is a calculation rather than a direct composition record, and the portion weight is conventional, so treat the range as indicative. |
| Cod, Atlantic, cooked, dry heat | 3.408 g glutamic acid (Glu + Gln pool) | 1 fillet (180 g) | 6.13 g Glu + Gln | 41% of the 15 g/day dose | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 171956 (3.4 g/100 g confirmed, protein 22.8 g). The 180 g fillet weight was not independently confirmed but is the standard USDA portion for this record. |
| Natto (fermented soybean) | 3.337 g glutamic acid (Glu + Gln pool) | 1 cup (175 g) | 5.84 g Glu + Gln | 39% of the 15 g/day dose | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 172443 (3.3 g/100 g confirmed, protein 19.4 g). |
| Soy protein isolate, powder | roughly 17-19 g glutamic acid (Glu + Gln pool) | about 30 g (conventional scoop) | 5.1-5.7 g Glu + Gln | 34-38% of the 15 g/day dose | Derived figure from soy protein isolate protein content of about 88 g/100 g and the glutamate share of soy protein, as supplied in the verification report. A calculation rather than a direct composition record, with a conventional portion weight; indicative. |
| Chicken breast, meat only, roasted | 4.645 g glutamic acid (Glu + Gln pool) | half a breast, bone and skin removed (86 g) | 4.00 g Glu + Gln | 27% of the 15 g/day dose | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 171477; per-100 g value is the standard USDA figure and the arithmetic was re-verified. |
| Beef, top sirloin cap steak, grilled | 4.251 g glutamic acid (Glu + Gln pool) | 3 oz (85 g) | 3.61 g Glu + Gln | 24% of the 15 g/day dose | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 173061; arithmetic re-verified. |
| Lentils, mature seeds, cooked, boiled | 1.399 g glutamic acid (Glu + Gln pool) | 1 cup cooked (198 g) | 2.77 g Glu + Gln | 18% of the 15 g/day dose | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 172421; arithmetic re-verified. |
| Parmesan cheese, hard | 8.209 g glutamic acid (Glu + Gln pool), the densest ordinary animal-food source but well below vital wheat gluten | 1 oz (28.35 g) | 2.33 g Glu + Gln | 16% of the 15 g/day dose | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 170848; the 8.209 g/100 g is the standard USDA value. The submission's claim that this is 'the densest ordinary food source' is false and has been removed: vital wheat gluten runs roughly three to four times higher. |
| Tofu, firm (calcium sulfate and nigari) | 1.721 g glutamic acid (Glu + Gln pool) | half a cup (126 g) | 2.17 g Glu + Gln | 14% of the 15 g/day dose | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 172448; arithmetic re-verified. |
| Milk, whole, 3.25% fat | 0.708 g glutamic acid (Glu + Gln pool) | 1 cup (244 g) | 1.73 g Glu + Gln | 12% of the 15 g/day dose | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 171265; the per-100 g value was not independently retrieved but is consistent with whole milk protein of about 3.2 g/100 g. |
| Egg, whole, hard-boiled | 1.644 g glutamic acid (Glu + Gln pool) | 1 large egg (50 g) | 0.82 g Glu + Gln | 5% of the 15 g/day dose | USDA FoodData Central legacy record 173424; arithmetic re-verified. |

- **To match the supplement dose:** The trial dose is 5 g three times daily, 15 g/day for 8 weeks, in post-infectious diarrhoea-predominant IBS with increased intestinal permeability (Zhou et al., Gut 2019;68:996-1002, PMID 30108163). On the combined Glu plus Gln figures, 15 g comes from about 50-60 g of vital wheat gluten, about 79-88 g of soy protein isolate, about 183 g of parmesan, about 323 g of cooked chicken breast, about 440 g of cod (about 2.5 fillets), about 450 g of natto (corrected from the submission's 865 g, which was wrong by 1.9x) or roughly 1.1 kg of cooked lentils. Correcting for the fact that only about half that pool is glutamine, matching 15 g of glutamine specifically needs roughly double: about 100-120 g of vital wheat gluten a day, or 600-700 g of cooked chicken breast. The baseline matters more than the ceiling. Glutamine is roughly 4-8% of food protein, so a diet supplying 80-100 g of protein delivers about 4-7 g/day, consistent with the conventional 3-6 g/day estimate. A 15 g/day supplement is therefore a three- to four-fold increase over habitual intake, not the doubling the submission claimed, which makes this a moderate gap rather than a small one. It is still nothing like creatine or NMN, where food cannot get close.
- **Food versus supplement:** The difference is in the delivery rather than the amount. The trial gave 5 g of free crystalline L-glutamine three times a day on an empty-stomach schedule, producing a sharp luminal and plasma bolus; dietary glutamine arrives protein-bound, released slowly by digestion, and a large fraction is consumed by the enterocytes themselves on first pass. Whether the clinical effect depends on that bolus pattern has not been tested against a food-protein comparator, so 'just eat more protein' remains plausible and unproven. Two practical points: free glutamine is heat-labile and degrades in aqueous solution, so cooking and storage reduce free glutamine in food while leaving protein-bound glutamine intact; and glutamine supplementation is not risk-free in everyone, since it is a nitrogen donor and high doses warrant caution in hepatic or renal impairment, whereas the same amount arriving as ordinary food protein raises no comparable concern.
- **Absorption:** Absorbed as free amino acid and as di- and tripeptides, then extensively extracted by the small intestinal mucosa on first pass, which is the mechanistic argument for a gut-directed effect and also the reason plasma appearance underestimates delivery to the target tissue. Because USDA reports Glu and Gln as one pooled value, no composition table can give the true glutamine content of a food, and any per-food glutamine figure quoted to two decimal places should be treated with suspicion. In the trial, the measured mechanism was normalisation of an elevated urinary lactulose/mannitol ratio, so the endpoint was permeability rather than amino acid status; a food equivalent would have to reproduce the permeability effect, which has not been shown.
- **Data note:** Every USDA per-100 g glutamic acid value and every per-portion calculation in the original list was re-verified and stands. The weaknesses are structural and additive: the Glu plus Gln pooling means no table reports glutamine itself, the two densest rows (vital wheat gluten, soy protein isolate) are calculations from protein content and a literature glutamate fraction rather than direct composition records, and the corrected habitual baseline of 4-7 g/day rests on the conventional 3-6 g/day estimate rather than a measured survey

*References: Zhou Gut 2019 PMID 30108163; REDOXS PMID 23594003; Niihara NEJM PMID 30021096*

### Prebiotics: inulin, FOS, GOS

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** 5-15 g/d of inulin-type fructans, clustering around 10-12 g/d in constipation trials. Tolerance is the binding constraint above 10-15 g/d.
- **Who it is for:** Adults with chronic constipation and habitually low fibre intake who tolerate fermentation.

**Pros**

- Inulin does something measurable for constipation: stool frequency SMD 0.69 and Bristol stool consistency SMD 1.07 across 5 trials and 252 subjects.
- It reliably increases faecal bifidobacteria, and it is cheap.
- EFSA has an authorised claim for native chicory inulin at 12 g/d for maintenance of normal defecation, which is more regulatory support than most items here have.

**Cons**

- Feed your good bacteria is the current premium reframing of what used to be sold as roughage, at roughly ten times the price per gram of fibre.
- The endpoint switch is the specific dishonesty: marketing leans on the reliable rise in faecal bifidobacteria, which is a microbiological measurement, and lets the consumer infer a clinical benefit.
- The inulin meta-analysis states explicitly that pain and bloating do NOT improve. The largest systematic review of prebiotics in IBS found the data sparse and insufficient to establish efficacy.
- Self-defeating for IBS patients on or considering a low-FODMAP diet, because inulin, FOS and GOS ARE the fermentable oligosaccharides that diet removes. Anyone whose dominant symptom is bloating or flatulence should not take these.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose partly reachable
- **Data confidence:** Medium

This item bundles three substances with two different answers. Inulin and fructo-oligosaccharide (FOS) are RICH: chicory root at 15-20 g per 100 g fresh weight, Jerusalem artichoke at roughly 8-22 g, garlic at about 9-16 g, onion at about 2 g, with smaller amounts in globe artichoke, shallot, salsify, leek, asparagus, banana, wheat and rye. Beta-galacto-oligosaccharide (GOS) is SYNTHETIC and should not be carried under the same heading: the beta-GOS used in trials is manufactured enzymatically by transgalactosylation of lactose and has no meaningful presence in ordinary food. The galacto-oligosaccharides that do occur in legumes are alpha-galactosides (raffinose, stachyose, verbascose), a chemically different family with different bacterial targets. Habitual dietary intake of the fructan class is low: mean 1.6-1.7 g/day in a Slovenian survey (DOI 10.3389/fnut.2024.1446771) and median inulin 1.3 g/day in UK schoolchildren (PMID 40808333).

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Jerusalem artichoke, raw tuber | roughly 8-22 g inulin per 100 g fresh tuber, depending on cultivar, season and storage (HPLC studies 8.16-13.46%; a British Journal of Nutrition source 14-19%; Van Loo 16-20%) | 1 cup slices (150 g) | roughly 12-33 g inulin | 240-660% of a 5 g/day supplement dose; 1.2-3.3x the 10 g/day tolerated ceiling for native inulin | Composition literature on Jerusalem artichoke inulin (8-22 g/100 g fresh weight) and Van Loo et al., Crit Rev Food Sci Nutr 1995;35:525-52, PMID 8777017; portion weight USDA FDC ID 169236. The submission's narrower 12-13.6 g/100 g used the low end of a wide range. |
| Salsify, raw root | named as inulin-rich in the 15.6-16.7 g/100 g group (Nutr Bull 2025, PMID 40808333, citing Van Loo et al. 1995); no salsify-specific value was confirmed | 1 cup sliced (133 g), conventional portion | roughly 21 g inulin if the group figure applies to salsify; indicative only | roughly 4x a 5 g/day supplement dose on that unconfirmed figure; about 30 g of root would supply 5 g | Nutrition Bulletin 2025 (PMID 40808333) names salsify in the same sentence as the 15.6-16.7 g/100 g range, which spans four foods and is not salsify's own measured value. Indicative and unconfirmed; portion weight conventional. |
| Chicory root, raw (industrial inulin source; not eaten as a vegetable) | 15-20 g inulin per 100 g fresh root | 1 root (60 g) | roughly 9-12 g inulin | 180-240% of a typical 5 g/day supplement dose, and at or above the 10 g/day tolerance ceiling for native inulin | Inulin range from extraction and composition literature (chicory 15-20 g/100 g fresh weight); portion weight USDA FDC ID 169993, whose own total dietary fibre field reads 1.5 g/100 g because the method used does not capture inulin. The submission's Molecules 2025 citation is a fruit-snack formulation paper and is a secondary source for the value. |
| Globe artichoke (Cynara scolymus), raw | placed in the top fructan group by Muir et al. 2007 (PMID 17625872), whose high-fructan range is 1.2-17.4 g/100 g; no artichoke-specific value confirmed here | 1 medium head, edible portion (about 128 g), conventional portion | grams rather than milligrams, but not resolvable from the confirmed group range | likely a meaningful fraction of a 5 g dose, and possibly above it; cannot be stated precisely from the confirmed sources | Muir HJ et al. 2007, PMID 17625872, top fructan group. Indicative and unconfirmed at the individual-food level; portion weight conventional. |
| Onion, mature, raw | 1.97 +/- 0.05 g fructan (AOAC 999.03) | 1 medium onion (110 g) | 2.2 g fructan | 43% of a 5 g/day supplement dose | Ferjancic B et al., Front Nutr 2024;11:1446771, DOI 10.3389/fnut.2024.1446771 (value verified verbatim); portion weight USDA FDC ID 170000. In that survey onion was the largest single contributor to habitual fructan intake at 0.72 g/day. |
| Shallot, raw | placed in the top fructan group by Muir et al. 2007 (PMID 17625872), high-fructan range 1.2-17.4 g/100 g; no shallot-specific value confirmed here | 1 shallot (about 25 g), conventional portion | roughly 0.3-4.4 g fructan on the group range; too wide to be treated as a figure | 6-87% of a 5 g/day dose on that range; indicative only | Muir HJ et al. 2007, PMID 17625872, top fructan group. Indicative and unconfirmed at the individual-food level; portion weight conventional. |
| Garlic, raw | about 9-16 g inulin | 1 clove (3 g) | 0.27-0.48 g inulin, about 0.38 g at the midpoint | about 7.5% of a 5 g/day supplement dose at the midpoint | Van Loo et al., Crit Rev Food Sci Nutr 1995;35:525-52, PMID 8777017 (garlic roughly 9-16 g/100 g); portion weight USDA FDC ID 169230. The submission's 15.6-16.7 g/100 g was a misreading: that range is given in Nutr Bull 2025 (PMID 40808333) across four foods (Jerusalem artichoke, chicory root, salsify, garlic), not as garlic's own value. |
| Asparagus, raw spear | 1.48 +/- 0.17 g fructan per 100 g dry matter, which converts to about 0.1 g per 100 g fresh at 7% dry matter; other datasets give up to about 0.5 g per 100 g fresh | 1 cup raw (134 g) | roughly 0.13-0.67 g fructan | roughly 3-13% of a 5 g/day supplement dose | Goni I et al., Foods 2024;13(8):1154, DOI 10.3390/foods13081154, PMID 38672827 (dry-matter basis, abstract verified); comparative fructan datasets for the higher fresh-weight figures; portion weight USDA FDC ID 168389. The fresh-weight value is uncertain by roughly fivefold, so it is given as a range rather than the submission's single 0.1 g figure. |
| Leek, bulb and lower leaf, raw | 0.24 +/- 0.00 g fructan (AOAC 999.03) | 1 leek (89 g) | 0.21 g fructan | 4% of a 5 g/day supplement dose | Ferjancic B et al., DOI 10.3389/fnut.2024.1446771 (value verified verbatim); portion weight USDA FDC ID 169246. Muir et al. (PMID 17625872) found 1.2-17.4 g/100 g across high-fructan alliums including leek bulb, so leek is highly variable by part and cultivar. |
| Banana, raw | 0.11 +/- 0.01 g fructan (AOAC 999.03) | 1 medium banana (118 g) | 0.13 g fructan | under 3% of a 5 g/day supplement dose | Ferjancic B et al., DOI 10.3389/fnut.2024.1446771 (value verified verbatim); portion weight USDA FDC ID 173944. |
| White wheat bread and white wheat flour | 0.14 +/- 0.03 g fructan in white bread; 0.75 +/- 0.04 g in white wheat flour T500 | 2 slices bread (56 g, at a 28 g slice) | 0.08 g fructan | under 2% of a 5 g/day supplement dose | Ferjancic B et al., DOI 10.3389/fnut.2024.1446771 (values verified verbatim). In that Slovenian survey white flour T500 was the second largest contributor at 0.41 g/day, behind onion at 0.72 g/day; the claim that bread and flour supplied the largest share belongs to the separate UK children's study (PMID 40808333), where cereals supplied 58.1% of inulin intake and wheat 69% in the comparative data. Slice weight is 28 g; the submission's 32 g slice was unconfirmed. |

- **To match the supplement dose:** For inulin and FOS the answer depends on which food. A 5 g/day dose is reachable from about 250 g of raw onion (two to three medium onions), about 10-19 cloves of garlic on the corrected 9-16 g/100 g range (the submission's 30 cloves was wrong by roughly 3x and contradicted its own food row), about 23-63 g of Jerusalem artichoke, or about 30 g of salsify on an unconfirmed figure. It is out of reach from banana (about 4.5 kg), leek (about 2.1 kg) and asparagus (roughly 1-5 kg, the range reflecting a fivefold uncertainty in the fresh-weight figure; the submission's flat 5 kg used the low end only). The tolerance ceiling is the more useful number than the dose: up to 10 g/day of native inulin and up to 5 g/day of oligofructose were well tolerated in a randomised crossover trial, while 10 g of oligofructose substantially increased symptoms, chiefly flatulence then bloating (Bonnema et al., PMID 20497775). One 150 g cup of Jerusalem artichoke delivers roughly 12-33 g of inulin, so between one and three times the tolerated daily ceiling in a single sitting. For GOS the answer is a flat no: the beta-GOS doses used in trials are made industrially and no ordinary food supplies them.
- **Food versus supplement:** Chicory root is the classic textbook answer and a poor recommendation: it is the industrial source of inulin and is roasted as a coffee substitute rather than eaten as a vegetable, so its 15-20 g/100 g headline is not a dietary route. Dandelion greens, which are eaten as a vegetable, and burdock root and yacon are recognised high-inulin foods that answer that objection, though no confirmed figures for them were available here. Chicory also illustrates why fibre tables and fructan assays disagree, since USDA lists chicory root at only 1.5 g total dietary fibre per 100 g (FDC 169993). Purified inulin and oligofructose are safe as food ingredients, and concentration is what causes symptoms: the same molecule that is unremarkable spread across a day's vegetables provokes flatulence and bloating as a single 10 g bolus (PMID 20497775). Briet et al. (PMID 7588500) reported a symptom ladder for FOS (excess flatus above 30 g/day, borborygmi and bloating above 40 g, cramps and diarrhoea at 50 g, with no tolerance developing on chronic dosing); the citation is correct and on topic, but those specific thresholds could not be retrieved from the full text and are unconfirmed. The reverse caveat matters clinically: the 'eat more prebiotic vegetables' list (onion, garlic, leek, globe artichoke, Jerusalem artichoke, wheat, rye) is precisely the list a low-FODMAP diet removes, so in IBS the food route and the supplement route can both make things worse. Finally, do not treat legume alpha-GOS as a food form of supplemental beta-GOS; the reported contents in Phaseolus vulgaris (raffinose 0.8-7.4 mg/g, stachyose 20.0-30.9 mg/g, verbascose 0.3-1.3 mg/g, DOI 10.3390/foods15020391) come from a genuine and on-topic paper whose individual values could not be confirmed, and they are different molecules with a flatulence profile of their own.
- **Absorption:** Fructans are not absorbed at all, so the variable is where and how fast they ferment, and chain length decides that. Oligofructose (DP 2-8) ferments proximally and quickly, which is why its tolerated dose was half that of long-chain native inulin in the same trial, 5 g against 10 g (PMID 20497775). Assay and plant part also drive the numbers: onion measured 1.97 g/100 g and leek 0.24 g/100 g by AOAC 999.03 in one national survey (DOI 10.3389/fnut.2024.1446771), while an Australian survey using the Megazyme assay reported 1.2-17.4 g/100 g across high-fructan alliums including leek bulb (PMID 17625872). Storage matters too, since fructan is depolymerised during onion storage. Single figures should be read as indicative rather than exact. GOS is fermented similarly but is a distinct substrate: beta-GOS and legume alpha-galactosides are not interchangeable.
- **Data note:** The Ferjancic fructan values (onion, leek, banana, bread, flour) and the Bonnema tolerance thresholds were verified verbatim, and the chicory contrast between 15-20 g inulin and a 1.5 g USDA fibre record holds. Against that: the garlic figure was a source misreading now corrected to a wide 9-16 g/100 g range, Jerusalem artichoke spans 8-22 g/100 g, the asparagus fresh-weight value is uncertain by fivefold, the Briet symptom ladder and the Phaseolus oligosaccharide values could not be confirmed, and the three added foods (salsify, globe artichoke, shallot) rest on group-level ranges rather than food-specific measurements

*References: Ford PMID 30294792; Collado Yurrita PMID 25208775; Reynolds Lancet 2019 PMID 30638909*

### Saffron, SAMe and other mood botanicals

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** Saffron 30 mg/d of standardised extract. SAMe 1,600-3,200 mg/d as an adjunct.
- **Who it is for:** At most, adults with mild to moderate depression taking no serotonergic medication, and only with a clinician involved.

**Pros**

- Saffron: 21 randomised trials found Beck Depression Inventory -4.86 (-6.58 to -3.14) and Beck Anxiety Inventory -5.29.
- SAMe: one 2024 meta-analysis of 23 trials and 2,183 participants found monotherapy better than placebo, SMD -0.58 (-0.93 to -0.23), at moderate certainty.

**Cons**

- Saffron effect sizes are implausibly large for the trial size. One meta-analysis reported SMD -1.22 versus placebo, which is better than licensed antidepressants achieve. Effect sizes that large in small short trials usually indicate small-study effects and a narrow research base, so treat it as a red flag rather than a selling point.
- Self-report instruments move while clinician-rated instruments do not, which is the classic pattern of an unblinded expectancy effect.
- SAMe has directly conflicting meta-analyses published in the same year. A second 2024 analysis of 14 trials and 1,522 participants found NO significant difference versus placebo, versus imipramine or versus escitalopram. Report both.
- SAMe is associated with switching into mania or hypomania in bipolar disorder, and homocysteine can rise on it. Both saffron and SAMe carry serotonin syndrome risk with serotonergic medication. Saffron should be avoided in pregnancy on the basis of traditional emmenagogue use.
- Depression is a condition where delaying effective treatment carries real risk. Neither of these should displace a clinical assessment.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Medium

This item bundles three different answers, so each gets its own sentence. Saffron is RICH: it is a supermarket spice with USDA composition and portion entries, its clinical dose is a cooking quantity, and it is listed under foods below, which is why the previous NOT_FOOD label on the group was the clearest error in the submission. SAMe has no dietary source: there is no meaningful preformed SAMe in food and no composition-table entry, the body synthesises it continuously from methionine and ATP by methionine adenosyltransferase, and the commercial material is made by Saccharomyces cerevisiae fermentation and stabilised as the tosylate disulfate or 1,4-butanedisulfonate salt, so it is biosynthetic in origin and the SYNTHETIC label holds in the sense of 'no dietary source' rather than 'chemically synthesised'. St John's wort aerial parts, rhodiola root, ashwagandha root and Griffonia simplicifolia seed (the source of 5-HTP) are NOT_FOOD: nobody eats them in any normal diet, they have no composition-table entry, and they have no portion size.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Saffron, dried stigmas (Crocus sativus) | 100 g of saffron is 3,333 clinical doses of 30 mg. No apocarotenoid percentage is stated here: the previously cited '8% of dry weight' for crocins, picrocrocin, safranal and crocetin combined and the 'roughly 3.4 mg of crocins per 30 mg dose' could not be confirmed against any source reached, and they contradict each other, since 3.4 mg in 30 mg is 11.3% crocins alone, above the claimed 8% ceiling for all apocarotenoids | 1 teaspoon (0.7 g) | 700 mg of saffron, 23 times the 30 mg dose used in depression RCTs | 2,300% of a 30 mg trial dose | USDA portion weights for Spices, saffron (1 tbsp = 2.1 g, so 1 tsp = 0.7 g) independently confirmed; the FDC ID 170934 previously cited could not be confirmed against a live lookup. Trial dose: Akhondzadeh S, Tahmacebi-Pour N, Noorbala AA et al., Crocus sativus L. in the treatment of mild to moderate depression: a double-blind, randomized and placebo-controlled trial, Phytother Res 2005;19(2):148-51, PMID 15852492 (30 mg/day given as 15 mg twice daily for 6 weeks, 40 outpatients with DSM-IV major depression and baseline HAM-D of 18 or more, against PLACEBO). The fluoxetine comparison belongs to a separate trial (Noorbala AA et al., J Ethnopharmacol 2005) |
| Saffron, culinary pinch as used to season a dish | as above | a generous pinch, about 0.1 g, seasoning a dish for four (roughly 50 threads at published thread weights near 2 mg; the commonly quoted 20 to 30 threads per 0.1 g would imply a heavier 3 to 5 mg thread). For reference the FDA reference amount customarily consumed for saffron is 0.125 g | about 25 mg of saffron per person from a dish for four; 125 mg for a single 0.125 g reference serving | 83% of the 30 mg trial dose per serving of the dish; over 400% for one 0.125 g reference serving | USDA portion and reference-serving figures for Spices, saffron (portion weights confirmed, FDC ID unconfirmed); trial dose PMID 15852492 (placebo-controlled) |

- **To match the supplement dose:** For saffron the gap is zero or negative, which is the most useful finding in the set: the 30 mg/day used in the depression RCTs is about 15 threads at published thread weights near 2 mg, or about 6 to 9 threads if a thread weighs the 3 to 5 mg implied by the commonly quoted 20 to 30 threads per 0.1 g, which is under a generous culinary pinch, and one teaspoon of saffron in a paella is over twenty doses. The barrier is cost rather than physiology. For SAMe there is nothing to compute, because there is no food source: trials use 400 to 1,600 mg/day of a manufactured molecule, while the body's own SAMe turnover (on the order of grams per day) is fed by roughly 1 g/day of dietary methionine from protein foods, which is a different intervention from swallowing SAMe. For the remaining botanicals (St John's wort typically 300 mg three times daily of a 0.3% hypericin extract, rhodiola 200 to 600 mg, ashwagandha 300 to 600 mg root extract, 5-HTP 100 to 300 mg) the food gap is undefined because the source plants are not foods at any quantity.
- **Food versus supplement:** Saffron is the mildest food-versus-extract case in this whole set, since trials use either powdered stigma or a hydroalcoholic extract standardised to crocins and safranal, so kitchen saffron is close to the trial material. The caveat is at the top end: saffron is toxic at gram doses, with roughly 5 g reported as a toxic dose and 12 to 20 g as potentially lethal, and it is a traditional abortifacient, so scaling from a pinch to a spoonful is genuinely hazardous. Standardised extracts also concentrate crocins, so milligram for milligram an extract is not the spice. For the rest of the group the form gap is total: St John's wort is a potent CYP3A4 and P-glycoprotein inducer that has caused transplant rejection, contraceptive failure and serotonin syndrome with SSRIs; ashwagandha root extracts have accumulated a documented cluster of hepatotoxicity case reports, including in regulatory reviews, while traditional use was of small amounts of root powder in milk as medicine and never as a food; purified 5-HTP bypasses the rate-limiting tryptophan hydroxylase step that normally regulates serotonin synthesis, which is exactly why it carries serotonin syndrome risk with serotonergic drugs and why no food behaves like it.
- **Absorption:** Saffron's crocins are poorly absorbed intact and are hydrolysed in the gut to crocetin, which is what appears in plasma; safranal is volatile and is partly lost in long simmering, so a slow-cooked dish delivers a different apocarotenoid profile than a capsule taken cold, and blooming saffron in warm liquid before adding it is the culinary analogue of extraction. SAMe is chemically unstable and is degraded in gastric acid unless enterically coated, and oral bioavailability is only about 1% because of extensive first-pass metabolism, which is why oral doses are 400 to 1,600 mg while parenteral doses are far smaller. The classic bad recommendation in this space is 'eat turkey or eggs for 5-HTP': food supplies tryptophan, and dietary tryptophan competes with the other large neutral amino acids in the same protein meal for LAT1 transport across the blood-brain barrier, so a high-protein meal can lower rather than raise brain tryptophan availability. Food is a poor delivery route for this entire group.
- **Data note:** The saffron portion weights, the 3,333-doses arithmetic and the trial's 30 mg/day for 6 weeks all verified exactly, and the trial's design has been corrected against the abstract. The apocarotenoid content figures could not be confirmed anywhere and have been removed, the thread-weight basis remains unsettled between two mutually inconsistent conventions, and the FDC ID is unconfirmed

*References: Yang PMID 29849461; Ghaderi PMID 31987241; Limveeraprajak PMID 38423354; Peng PMID 38199136*

### Quercetin and taurine

**Verdict:** SKIP &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** Quercetin at least 500 mg/d for the blood pressure effect. Taurine 0.5-6 g/d.
- **Who it is for:** At most, people with elevated blood pressure or components of metabolic syndrome, for a small surrogate effect.

**Pros**

- Taurine has the strongest human surrogate data of any longevity compound here: 25 trials, 1,024 participants, systolic -4.0 mmHg (-7.29 to -0.71), diastolic -1.51 mmHg, fasting glucose -5.9 mg/dL, all dose-dependent.
- Quercetin at 500 mg/d or more gave systolic -4.45 mmHg and diastolic -2.98 mmHg in the dose-stratified analysis.

**Cons**

- The taurine longevity story was directly refuted in humans in 2025. The 2023 Science paper showed taurine declines with age in mice, monkeys and humans and extends lifespan in mice. A 2025 Aging Cell paper found circulating taurine is NOT associated with age, muscle mass, strength, physical performance or mitochondrial function in humans.
- Quercetin effect sizes are shrinking as the evidence matures. The 2016 meta-analysis gave systolic -3.04 mmHg; the 2023 umbrella review of meta-analyses found only -1.9 mmHg with heterogeneity of 88%, and nothing else significant, at very low certainty.
- A 2 to 4 mmHg systolic reduction is small next to any antihypertensive drug or to sodium reduction.
- Quercetin inhibits CYP3A4 and P-glycoprotein, so treat it as an interaction risk with ciclosporin, tacrolimus, CYP3A4-metabolised statins, warfarin and many chemotherapeutics. It is also a topoisomerase inhibitor in vitro, which is a specific reason not to combine it with chemotherapy.
- Taurine effects are additive with antihypertensives, insulin and sulfonylureas.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Medium

Quercetin is present across ordinary plant food: raw capers 233.84 mg/100 g, lovage leaves 170.00, dock (Rumex) 86.20, radish leaves 70.37, wild rocket 66.19, fresh dill 55.15, coriander leaves 52.90, yellow wax hot peppers 50.63, fennel leaves 48.80 and red onion 39.21, against habitual intakes of tens of milligrams a day, so naming capers as the only rich source overstated how narrow the food route is. Taurine is present in animal food only, richest in shellfish, with beef muscle at 61.1 mg/100 g wet weight and chicken thigh at 47.34 mg/100 g; it was undetectable in all 48 plant foods Laidlaw analysed, so a vegan diet supplies none, while quercetin intake is unaffected by avoiding animal food. The two compounds are RICH for opposite populations, which is why they are described separately throughout this entry.

| Hard clam meat, Mercenaria mercenaria (taurine) | 2,434.33 mg/100 g DRY weight; approximately 410-490 mg/100 g as eaten if 80-83% moisture is assumed. The dry-weight value is sourced; the wet-weight conversion is this dataset's own assumption and is unconfirmed. | 1 serving of clam meat, 100 g as eaten | approximately 410-490 mg (estimated from a dry-weight value) | 14-16% of a 3 g taurine dose (estimate) | Li Z et al.
