| Juniper berries (quercetin) | 46.61 mg quercetin | a few berries, 2 g | 0.9 mg | 0.2% of a 500 mg quercetin dose | USDA Flavonoid Database Release 3.3 (2018), value identified by the verification report; NDB number not stated there. |

- **To match the supplement dose:** QUERCETIN: trials use 500-1,000 mg/day. From raw capers at 233.84 mg/100 g that is 214-428 g per day, 14 to 29 tablespoons of a salt-cured condiment and a salt load nobody should take; from dock leaves at 86.20 mg/100 g it is 580 g to 1.16 kg; from wild rocket at 66.19 mg/100 g it is 756 g to 1.51 kg; from raw red onion at 39.21 mg/100 g it is 1.28-2.55 kg; from apples with skin at 4.01 mg/100 g it is 12.5-25 kg, about 70 to 140 apples. A 500 mg capsule is roughly 25 to 50 times what a diet delivers. TAURINE: trials use 1-6 g/day, commonly 3 g. From beef at 61.1 mg/100 g that is 4.9 kg of raw steak per day, from chicken thigh at 47.34 mg/100 g it is 6.3 kg, and from clam meat at roughly 450 mg/100 g as eaten it is about 670 g of clam meat daily, the only route within an order of magnitude of feasible and still nothing a person eats. Neither dose is reachable by eating normally.
- **Food versus supplement:** QUERCETIN, MAJOR. Supplements are almost always the aglycone, extracted from Sophora japonica flower buds or Dimorphandra mollis pods, which are not foods anyone eats; food quercetin is glycosides (glucosides in onions, rutin in tea and apples). The forms are not interchangeable in the gut, and a 500-1,000 mg aglycone dose is a pharmacological exposure that inhibits drug-metabolising enzymes and transporters, whereas 10-30 mg/day from onions and apples never approaches it. Capers are the richest source per 100 g and the worst practical recommendation, since the value is for a salt-cured condiment eaten a tablespoon at a time and the canned figure ranges 45.05-519.85 mg/100 g across 20 samples, an elevenfold spread; fresh herbs, dock, radish leaves and wild rocket at 48-86 mg/100 g are the more usable everyday sources. Apples with skin are the classic textbook answer and are roughly 60 times too dilute; peeling drops them from 4.01 to 1.05 mg/100 g. TAURINE, MODERATE. The supplement is synthetic and chemically identical to food taurine, so there is no extract-versus-food problem here. The two real caveats are analytical and culinary. Shellfish taurine numbers in circulation are often dry-weight figures presented as if they were per 100 g of food, which inflates them four- to sixfold; the three bivalve rows above are dry-weight values from an umami study, converted with an assumed 80-83% moisture that is labelled as an assumption in each row. And taurine is a free amino acid rather than protein-bound, so it leaches: taurine and creatine were the compounds most reduced by cooking beef, partly into the cooking juices (Purchas RW, Rutherfurd SM, Pearce PD, Vather R, Wilkinson BHP, Meat Sci 2004;68(2):201-7, PMID 22062229; the companion 90 min at 70 C result is from lamb muscle, Purchas RW et al., Meat Sci 2004;66(3):629-37, PMID 22060873). A stew keeps its taurine in the broth; a grilled steak with the juices discarded does not.
- **Absorption:** QUERCETIN, quantified and decisive. In healthy ileostomy volunteers absorption was 52 plus or minus 15% for quercetin glucosides from fried onions, 17 plus or minus 15% for quercetin rutinoside (the main form in tea and apples) and 24 plus or minus 9% for pure quercetin aglycone, the form used in supplements; conjugation with glucose enhances absorption (Hollman PC, de Vries JH, van Leeuwen SD, Mengelers MJ, Katan MB, Am J Clin Nutr 1995;62(6):1276-82, PMID 7491892, citation and values confirmed). So per milligram, onion quercetin is roughly twice as absorbable as the capsule and three times as absorbable as tea or apple rutin, which narrows the effective gap a little and nowhere near enough to close it, since urinary recovery was only 0.5% of the amount absorbed. TAURINE. It is present in food as the free amino acid rather than bound in protein, absorbed by the TauT transporter with high oral bioavailability, and the supplement form is identical, so absorption is not where food loses. Food loses on concentration and on cooking: the content of a portion depends heavily on whether the cooking liquid is consumed, and reported values vary severalfold with muscle type (beef taurine ranged 0.552 to 23.861 umol/g across 21 muscles, PMID 30811817) and with breed in poultry (39.29-47.34 mg/100 g, PMID 36230082). Reading dry-weight shellfish values as wet-weight is the single commonest error in this literature, and it is the weak point of the shellfish rows here.
- **Data note:** overall, and the two halves differ. The quercetin block is the most thoroughly verified material in the submission: all eight original values, their sample counts, min-max ranges and NDB numbers matched Flavonoid Release 3.3 exactly, and Hollman 1995 and the 74% peeling loss were confirmed. The taurine rows are weaker. The three bivalve figures are dry-weight values from an umami and taste-compound study, located in Figure 1B rather than the cited Table 3, converted to as-eaten values with a self-supplied 80-83% moisture assumption, and one of them (Kumamoto oyster) was wrong at source by about 11%. Laidlaw 1990, which analysed 29 meats on an as-eaten basis, was cited only for the negative plant finding, so no wet-weight primary source backs any shellfish row

*References: Tzang PMID 38755142; Singh Science 2023; Marcangeli Aging Cell 2025 PMC12507425; Serban PMID 27405810; Arabi PMID 37654199*

> **Skip does not mean dangerous.** Everything in this section is a money problem rather than a safety problem, with three exceptions flagged in their Cons: curcumin and green tea extract appear on liver-injury registries, and L-glutamine carries a mortality signal in the critically ill.

## 7. Avoid it

### Beta-carotene supplements

**Verdict:** AVOID &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** No effective dose exists. The doses tested and found harmful were 20 mg/d and 30 mg/d combined with retinyl palmitate.
- **Who it is for:** Essentially nobody at supplemental doses. Narrow exceptions: clinical vitamin A deficiency and erythropoietic protoporphyria.

**Pros**

- Dietary carotenoids from vegetables are not implicated in any of the harm below. Eating carrots is fine.

**Cons**

- ATBC (n=29,133 male smokers, Finland, 5-8 years): 20 mg/d increased lung cancer incidence by 18% (95% CI 3 to 36%) and total mortality by 8% (1 to 16%).
- CARET (n=18,314 smokers, former smokers and asbestos-exposed workers): lung cancer RR 1.28 (1.04-1.57), all-cause death RR 1.17 (1.03-1.33), lung cancer death RR 1.46 (1.07-2.00). Stopped early.
- Cochrane, 78 randomised trials and 296,707 participants: beta-carotene increased mortality, RR 1.05 (1.01-1.09).
- This is the canonical case where the industry own logic failed in public. The observational data were strong, the mechanism was plausible, the trials were large and well conducted, and the answer was that the pill killed people.
- Absolute avoidance in current smokers, former smokers and anyone with asbestos exposure. USPSTF grade D recommendation.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose partly reachable
- **Data confidence:** Medium

Carrots, carrot juice, sweet potato, squash and dark leafy greens are dense, ordinary sources: baked sweet potato flesh carries 11,509 µg beta-carotene per 100 g, carrot juice about 9,300 and raw carrot 8,285. Red palm oil is denser again at roughly 20,000 to 40,000 µg total carotenes per 100 g. By mass the 20 to 30 mg doses used in the intervention trials sit within reach of a few large portions, which makes this the one case where food and supplement look superficially comparable and behave completely differently.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|

- **To match the supplement dose:** By label mass the trial doses are reachable: 20 mg/day is about 241 g of raw carrot (four medium carrots), 174 g of baked sweet potato, or 215 g of carrot juice, and 30 mg/day is about 362 g of raw carrot. Carrot juice is the case that really tests this, because it is the one form anyone actually consumes in the volume required. By absorbed dose the answer flips. The Dietary Reference Intake conversion factors treat 12 µg of food beta-carotene as equivalent to 1 µg retinol activity against 2 µg for supplemental beta-carotene in oil, implying the supplement form is about six times more available (Institute of Medicine, DRIs for Vitamin A and related micronutrients, 2001, DOI 10.17226/10026). Matching the absorbed exposure of a 20 mg capsule therefore takes on the order of 120 mg of food beta-carotene, roughly 1.45 kg of raw carrots daily, which nobody eats and the gut would not absorb anyway. Food matches the label number and does not match the plasma exposure that produced the trial results.
- **Food versus supplement:** This is the clearest food-versus-extract case in the set. Supplement beta-carotene is synthetic all-trans-beta-carotene or algal/fungal beta-carotene, formulated in oil or water-dispersible beadlets specifically to defeat the poor bioavailability of the food form, so it is engineered to do what a carrot cannot. Food never delivers beta-carotene alone: the same carrot supplies 3,477 µg/100 g of alpha-carotene, red palm oil is roughly a third alpha-carotene, and dark greens supply lutein with less beta-carotene. Dietary carotenoids carry none of the supplement harm for three reasons. First, uptake and cleavage are feedback-regulated, since absorbed beta-carotene raises intracellular retinoic acid, which induces ISX, which represses SR-B1 and BCO1 in the enterocyte, so absorption falls as vitamin A status rises (Reboul E, Nutrients 2019;11(4):838, PMID 31013870; the ISX loop itself traces to Lobo et al. and Widjaja-Adhi et al.). A capsule of purified beta-carotene in oil pushes plasma concentrations well past anything food produces, and the IOM 2:1 factor strictly applies to purified beta-carotene in oil, so applying it to beadlet formulations is an approximation. Second, the harm signals came from isolated high doses in high-risk groups: 20 mg/day raised lung cancer incidence by 18% in male smokers (ATBC, N Engl J Med 1994;330:1029-35, PMID 8127329) and 30 mg/day plus 25,000 IU retinyl palmitate produced about 28% more lung cancers, weighted incidence RR 1.36 (95% CI 1.07 to 1.73, P = .01) with mortality RR 1.59, in smokers and asbestos-exposed workers (CARET, Omenn et al., J Natl Cancer Inst 1996;88:1550-9, PMID 8901853). CARET cannot be attributed to beta-carotene alone, since it co-administered a large preformed retinol dose. Third, overloading on carrots produces carotenodermia, a harmless orange discolouration, rather than hypervitaminosis A, because the conversion step is regulated. Eating carrots and taking 20 mg of beta-carotene are two different interventions. Several everyday sources are absent from the table only because no figure was available to tabulate: dried parsley, basil and coriander, paprika, fresh apricots, persimmon, papaya, sweet red pepper, spring greens and collards, watercress, Swiss chard and romaine lettuce, all of which outrank the baby spinach originally chosen as the green exemplar.
- **Absorption:** Beta-carotene in raw carrot and raw spinach is locked in chromoplasts behind intact plant cell walls, so absorption from raw vegetables is low; chopping, pureeing, juicing, cooking and eating with fat all raise it, and cooking does not destroy it. The formal expression of this is the 12:1 food against 2:1 supplement-in-oil conversion factor (IOM 2001, DOI 10.17226/10026). One retention argument from the original entry has to be withdrawn: boiled carrot at 9,616 µg/100 g against raw carrot at 8,285 now compares a CoFID value with a USDA one, so it cannot be read as a within-dataset cooking effect. Conversion efficiency varies substantially between individuals through BCO1 genotype and vitamin A status, which is why plasma responses to the same carrot intake differ several-fold between people (PMID 31013870, a review stretched somewhat to cover the genotype point). Spinach needs its own correction: it is not the weak green the submission made it, since USDA raw spinach at 5,626 µg/100 g is about two thirds of raw carrot rather than under a fifth, and the honest caution about it is the oxalate load for people managing kidney stones.
- **Data note:** The load-bearing values are now USDA-anchored, since five food figures were checkable and have been replaced (raw carrot, baked sweet potato, raw and cooked spinach, dried apricots, butter beta-carotene), and the two OK values (kale, mango) were within 9% of USDA. The dose arithmetic and the IOM conversion factors are confirmed. What holds confidence down is that four remaining rows are unconfirmed CoFID codes, two of which now sit oddly against USDA values for the same vegetable (boiled sweet potato at about half the USDA baked figure), the published range for kale is unusually wide, and the four added foods rest on single indicative figures

*References: ATBC PMID 8127329; CARET PMID 8602180; Cochrane Bjelakovic PMID 22419320; USPSTF PMID 35727271*

### Vitamin E at or above 400 IU/d

**Verdict:** AVOID &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** No effective preventive dose exists. 400 IU/d was the harmful dose. ATBC low-dose 50 mg/d produced neither benefit nor lung cancer harm.
- **Who it is for:** Documented deficiency, which in practice means fat malabsorption: abetalipoproteinaemia, cholestatic liver disease, cystic fibrosis, short bowel.

**Pros**

- Ataxia with vitamin E deficiency requires high-dose replacement, and that is a genuine indication.
- There is a narrow niche in biopsy-proven non-diabetic non-alcoholic steatohepatitis, where 800 IU/d improved histology in the PIVENS trial. That is a hepatology decision, not a self-care one.

**Cons**

- A dose-response meta-analysis of 19 trials and 135,967 participants found high-dose trials produced 39 additional deaths per 10,000 persons (95% CI 3 to 74, P=0.035). Low-dose trials showed -16 per 10,000.
- SELECT (n=34,887 men): 400 IU/d of synthetic all-rac-alpha-tocopheryl acetate raised prostate cancer incidence by 17%, HR 1.17 (99% CI 1.004-1.36, P=0.008). The excess emerged in extended follow-up after supplementation had already stopped.
- Haemorrhagic stroke RR 1.22 (1.00-1.48) across 9 trials and 118,765 participants, which is one additional haemorrhagic stroke per 1,250 people treated.
- Contraindicated with warfarin, direct oral anticoagulants, aspirin, clopidogrel and other antiplatelets, because impaired coagulation is the endpoint EFSA used to set its limit.
- USPSTF grade D. Note the limits diverge sharply: 1,000 mg/d supplemental alpha-tocopherol in the US, but 300 mg/d from ALL sources in the EU.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Medium

Alpha-tocopherol is abundant in ordinary foods: seed and nut oils, sunflower seeds, almonds, hazelnuts, paprika and wheatgerm all supply meaningful amounts, and a normal diet covers the 15 mg/day RDA without effort. This is the clearest case in the group where the two questions come apart, because the nutrient is genuinely rich in food while the dose that defines this item is not obtainable from any diet. The original single RICH label sat directly above the entry's own sentence saying the dose 'cannot be reached by eating', which is why the two answers are now separated.

- **To match the supplement dose:** 400 IU of natural vitamin E is 268 mg of RRR-alpha-tocopherol. Reaching that every day from food means about 762 g of sunflower seeds (roughly 4,450 kcal), or 1.05 kg of almonds, or 179 g of wheatgerm oil (about 13 tablespoons of neat oil), or 550 g of sunflower oil at the CoFID value and 654 g at USDA's lower one. If the capsule is synthetic all-rac (180 mg for 400 IU), it is still 512 g of sunflower seeds or about 8.9 tablespoons of wheatgerm oil daily. Habitual intakes cluster around 5 to 20 mg/day, so 400 IU sits one to two orders of magnitude above anything eating produces. No dietary pattern gets there.
- **Food versus supplement:** Most 400 IU capsules contain all-rac-alpha-tocopheryl acetate, a synthetic mixture of eight stereoisomers of which only one eighth is the RRR form that human alpha-tocopherol transfer protein preferentially retains. Food contains only RRR, which is exactly why the label conversions differ (1 IU = 0.67 mg natural against 0.45 mg synthetic). Food also never delivers alpha-tocopherol in isolation: sunflower oil carries 48.7 mg alpha with 5.1 mg gamma per 100 g, rapeseed oil carries 18.4 mg alpha with 38.0 mg gamma (CoFID 17-045 and 17-041, both unconfirmed), red palm oil is tocotrienol-rich, and nuts add tocotrienols too. High-dose alpha-tocopherol actively removes part of what food supplies: 400 IU/day of RRR-alpha-tocopheryl acetate cut serum gamma-tocopherol by a median 58% (95% CI 51 to 66%) and reduced detectable delta-tocopherol in a two-month randomised placebo-controlled trial of 184 adult nonsmokers (Huang HY and Appel LJ, J Nutr 2003;133:3137-40, PMID 14519797, all elements confirmed). A 400 IU capsule is therefore something other than a concentrated handful of sunflower seeds; it shifts the balance of vitamin E forms in blood in a direction food never does.
- **Absorption:** Alpha-tocopherol needs dietary fat and bile for absorption, which nuts and oils supply intrinsically, so food forms are absorbed efficiently in ordinary meals. Fractional absorption falls as the single dose rises, and hepatic alpha-TTP selectively retains only the 2R stereoisomers, so the 2S half of a synthetic all-rac dose is largely excreted. The useful fraction of a 400 IU synthetic capsule is therefore well below its stated IU, plasma alpha-tocopherol does not scale linearly with dose, and the displacement of gamma- and delta-tocopherol documented by Huang et al. (PMID 14519797) does.
- **Data note:** Six of the food values were checkable against USDA and have been replaced or ranged, so the corrected list is USDA-anchored where it matters most. The dose arithmetic is the strongest part of the item: the 268 mg and 180 mg conversions and every food requirement recompute exactly, and the conclusion is insensitive to residual uncertainty of a few tens of percent. Against that, the sunflower oil figure is unverifiable, several added foods (hazelnut, almond, safflower and cottonseed oils, paprika, peanuts, corn and soybean oils) rest on single indicative figures from the verification report, and the remaining CoFID rows could not be confirmed

*References: SELECT Klein PMID 21990298; Miller PMID 15537682; Schurks BMJ PMID 21051774; USPSTF PMID 35727271; EFSA J 2024 PMID 39099617*

### Selenium at 200 µg/d

**Verdict:** AVOID &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** RDA is 55 µg/d and food intake meets it in most Western diets. 200 µg/d is the dose used in the failed and harmful trials.
- **Who it is for:** People with genuinely low selenium status: low-soil regions, long-term parenteral nutrition, severe malabsorption.

**Pros**

- It prevents Keshan disease, a selenium-deficiency cardiomyopathy, in genuinely deficient populations. That is a real and important indication.
- There is a Grade B signal for mild Graves orbitopathy in selenium-suboptimal European populations at 200 µg/d for 6 months, resting on a single 159-patient trial.

**Cons**

- Cochrane, 10 randomised trials and 27,232 participants: any cancer RR 1.01 (0.93-1.10) from the low-risk-of-bias studies. That is HIGH-certainty evidence of NO benefit. The cancer-prevention reputation rests on a secondary endpoint of a 1996 skin cancer trial that was not designed to test it.
- SELECT (n=35,533 men, 200 µg/d L-selenomethionine) found prostate cancer HR 1.09 (99% CI 0.93-1.27). No preventive effect, and the trial stopped early for futility.
- Incident type 2 diabetes hazard ratio 1.55 (1.03-2.33) over a mean 7.7 years at 200 µg/d, with excess risk concentrated in those already in the highest tertile of baseline plasma selenium. This harm appears at HALF the US upper limit.
- The therapeutic window is narrow. EFSA lowered its upper limit to 255 µg/d in 2023 with alopecia as the critical endpoint, and derived its lowest-observed-adverse-effect level of 330 µg/d from the SELECT trial itself.
- Stacking is how people get there: a 200 µg tablet on top of a multivitamin containing 55-70 µg plus dietary intake.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** High

Selenium is genuinely abundant in ordinary food, and offal and marine fish carry it most reliably: braised pork kidney is 312 ug per 100 g, braised lamb kidney 219 ug, cooked yellowfin tuna 108 ug and braised lamb's liver 111 ug. Brazil nuts are the richest known food source at a USDA average of 1,920 ug/100 g, about 96 ug in a single 5 g kernel, which makes them the one food where a supplement dose is met by two nuts, and that is a hazard rather than a reassurance because their selenium is set by the soil the tree grew in and varies more than thirty-fold between Amazon regions. Reference points used in the table: EFSA AI 70 ug/d for adults, and the EFSA tolerable upper intake level of 255 ug/d for adult men and women including pregnancy and lactation, set from alopecia as the critical endpoint with a LOAEL of 330 ug/d taken from the SELECT trial and an uncertainty factor of 1.3 (EFSA Journal 2023;21(1):e07704, DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2023.7704; AI from EFSA, Dietary Reference Values for nutrients Summary report, doi 10.2903/sp.efsa.2017.e15121, Tables 5 and 7). The 200 ug/d dose comes from two landmark trials: selenised yeast in the Nutritional Prevention of Cancer trial and 200 ug/d L-selenomethionine in SELECT.

Replaces the raw record (171978, 19.7 ug/100 g), which halved the figure while the zinc item used the cooked and canned records; canned is 35.8 ug/100 g (171981). |
| Beef liver, braised | 36.1 ug | 1 slice (68 g) | 24.5 ug | 35% of AI; 12% of dose | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168626 (SR Legacy) |
| Sunflower seed kernels, dry roasted | 79.3 to 80.4 ug | 1 oz (28 g) | 22.2 to 22.5 ug | 32% of AI; 11% of dose | USDA FoodData Central FDC IDs 170563 and 325524, per-100 g range supplied by the verification report. Portion conventional, per-portion derived. |
| Wheat germ, crude | 79.2 ug | 1 oz (28 g) | 22.2 ug | 32% of AI; 11% of dose | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168892 (SR Legacy), per-100 g value supplied by the verification report. Like all plant selenium this tracks the soil the wheat grew in; the value is North American. Portion conventional, per-portion derived. |
| Egg, whole, Grade A large | 31.1 ug | 1 large egg without shell (50 g) | 15.6 ug | 22% of AI; 8% of dose | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 748967 (Foundation Foods) |
| Brazil nuts, Mato Grosso state (low-selenium soil) | 207 ug (median 2.07 mg/kg) | 1 kernel (5 g) | 10.4 ug | 15% of AI; 5% of the 200 ug dose from the same-looking nut | PMID 28923728, on USDA's 5.0 g kernel measure |
| Wholemeal bread, US wheat | 25.7 ug | 1 slice (32 g) | 8.2 ug | 12% of AI; 4% of dose. Does NOT transfer to UK or EU wheat | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 172688 (SR Legacy); regional caveat PMID 18346308 |

- **To match the supplement dose:** There is almost no gap, and that is the finding. 200 ug/d is met by 2.1 average Brazil nuts (10 g), or 64 g of braised pork kidney, or 91 g of braised lamb kidney, or 185 g of cooked yellowfin tuna, or 180 g of braised lamb's liver, or 223 g of cooked blue mussels, or 243 g of simmered chicken liver, or 263 g of canned light tuna in oil, or 532 g of cooked cod, or 4 tins of sardines, or 13 eggs. Kidney and Brazil nuts overshoot in one normal portion: an 85 g portion of braised pork kidney is 265 ug, already 133 percent of the supplement dose and above the EFSA upper level on its own, and an 85 g portion of braised lamb kidney is 186 ug, 93 percent of the dose. Because the dose is so easy to reach, the useful calculation runs the other way. The EFSA AI is 70 ug/d and the EFSA upper level is 255 ug/d, a working margin of about 185 ug. A person on a 200 ug/d supplement plus an ordinary diet is at roughly 270 ug and already over the upper level; add two average Brazil nuts and they are at about 462 ug. The UL derives from alopecia observed in SELECT at a LOAEL of 330 ug/d, so this is an observed human effect at achievable intakes rather than a theoretical ceiling (DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2023.7704). Selenium is the case where food easily matches the supplement, and the honest advice is to pick one route.
- **Food versus supplement:** Brazil nuts are the textbook answer and a genuinely bad way to dose selenium deliberately, because the dose is unknowable at the point of eating. Median selenium varied from 2.07 mg/kg in Mato Grosso state to 68.15 mg/kg in Amazonas, which the authors attribute to soil selenium, soil pH and texture, location and genotype (PMID 28923728). On the 5 g kernel used throughout this item's table, one nut therefore supplies about 15 percent to about 490 percent of the 70 ug adult AI. The paper's own '11 percent to 288 percent' phrasing implies a 3.0 to 3.7 g kernel and cannot stand alongside a 5 g table, so the figures have been put on one basis. Nuts from the two extremes are visually identical, are not labelled by state of origin, and are routinely blended, so 'two Brazil nuts a day' is a dose ranging from about 20 ug to about 680 ug depending on a fact the eater cannot access, spanning deficient to well over the upper level. On form equivalence, selenium is unusual in a helpful way: the dominant species in Brazil nuts and cereals is selenomethionine, the same species used as the supplement in SELECT, so food and that supplement are pharmacologically comparable (PMID 18346307). But 200 ug of selenium is not one thing. The selenised yeast used in the earlier NPC trial was a poorly speciated mixture whose delivered selenium was variable, and the two trials produced opposite results, so the number 200 ug carries no single meaning across the literature (PMID 18346308, PMID 18346307). Finally, the case for supplementing collapses in a selenium-replete person: SELECT tested 200 ug/d L-selenomethionine in selenium-replete men, found no protection against prostate or other major cancers, and produced the alopecia signal that now defines the UL.
- **Absorption:** Selenium bioavailability depends on chemical species more than on food matrix. Selenomethionine, the form in Brazil nuts, cereals and meat, is absorbed almost completely and is incorporated non-specifically into body proteins as a storage pool, which is why it raises plasma selenium readily and why its kinetics differ from inorganic selenite and selenate (PMID 18346307, PMID 28412747). That has a practical consequence the composition tables hide: the selenium content of any plant food is a proxy for the soil it grew in rather than a property of the species. The USDA wholemeal bread figure of 25.7 ug/100 g and the wheat germ figure of 79.2 ug/100 g both reflect North American wheat grown on selenium-rich soils. European and UK wheat grows on selenium-poor soils, so the same bread and the same wheat germ cannot be assumed to deliver the same selenium, and the wide between-country variation in intake driven by exactly this mechanism is the central theme of the food-chain intake review (PMID 18346308). Animal foods hold their values better across countries, because livestock feed is commonly selenium-supplemented, which is why kidney, liver, tuna, mussels and eggs travel better than bread or nuts do. Ranking for a reader who wants reliable selenium without a variable dose: kidney and liver first, then marine fish and shellfish including canned tuna and mussels, eggs as a steady background contributor, and Brazil nuts last precisely because they are the richest source.
- **Data note:** Every original per-100 g figure matched FoodData Central to the digit (312, 1,920, 108, 111, 37.6, 52.7, 36.1, 31.1, 25.7, 19.7), the Brazil nut state medians were confirmed verbatim from PMID 28923728, and the EFSA UL derivation was confirmed word for word: alopecia as critical endpoint, LOAEL 330 ug/d from SELECT, uncertainty factor 1.3, UL 255 ug/d including pregnancy and lactation. EFSA itself notes that consumers are unlikely to exceed the UL except regular users of food supplements or regular consumers of Brazil nuts. The seven added rows use conventional portion sizes rather than USDA measure-table entries, so their per-portion figures are derived and flagged in place

*References: Cochrane Vinceti PMID 29376219; SELECT PMID 19066370 and PMID 21990298; Stranges PMID 17620655; EFSA 2023*

### Preformed vitamin A at or above 10,000 IU

**Verdict:** AVOID &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** RDA 900 µg RAE for men and 700 µg for women. The upper limit is 3,000 µg/d, which is exactly 10,000 IU.
- **Who it is for:** Clinical vitamin A deficiency in low-income settings, fat malabsorption, cystic fibrosis. In replete Western adults, nobody.

**Pros**

- WHO supplementation saves lives in vitamin-A-deficient children, particularly in measles. That is one of the highest-value public health interventions in existence, and it is not what an over-the-counter 10,000 IU capsule is for.

**Cons**

- CARET showed net harm: 25,000 IU/d of retinyl palmitate plus 30 mg beta-carotene gave all-cause death RR 1.17 (1.03-1.33) and lung cancer death RR 1.46 (1.07-2.00).
- Over-the-counter products at 10,000 IU/d sit exactly AT the upper limit, leaving zero headroom for diet, liver consumption or a second supplement.
- It is teratogenic. Preformed vitamin A above the upper limit in pregnancy is a hard contraindication, and prenatal products should use beta-carotene rather than retinol.
- Unlike beta-carotene, preformed vitamin A is not subject to feedback regulation on absorption, so chronic excess accumulates and causes hepatotoxicity, intracranial hypertension and bone loss.
- Contraindicated in liver disease of any kind, since retinol is stored in and toxic to the liver.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** Low

Liver is far more concentrated in preformed retinol than the supplement: 10,000 IU is 3,000 µg retinol, and every common liver in the UK tables carries 10,500 to 25,200 µg per 100 g. Hard cheese, butter, fortified fat spreads and egg yolk supply an everyday retinol baseline of tens to about a hundred micrograms per portion on top of that. This is the one item in the group where the food beats the pill, which is why liver carries a public-health warning rather than a recommendation.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Liver, calf, fried in corn oil | 25,200 µg retinol (84,000 IU) | 1 serving (100 g) | 25,200 µg (84,000 IU) | 840% of a 10,000 IU dose; 3,600% of the 700 µg UK RNI | McCance and Widdowson CoFID 2021, food code 18-410 (10 samples); indicative and unconfirmed. The CoFID dataset could not be retrieved, so no code in this liver series is corroborated. All five IU conversions and the 12 g equivalence recompute exactly and the magnitudes match published liver retinol data |
| Liver, pig, stewed | 22,600 µg retinol (75,300 IU) | 1 serving (100 g) | 22,600 µg (75,300 IU) | 753% of a 10,000 IU dose; 3,229% of the 700 µg RNI | McCance and Widdowson CoFID 2021, food code 18-418 (18 samples); indicative and unconfirmed |
| Liver, lamb, fried in corn oil | 19,700 µg retinol (65,700 IU) | 1 serving (100 g) | 19,700 µg (65,700 IU) | 657% of a 10,000 IU dose; 2,814% of the 700 µg RNI | McCance and Widdowson CoFID 2021, food code 18-494 (10 samples); indicative and unconfirmed |
| Liver, ox, stewed | 17,300 µg retinol (57,700 IU) | 1 serving (100 g) | 17,300 µg (57,700 IU) | 577% of a 10,000 IU dose; 2,471% of the 700 µg RNI | McCance and Widdowson CoFID 2021, food code 18-416 (18 samples); indicative and unconfirmed |
| Liver, chicken, fried in corn oil | 10,500 µg retinol (35,000 IU) | 1 serving (100 g) | 10,500 µg (35,000 IU) | 350% of a 10,000 IU dose; 1,500% of the 700 µg RNI | McCance and Widdowson CoFID 2021, food code 18-412 (10 samples); indicative and unconfirmed |

Per-portion arithmetic recomputes exactly, provenance not confirmed |
| Cheddar, hard cheese | about 325 µg retinol (1,083 IU) | 1 matchbox piece (30 g) | 98 µg (325 IU) | 3.3% of a 10,000 IU dose; 14% of the 700 µg RNI | Figure as given in the verification report's missing-foods list; indicative and unconfirmed. Added because omitting the dairy tier while listing butter and egg yolk understates the everyday retinol baseline |
| Margarine and fat spread, fortified | about 800 to 900 µg retinol (2,670 to 3,000 IU) | 1 pat (10 g) | 80 to 90 µg (267 to 300 IU) | 2.7% to 3.0% of a 10,000 IU dose; 11% to 13% of the 700 µg RNI | Range as given in the verification report's missing-foods list; indicative and unconfirmed. Added because fortified spread matches or beats butter, is eaten the same way, and was missing from a UK-framed list |
| Butter, salted | 671 µg retinol (2,237 IU); USDA also gives 684 µg RAE/100 g | 1 pat (10 g) | 67 µg (224 IU) | 2.2% of a 10,000 IU dose; 10% of the 700 µg RNI | USDA salted butter, 33.55 µg retinol per 5 g = 671 µg/100 g, via FDC mirror. Replaces the submission's 958 µg/100 g, which ran 43% high, and the per-pat figures of 96 µg / 319 IU / 14% |
| Egg yolk, chicken, raw | 371 µg retinol (1,237 IU) | 1 large yolk (17 g) | 63 µg (210 IU) | 2.1% of a 10,000 IU dose; 9% of the 700 µg RNI | USDA raw egg yolk, 63.07 µg retinol per 17 g = 371 µg/100 g, via FDC mirror. Replaces the submission's 447 µg/100 g, which ran 17% high, and the per-yolk figures of 76 µg / 253 IU / 11% |

- **To match the supplement dose:** There is no gap to close. About 12 g of fried calf liver supplies the full 3,000 µg (10,000 IU) of a high-dose retinol capsule, and a normal 100 g portion of any liver delivers 3.5 to 8.4 times that dose in one sitting. A 40 g serving of liver pate is functionally a 10,000 IU tablet at 2,920 µg (9,730 IU). A tablespoon of cod liver oil is 2,448 µg (8,160 IU). Anyone eating liver weekly is already taking supraphysiological preformed vitamin A intermittently, and a 10,000 IU supplement on top compounds a load the body stores rather than clears. For scale, the US tolerable upper intake level for preformed vitamin A is 3,000 µg/day, which is the supplement dose itself, against a UK RNI of 700 µg for men and 600 µg for women.
- **Food versus supplement:** The retinol in liver is the same molecule as the retinyl palmitate or acetate in capsules, absorbed by the same route and stored in the liver the same way, so the usual reassurance that food forms are safer does not apply here. Pregnancy is the sharp edge: UK advice is that people who are pregnant or who might become pregnant should avoid liver and liver products including pate, and should avoid supplements containing vitamin A including fish liver oils, because excess retinol is teratogenic (NHS pregnancy vitamins guidance). Cod liver oil is the common trap, bought for omega-3 or vitamin D and delivering 60,000 IU of retinol per 100 g, and the same trap has sharper versions that are eaten as ordinary food rather than dosed: canned cod liver (Icelandic and Polish tinned), which carries retinol in the cod-liver-oil range, and halibut liver oil, which is more concentrated again and sold in the same aisle. Liver is also eaten unnoticed as liver sausage, liverwurst, haggis, foie gras, and goose and duck liver, all in the same range as the livers tabulated. Outside pregnancy, chronic preformed retinol above the 3,000 µg/day UL is associated with reduced bone mineral density and fracture risk, which is why UK advice is to eat liver no more than once a week. Beta-carotene-rich foods are the safe substitute precisely because their conversion is regulated.
- **Absorption:** Retinyl esters from liver and from supplements are absorbed at high efficiency and, critically, absorption is not down-regulated when stores are full, so intake accumulates in hepatic stellate cells over weeks and months. Carotenoid absorption works the opposite way, feedback-limited through retinoic-acid induction of the intestinal transcription factor ISX and consequent repression of SR-B1 and BCO1 (reviewed in Reboul E, Nutrients 2019;11(4):838, PMID 31013870, DOI 10.3390/nu11040838; the feedback loop itself originates with Lobo et al. and Widjaja-Adhi et al.). Cooking concentrates liver retinol rather than destroying it, through water loss: CoFID shows raw lamb liver at 17,300 µg/100 g rising to 19,700 fried, and raw ox liver at 14,200 rising to 17,300 stewed (codes 18-413, 18-494, 18-415, 18-416, all unconfirmed along with the rest of the CoFID series).
- **Data note:** The item's central claim is robust, since the conclusion that food exceeds the supplement dose survives any plausible revision of the numbers, and every IU conversion and dose equivalence recomputes exactly. But the entire liver series, which carries the whole argument, rests on McCance and Widdowson CoFID 2021 codes that could not be retrieved or confirmed, and the two values in this item that were checkable against USDA both came back overstated (butter retinol by 43%, egg yolk by 17%), so the precision quoted for the liver figures is not yet earned. The added cheddar and fortified spread figures are indicative only

*References: CARET PMID 8602180; Cochrane PMID 22419320; IOM DRI NBK56068*

### High-dose vitamin B6

**Verdict:** AVOID &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** RDA is 1.3 mg/d. EFSA set the upper limit at 12 mg/d in 2023. The US IOM figure is still 100 mg/d.
- **Who it is for:** People on isoniazid, cycloserine, hydralazine or penicillamine, where B6 is protective against drug-induced neuropathy. Pregnant women with nausea and vomiting, at obstetric direction.

**Pros**

- It genuinely prevents isoniazid-induced peripheral neuropathy at 10-50 mg/d, which is a real and important indication.
- Pyridoxine with doxylamine is a first-line option for nausea and vomiting of pregnancy, ahead of antiemetics.

**Cons**

- This is the clearest case in this document where the product category actively causes the harm it claims to prevent. Nerve support, neuropathy and PMS formulas routinely deliver 50-100 mg of B6 to people with no deficiency, which is four to eight times the current European upper limit.
- Sensory peripheral neuropathy is the dose- and duration-dependent toxicity: numbness, burning, ataxia and loss of proprioception. EFSA set its 12 mg/d limit on exactly this, from a reference point of 50 mg/d in case reports.
- Choosing pyridoxal-5-phosphate (P5P) at a premium does NOT prevent it. The toxicity is not form-specific.
- Audit your total intake. Anyone on a B-complex, a fortified energy drink and a nerve or stress formula can easily stack past 100 mg without noticing.
- An observational safety signal exists: in men, a 10-year average intake above 20 mg/d from individual supplements gave lung cancer HR 1.82 (1.25-2.65). Confounding by smoking is possible, but the direction is not reassuring.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** High

Vitamin B6 itself is abundant and widely distributed, with fish, organ meat, poultry, potatoes, bananas, seeds, nuts and fortified cereals all carrying useful amounts, and dietary deficiency uncommon in people eating normally. The marketed high-dose object is a different thing: pyridoxine hydrochloride, a manufactured crystalline salt sold at 50, 100 and sometimes 250-500 mg per tablet, which is 40 to 400 times the 1.3 mg adult RDA and has no dietary counterpart at that concentration. Both statements are true at once, which is why the label is split here: the molecule is RICH in food (so RICH stands rather than being reclassified as synthetic), while the product as sold is unreachable by eating, which is what dose_from_food now records. All percentages below use the 1.3 mg RDA for ages 19-50; over 50 the RDA rises to 1.7 mg for men and 1.5 mg for women, so these percentages overstate the contribution for older adults.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Salmon, Atlantic, farmed, cooked, dry heat | 0.647 mg | half fillet (178 g) | 1.152 mg | 89% of the 1.3 mg RDA (ages 19-50); 1.2% of a 100 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175168, SR Legacy |
| Yellowfin tuna, fresh, raw | 0.933 mg | 3 oz (85 g) | 0.793 mg | 61% of the 1.3 mg RDA (ages 19-50); 0.79% of a 100 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175159, SR Legacy. Raw basis, stated as raw: cooking costs B6, so where a cooked composition entry exists it should be preferred over applying a retention factor to this figure. |
| Beef liver, cooked, braised | 1.02 mg | 1 slice (68 g) | 0.694 mg | 53% of the 1.3 mg RDA (ages 19-50); 0.69% of a 100 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168626, SR Legacy |
| Potatoes, baked, flesh and skin, without salt | 0.311 mg | 1 medium potato (173 g) | 0.538 mg | 41% of the 1.3 mg RDA (ages 19-50); 0.54% of a 100 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170093, SR Legacy |
| Chicken breast, meat only, cooked, roasted | 0.600 mg | half breast, bone and skin removed (86 g) | 0.516 mg | 40% of the 1.3 mg RDA (ages 19-50); 0.52% of a 100 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171477, SR Legacy |
| Instant oatmeal, flavoured, dry, B6-fortified (QUAKER maple and brown sugar) | 1.20 mg (fortification, not intrinsic) | 1 packet (43 g) | 0.516 mg | 40% of the 1.3 mg RDA (ages 19-50); 0.52% of a 100 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173907 |
| Bananas, raw | 0.367 mg | 1 medium, 7 to 7-7/8 inch (118 g) | 0.433 mg | 33% of the 1.3 mg RDA (ages 19-50); 0.43% of a 100 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173944, SR Legacy |
| Sunflower seed kernels, dried | 1.34 mg | 1 oz (28.35 g) | 0.380 mg | 29% of the 1.3 mg RDA (ages 19-50); 0.38% of a 100 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170562 for the 1.34 mg/100 g figure as verified in the verification report; the 1 oz (28.35 g) portion basis is stated here rather than taken from a USDA household measure. Richer per 100 g than pistachios, and behind them per portion only because the portion is the same size. |
| Pistachio nuts, dry roasted, without salt | 1.12 mg (analytical range 0.484-1.80 across 13 data points) | 1 oz, 49 kernels (28.35 g) | 0.317 mg | 24% of the 1.3 mg RDA (ages 19-50); 0.32% of a 100 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170185, SR Legacy (13 data points, min 0.484, max 1.80) |
| Garlic, raw | 1.24 mg | 1 clove (3 g) | 0.0372 mg | 2.9% of the 1.3 mg RDA (ages 19-50); 0.04% of a 100 mg tablet | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 169230 for the 1.24 mg/100 g figure as verified in the verification report; the 1 clove (3 g) portion basis is stated here rather than taken from a USDA household measure. Listed to show that per-100 g richness can vanish at the realistic portion: garlic outranks pistachios per 100 g and comes last per clove. |

- **To match the supplement dose:** A 100 mg pyridoxine tablet is 77 times the RDA. Matching it by eating, in one day, takes 7.46 kg of dried sunflower kernels, 8.93 kg of pistachios, 9.80 kg of braised beef liver, 10.7 kg of raw yellowfin tuna, 16.7 kg of roasted chicken breast, or 27.2 kg of banana, about 231 medium bananas. A 50 mg tablet still needs 4.46 kg of pistachios or 116 bananas. Even the regulatory ceiling sits outside dietary reach: EFSA's 2023 tolerable upper intake level of 12 mg/day for adults corresponds to about 1.07 kg of pistachios or 3.27 kg of banana, roughly 28 bananas, every single day. Nobody eats any of that. Ordinary diets deliver a few mg per day, an order of magnitude below the UL and two to three orders below the supplement, and that is the mechanical reason dietary B6 has never produced the sensory neuropathy that supplements produce: no food is concentrated enough to approach the toxic range. The syndrome was described in seven adults taking megadose pyridoxine (Schaumburg H et al., 'Sensory neuropathy from pyridoxine abuse. A new megavitamin syndrome', N Engl J Med 1983;309:445-8, PMID 6308447), all of whom improved after withdrawal; the widely quoted 2-6 g/day range is secondary-literature summary that does not appear in the abstract, so treat it as unconfirmed and source any dose figure to the full text or to EFSA's case tabulation. Cases have since been reported at far lower supplemental doses, and EFSA cut the adult UL from the SCF's 25 mg/day to 12 mg/day on peripheral neuropathy grounds, from a 50 mg/day reference point with an uncertainty factor of 4 (EFSA NDA Panel, EFSA J 2023;21:e08006, PMID 37207271, DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2023.8006). Not one case has been attributed to food B6.
- **Food versus supplement:** This is a genuine case where the food is safe and the concentrated form is not, and the reason is dose plus kinetics rather than any exotic compound. Food B6 arrives as pyridoxal phosphate and pyridoxamine phosphate bound in the matrix, plus glycosylated pyridoxine in plant foods, released and absorbed gradually across a meal. A tablet delivers a bolus of free pyridoxine. The accepted mechanism for the neuropathy is that free pyridoxine at high concentration outruns conversion to pyridoxal 5-phosphate and acts at PLP-dependent sites in the dorsal root ganglion, which lacks a blood-nerve barrier. Pyridoxal-5-phosphate supplements are marketed as the safe alternative on the strength of that mechanism, but the human evidence is thin and EFSA applies its 12 mg/day UL to total B6 regardless of form, so treating P5P as exempt has no support. One practical consequence: because B6 is added to energy drinks, fortified cereals and B-complex products, people accumulate supplemental doses without intending to, and the UL is easy to cross by stacking products while eating a diet that contributes almost nothing to the risk.
- **Absorption:** Roughly 75% of dietary B6 is absorbed. Plant B6 is partly pyridoxine-5'-beta-D-glucoside, which is about half as available as free pyridoxine and can act as a weak antagonist: a diet supplying 27% of its B6 as PNG lowered plasma total B6 and red cell PLP in women, an effect the authors quantified as a 15-18% loss of total B6 intake (Hansen, Leklem and Miller, J Nutr 1996;126:2512-8, PMID 8857512). That is a real discount on high-PNG plant diets and a modest one, so it should not be stretched into a claim that plant-food B6 numbers are broadly unreliable. Cooking removes more. USDA Table of Nutrient Retention Factors Release 6 (2007) gives B6 retention of 50% for roasted beef (code 0601), 35% for beef braised or simmered without the drippings (0651/0653), 45% for liver simmered without drippings (1152), 80% for roasted chicken (0805), 50% for legumes boiled 2-2.5 h (0541), 65% for legumes boiled 45-75 min and drained (0521) and 85% for greens boiled in water to cover (3005). A raw-basis figure such as the 0.933 mg/100 g for raw tuna is not what lands on the plate.
- **Data note:** All nine composition values and all gram weights verified against named FoodData Central records, including the pistachio data-point count of 13 and its 0.484-1.80 analytical range, and every dose-gap quantity recomputed line by line. The EFSA 2023 opinion, its 12 mg/day UL, the 50 mg reference point and the uncertainty factor of 4 all check out. The one weak link is flagged in place: the 2-6 g/day figure attached to Schaumburg 1983 is secondary-literature summary and is not in the abstract, so it is presented as unconfirmed

*References: EFSA PMID 37207271; Cochrane Matthews PMID 26348534; Brasky JCO PMID 28829668*

### Green tea extract (EGCG)

**Verdict:** AVOID &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** Commercial products commonly deliver 250-500 mg EGCG/d. EFSA found significant transaminase elevation at or above 800 mg/d.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody for the marketed indications. Drinking green tea is fine and is a different exposure.

**Pros**

- Green tea as a beverage has no meaningful hepatotoxicity signal and is a perfectly reasonable drink.

**Cons**

- EFSA was unable to identify ANY dose of EGCG from green tea extracts that can be considered safe, because catechin composition varies with cultivar, growing conditions and extraction method. That is an unusual regulatory conclusion.
- NIH LiverTox documents more than 100 published cases of clinically apparent liver injury: median latency 42 days, 95% hepatocellular, severe in 35%, and transplantation in 8%, with fatal acute liver failure reported.
- HLA-B*35:01 was present in 72% of green tea liver-injury cases versus 11-15% of controls, and essentially nobody is tested for it.
- The weight-loss claim, which is why most of it is sold, has clinically trivial effect sizes where measurable.
- It was the second most used of six hepatotoxic botanicals in US national survey data. Avoid entirely with pre-existing liver disease, raised transaminases, or another hepatotoxic agent such as methotrexate, isoniazid, high-dose paracetamol or alcohol.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** High

Green tea, matcha, white tea and gunpowder green tea all deliver EGCG at tens to over a hundred milligrams per cup or bowl from ordinary shop-bought products, so this is the one entry in the set where a normal beverage reaches the doses used in supplement trials. EFSA's exposure assessment puts mean adult intake at 86 to 321 mg EGCG/day across dietary surveys and high-level (95th percentile) intake at 308 to 866 mg/day, so the top of habitual tea drinking brushes the 800 mg supplement threshold. Oolong is often grouped in here, but no measured per-cup figure survived verification, so it gets no row; bottled ready-to-drink green tea is the commercially dominant form and is far weaker than anything listed, and it also lacks a verified figure here.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Green tea, brewed infusion (traditional preparation) | 70.2 mg EGCG per 100 mL (mean of 100 brewed samples from 12 references; range 2.3 to 203 mg/100 mL) | 1 cup (250 mL) | 176 mg EGCG at the mean (range 6 to 508 mg depending on leaf quantity, water temperature and steeping time) | 22% of an 800 mg extract dose per cup at the mean strength | EFSA ANS Panel, Scientific opinion on the safety of green tea catechins, EFSA Journal 2018;16(4):5239, DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2018.5239, Table 2 (PMC7009618). Full text verified, including the sample count |
| White tea, dry leaf | 5,230 to 9,490 mg EGCG per 100 g dry leaf (5.23 to 9.49 g/100 g), overlapping green tea at 4,400 to 9,600 mg/100 g | 2 g of leaf, the amount used for one cup | 105 to 190 mg EGCG present in the leaf; the infusion delivers part of it | 13 to 24% of an 800 mg dose | Abiri B et al., Tea's anti-obesity properties, cardiometabolic health-promoting potentials, bioactive compounds, and adverse effects: a review focusing on white and green teas, Food Sci Nutr 2023;11(10):5818-36, PMID 37823174, DOI 10.1002/fsn3.3595. Indicative and unconfirmed: the citation is genuine but its abstract carries no EGCG figures. The magnitude is consistent with independently measured 23.29 to 70.22 mg/g across commercial green teas (Plant Foods Hum Nutr 2023, PMC10665233) |
| Green tea, dry leaf | 7,116 mg EGCG per 100 g dry leaf (mean; range 1,600 to 20,320 mg/100 g, n=68) | 2 g of leaf, the amount used for one cup | 142 mg EGCG present in the leaf at the mean, of which brewing transfers a variable fraction | 18% of an 800 mg dose | EFSA ANS Panel, EFSA Journal 2018;16(4):5239, DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2018.5239, Table 2 (verified) |
| Gunpowder green tea, dry leaf (highest-EGCG product measured in the commercial survey) | 7,022 mg EGCG per 100 g dry leaf (70.22 mg/g), the highest of 15 commercial products and above both matcha grades; the survey range across commercial green teas was 23.29 to 70.22 mg/g | 2 g of leaf, the amount used for one cup | 140 mg EGCG present in the leaf, of which brewing transfers a variable fraction | 18% of an 800 mg dose | Catechin Composition of Commercially-Available Green Teas, Plant Foods Hum Nutr 2023, PMID 37923855, PMC10665233 (15 commercial products, HPLC) |
| Matcha powder (whisked, whole leaf consumed) | 5,053 to 5,657 mg EGCG per 100 g powder (culinary matcha 50.53 +/- 2.41 mg/g, ceremonial matcha 56.57 +/- 2.19 mg/g; individual ceremonial samples 49.03 to 69.73 mg/g) | 1 bowl (2 g powder) | 101 to 113 mg EGCG, all of it consumed because the whole leaf is drunk (98 to 139 mg across the individual ceremonial samples) | 13 to 14% of an 800 mg dose per bowl | Catechin Composition of Commercially-Available Green Teas, Plant Foods Hum Nutr 2023, PMID 37923855, PMC10665233 (15 commercial products, HPLC). The previously cited Kolackova T et al., Antioxidants 2022;11(5):889, PMID 35624753 contains no EGCG value at all and has been dropped as the source |

- **To match the supplement dose:** 800 mg EGCG, the dose at which supplements produce measurable liver enzyme rises, is about 4 to 5 cups of green tea at EFSA's mean brewed concentration of 70.2 mg/100 mL, or 10 to 16 cups of more weakly brewed retail tea, or about 7 to 8 bowls of matcha at 2 g each. EFSA's high-level consumers already reach 308 to 866 mg/day from infusions alone, so the top of that range is at the supplement dose. What differs is the vehicle: the tea drinker's 800 mg arrives spread over a day in two to three litres of water, usually with food, while the supplement delivers it as a single fasted bolus.
- **Food versus supplement:** State this plainly: the beverage carries no meaningful liver signal, the extract does. EFSA concluded that intake at or above 800 mg EGCG/day taken as a food supplement causes a statistically significant increase in serum transaminases, while catechins from green tea infusion prepared in the traditional way, and reconstituted drinks of equivalent composition, are in general considered safe. Same molecule, different product. Risk concentrates in high-dose, single-bolus, fasted dosing of concentrated extracts, and Commission Regulation (EU) 2022/2340 now prohibits supplements delivering 800 mg or more EGCG per day in the EU. Green tea extract is also the ingredient behind a large share of liver injury cases attributed to weight-loss and fat-burner blends, which is a warning about the extract rather than about tea.
- **Absorption:** Systemic bioavailability of EGCG is low, on the order of 0.1 to 1% of an oral dose as the unchanged compound, with heavy glucuronidation, sulfation and methylation plus colonic degradation. The critical modifier is food: fasted dosing raises EGCG Cmax severalfold compared with the same dose taken with a meal, and fasted administration is both the way extracts are typically taken and the condition associated with hepatotoxicity. Milk and food proteins bind catechins and blunt peak levels, so tea with milk is pharmacologically not tea on an empty stomach. Brewing extracts only part of the leaf's EGCG whereas matcha delivers the whole leaf. The corrected numbers support the point that matcha is no many-fold EGCG upgrade per gram of leaf: matcha measures 50.53 to 56.57 mg/g against gunpowder green tea at 70.22 mg/g in the same 15-product HPLC survey, which found no statistically significant EGCG difference across tea types.
- **Data note:** Every EFSA figure verified exactly (70.2 mg/100 mL from 100 samples across 12 references, 7,116 mg/100 g dry leaf, the intake ranges, the 800 mg transaminase conclusion), and the corrected matcha and gunpowder values come from an HPLC survey of 15 commercial products. The white tea row is the only indicative entry

*References: EFSA J 2018;16(4):5239; NIH LiverTox NBK547925; Likhitsup PMID 39102266*

### Ashwagandha (Withania somnifera)

**Verdict:** AVOID &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** C

- **Dose tested:** 300-600 mg/d of root extract standardised to about 5% withanolides. Liver injury occurred at ordinary retail doses.
- **Who it is for:** No population with a demonstrated clinical need. Anyone with liver disease should be steered away entirely.

**Pros**

- There is a real but small and fragile efficacy signal: pooled Perceived Stress Scale -4.72 (-8.45 to -0.99), Hamilton Anxiety Scale -2.19 (-3.83 to -0.55) and serum cortisol -2.58 across 9 randomised trials and 558 patients.
- A sleep meta-analysis of 5 trials and 400 participants found SMD -0.59, with larger effects at 600 mg/d or more over 8 weeks or longer.

**Cons**

- Hepatotoxicity is documented in a prospective case series from Iceland and the US DILIN network: jaundice with nausea, lethargy and pruritus after a latency of 2 to 12 weeks, cholestatic or mixed injury, symptoms lasting 5 to 20 weeks.
- In an Indian case series, 5 of 23 patients had pre-existing chronic liver disease, 3 developed acute-on-chronic liver failure and ALL 3 DIED. Pre-existing liver disease is the hard contraindication.
- Denmark banned it in food supplements in 2023 after its national food institute concluded no safe intake level could be established.
- It raises T4 and can precipitate thyrotoxicosis or require a levothyroxine dose reduction. Published cases exist.
- Avoid in pregnancy (traditional abortifacient use), in autoimmune disease and in transplant recipients (immunostimulant effects), and with sedatives (additive).
- The trials are small, short, mostly single-centre and frequently manufacturer-sponsored, with subjective questionnaire endpoints and no hard endpoint anywhere in the literature.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** From a plant not eaten as food
- **Dose from food:** Dose NOT reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** High

Ashwagandha is the root, sometimes root plus leaf, of Withania somnifera, a small shrub in the Solanaceae. The root is a medicinal drug rather than a dietary item: bitter, taken as a churna or a decoction in milk, and present in no cuisine as an ingredient. The active steroidal lactones, withanolides such as withaferin A, withanolide A and withanone, occur in Withania and a few related genera and are absent from the food supply. The nightshades people actually eat, tomato, potato, aubergine, sweet and chilli peppers, contain a different class of steroidal compounds (glycoalkaloids such as tomatine and solanine), so botanical relatedness buys nothing here. A related species, Withania coagulans, has fruit used regionally as a vegetarian milk-clotting agent, which is a processing aid rather than a dietary source of withanolides.

*No food rows: there is no ordinary food that supplies this in a meaningful amount.*

- **To match the supplement dose:** Trials use 300-600 mg/day of standardised root extract. KSM-66 is a root-only extract standardised to a minimum 5% withanolides, Sensoril is a root-and-leaf extract at 10%, and Shoden is standardised to 35% withanolide glycosides, so a typical daily dose is roughly 15-60 mg of withanolides. Traditional practice uses 3-6 g/day of dried root powder to reach a comparable place. No food quantity supplies this at any size, because no food plant contains withanolides. Quoting an equivalent weight of an ordinary food would require inventing a content value, so the honest answer is that the dietary route does not exist.
- **Food versus supplement:** Plant part matters and so does the regulator's view. Root-only preparations have a better documented safety profile than preparations including leaf and aerial parts, which are richer in withaferin A, the most cytotoxic of the withanolides. Ashwagandha is banned in food supplements in Denmark, the Dutch national institute RIVM has published a risk assessment of Withania preparations (RIVM report 2024-0029), French authorities have advised against use in specific groups, the UK Food Standards Agency has logged rising incident reports and its committee reviewed associations with thyroid toxicity, hypoglycaemia and liver toxicity, and Australia's TGA issued a medicine safety update in July 2026 on continuing reports of ashwagandha-associated liver injury judged to be idiosyncratic. KSM-66's milk-based extraction is a marketing distinction rather than a safety one. Withanolide content also varies with chemotype, cultivation and plant part, so two products at the same milligram dose can differ several-fold in the compounds that matter.
- **Absorption:** Human pharmacokinetic data are thin and no standard bioavailability figure can be quoted with confidence. Withanolides are lipophilic and poorly water-soluble, which is why traditional preparation uses milk or ghee and why modern products add phospholipid or cyclodextrin carriers. Withanolide glycosides (withanosides) are more water-soluble and are the markers Shoden standardises on, so percent withanolides on two labels may refer to chemically different pools measured by different assays. Because there is no food form there is no food-versus-supplement absorption comparison to make; the meaningful comparison is between extract types, and it is not standardised.
- **Data note:** for the food and dose determination. The NOT_FOOD call was challenged and upheld, the withanolide dose arithmetic (15-60 mg from 300-600 mg of extract at 5-10%) was verified as correct, and there are no food-composition figures in this entry to be wrong. The verification pass specifically endorsed the refusal to invent a withanolide food content value. The regulatory statements were outside the scope of the numeric verification

*References: Bjornsson Liver Int PMID 31991029; Arumugam PMID 39348746; Cheah PMID 34559859; DTU Denmark 2020*

### Weight-loss, fat-burner and energy blends

**Verdict:** AVOID &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** Garcinia products commonly supply 500-1,500 mg of extract. No effective dose has been demonstrated for clinically meaningful weight loss.
- **Who it is for:** No identifiable group. This is the worst-behaving corner of the entire supplement market.

**Pros**

- None identified. The efficacy literature does not support clinically meaningful weight loss from these products.

**Cons**

- Weight-loss and energy products account for 71.8% (67.6-76.1) of the supplement-related emergency department visits presenting with cardiac symptoms, out of an estimated 23,005 visits per year in the US.
- Adults aged 20-34 accounted for 28.0% of all such visits, so the demographic bearing the harm is young and otherwise healthy.
- The FDA acted against Hydroxycut in 2009 after hepatotoxicity reports including a death. NIH LiverTox maintains a dedicated monograph for Garcinia cambogia acute liver injury including fulminant hepatic failure.
- These are proprietary blends, and 40.9% of all adulterated supplements in the FDA database are weight-loss products. Undeclared sibutramine, withdrawn from all major markets in 2010, and prohibited stimulants such as DMAA, deterenol, octodrine, oxilofrine and phenpromethamine are repeatedly found.
- Serotonin syndrome has been reported with garcinia in combination with SSRIs, SNRIs and MAOIs. Contraindicated in liver disease, arrhythmia and hypertension.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose partly reachable
- **Data confidence:** Medium

This category holds four different answers and the earlier single TRACE label hid the most important one. Caffeine is RICH and is the only component with a consistent thermogenic effect, available from coffee and tea by the cup. p-Synephrine is RICH as well: citrus juices carry up to 25 to 40 mg per quarter litre and Seville orange juice about 13 mg per 240 mL glass, which sits inside the clinical dose range, so the earlier NOT_FOOD label for bitter orange was wrong. Garcinia gummi-gutta rind (kodampuli, Malabar tamarind) is a genuine culinary souring agent in Kerala and parts of Southeast Asia, used in fish curry and asam laksa, and carries hydroxycitric acid at 10 to 30% of dry weight. The remainder splits differently: raspberry ketone is TRACE (up to 4.3 mg/kg in raspberries against 100 to 1,400 mg/day sold), green coffee chlorogenic acids come from unroasted beans that nobody eats, yohimbe bark and Coleus forskohlii root are NOT_FOOD, and higenamine and DMAA are synthetic with no food origin at all.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Garcinia gummi-gutta dried fruit rind (kodampuli / Malabar tamarind), culinary souring agent | 10 to 30 g hydroxycitric acid per 100 g of dried pericarp (10 to 30% w/w of dry weight, the commonly quoted range). Commercial extracts are standardised to 40% HCA or more | about 3 g of dried rind, a couple of pieces as used to sour a pot of fish curry for four | 300 to 900 mg HCA in the pot, about 75 to 225 mg per person, and less again because HCA transfers only partly from rind into broth and the rind is discarded rather than eaten | about 8 to 25% of the 900 mg top supplement dose per person; the whole pot spans the 72 to 900 mg HCA range delivered by supplements | van Breemen RB, Roe AL, Akhtar N, Hepatotoxicity of dietary supplements containing Garcinia gummi-gutta (L.) N. Robson, Pharmaceutical Biology 2025;63(1):912-24, PMID 41262061, DOI 10.1080/13880209.2025.2591467. Indicative and unconfirmed: the cited review's abstract carries neither an HCA percentage for the rind nor the 72 to 2,800 mg extract / 72 to 900 mg HCA dose range, and 30% is the top of the commonly quoted 10 to 30% of dry weight rather than a measured central value |
| Seville (bitter) orange juice, and citrus juice generally | about 5.7 mg p-synephrine per 100 mL for Seville orange juice (measured at roughly 57 ug/mL); citrus juices generally are reported at up to 25 to 40 mg per quarter litre, i.e. 10 to 16 mg/100 mL | 1 glass (240 mL, 8 fl oz) | about 13 mg p-synephrine from Seville orange juice; up to 25 to 40 mg per 250 mL across citrus juices | sits inside the 10 to 53 mg/day p-synephrine range used in the pooled clinical studies (individual study doses 5.5 to 80 mg), so one glass is dose-equivalent to a capsule | Stohs SJ et al., A review of the human clinical studies involving Citrus aurantium (bitter orange) and p-synephrine, Int J Med Sci 2012, PMC3444973. Figures taken from the verification report's authoritative check rather than re-read here, so treat the per-100 mL conversion as indicative |
| Coffee, brewed filter (Arabica, roasted) | 30 mg caffeine per 100 mL. The chlorogenic acid bean percentages previously given here (0.9 to 1.9 g/100 g roasted against 5.4 g/100 g green) are withdrawn: the cited study measured coffee solutions, reporting green coffee highest in chlorogenic acid at 543.23 mg/L and medium roast highest in caffeine at 203.63 mg/L, and gives no per-bean values | 1 cup (150 mL) | 45 mg caffeine per cup; espresso gives 112 mg per 40 mL and instant coffee 54 mg per 150 mL. A 240 mL US/UK mug is roughly 95 mg on USDA figures of about 40 mg/100 g | 23% of a 200 mg pre-workout caffeine dose per 150 mL cup, and about 48% for a 240 mL mug | Caffeine: Rocha PLA, Lima ALC, Saunders B, Reis CEG, Development of a caffeine content table for foods, drinks, medications and supplements typically consumed by the Brazilian population, Nutrients 2022;14(20):4417, PMID 36297100, PMC9608580 (table verified to the digit). Mug figure from USDA FoodData Central for brewed coffee. Chlorogenic acid: Awwad S, Issa R, Alnsour L, Albals D, Al-Momani I, Molecules 2021;26(24):7502, PMID 34946584, which reports solution concentrations only; green Arabica beans are usually quoted at roughly 4 to 8% chlorogenic acids by dry weight, an indicative literature range with no page-level source given here |
| Green tea, brewed (the EGCG and caffeine component of most fat-burner blends) | 20 mg caffeine per 100 mL; for EGCG see the green tea extract entry (70.2 mg/100 mL brewed) | 1 cup (200 mL) | 40 mg caffeine, plus roughly 140 mg EGCG at EFSA's mean brewed concentration | 20% of a 200 mg caffeine dose per cup | Rocha PLA et al., Nutrients 2022;14(20):4417, PMID 36297100, PMC9608580 (green tea infused, 20 mg/100 mL and 40 mg per 200 mL cup, verified); EGCG from EFSA Journal 2018;16(4):5239, DOI 10.2903/j.efsa.2018.5239 |
| Raspberries, raw | up to 0.43 mg raspberry ketone per 100 g (up to 4.3 mg/kg); published values for raspberries generally cluster around 1 to 4 mg/kg | 1 cup / small punnet (125 g) | up to 0.54 mg at the highest reported natural concentration, and typically far less | up to 0.5% of a 100 mg supplement dose | Bredsdorff L et al., Raspberry ketone in food supplements: high intake, few toxicity data, a cause for safety concern?, Regul Toxicol Pharmacol 2015;73(1):196-200, PMID 26160596, DOI 10.1016/j.yrtph.2015.06.022 (abstract verified: natural occurrence up to 4.3 mg/kg, recommended intake 100 to 1,400 mg/day, 100 mg is 56 times the 1,800 ug/day threshold of toxicological concern for Class 1 substances). The lower bound of 0.001 mg/kg previously stated does not appear in the paper and has been removed |

- **To match the supplement dose:** Component by component. Caffeine: 200 mg is four to five 150 mL cups of filter coffee on the cited Brazilian table, or about two 240 mL US/UK mugs at roughly 95 mg each, or five cups of green tea, so this one is trivially reachable (guarana powder, yerba mate, cocoa and energy drinks belong in the same picture and appear in the same cited caffeine table, but no per-portion figure was verified here, so they get no row). p-Synephrine: a single 240 mL glass of Seville orange juice at about 13 mg already sits inside the 10 to 53 mg/day range used in the pooled overweight and obese subjects, whose individual study doses spanned 5.5 to 80 mg. Garcinia: 3 g of dried rind souring a pot for four carries 300 to 900 mg HCA at 10 to 30% w/w, about 75 to 225 mg per person against supplement doses of 72 to 900 mg HCA, and less again because HCA transfers only partly into the broth and the rind is fished out rather than eaten, so culinary use lands in the lower middle of the supplement range rather than at its top. Raspberry ketone: 100 mg, the smallest common dose, is about 23 kg of fresh raspberries at the highest natural concentration of 4.3 mg/kg, and the 1,400 mg top dose about 326 kg, so it is unreachable by three orders of magnitude, and the safety review notes even the lowest recommended dose is about 56 times the threshold of toxicological concern for its structural class. Green coffee: extracts standardised to 45 to 50% chlorogenic acids at 400 to 1,600 mg/day supply roughly 200 to 800 mg CGA, and the concentrated dose exists only because green beans are inedible; the cups-of-coffee comparison cannot be stated here, because the bean percentages it rested on were misattributed and have been withdrawn.
- **Food versus supplement:** The governing pattern in this category is that the food is safe while the concentrate is not, and Garcinia is the cleanest illustration. Centuries of culinary use of the dried rind as a souring agent in South and Southeast Asian cooking carry no liver-injury signal, while concentrated HCA supplements have produced 34 published hepatotoxicity case reports including nine liver transplants and one death, plus more than 200 adverse events of liver injury and 93 international case reports reviewed by Health Canada, with 17 cases meeting CIOMS/RUCAM causality. The injury looks idiosyncratic rather than dose-dependent, with a proposed HLA-B*35:01 association, so the absence of harm from culinary use says something about the product form rather than about a lower dose, and on corrected arithmetic the culinary serving delivers the middle of the supplement HCA range rather than the top of it. The same shape repeats across the category: green tea extract in fat-burner blends against drinking tea (see that entry), and bitter orange p-synephrine stacked with caffeine and linked to cardiovascular events, where the honest food comparison is a glass of citrus juice carrying a similar p-synephrine dose without the stimulant stack. Yohimbe bark extract has no edible counterpart at all, and raspberry ketone at food flavouring levels of milligrams per kilogram bears no relation to 100 to 1,400 mg/day capsules with essentially no human toxicology. Add the labelling problem: proprietary blends routinely under-declare caffeine and stack multiple stimulants, so the only reliable comparison available is to count your caffeine in cups and treat the rest of the label as unquantified.
- **Absorption:** Caffeine is the outlier and the reason these blends do anything: it is almost completely absorbed, peaks in 30 to 60 minutes, and behaves much the same from coffee as from a tablet, with coffee's chlorogenic acids only modestly slowing absorption. Chlorogenic acids themselves are poorly bioavailable, with roughly a third absorbed in the small intestine and the remainder extensively metabolised by colonic microbiota into hydroxycinnamic acid derivatives, so the milligrams of CGA printed on a green coffee label map badly onto systemic exposure. Raspberry ketone is rapidly conjugated and cleared, which is one reason the human evidence for it is close to non-existent. Hydroxycitric acid absorption from Garcinia is incomplete and depends on the salt form (calcium, potassium or magnesium salts differ in solubility), which helps explain why the pooled human weight-loss effect is at best around 0.9 kg over placebo, despite doses in the same order as what a serving of Kerala fish curry supplies.
- **Data note:** The BraCaffT caffeine table, the raspberry ketone occurrence and TTC figures, and the Garcinia hepatotoxicity case counts all verified exactly. The Garcinia rind HCA percentage and the supplement HCA dose range could not be traced to the cited review, the chlorogenic acid bean percentages were misattributed and have been withdrawn, and the p-synephrine figures come from the verification report's check rather than a direct read

*References: Geller NEJM PMID 26465986; Tucker PMID 30646238; LiverTox NBK548087; Cohen Clin Toxicol 2021*

### Bodybuilding and test-booster products

**Verdict:** AVOID &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** Not applicable. Dosing is label-driven and the label does not reflect the contents.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody. Any muscle gain is attributable to undeclared controlled substances the user did not consent to take.

**Pros**

- None. There is no hype-versus-reality gap to argue about, because the products contain drugs they do not declare.

**Cons**

- The DILIN prospective series enrolled 44 men, mean age 33, median latency 73 days of use. 100% were jaundiced, 84% had pruritus, 71% required hospitalisation, and liver biopsy in 59% showed mild hepatitis with profound cholestasis.
- Chemical analysis of the implicated products repeatedly finds anabolic androgenic steroids not declared on the label. These are controlled substances.
- Of 44 products sold as SARMs, only 23 (52%) contained any SARM, 17 (39%) contained a different unapproved drug, 4 (9%) contained no active compound at all, and only 18 (41%) had accurate labels.
- Muscle building accounted for 11.9% of adulterated products in the FDA database, and bodybuilding products caused 45 of 130 herbal and dietary supplement liver injury cases in the DILIN cohort.
- ABCB11 gene variants were over-represented in cases versus ethnically matched controls, so some individuals are genetically far more vulnerable and have no way to know.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** No food source, manufactured
- **Dose from food:** No dose to compare
- **Data confidence:** High

There is no food source, because the active ingredients are undeclared anabolic androgenic steroids: manufactured pharmaceuticals and controlled substances. The label ingredients are largely decorative. Chemical analysis of the products implicated in liver injury repeatedly finds steroids the label does not mention.

*No food rows: there is no ordinary food that supplies this in a meaningful amount.*

- **To match the supplement dose:** Not applicable. You cannot eat your way to an undeclared steroid dose, and you should not want to.
- **Food versus supplement:** The legitimate food-adjacent route to the advertised outcome is dull and well evidenced: enough total protein to reach about 1.6 g/kg/day, and creatine monohydrate. Both have their own entries with food tables. Neither requires buying a proprietary blend.
- **Absorption:** NONE. This entry is a product category rather than a single substance, so there is no single absorption comparison to make.
- **Data note:** The classification follows from what the product is, not from composition data

*References: Stolz PMID 30934130; Navarro DILIN PMID 25043597; Van Wagoner JAMA PMID 29183075; Geller PMID 26465986*

### Sexual-enhancement products

**Verdict:** AVOID &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** A

- **Dose tested:** Not applicable. Undeclared pharmaceutical doses are present at pharmacological levels.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody, from any retailer, at any price.

**Pros**

- None. The only defensible use of this evidence is categorical avoidance.

**Cons**

- Sexual enhancement is the single largest adulterated category: 353 of 776 products (45.5%) in the FDA tainted-products database, from 146 different companies.
- The adulterants are PDE5 inhibitors, chiefly sildenafil and structural analogues designed to evade detection. Undeclared sildenafil taken by someone on nitrates for angina is a route to catastrophic hypotension in a patient who does not know they took a PDE5 inhibitor.
- Enforcement does not work on this category. Of 27 FDA-recalled supplements still purchasable and re-analysed a mean 34.3 months after recall, 18 (66.7%) still contained banned drugs and 63% contained the same adulterant the FDA had already ordered removed.
- Tucker found 157 products (20.2%) contained more than one unapproved ingredient, and on resampling 19 of 28 repeatedly flagged products (67.9%) had merely switched to a different adulterant.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** No food source, manufactured
- **Dose from food:** No dose to compare
- **Data confidence:** High

There is no food source. The active ingredients are undeclared PDE5 inhibitors, chiefly sildenafil and structural analogues designed to evade laboratory detection. These are prescription pharmaceuticals, not nutrients, and they are present because the product would otherwise do nothing.

*No food rows: there is no ordinary food that supplies this in a meaningful amount.*

- **To match the supplement dose:** Not applicable. No quantity of any food contains a pharmaceutical PDE5 inhibitor.
- **Food versus supplement:** The danger runs the other way from a normal form caveat. Here the product contains more than the label admits rather than less. Someone taking nitrates for angina who swallows an undeclared PDE5 inhibitor can reach catastrophic hypotension without knowing what they took.
- **Absorption:** NONE. This entry is a product category rather than a single substance, so there is no single absorption comparison to make.
- **Data note:** The classification follows from what the product is, not from composition data

*References: Tucker PMID 30646238; Cohen JAMA 2014; FDA CDER tainted products database*

### High-dose folic acid plus B12 for cardiovascular prevention

**Verdict:** AVOID &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D
