- **Dose tested:** The Norwegian trials used folic acid 0.8 mg/d plus B12 0.4 mg/d, with or without B6 40 mg/d.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody for cardiovascular prevention. Periconceptional folic acid at 400 µg is a completely different indication and should not be conflated with this.

**Pros**

- B vitamins do reliably lower homocysteine, which was the mechanistic rationale. The mechanism worked. The clinical benefit did not follow.

**Cons**

- The homocysteine hypothesis is dead as a cardiovascular target. HOPE-2 (n=5,522, 5 years) lowered homocysteine with no effect on major vascular events (RR 0.95, 0.84-1.07). SEARCH (n=12,064, 6.7 years) cut it 28% with RR 1.04 (0.97-1.12) for major vascular events.
- There is a positive cancer harm signal. Pooled Norwegian trials in 6,837 patients with ischaemic heart disease: cancer incidence HR 1.21 (1.03-1.41), cancer mortality HR 1.38 (1.07-1.79), and all-cause mortality also raised.
- Anyone with a history of cancer or at elevated cancer risk should not take high-dose folic acid for cardiovascular indications.
- Vitamin B12 use before and during chemotherapy was associated with worse disease-free survival (HR 1.83, 1.15-2.92) and worse overall survival (HR 2.04, 1.22-3.40) in a prospective study nested in a phase 3 trial.
- The upper limit for synthetic folic acid is 1,000 µg/d, and it was set to prevent masking of B12 deficiency neuropathy.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** No dose to compare
- **Data confidence:** High

Both nutrients are abundant in ordinary food, so see the folic acid and vitamin B12 entries for the food tables. The distinction that matters here is the dose and the purpose. Food folate and food B12 at dietary levels carry none of the cancer signal seen in the Norwegian trials, which used 0.8 mg/d of synthetic folic acid plus 0.4 mg/d of B12 for a cardiovascular indication.

*No food rows: there is no ordinary food that supplies this in a meaningful amount.*

- **To match the supplement dose:** Not applicable as a target. There is no food quantity to aim for, because the trials that produced the cancer signal were testing a supplemental dose for a purpose that does not work. The dietary answer is to meet the reference intake from food and stop there.
- **Food versus supplement:** This is the clearest case in the document where the food and the supplement diverge on outcome rather than on amount. Periconceptional folic acid at 400 µg remains the strongest supplement result in existence; the same molecule at twice the dose given to cardiac patients raised cancer incidence. Same compound, different dose, different population, opposite conclusion.
- **Absorption:** NONE. This entry is a product category rather than a single substance, so there is no single absorption comparison to make.
- **Data note:** The classification follows from what the product is, not from composition data

*References: Ebbing JAMA 2009 PMID 19920236; HOPE-2; SEARCH; Ambrosone JCO PMID 31855498; USPSTF PMID 35727271*

### Antioxidants during chemotherapy

**Verdict:** AVOID &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** D

- **Dose tested:** Not applicable. No dose is recommended and the concern is temporal rather than numeric.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody. Documented deficiency should be corrected under oncology supervision, with timing discussed against the chemotherapy schedule.

**Pros**

- A plain reference-intake-level multivitamin showed no adverse association in the same dataset, so ordinary nutritional support is not the concern here.

**Cons**

- The theoretical objection is direct and mechanistic: several cytotoxic classes including anthracyclines, platinum agents and alkylating agents work partly through oxidative damage, and radiotherapy works through free radical generation. Supplementing antioxidants may blunt the treatment.
- SWOG S0221 (n=1,134 women with high-risk early breast cancer on cyclophosphamide, doxorubicin and paclitaxel): any antioxidant (vitamins A, C, E, carotenoids, CoQ10) taken both before and during chemotherapy gave recurrence adjusted HR 1.41 (0.98-2.04, P=0.06).
- In the same study, vitamin B12 taken before and during chemotherapy gave disease-free survival HR 1.83 (1.15-2.92) and overall survival HR 2.04 (1.22-3.40). Iron was also associated with worse outcomes.
- SELECT establishes in a randomised setting that high-dose antioxidant supplementation can INCREASE cancer incidence: vitamin E 400 IU/d raised prostate cancer HR 1.17 in 34,887 men.
- Honest caveat: SWOG S0221 is observational within a randomised trial, and the headline antioxidant hazard ratio crossed 1 with P=0.06. Nobody should overstate it. But cancer patients are the most aggressively targeted and least protected supplement consumers, and support your body through chemo is sold with no evidence and a plausible mechanism of harm.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** No dose to compare
- **Data confidence:** High

Vitamins A, C and E, carotenoids and CoQ10 are all present in ordinary food, and each has its own food table elsewhere in this document. Food is not the concern here. The concern is supplemental doses taken alongside cytotoxic treatment.

*No food rows: there is no ordinary food that supplies this in a meaningful amount.*

- **To match the supplement dose:** Not applicable, and deliberately so. Eating fruit and vegetables during chemotherapy is not the exposure that SWOG S0221 measured. That study looked at supplement use before and during treatment, at doses far above what food supplies.
- **Food versus supplement:** The gap between food and supplement is the whole point. A portion of berries and a 1,000 mg vitamin C capsule are not the same intervention. Nobody has suggested that dietary antioxidant intake during treatment is harmful, and no oncology body recommends restricting fruit and vegetables. Any change to supplements during cancer treatment belongs with the treating oncologist.
- **Absorption:** NONE. This entry is a product category rather than a single substance, so there is no single absorption comparison to make.
- **Data note:** The classification follows from what the product is, not from composition data

*References: Ambrosone SWOG S0221 JCO PMID 31855498; SELECT PMID 21990298; Cochrane PMID 22419320*

### Probiotics in critical illness

**Verdict:** AVOID &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** Not applicable. The relevant ceiling is a population restriction rather than a milligram figure.
- **Who it is for:** Nobody in critical illness. See the strain-specific entry for the two indications where probiotics do work.

**Pros**

- None in this population. In non-critically-ill patients there are two narrow indications with real evidence, covered separately.

**Cons**

- PROPATRIA is the most important negative trial in this entire field. In 296 patients with predicted severe acute pancreatitis given a multispecies synbiotic enterally twice daily for 28 days: mortality 24 of 152 (16%) versus 9 of 144 (6%), RR 2.53 (1.22-5.25). Infectious complications were not reduced (RR 1.06).
- Nine cases of bowel ischaemia occurred, eight of them fatal, versus zero on placebo. The plausible mechanism is that fermentable substrate plus bacteria in a gut with compromised splanchnic perfusion increases local oxygen demand.
- The FDA warned providers after a preterm infant died of probiotic-associated Bifidobacterium sepsis, and cases of severe or fatal infection in premature infants receiving probiotics are documented.
- Saccharomyces boulardii fungaemia is documented in ICU patients with central venous catheters, which is the dominant risk factor.
- Absolute contraindications: predicted severe acute pancreatitis, multi-organ failure, central venous catheter, neutropenia, severe immunocompromise, and preterm infants outside a supervised NICU protocol.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** No dose to compare
- **Data confidence:** High

Live microorganisms are abundant in fermented foods, and the counts are given in the probiotics entry. That is not the relevant question for this item, because the population is patients in intensive care.

*No food rows: there is no ordinary food that supplies this in a meaningful amount.*

- **To match the supplement dose:** Not applicable, and the direction is reversed. There is no food target to reach. The same caution that applies to probiotic capsules applies to fermented foods in these patients, and enteral feeding decisions in intensive care are made by the clinical team.
- **Food versus supplement:** Fermented foods are a reasonable habit for healthy people and are not a substitute for a named strain at a defined dose. In critical illness, compromised gut perfusion and mucosal barrier injury are what make live organisms hazardous, and that hazard does not care whether the organism arrived in a capsule or in a spoonful of yoghurt.
- **Absorption:** NONE. This entry is a product category rather than a single substance, so there is no single absorption comparison to make.
- **Data note:** The classification follows from what the product is, not from composition data

*References: PROPATRIA Besselink Lancet PMID 18279948; NCCIH; case report PMID 41476631*

### Calcium supplements on top of an adequate diet

**Verdict:** AVOID &nbsp;|&nbsp; **Grade:** B

- **Dose tested:** The trials used at least 500 mg/d elemental calcium. Adult reference intake is 1,000-1,200 mg/d TOTAL from all sources.
- **Who it is for:** People with genuinely low dietary calcium, below about 600-700 mg/d, particularly institutionalised elderly, and patients on long-term glucocorticoids.

**Pros**

- With vitamin D in frail, calcium-and-D-deplete institutionalised elderly, it cuts hip fracture: RR 0.84 (0.74-0.96) across 9 trials and 49,853 people. That population is real and the effect is real.
- Correcting a genuine dietary shortfall is sensible, and food sources are preferable where possible.

**Cons**

- It does very little for fractures in ordinary community-dwelling adults. WHI, the largest trial ever run (n=36,282 postmenopausal women, 1,000 mg calcium plus 400 IU vitamin D, 7 years) gained 1.06% hip bone density but produced hip fracture HR 0.88 (0.72-1.08) and spine 0.90 (0.74-1.10). Both null.
- There is a cardiovascular harm signal. Bolland pooled 15 placebo-controlled trials of calcium at least 500 mg/d without vitamin D: patient-level myocardial infarction HR 1.31 (1.02-1.67, P=0.035) across 5 studies and 8,151 participants. A separate WHI reanalysis plus updated meta-analysis gave myocardial infarction RR 1.24 (1.07-1.45, P=0.004).
- Kidney stones increased significantly in WHI, HR 1.17 (1.02-1.34). This is the clearest confirmed harm in that trial.
- Supplementing on top of adequate dietary intake gets you the possible cardiovascular downside and no bone upside. Supplement the shortfall only.
- Absorption falls above about 500 mg per dose, so split doses. Chelates levothyroxine, bisphosphonates, tetracyclines and fluoroquinolones. Avoid in hypercalcaemia, primary hyperparathyroidism, sarcoidosis and a history of calcium-containing stones.

**Food sources**

- **In food:** Rich food sources
- **Dose from food:** Dose reachable by eating
- **Data confidence:** High

Calcium is among the easiest nutrients to get from ordinary food. Dairy, calcium-set tofu, hard cheeses, tinned fish eaten with the bones, whole small fish, fortified plant milks and fortified juice each deliver 200 to 860 mg per normal portion, and low-oxalate brassicas add 150 to 270 mg per cooked cup with fractional absorption in the same band as milk. In the UK, non-wholemeal flour is fortified with calcium carbonate by law, so bread is one of the largest single contributors to national intake at about 50 mg per slice. The adult reference intake of 1,000 mg/day (1,200 mg for women 51+, Institute of Medicine 2011) is reachable in three or four food choices, and this entry concerns adding a supplement to a diet that already meets that target, which is a different question from correcting a real deficit.

| Food | Per 100 g | Portion | Per portion | Share | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Tofu, raw, firm, prepared with calcium sulfate | 683 mg calcium | 1/2 cup (126 g) | 861 mg | 86% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 172475, including the 0.5 cup = 126 g portion. Absorption 39-49% at a 200-250 mg load |
| Whitebait, coated in flour, fried (whole small fish, bones included) | 860 mg calcium | 80 g starter portion | 688 mg | 69% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | CoFID 16-231. Added on verification as the whole-small-fish archetype, richer than either canned fish below. The 80 g portion is a conventional serving weight rather than a sourced portion, so the per-portion figure is indicative |
| Instant hot oat cereal, fortified, dry | 1,336 mg calcium | 30 g sachet | 401 mg | 40% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | CoFID 11-792. Added on verification. All of it is added mineral. The 30 g sachet is a conventional weight rather than a sourced portion, so the per-portion figure is indicative |
| Dried skimmed milk, fortified | 1,280 mg calcium | 30 g powder, about a 250 ml glass reconstituted | 384 mg | 38% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | CoFID 12-521. Added on verification. The 30 g powder weight is a conventional reconstitution rather than a sourced portion, so the per-portion figure is indicative |
| Parmesan and other hard cheeses | 1,184 mg calcium | 30 g piece or grated over a dish | 355 mg | 36% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170848; CoFID 12-526 Parmesan gives 1,025 mg/100 g, Emmental 1,025 (12-493) and Gruyere 950 (12-164). Added on verification: a 30 g piece of Parmesan beats the 1 oz Cheddar slice below by 156 mg, and hard cheeses were the largest omission on the original list |
| Sardines, Atlantic, canned in oil, drained solids with bone | 382 mg calcium | 1 small can (3.75 oz, 92 g) | 351 mg | 35% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175139, including the 92 g can; CoFID 16-440 gives 500 mg/100 g for sardines in olive oil. The bones are the calcium, so boneless fillets do not count. The nearest CoFID contrast, 16-438 at 63 mg/100 g, is grilled sardine flesh rather than canned, so that comparison spans a change of preparation as well as the removal of bones |
| Calcium-fortified orange juice | 140 mg calcium | 1 cup (249 g) | 349 mg | 35% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 169920 ('with added calcium'; no added vitamin D in this entry). Unfortified chilled orange juice is 11 mg/100 g (FDC 169100), so the entire figure is added mineral |
| Yogurt, plain, low fat | 183 mg calcium | 1 pot (6 oz, 170 g) | 311 mg | 31% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170886, including the 170 g container; CoFID 12-184 whole milk plain yogurt gives 200 mg/100 g. Fractional absorption 24.2% at a 523 mg load |
| Fortified soy drink (with added calcium, vitamins A and D) | 123 mg calcium | 1 cup (243 g) | 299 mg | 30% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 172456; CoFID 12-523 gives 130 mg/100 g. Unfortified soy drink is 25 mg/100 g (FDC 172446), so all of it is added. Calcium-carbonate-fortified soymilk absorbs as well as cow milk (21.1% against 21.7%, Zhao et al. 2005), so the fortificant is not the weak point; the sedimentation problem is |
| Milk, whole, 3.25% fat, with added vitamin D | 113 mg calcium | 1 cup / 240 ml (244 g) | 276 mg | 28% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 171265; CoFID 12-596 gives 120 mg/100 g. Fractional absorption across the cited table spans 21.7-66.0% depending on calcium load (14.7 to 563 mg), typically around 30-32% at a 250 mg load |
| Collard greens, boiled, drained | 141 mg calcium | 1 cup chopped (190 g) | 268 mg | 27% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170407. Low oxalate, so absorption is in the brassica range of roughly 48-53% |
| Tofu, firm, prepared with calcium sulfate and magnesium chloride (nigari) | 201 mg calcium | 1/2 cup (126 g) | 253 mg | 25% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 172448. The original submission described this as nigari-set 'rather than' calcium sulfate; USDA's own description is a dual-coagulant product using calcium sulfate and magnesium chloride together, so it cannot carry that contrast. The coagulant argument rests instead on the calcium-sulfate firm tofu above at 683 mg/100 g against MORI-NU silken extra firm at 31 mg/100 g (FDC 172462, a single branded product). The same food spans a twenty-fold range and the label has to be checked |
| Salmon, pink, canned, drained solids (bones included) | 283 mg calcium | 3 oz (85 g) | 241 mg | 24% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175175. The same fish with skin and bones removed drops to 60 mg/100 g (FDC 173724), a 4.7-fold penalty for picking the bones out. NIH ODS quotes a different canned-salmon entry at 181 mg per 3 oz, so 283 mg/100 g is specific to the ID cited |
| Jack mackerel, canned, drained solids with bones | 241 mg calcium | 3 oz (85 g) | 205 mg | 21% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 175121. Added on verification. The 85 g weight is the standard 3 oz serving used for the canned salmon row above |
| Cheddar cheese | 710 mg calcium | 1 slice (1 oz, 28 g) | 199 mg | 20% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 173414, including the 28 g slice; CoFID 12-346 English Cheddar gives 739 mg/100 g. Fractional absorption 37.4% at a 498 mg load |
| Turnip greens, boiled, drained | 137 mg calcium | 1 cup chopped (144 g) | 197 mg | 20% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170466 |
| Kale, boiled, drained | 150 mg calcium | 1 cup cooked (118 g) | 177 mg | 18% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 169238; CoFID 13-649 curly kale boiled gives 231 mg/100 g. Caveat added on verification: NIH ODS and newer FNDDS-based entries put cooked kale nearer 94 mg per cup, so this SR Legacy figure is the high end of current data. Fractional absorption 40.9% at a 288 mg load and 52.7% at an 83 mg load |
| Bok choy / pak choi, boiled, drained | 93 mg calcium | 1 cup shredded (170 g) | 158 mg | 16% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170391. The original claim that this is the best-absorbed calcium food is withdrawn: in Heaney et al. (J Food Sci 1993;58:1378-1380) at an identical 83 mg load, bok choy stems were 51.9% and leaves 52.0% alongside kale 52.7%, broccoli 47.8% and milk 46.3%, and the authors reported the differences as absolutely small and statistically insignificant and declined to rank them |
| Almonds, whole raw kernels | 269 mg calcium | 1 oz, about 23 kernels (28.35 g) | 76 mg | 8% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170567. Added on verification, which noted almonds appeared only under magnesium despite being a legitimate calcium source |
| Tahini (sesame paste) from roasted and toasted kernels | 426 mg calcium | 1 tbsp (15 g) | 64 mg | 6% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 170189; CoFID 14-847 gives 680 mg/100 g. Whole sesame seeds are 670 mg/100 g (CoFID 14-844), but a tablespoon is only 8-15 g, so the portion does the work |
| Bread, white, average (UK, flour fortified with calcium carbonate by law) | 155 mg calcium | 1 slice (32 g) | 50 mg | 5% of the 1,000 mg reference intake | CoFID 11-1145; CoFID wholemeal 11-981 is 106 mg/100 g, about 34 mg per slice, so here the white loaf beats the wholemeal one because the fortification applies to non-wholemeal flour. Added on verification: bread is one of the largest contributors to UK calcium intake and was absent from a list that otherwise uses CoFID throughout. Four slices a day is about 200 mg with no thought given to it |
| Spinach, boiled, drained (the answer that should not be given) | 136 mg calcium | 1 cup cooked (180 g) | 245 mg on the label, roughly 12 mg actually absorbed | 25% of the reference intake nominally, about 1% in reality | USDA FoodData Central FDC ID 168463 for composition; fractional absorption 5.1% at a 200 mg load from the Heaney and Weaver spinach work, tabulated in the Nutrients 2022 review of calcium absorption and food matrix effects (PMC8746734, PMID 35011055). Oxalate precipitates the calcium in the gut, so a cup of spinach delivers about the calcium of a tablespoon of milk. Placed last because the list is ordered by what a portion actually delivers, and on that basis this row belongs at the bottom despite a mid-table label figure |

- **To match the supplement dose:** Calcium supplements are sold at 500 to 600 mg elemental per tablet, and the trials that generated the safety signals gave 1,000 to 1,200 mg/day on top of whatever participants were already eating. Matching 1,000 mg from food takes 3.6 cups of milk, or 141 g of Cheddar, or about 85 g of Parmesan, or one 1/2-cup serving of calcium-set tofu (861 mg) plus one cup of cooked kale (177 mg) for 1,038 mg, or 3.2 pots of low-fat yogurt (about 546 g), or two small cans of sardines plus a glass of fortified soy drink (1,001 mg). The 'three pots of yogurt' route quoted in the original submission is wrong: 3 x 311 mg is 933 mg, 93% of the target and short of it. Every one of the corrected routes is a normal day of eating, so in a person whose diet already provides 1,000 mg a 1,000 mg supplement does not fill a deficit, it doubles the intake and delivers the added half as a single soluble bolus. The supplement is additive rather than corrective, which is the whole point of this entry.
- **Food versus supplement:** The harms recorded in the trials attach to supplements, not to dietary calcium, and the proposed mechanism is the form itself. Food calcium arrives in small amounts inside a matrix across a whole meal and produces no sharp rise in serum calcium; a 500-1,000 mg dose of calcium carbonate or citrate produces a measurable postprandial spike, and that spike is the hypothesised driver of the vascular signal. The specific findings: in the Women's Health Initiative randomised trial, 36,282 postmenopausal women given 1,000 mg calcium carbonate plus 400 IU vitamin D3 daily for a mean 7 years had more clinically diagnosed urinary tract stones, hazard ratio 1.17 (95% CI 1.02-1.34), unrelated to baseline calcium intake (PMID 21525191). Bolland and colleagues' meta-analysis of calcium supplements without co-administered vitamin D reported a 27-31% relative increase in myocardial infarction (BMJ 2010, DOI 10.1136/bmj.c3691). In the 10-year MESA follow-up of 5,448 adults, supplement users had higher incident coronary artery calcification while the highest quintile of dietary calcium was associated with lower risk (Anderson et al., DOI 10.1161/JAHA.116.003815). Observational work in the same populations consistently finds higher dietary calcium associated with fewer kidney stones, the opposite direction to the supplement result. This is contested territory: a 2016 National Osteoporosis Foundation and American Society for Preventive Cardiology review concluded there was no demonstrable cardiovascular harm at total intakes up to 2,000-2,500 mg/day, and the meta-analyses have been criticised for relying on secondary endpoints and self-reported events. The defensible summary is that food calcium has no such signal attached to it in any dataset, so where the diet already reaches the target the supplement carries risk without a corresponding benefit. Two practical form points: calcium carbonate requires gastric acid and must be taken with food, so it is a poor choice for anyone on a proton pump inhibitor or with achlorhydria (calcium citrate does not have this dependency), and doses above about 500 mg saturate absorption, so a single 1,000 mg tablet is worse value than two 500 mg doses.
- **Absorption:** Fractional absorption reorders the raw milligram ranking, though less dramatically than the original submission claimed. From the tabulated studies (Nutrients 2022 review, PMC8746734; Heaney et al., J Food Sci 1993;58:1378-1380), at an identical 83 mg load: kale 52.7%, bok choy leaves 52.0%, bok choy stems 51.9%, broccoli 47.8% and cow milk 46.3%, with the authors stating the differences were absolutely small and statistically insignificant. No vegetable can be called the best-absorbed calcium food, and the superlative attached to bok choy in the original entry is refuted by the experiment it cited. Across loads of 14.7 to 563 mg the milk range is 21.7-66.0%, about 30-32% at a typical 250 mg load, so milk's upper bound exceeds every brassica figure and the claim that brassicas beat milk holds only at matched loads where the difference is not significant. Calcium-set tofu is 39.0-49.3%, Cheddar 37.4%, yogurt 24.2%, calcium carbonate supplement roughly 22-24%, calcium citrate roughly 24%, and spinach 5.1%. Two conclusions survive. Low-oxalate brassicas comfortably beat the supplement forms per milligram consumed and match milk at comparable loads, so a cup of bok choy at 158 mg delivers about as much absorbed calcium as 250 ml of milk (roughly 82 mg against 83 mg). And spinach, rhubarb, beet greens and Swiss chard should be struck off calcium lists entirely, since oxalate precipitates the calcium in the gut and the food is close to useless for this purpose despite good label numbers. On fortified plant milks, the carbonate fortificant performs as well as dairy calcium: Zhao, Martin and Weaver (J Nutr 2005;135(10):2379, PMID 16177199) titled their paper on the finding that calcium-carbonate-fortified soymilk is equivalent to cow milk in young women (21.1% against 21.7% at a 250 mg load), and only the tricalcium phosphate form is genuinely lower at 18.1%. The sedimentation problem is real and separate: the added salt is insoluble and settles to the bottom of the carton, so an unshaken carton delivers a fraction of the label value and the last glass delivers a dose nobody intended.
- **Data note:** for composition and arithmetic. Verification traced every cited FDC ID to USDA SR Legacy April 2018 and every CoFID code to CoFID 2021, with every food description, per-100 g value and portion weight matching, and all per-portion multiplications reproducing. The corrections here are inference-level (three claims that misstated their own cited sources) plus one arithmetic slip on the yogurt route. Lower confidence is confined to items flagged in place: the whitebait, fortified milk powder and fortified oat cereal portion weights are conventions rather than dataset portions, the SR Legacy kale value sits at the high end of current data, and the silken tofu comparator is a single branded product

*References: WHI Jackson NEJM PMID 16481635; Bolland BMJ 2010 PMID 20671013; Bolland BMJ 2011 PMID 21505219; Cochrane PMID 24729336*

## 8. Dose and upper-limit reference

Where the US and EU disagree, both are given. These are not rounding differences.

| Nutrient | Effective dose / reference intake | US upper limit | EU upper limit |
|---|---|---|---|
| Vitamin D | RDA 600 IU (800 IU age 71+); supplement 800-2,000 IU/d | 4,000 IU (100 ug) | 4,000 IU (100 ug) |
| Vitamin A (preformed) | 900 ug RAE men / 700 ug women | 3,000 ug (10,000 IU) | see 2024 opinion |
| Vitamin E | RDA 15 mg | 1,000 mg supplemental | **300 mg, all sources** |
| Vitamin C | 90 mg men / 75 mg women (+35 smokers) | 2,000 mg | none set (SCF 2004) |
| Vitamin K | AI 120 ug men / 90 ug women (IOM); 70 ug (EFSA) | none set | none set |
| Folate | 400 ug DFE (600 ug pregnancy) | 1,000 ug **synthetic** | 1,000 ug |
| Vitamin B12 | 2.4 ug | none (not determinable) | none |
| Vitamin B6 | 1.3 mg (1.7 men / 1.5 women over 50) | **100 mg** | **12 mg** |
| Niacin | 16 mg NE | 35 mg synthetic | 900 mg nicotinamide |
| Calcium | 1,000-1,200 mg total | 2,500 mg (2,000 mg over 51) | 2,500 mg |
| Magnesium | 310-420 mg total | **350 mg supplemental only** | **250 mg supplemental** |
| Iron | 8 mg men / 18 mg women 19-50 / 27 mg pregnancy | 45 mg | no UL; safe level 40 mg |
| Zinc | 11 mg men / 8 mg women | 40 mg elemental | 25 mg |
| Selenium | 55 ug | **400 ug** | **255 ug** |
| Iodine | 150 ug (220 pregnancy, 290 lactation) | 1,100 ug | 600 ug |
| Potassium | AI 3,400 mg men / 2,600 mg women | none set | none set |
| Omega-3 EPA+DHA | 2-4 g/d for triglycerides | none | 5 g/d combined no concern; DHA alone 1 g/d |
| Caffeine | 3-6 mg/kg pre-exercise | not applicable | 400 mg/d; 200 mg single; **200 mg/d pregnancy** |
| Creatine | 3-5 g/d | none set | none set |
| Protein | 1.6 g/kg/d plateau; RDA 0.8 g/kg | none set | none set |
| Fibre | AI 25-38 g/d | none set | none set |

> **Elemental mass is the commonest labelling deception in the mineral aisle.** Magnesium oxide is about 60.3% elemental magnesium, citrate about 16%. A capsule labelled 500 mg magnesium citrate delivers roughly 80 mg of magnesium. Ferrous sulfate 325 mg delivers 65 mg of elemental iron.

## 9. Drug interactions worth knowing

| Drug | Supplements that matter |
|---|---|
| **Warfarin and other vitamin K antagonists** | Vitamin K1 and K2 (absolute), high-dose fish oil, vitamin E, ginkgo, glucosamine plus chondroitin (20 FDA MedWatch reports of raised INR), CoQ10, curcumin, St John wort (lowers INR by enzyme induction), and any multivitamin containing vitamin K, which is a dosing variable rather than inert background. |
| **SSRIs, SNRIs, MAOIs, tramadol, triptans** | St John wort, 5-HTP, SAMe, saffron and tryptophan all carry serotonin syndrome risk. Also note 8 of 31 melatonin products contained undeclared serotonin. |
| **Ciclosporin, tacrolimus and other transplant immunosuppressants** | Berberine raised ciclosporin trough by 88.9%. St John wort has caused graft loss. Also quercetin, curcumin, and any live probiotic through translocation risk. |
| **Levothyroxine** | Calcium, iron and magnesium chelate it, so separate by 4 hours. Ashwagandha raises T4. Iodine and kelp. Biotin causes assay interference rather than a true interaction, which can mimic thyroid disease on a blood test. |
| **Metformin** | Depletes B12 by about 19% over 4.3 years. Berberine is additive on glucose lowering. |
| **Statins** | Red yeast rice contains monacolin K, chemically identical to lovastatin, so it is an unlabelled statin sold without a prescription. Quercetin and curcumin act via CYP3A4. |
| **Chemotherapy and radiotherapy** | High-dose antioxidants (vitamins A, C, E, carotenoids, CoQ10) and vitamin B12. See the SWOG S0221 figures in the Avoid section. |
| **Antibiotics (tetracyclines, fluoroquinolones)** | Magnesium, calcium, zinc and iron all chelate them. Separate by 2 to 4 hours. |
| **Nitrates and PDE5 inhibitors** | Dietary nitrate and beetroot are additively hypotensive. Adulterated sexual-enhancement products containing undeclared sildenafil are the serious version of this hazard. |
| **ACE inhibitors, ARBs, spironolactone** | Potassium supplements and salt substitutes risk hyperkalaemia. The SSaSS salt-substitution trial explicitly excluded chronic kidney disease. |

## 10. How to buy, if you are buying

1. Decide what deficiency or endpoint you are treating. If you cannot name it, the answer is no supplement.
2. Prefer single ingredients at tested doses. Proprietary blends hide doses behind two words, and that is where adulteration lives.
3. Know what each certification does NOT cover. Informed Sport tests every manufactured batch against the WADA list and is the only appropriate standard under anti-doping strict liability. NSF Certified for Sport screens 280 or more banned substances on a variable batch model. USP Verified covers identity, declared potency, dissolution and contaminant limits, and does NOT screen for substances banned in sport, so a USP Verified product can legally contain a prohibited stimulant. None of the three assesses whether the product works.
4. A seller-supplied certificate of analysis is not certification. Ask which lot it covers, which laboratory produced it, whether that laboratory is ISO 17025 accredited, and who chose the laboratory.
5. Categorically avoid anything marketed for erections, rapid fat loss, or anabolic and test-booster effects, from any retailer, at any price.
6. Read the US disclaimer literally. The sentence stating that the claim has not been evaluated by the FDA and the product is not intended to diagnose, treat, cure or prevent any disease is the regulator actual assessment of the product, and it is the most informative sentence on the bottle.
7. Treat FDA registered facility and FDA regulated as marketing phrases. Neither is a safety finding.
8. Tell your doctor and pharmacist what you take, especially before surgery, before starting an anticoagulant, and during cancer treatment. Post-market surveillance is the entire safety system and its sensitivity depends on clinicians reporting.

> **An honest limitation on certification.** No published head-to-head study shows that certified products fail less often than uncertified ones. The case for certification is inferential.

## 11. Method, and what to distrust here

**Construction.** Ten domain researchers ran live literature searches under a rule that only papers located in a search result with a resolvable PMID or DOI could be cited. Each submission then went to an independent adversarial fact-checker instructed to default to does-not-exist if a paper could not be confirmed, to re-check every dose against NIH ODS, EFSA and IOM/NASEM, and to flag over-generous grades.

> **Result of the fabrication hunt: nothing.** Across 428 citation checks in ten domains the verifiers found zero fabricated papers, zero invented PMIDs and zero wrong DOIs.

| Defect | Correction applied |
|---|---|
| Evidence grades set roughly half a grade too generously | Corrected here. Over-the-counter fish oil is no longer Grade A for cardiovascular outcomes, calcium is no longer Grade A for fractures, dietary nitrate and beta-alanine moved to C, and vitamin K2 as MK-4 moved to D. |
| Five materially wrong doses | Including a sickle-cell L-glutamine figure that overstated the FDA-approved maximum, an EFSA child-selenium table transposed from the infant row, a VSL#3 CFU figure understated fourfold, and an MSM trial dose reported as 3-6 g/d when the trial used 2 g/d. |
| Selective reporting by omission | Results quoted for the flattering subgroup with the null overall effect left out. Corrected throughout, most importantly for beta-alanine and dietary nitrate, where the capacity result was quoted and the null performance result was not. |
| One unverifiable regulatory claim | A stated Polish limit on ashwagandha could not be traced to any primary or indexed source and has been removed. The Danish ban is corroborated and remains. |

**Residual limitations.** NIH ODS fact sheets return HTTP 403 to automated retrieval, so reference-intake values come from the underlying IOM/NASEM reports and EFSA opinions. Several entries rest on single unreplicated trials and say so in their Cons. Market-size figures are unaudited commercial estimates.

## 12. Known gaps in this pass

| Missing item | Why it matters |
|---|---|
| **Red yeast rice** | Contains monacolin K, chemically identical to lovastatin. It is an unlabelled statin sold without a prescription, carrying rhabdomyolysis, hepatotoxicity and statin drug-interaction risk. |
| **Enteric-coated peppermint oil** | Sold in the same aisle, with multiple meta-analyses in irritable bowel syndrome and a stronger effect estimate than almost any probiotic reviewed here. |
| **Biotin** | One of the largest single-B-vitamin retail categories, routinely sold at 5,000-10,000 ug against a 30 ug adequate intake, on essentially no efficacy evidence. It carries an active FDA safety communication for interference with streptavidin-biotin immunoassays including troponin. |
| **Sodium bicarbonate** | Named on the IOC short list of supplements with robust performance evidence alongside caffeine, creatine, beta-alanine and nitrate, and it has its own ISSN position stand. |
| **Urolithin A (Mitopure)** | The flagship commercial mitophagy and longevity product, with company-run randomised trials that were not assessed in this pass. |
| **Lavender oil / Silexan** | A licensed anxiolytic medicine in Germany with multiple placebo-controlled and active-comparator trials in generalised anxiety disorder, and a stronger evidence base than rhodiola, bacopa or lion mane. |
| **Calcifediol / 25-hydroxyvitamin D3** | Sold over the counter across Europe and by prescription in the US. It is two to three times more potent per microgram than D3 and raises serum 25(OH)D far faster. |
| **CBD and hemp-derived cannabinoids** | The largest single regulatory gap: excluded from the US dietary supplement definition by the drug-exclusion rule, and treated as a novel food requiring authorisation by the UK FSA. |

---

Compiled 29 July 2026 from a 20-agent research workflow: 10 domain literature sweeps plus 10 independent adversarial verification passes. 456 papers, 428 citations re-checked, 1,309 tool calls. This document is a research synthesis and not medical advice.
